# Majmu' al-Fatawa - Tome 4

## Page 1323

Part Four
The Book of the Detailed Exposition of Creed

In the name of Allah, the Most Merciful, the Especially Merciful. All praise belongs to Allah alone, and blessings and peace be upon him after whom there is no prophet.

Shaykh al-Islām Ahmad ibn Taymiyyah—may Allah sanctify his soul—was asked:

“What is your view of the approach of the early generations to matters of creed and the approach of those who came later? Which of the two is correct, and which do you yourselves follow?

“And concerning the people of hadith: are they more likely to be correct than others? Are they the ones meant by the saved sect? Have branches of knowledge emerged after them that they did not know, but others came to know?”

He replied:

All praise belongs to Allah. A detailed discussion of these questions could fill volumes, but we shall indicate the most important points. Allah alone grants success.

Allah Most High says:

“Whoever opposes the Messenger after guidance has become clear to him and follows other than

---

## Page 1324

the way of the believers, We shall leave him to the path he has chosen and cast him into Hell—an evil destination.”

Allah has testified to the faith of the Companions of His Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—and those who followed them in goodness. Thus, it is known with certainty that they are the ones intended in this noble verse. Allah Most High says:

“And the first forerunners among the Emigrants and the Helpers and those who followed them in goodness—Allah is pleased with them, and they are pleased with Him. He has prepared for them gardens beneath which rivers flow, wherein they will abide forever. That is the great triumph.”

And Allah Most High says:

“Allah was certainly pleased with the believers when they pledged allegiance to you beneath the tree. He knew what was in their hearts, so He sent down tranquillity upon them and rewarded them with a victory near at hand.”

Since it is established that whoever follows a path other than theirs will be left by Allah to what he has chosen and made to enter Hell, their approach to creed includes:

“Belief in the attributes and names of Allah Most High”

with which He has described and named Himself in His revealed Book or through His Messenger, without adding to them, subtracting from them, going beyond them, explaining them, reinterpreting them in a way contrary to their apparent meaning, or likening them to the attributes of creatures or the characteristics of created beings. Rather, they accepted them as they came and entrusted knowledge of them to the One who uttered them, and their meaning to the One who spoke them.

One of them said—and this is also reported from al-Shāfiʿī:

“I believe in what has come from Allah and in what has come from the Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—in accordance with what the Messenger of Allah intended.”

They knew that the One who spoke these words was truthful, with no doubt concerning His truthfulness, so they believed Him. They did not know the reality of their meaning, so they remained silent about what they did not know. Later generations received this from earlier ones, and some of them exhorted

---

## Page 1325

one another to follow faithfully and to stop where their predecessors had stopped. They warned against going beyond them or departing from their way, and explained their path and approach to us. We hope that Allah Most High will make us among those who follow their example in explaining what they explained and following the path they followed.

The evidence that their approach was as we have described is that they transmitted to us the magnificent Quran and the reports of the Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—as people who affirmed their truth, believed in them, and accepted them, neither doubting them nor questioning the truthfulness of the one who spoke them. They did not explain the passages concerning the divine attributes, reinterpret them, or liken them to the attributes of created beings. Had they done any of this, it would have been transmitted from them; it could not have been concealed altogether. For colluding to conceal what needs to be transmitted and known is impermissible, being as reprehensible as colluding to transmit falsehood and commit unlawful acts.

Indeed, so scrupulously did they refrain from discussing this that, whenever they saw someone asking about ambiguous passages, they would firmly restrain him—sometimes with harsh words, sometimes with blows, and sometimes by turning away in a manner that showed their intense aversion to his questioning.

Thus, when ʿUmar—may Allah be pleased with him—heard that Subaygh was asking about ambiguous passages, he prepared date-palm stalks for him. While ʿUmar was delivering a sermon, Subaygh stood up and asked him about:

“By those that scatter far and wide,”

“and those that bear a heavy load,”

and the verses that follow.

ʿUmar came down and said:

“Had I found your head shaven, I would have struck your head with the sword.”

He then ordered that he be severely beaten and sent him to Basra, instructing its people not to sit with him. There he became like a mangy camel: whenever he approached a gathering, they would say, “The Commander of the Faithful’s command!” and disperse, leaving him alone. Eventually, he repented and swore by Allah that nothing remained of what he had previously harboured within himself. ʿUmar then permitted people to sit with him.

---

## Page 1326

Then, when the Khawārij rose in rebellion, someone came to him and said, “Now is your opportunity!” He replied, “No! The righteous servant’s admonition has benefited me.”

When Mālik ibn Anas—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked, “Abū ‘Abd Allāh, ‘The Most Merciful rose over the Throne’—how did He rise over it?” Mālik lowered his head and broke into a sweat. The people waited to hear his response. He then raised his head towards the questioner and said:

“Rising over is not unknown; its manner cannot be comprehended. Belief in it is obligatory, and asking about it is an innovation. I consider you an evil man.”

He ordered that the man be removed, and he was taken out. Whoever interprets rising over as taking dominion has given an answer different from Mālik’s and followed a path other than his. Mālik’s answer—may Allah have mercy on him—concerning rising over is fully satisfactory and sufficient for all the divine attributes, such as descent, coming, the hand, the face, and others.

Thus, concerning descent, for example, one should say:

“Descent is known; its manner is unknown. Belief in it is obligatory, and asking about it is an innovation.”

The same should be said concerning all the other attributes, since they stand on the same footing as rising over, which is mentioned in the Quran and Sunnah.

It is also established that Muhammad ibn al-Hasan—the companion of Abū Hanīfa—said:

“All the jurists, from east to west, have agreed upon believing in the Quran and the hadith transmitted by trustworthy narrators from the Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—concerning the attributes of the Lord, Mighty and Majestic, without explanation or

---

## Page 1327

description or likening Him to creation. Whoever explains any of this has departed from the way of the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—and separated himself from the community. For they neither described nor explained, but believed in what is in the Book and Sunnah and then remained silent. Whoever adopts Jahm’s position has separated himself from the community.” End of quotation.

Consider—may Allah have mercy on you—how this imam reported consensus on this matter. There is no good in anything that departs from their consensus. Had refraining from reinterpreting these attributes entailed attributing corporeality to Allah, they would have shunned it and reinterpreted them, for they knew better than anyone else in the Muslim community what is possible and what is impossible with respect to Allah.

It is also established that Ismā‘īl ibn ‘Abd al-Rahmān al-Sābūnī said:

“Those devoted to hadith who adhere to the Book and Sunnah know their Lord—blessed and exalted is He—through His attributes, which His Book and revelation declare and to which His Messenger testified, as conveyed in authentic reports transmitted by upright, trustworthy narrators. They do not believe that His attributes resemble those of His creation, nor do they specify their manner as those who liken Him to creation do. Nor do they distort words from their proper meanings as the Mu‘tazila and Jahmiyya do. Allah has protected ‘Ahl al-Sunnah’ from distortion and assigning a manner, and has blessed them with understanding and knowledge. Thus they have followed the path of affirming Allah’s oneness and His freedom from imperfection, abandoning the denial of His attributes and the likening of Him to creation. In denying imperfections, they have contented themselves with His words—exalted is He who says:

‘There is nothing like unto Him, and He is the All-Hearing, the All-Seeing.’

And His words, Most High:

‘And there is none comparable to Him.’”

Sa‘īd ibn Jubayr said:

“Whatever was unknown to the participants in the Battle of Badr is not part of the religion.”

It is established that al-Rabī‘ ibn Sulaymān said:

“I asked al-Shāfi‘ī—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—

---

## Page 1328

about the attributes of Allah Most High. He replied:

‘It is forbidden for minds to conceive a likeness of Allah Most High; for imaginations to set bounds to Him; for conjectures to reach definitive conclusions about Him; for souls to speculate about Him; for innermost thoughts to delve deeply; for passing thoughts to encompass Him; and for minds to comprehend anything beyond what He has described of Himself, or conveyed through His Prophet’—may blessings and peace be upon him.”

It is established that al-Hasan al-Basrī said:

“Muttarif spoke from this wooden pulpit words that had never been spoken before him and will never be spoken after him.”

They asked, “What were they, Abū Sa‘īd?”

He said:

“Praise be to Allah: part of faith in Him is to know nothing of Him beyond what He has described of Himself.”

Sahnūn said:

“Part of knowing Allah is to remain silent about anything beyond what He has described of Himself.”

It is established that al-Humaydī, Abū Bakr ‘Abd Allāh ibn al-Zubayr, said, “The foundations of the Sunnah”—then mentioned several matters before saying:

“As for what the Quran and hadith declare, such as:

‘The Jews say, “Allah’s hand is fettered.” May their hands be fettered!’

and:

‘And the heavens will be rolled up in His right hand,’

and similar passages in the Quran and hadith, we neither add to them nor explain them. We stop where the Quran and Sunnah stop, and we say:

‘The Most Merciful rose over the Throne.’

Whoever claims otherwise is a Jahmī.”

Thus, the way of the early generations—may Allah be pleased with them—is to affirm the attributes, take them according to their apparent meaning, and negate any modality for them. For discussion of the attributes derives from discussion of the Essence: affirming the Essence means affirming existence, not affirming a modality; the same applies to affirming the attributes. And on

---

## Page 1329

this basis all the early generations proceeded. Were we to recount all the statements of the early generations on this subject that we have encountered, we would go beyond the purpose of this answer. Anyone whose aim is truth and making clear what is right will find what we have presented sufficient. As for anyone intent on disputation, idle controversy, and obstinate denial, a lengthier discussion would only lead him further from the straight path. Allah alone grants success.

What we have asserted concerning the position of the early generations—may Allah be pleased with them—has been established by what we have transmitted from them, both generally and in detail, and by the acknowledgment of all the scholars versed in transmitted knowledge.

I know of none of them who disagreed on this matter. Indeed, I have received reports that even some of their leading scholars who adopted reinterpretation of these verses and reports acknowledged that the position of the early generations concerning them was as we have stated. I also found this acknowledgment in a book by one of their teachers, who said:

“Our scholars differed concerning the reports about the divine attributes. Some let them stand as they came, without explanation or reinterpretation, while denying any resemblance to created beings. This is the position of the early generations.”

Thus, the acknowledgment of the opposing party establishes consensus on the correctness of what we have stated. Praise be to Allah. How excellent are the words reported from ‘Abd al-‘Azīz ibn ‘Abd Allāh ibn Abī Salama, who said:

“Adhere firmly to the Sunnah, for, by Allah’s permission, it will safeguard you. The Sunnah was established to be followed and to serve as the limit beyond which one does not venture. Those who established it knew the slips, errors, folly, and excessive probing involved in departing from it. Be content for yourself with what they were content with for themselves. For they stopped where they did on the basis of knowledge, and refrained with penetrating insight. They were better able to uncover these matters, and better qualified to expound them in detail, had that been appropriate.

---

## Page 1330

And they were indeed the foremost, and word had reached them from their Prophet of the disagreements that would arise after the first three generations. If what you follow is guidance, then you have reached it before they did! And if you say that something new arose after them, it was introduced only by someone who followed a path other than theirs, disdained their way, and preferred what his own mind had fashioned to what they had received from their Prophet and what those who followed them faithfully had received from them.

They explained enough of these matters and spoke of them in a way that dispels doubt. Whoever falls short of them is remiss, and whoever goes beyond them is excessive. Some fell short of them and thus strayed away; others sought to go beyond them and thus went to excess. They, however, followed straight guidance between these two extremes.”

---

## Page 1331

Section

As for their being more knowledgeable and wiser than those who came after them, and the charge of ignorance and vacuity applying more aptly to their opponents, we shall demonstrate this through rational comparison, without appealing to faith in the Messenger itself. As Allah says:

“We will show them Our signs in the horizons and within themselves until it becomes clear to them that it is the truth.”

He thus states that He will show them visible, observable signs until it becomes clear to them that the Quran is true. Then He says:

“Is it not sufficient that your Lord is a Witness over all things?”

That is, through what Allah, your Lord, declares in the Quran and His testimony to that effect.

We say: It is well known that the people of hadith share with every group the qualities of excellence with which that group adorns itself, while surpassing them in qualities they do not possess. Anyone who disputes with them must invoke some other method concerning the points on which he disagrees with them—such as reason, analogy, considered opinion, discourse, reflection, inference, argument, debate, spiritual unveiling, spiritual address, ecstasy, spiritual taste, and the like.

The people of hadith possess the purest and finest elements of all these methods. They are the most complete in intellect, the fairest in analogy, the most accurate in opinion, the soundest in discourse, the most correct in reflection, the best guided in inference, the most upright in debate, the fullest in discernment, the truest in inspiration, the keenest in vision and spiritual unveiling, and the most accurate in hearing

---

## Page 1332

and spiritual address, and the greatest and finest in ecstasy and spiritual taste. This holds for Muslims in relation to all other nations, and for the people of Sunnah and hadith in relation to all other religious communities. Anyone who surveys the conditions of the world finds that Muslims have sharper and sounder intellects, and that in a short time they attain many times more of the realities of knowledge and practice than others attain over centuries and generations. Likewise, you find the people of Sunnah and hadith enjoying these advantages.

This is because belief in established truth strengthens understanding and makes it sound. Allah Most High says:

“And those who are guided—He increases them in guidance.”

And He says:

“If they had done what they were admonished to do, it would have been better for them and would have strengthened them more firmly. Then We would have granted them a great reward from Us, and We would have guided them to a straight path.”

This can sometimes be known by examining the points of dispute between them and others: you will find no issue on which they have been opposed without its becoming clear that the truth is with them. Sometimes it is known through their opponents’ acknowledgment and their return to them rather than to others, or through their testimony that their opponents are misguided and ignorant. Sometimes it is known through the testimony of the believers, who are Allah’s witnesses on earth. And sometimes it is known from the fact that every group relies on them in its disagreements with another, and levels harsher charges of misguidance at everyone who opposes it than it levels at them.

As for the testimony of the believers, who are Allah’s witnesses on earth, this is an evident matter, known through direct observation and widely transmitted testimony to anyone who has heard what Muslims say. You will find no one in the Muslim community held in greater esteem than they are. Nor will you find others esteemed except to the extent that they agree with them, just as they are disparaged only to the extent that they differ from them.

---

## Page 1333

so much so that you will find all their opponents acknowledging this when the moment of truth comes, as Imām Ahmad said:

“The sign distinguishing us from them will be the day of funerals.”

During life, because people share worldly interests, a man extols his own group. At death, however, people at large cannot but acknowledge the truth. Thus, no funeral like his has been known in Islam. Al-Mutawakkil measured the area where the funeral prayer was offered for him and found that there were 1,600,000 worshippers, apart from those who prayed in inns and houses. On that day, 20,000 Jews and Christians embraced Islam.

He attained distinction in the Muslim community only through following hadith and the Sunnah. Likewise, al-Shāfi'ī, Ishāq, and others attained distinction in Islam only through following the people of hadith and Sunnah. Al-Bukhārī and others like him attained distinction for the same reason. Mālik, al-Awzā'ī, al-Thawrī, Abū Hanīfah, and others likewise gained distinction throughout the Muslim community, and their views were accepted, because of their agreement with hadith and the Sunnah. Those among them who were criticized were criticized only concerning matters in which they had not succeeded in following hadith and the Sunnah—either because the relevant reports had not reached them, because they considered their indication weak, or because they considered other evidence stronger.

The same applies to doctrinal matters known through transmitted reports. No member or leader of any group has gained esteem in the Muslim community except through the affirmation and adherence to the Sunnah found in him. Take first the Mu'tazilah—the champions of speculative theology. They are praised and esteemed by their followers, and by those who overlook their faults on account of their merits in the eyes of Muslims, only because of their agreement with the doctrine of the adherents of affirmation, Sunnah, and hadith, and because of their refutation of some of the Rāfidah’s departures from the Sunnah and hadith concerning the legitimate leadership of the caliphs,

---

## Page 1334

and the uprightness of the Companions, acceptance of transmitted reports, distortion of words from their proper contexts, exaggeration concerning 'Alī, and similar matters. Likewise, the early Shī'ah were preferred over the Mu'tazilah because they differed from them in affirming the divine attributes, divine decree, intercession, and similar matters. They likewise earned praise for opposing the Khawārij in declaring 'Alī, 'Uthmān, and others unbelievers, and in declaring Muslims unbelievers because of their sins. They also earned praise for differing from the Murji'ah by including obligatory deeds within faith. For this reason, they espoused the doctrine of an intermediate status between belief and unbelief, although they did not find their way to the unadulterated Sunnah.

Likewise, the speculative theologians among the adherents of affirmation—such as the Kullābiyyah, Karrāmiyyah, and Ash'ariyyah—were accepted and followed, and earned praise among the Muslim community at large, only because they affirmed the foundations of faith: the existence of the Creator and His attributes, and prophethood; because they refuted the unbelievers among the polytheists and the People of the Book and exposed the contradictions in their arguments. They likewise earned praise for refuting the various doctrines of the Jahmiyyah, Mu'tazilah, Rāfidah, and Qadariyyah that conflict with those of the people of the Sunnah and the community.

Their merits thus fall into two categories: agreeing with the people of Sunnah and hadith, or refuting those who oppose the Sunnah and hadith by exposing the contradictions in their arguments. No one has followed the doctrine of al-Ash'arī or others like him except for one or both of these qualities. Every Muslim, including their scholars, who loves and supports him loves and supports him only

---

## Page 1335

for these reasons. Those who write about his merits and defend him against attacks and curses—such as al-Bayhaqī, Abū al-Qāsim al-Qushayrī, and Ibn 'Asākir of Damascus—support their case only by citing his statements that agree with the people of Sunnah and hadith, or his refutations of their opponents’ views. These two qualities alone form the basis of their defence of him before the Muslim community, its scholars, and its rulers. Had he not been among those of his kind closest to these qualities, they would have placed him alongside his peers who lacked them, such as his first teacher, “Abū 'Alī,” and that teacher’s son, “Abū Hāshim.”

But his agreement with the doctrine of Sunnah and hadith concerning the divine attributes, divine decree, the imamate, virtues, intercession, the Reservoir, the Bridge, and the Balance, together with his refutations of the Mu'tazilah, Qadariyyah, Rāfidah, and Jahmiyyah and his exposure of their contradictions, warranted distinguishing him from those others and acknowledging his due and his standing:

“Allah has already set for everything a measure.”

His agreement with Sunnah and hadith earned him the acceptance and following that he attained. Yet the kind of agreement that involves overcoming an opponent and exposing the falsity of his position is akin to the work of a victorious warrior.

Thus, one who refutes the people of religious innovation is a warrior striving in Allah’s cause. Indeed, “Yahyā ibn Yahyā” used to say:

“Defending the Sunnah is better than jihad.”

A warrior may or may not be just in his conduct, and he may even be wicked, as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Indeed, Allah supports this religion through a wicked man and through people who have no share in the Hereafter.”

For this reason, the established Sunnah has been to fight under every commander, whether righteous or wicked. Jihad is an act for which its doer undoubtedly deserves outward appreciation.

---

## Page 1336

and, when accompanied by a good intention, it merits appreciation inwardly as well as outwardly. It deserves this appreciation because it supports the Sunnah and the religion. Likewise, whoever champions Islam and the Sunnah deserves appreciation in this respect. People of every kind are thus praised by Allah, His Messenger, and His believing servants according to the extent to which they conform to Allah’s religion, His Messenger’s Sunnah, and His law; for praise is given only for good deeds.

Good deeds are those that accord with obedience to Allah and His Messenger: affirming the truth of what Allah has conveyed and obeying His commands. This is the Sunnah. All goodness—by the consensus of the Muslim community—lies in what the Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, brought. Likewise, those who depart from the Sunnah, the sacred law, and obedience to Allah and His Messenger are censured only for contravening these. Whenever people of faith criticize anyone—whether a scholar, a ruler, or someone else—they do so only because he has contravened the Sunnah and the sacred law.

On this basis, the early generations and the imams censured the proponents of speculative theology and the theologians who affirm the divine attributes, such as Ibn Karrām, Ibn Kullāb, and al-Ash‘arī. Those eminent figures of the Muslim community and its acknowledged imams—from all groups of jurists, hadith scholars, and Sufis—who spoke critically of him did so only concerning matters in which they said these theologians had opposed the Sunnah and hadith: either because it had escaped their notice, because they had turned away from it, or because a principle of analogical reasoning they had established required them to reject it. Something similar also occurs in [theoretical] matters (*).

> **Footnotes:**
> (*) Shaykh Nāsir ibn Hamad al-Fahd writes (p. 255): “Perhaps the intended reading is al-‘amaliyya (practical).”

---

## Page 1337

For a Muslim of sound faith goes against a scriptural text only because he is unaware of it or believes that what conflicts with it is sound. Yet when the Sunnah is clear and the matter weighty, such opposition arises from negligence and transgression on his part. He therefore deserves a degree of censure that he would not deserve in the case of an obscure text. Likewise, contravening the Sunnah is more serious in matters that cause division and disagreement.

For this reason, many rulers and scholars concerned themselves with the cause of Islam and with fighting its enemies, to the point that they began cursing the Rāfida, the Jahmiyya, and others from the pulpits. Indeed, they cursed every group in which they perceived a religious innovation.

Thus they cursed the Kullābiyya and the Ash‘ariyya, as happened in the dominion of the emir “Mahmūd ibn Sabuktakīn” and during the early period of Seljuq rule. The caliph al-Qādir likewise concerned himself with this at times and consulted the Mu‘tazilite jurists. They brought before him the case of the judge “Abū Bakr” and others like him, and intended to act against him, so that he had to go into hiding. He concealed himself only behind the school of Imam Ahmad and his agreement with it. Then al-Nizām took office, and they sought to have the curse lifted. They requested legal opinions from various Iraqi jurists, including al-Dāmghānī, the Hanafi, and Abū Ishāq al-Shīrāzī. The opinions of these two constitute an argument against the Hanafis and Shāfi‘īs in Khurasan.

It has been said that Abū Ishāq asked to be excused from this, but they compelled him. They ruled that cursing them was impermissible and that anyone who cursed them should receive discretionary punishment. Al-Dāmghānī gave as his reason that they were a group of Muslims. Abū Ishāq added that they had defended the Sunnah and refuted the proponents of religious innovation who opposed it. Thus the mufti could justify lifting censure only on the basis of conformity to the Sunnah and hadith.

[Likewise, among the legal opinions of the jurist Abū Muhammad, I saw a lengthy opinion containing some good points. He had been asked about several issues, and in it he said] (*):

> **Footnotes:**
> (*) Shaykh Nāsir ibn Hamad al-Fahd writes (p. 28):
> Some scholars have stated that the jurist Abū Muhammad mentioned here is Abū Muhammad al-Juwaynī, the father of Imām al-Haramayn. Others—including the editor of the standalone edition of Naqd al-Mantiq (Critique of Logic), p. 14—have identified him as al-‘Izz ibn ‘Abd al-Salām. The latter identification is correct for three reasons:
> 
> First: The shaykh frequently refers to Ibn ‘Abd al-Salām—may Allah have mercy on them both—as Abū Muhammad and describes him as a jurist. He mentions him in this treatise (4/65), saying: “The jurist Abū Muhammad ibn ‘Abd al-Salām was…” Elsewhere (2/131), he says: “The jurist Abū Muhammad ibn ‘Abd al-Salām said… The jurist Abū Muhammad therefore declared him an unbeliever.” There are other examples as well.
> 
> Second: In this treatise (4/88), the shaykh—may Allah have mercy on him—says: “As for Abū al-Ma‘ālī’s approach, Abū Muhammad followed him in his jurisprudence…” Yet Abū Muhammad al-Juwaynī was Abū al-Ma‘ālī’s father.
> 
> Third: Abū Muhammad al-Juwaynī and Abū Muhammad ibn ‘Abd al-Salām—may Allah have mercy on them both—differed in their partisan attachment to Ash‘arism, as their biographies and doctrinal beliefs show.

---

## Page 1338

“And it is not permissible to use mosques for singing, dancing, or mingling with beardless youths. Anyone who does so must receive a severe, deterrent discretionary punishment. As for wearing hoops, armlets, chains, and shackles, and wearing rings of iron or copper, this is religious innovation and ostentatious display. The worst matters are those newly introduced into religion. These are theirs in this world; they are the attire of the people of the Fire, and they will be theirs in the Hereafter if they die persisting in this. It is not permissible to prostrate to anyone other than Allah, whether living or dead, or to kiss graves. Anyone who does so must receive discretionary punishment. Anyone who curses a Muslim must receive severe discretionary punishment for doing so.

“A believer is not given to cursing—and what he acknowledged concerning the curse returning upon him.”

He said:

“It is not lawful to pray at graves or to walk over them, whether for men or women. Nor may they be made into places of prayer, for:

‘Allah’s wrath became intense against people who took the graves of their prophets as places of worship.’”

He said:

“As for scholars cursing the leading figures of the Ash‘ariyya, anyone who curses them must receive discretionary punishment, and the curse returns upon him. Whoever curses someone who does not deserve to be cursed brings the curse upon himself. The scholars defend the branches of religion, while the Ash‘ariyya defend its foundations.”

He said:

“As for their entering fires, this is a trial for anyone who does not hold fast to the Quran, and a cause of misguidance for those who see them, just as people are put to trial by what appears at the hands of the Dajjāl. Anyone through whom an extraordinary occurrence takes place must be weighed against the standard of revealed law. If he is upright, what occurs through him is a divinely granted wonder; if he is not upright, it is a trial, like the raising of the dead that occurs at the hands of the Dajjāl, and the appearance of his paradise and his fire. For Allah leads astray those who have no share of goodness through what appears at the hands of such people.

---

## Page 1339

But as for someone who holds fast to the sacred revealed law, even if he were to see one of these people flying through the air or walking on water, he would know that this is a trial for Allah’s servants.” End of quotation.

Thus, the jurist Abū Muhammad likewise prohibited cursing them and ordered discretionary punishment for anyone who did so only because of the “foundations of religion” that they defended. This means what we have already mentioned: agreement with the Quran, sunnah, and hadith, and refuting those who oppose the Quran, sunnah, and hadith.

For this reason, Shaykh Abū Ishāq used to say:

“The Ash‘ariyya gained acceptance among people only through their affiliation with the Hanbalīs.”

This is evident in his case and in that of the leading scholars among his followers, in their books and writings before the Qushayrī controversy broke out in Baghdad. Hence, Abū al-Qāsim ibn ‘Asākir said in his account of his virtues:

“In earlier times, the Hanbalīs and the Ash‘arīs remained united, without division, until the controversy involving Ibn al-Qushayrī arose.”

Both before and after that controversy, you will find no one praising al-Ash‘arī except where he agrees with the sunnah and hadith; nor do those who criticize him do so except for his opposition to the sunnah and hadith. This constitutes a consensus among all these groups on venerating the sunnah and hadith, and their testimonies agree that the truth lies therein.

For this reason, you find that those among them who agree most closely with the leading scholars of the sunnah and hadith are held in greater esteem by them all than those whose agreement is less. Al-Ash‘arī himself, being closer to the position of Imam Ahmad and the leading scholars of the sunnah who preceded him, was held by them in greater esteem than his followers. Likewise, the judge Abū Bakr ibn al-Bāqillānī, being the closest among them to that position, was held by them in greater esteem than others. As for figures such as the scholar Abū al-Ma‘ālī,

---

## Page 1340

and Abū Hāmid, and others like them who departed from his principles on certain points, you will find that they are esteemed only for those matters in which they agree with the sunnah and hadith. Most of this they adopted from al-Shāfi‘ī’s school of jurisprudence, which accords with the sunnah and hadith, and from what they stated concerning the foundations of religion that accords with the sunnah and hadith, together with their refutation of what contradicts the sunnah and hadith. It is on this basis that they claim adherence to the sunnah and lay claim to it; otherwise, that claim would not be valid.

The Rāfida (Rejectionists) and the Qarmatians—their scholars and rulers—had gained strength in the early days of Seljuk rule, until they took control of Syria and Iraq. They removed the caliph al-Qā’im from Baghdad to Tikrit and imprisoned him there during the well-known uprising of al-Basāsīrī. The Seljuks subsequently came, defeated them, conquered Syria and Iraq, subdued them in Khurasan, and confined them to Egypt.

During their rule, there were ministers such as Nizām al-Mulk and scholars such as Abū al-Ma‘ālī al-Juwaynī. Through upholding the sunnah and refuting the innovations of these groups and others like them, they attained a standing within the Muslim community commensurate with those efforts.

Likewise, the later followers of Mālik who agreed with him, such as Abū al-Walīd al-Bājī, the judge Abū Bakr ibn al-‘Arabī, and others like them, are esteemed only for their agreement with the sunnah and hadith. As for the great scholars, such as Ibn Habīb, Ibn Sahnūn, and others like them, they belong to a different category altogether. Likewise, Abū Muhammad ibn Hazm, in his writings on religions and sects, is praised only for his agreement with

---

## Page 1341

the sunnah and hadith, as in his discussions of divine decree, irjā’, and similar matters—not for his distinctive view concerning the relative merits of the Companions. Likewise, in his discussion of the divine attributes, he is praised for agreeing with the adherents of the sunnah and hadith: he holds firmly to authentic hadiths, esteems the early generations and the leading hadith scholars, and states that he agrees with Imām Ahmad on the question of the Quran and other matters. There is no doubt that he agrees with him and with them on some of these matters.

However, al-Ash‘arī and others like him agree more closely with Imām Ahmad ibn Hanbal and the imams before him concerning the Quran and the divine attributes, although Abū Muhammad ibn Hazm is sounder than others on questions of faith and divine decree, more knowledgeable about hadith, and more reverent toward it and its scholars. Yet he became involved with certain views of the philosophers and the Mu‘tazila concerning the divine attributes, which diverted him from agreeing with the hadith scholars on the substance of their position in these matters. Thus he agreed with the hadith scholars in wording, but with the others in meaning.

Such positions led those jurists, speculative theologians, and hadith scholars who criticized him to do so for his adherence to an outward sense devoid of inward substance—as in his denial of underlying meanings in commands, prohibitions, and word derivation, and his denial of departures from the ordinary course of events and similar matters relating to acts of worship of the heart. This is compounded by his disparagement of eminent scholars, his excessive denial of underlying meanings, and his claim to follow the apparent senses of texts—although he possesses faith, religious devotion, and extensive learning in many fields that only an obstinate person would deny. His books display wide familiarity with scholarly views and knowledge of circumstances,

---

## Page 1342

and reverence for the foundations of Islam and the prophetic mission, to a degree unmatched in others. On questions addressed by a hadith, his position is clearly the stronger one. His ability to distinguish authentic hadiths from weak ones, and his knowledge of the views of the early generations, are scarcely matched by other jurists.

The reverence shown by the community’s imams and ordinary believers for the sunnah, hadith, and their adherents—in matters of doctrine and practice, in word and deed—is too extensive to recount here.

You will find that whenever Islam and faith become more manifest and stronger, the sunnah and its adherents likewise become more manifest and stronger. When unbelief and hypocrisy gain ground, religious innovations emerge in corresponding measure. Consider, for example, the reigns of al-Mahdī, al-Rashīd, and others like them who revered Islam and faith and fought their enemies among the unbelievers and hypocrites. In those days, the adherents of the sunnah were stronger and more numerous, while the adherents of religious innovations were more abased and fewer. Al-Mahdī killed so many hypocritical zindīqs (heretics) that only Allah knows their number, while al-Rashīd frequently undertook military campaigns and performed the pilgrimage.

This was because, when Abbasid rule expanded, its supporters from the East and among the non-Arabs included groups of those whom the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, described when he said:

“Tribulation is here.”

Many religious innovations emerged at that time, and a number of non-Arab books—those of the Persian Magians, the Roman Sabians, and the Indian polytheists—were also translated into Arabic. Al-Mahdī was among the finest Abbasid caliphs and among the best in faith, justice, and generosity; he therefore proceeded to pursue the hypocritical zindīqs as well. The Abbasid caliphs were more diligent than the Umayyads in observing the prayers at their appointed times.

---

## Page 1343

for the latter frequently neglected the prescribed prayer times, as is stated in the hadiths concerning them:

“After me there will be rulers who delay the prayer beyond its appointed time. Pray at its appointed time, and let your prayer with them be voluntary.”

Nevertheless, religious innovations were suppressed during the three virtuous generations. The revealed law enjoyed greater strength and prominence, and greater efforts were devoted to fighting the enemies of the religion among the unbelievers and hypocrites.

During the reign of “Abū al-ʿAbbās al-Maʾmūn,” the “Khurramiyyah” and similar groups of hypocrites emerged. He had ancient works brought from Byzantine lands translated into Arabic, thereby spreading the doctrines of the Sabians. He also corresponded with the polytheist kings of India and others like them, until bonds of friendship developed between him and them. When unbelief and hypocrisy spread among the Muslims, and the polytheists and the People of the Book gained strength, the consequences included the ascendancy of the Jahmiyyah, the Rāfidah, and other misguided groups, as well as the favour shown to the Sabians and other such philosophers.

This arose from a way of thinking that its adherent considers rational and just, although it is nothing but ignorance and injustice. To place the believer and the hypocrite, or the Muslim and the unbeliever, on an equal footing is the greatest injustice; to seek guidance from the misguided is the greatest ignorance. This gave rise to the Jahmī ordeal, in which the Muslim community was subjected to trials involving the denial of Allah’s attributes, the rejection of His speech, and the denial that He can be seen. Imam Ahmad and others endured trials whose account would be lengthy.

In the days of “al-Mutawakkil,” Islam had gained such strength that non-Muslims under Muslim protection were required to abide by the conditions

---

## Page 1344

laid down by ʿUmar; they were also compelled to accept humiliating subjection. Thus, the Sunnah and the Muslim community gained strength, while the Jahmiyyah, the Rāfidah, and their like were suppressed.

The same was true during the reigns of “al-Muʿtadid,” al-Mahdī (*), al-Qādir, and other caliphs whose conduct was more praiseworthy and whose ways were better than those of others. Islam was stronger in their time, and the strength of the Sunnah reflected this.

Under the rule of the “Buyids” and others like them, the opposite was true, for various reprehensible doctrines were represented among them. Some were heretics, and among them were many Qarmatians, philosophical speculators, Muʿtazilah, and Rāfidah. These tendencies were widespread and predominant among them. During their reign, the people of Islam and the Sunnah suffered unprecedented weakness, to the point that the Christians seized the frontier regions of Islam, the Qarmatians spread throughout Egypt, the Maghrib, the East, and elsewhere, and many events occurred.

Since the realm of Mahmūd ibn Sebuktigin was among the best realms ruled by men of his kind, Islam and the Sunnah enjoyed greater strength in his dominion. He waged war against the polytheists of India and spread justice to an extent unmatched by rulers like him. Thus, during his reign, the Sunnah was manifest and religious innovations were suppressed.

The same was true of Sultan “Nūr al-Dīn Mahmūd,” who ruled in Syria. During his reign, the people of Islam and the Sunnah gained strength, while the unbelievers and adherents of religious innovation in Syria, Egypt, and elsewhere—including the Rāfidah, the Jahmiyyah, and their like—were humbled. The same was true of the Abbasid caliphate in his time

> **Footnotes:**
> Shaykh Nāsir ibn Hamad al-Fahd states (p. 255): “The reading should apparently be ‘and al-Muhtadī.’”

---

## Page 1345

and Ibn Hubayrah’s service as their vizier, for he was among the finest viziers in Islam. Thus, his care for Islam and hadith surpassed that of others.

The admissions of the leading scholars of speculative theology and philosophy, their testimony that they and their own peers were misguided, and their testimony against one another to the same effect are too numerous to recount here. Likewise, many of their leading scholars returned to the beliefs of ordinary adherents of the Sunnah and their elderly women. Yet none of the leading scholars of the Sunnah and hadith turns away from his beliefs, because:

“Once the sweetness of faith permeates the heart, no one comes to resent it.”

Likewise, they testify that the people of hadith are safe and free from the various forms of misguidance, whereas they testify to nothing but misguidance concerning the adherents of religious innovation. This is a vast subject, as we have already explained. All the opposing factions among those who follow their desires testify that the people of hadith are more upright than the others and closer to the truth. Thus, what the adherents of sects say about them, and how they deal with them, resemble what the followers of other religions say about Muslims and how they deal with them.

If we compare the two groups—the people of hadith and the people of speculative theology—those who criticize some adherents of hadith and the Muslim community for empty verbiage are, in fact, criticizing either their lack of knowledge or their lack of understanding.

The first consists in their citing weak or fabricated hadiths as evidence, or reports that are unsuitable as evidence. The second consists in their failing to understand the meaning of authentic hadiths. Indeed, they may advance two contradictory statements without knowing how to resolve the contradiction.

---

## Page 1346

And the matter comes down to two things: either an excess of statements that offer no benefit but are thought to do so, such as fabricated hadiths; or beneficial statements whose meaning they do not understand. Following hadith requires, first, that the hadith be authentic and, second, that its meaning be understood, just as with following the Quran. Their errors arise from neglecting one of these two prerequisites. Those who criticize them do so on this account.

There is no doubt that this occurs among some of them. They cite fabricated hadiths as evidence in “fundamental and subsidiary matters,” along with concocted reports and untrue stories. They quote passages from the Quran and hadith whose meaning they do not understand, and may misinterpret them and apply them where they do not belong. On the basis of this weak transmitted evidence and flimsy reasoning, they may then declare prominent members of the Muslim community unbelievers, pronounce them misguided and adherents of religious innovation, and accuse them of ignorance.

Some of them thus display neglect of the truth and transgression against others. Some of this may be forgiven error; some may be reprehensible speech and falsehood; and some may be religious innovations and forms of misguidance that warrant severe punishments. Only an ignorant or unjust person would deny this, and I have witnessed astonishing examples of it.

Yet, in this respect, their relationship to others is like that of Muslims to the followers of other religions. There is no doubt that among many Muslims there is injustice, ignorance, religious innovation, and wickedness whose extent is known only to the One whose knowledge encompasses all things. Nevertheless, every evil found among some Muslims is found among others

---

## Page 1347

to a greater extent; and every good found among others is found among them in a higher and greater degree. The same holds for the people of hadith in relation to others.

This can be explained as follows: the unprofitable excess of words mentioned above, believed to provide a path to conceptual understanding and assent to propositions, is many, many times more prevalent among adherents of speculative theology and logic than among the people of hadith. The people of hadith’s reliance on weak hadiths is matched by the theologians and logicians’ reliance on numerous fruitless definitions and syllogisms that yield no knowledge, but rather ignorance and misguidance. The people of hadith’s discussion of hadiths whose meaning they do not understand is matched by the theologians and logicians’ laboured pronouncements without knowledge, which are still greater and more numerous. How aptly Imam Ahmad said:

“A weak hadith is better than the opinion of so-and-so.”

Moreover, the people of hadith have this advantage: the statements they repeat, though some of them do not understand them, are true in themselves, and they believe in them. The speculative theologians, by contrast, labour to make statements that they neither understand nor know to be true. The people of hadith do not use a weak hadith to overturn a major foundational principle of the Sharia; they use it either to support such a principle or in a subsidiary matter. Those others, however, invoke faulty definitions and syllogisms to overturn true, established foundational principles.

With this understood, Allah Most High said concerning the followers of religious leaders who oppose the messengers:

“When their messengers came to them with clear proofs, they rejoiced in the knowledge they possessed.”

And He Most High said:

---

## Page 1348

“On the Day their faces are turned about in the Fire, they will say, ‘Oh, would that we had obeyed Allah and obeyed the Messenger!’”

Through to His words:

“and curse them with a mighty curse.”

There are many passages like this in the Quran. Since “happiness in this world and the Hereafter” lies in following the messengers, it is clear that those most entitled to it are those who know best the teachings transmitted from the messengers and follow them most closely. Those who know their words and deeds and follow them are the people of happiness in every time and place. They are the saved group among the followers of every religion, and in this community they are the people of the Sunnah and hadith. They share with the rest of the community whatever knowledge of the prophetic message those others possess, but are distinguished by their particular knowledge inherited from the Messenger—knowledge that others lack or deny.

The messengers—may Allah’s blessings and peace be upon them—were charged with conveying the message clearly, and they did so. As for the last of the messengers, Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, Allah revealed His Book, confirming the Scriptures that preceded it and serving as their guardian. It is thus the trustworthy custodian of all the Scriptures. The Prophet conveyed the message in the clearest, fullest, and most complete manner. He was the most sincere of all creation in seeking the good of Allah’s servants, and was compassionate and merciful towards the believers. He conveyed the message, fulfilled the trust, strove in Allah’s cause as he ought, and worshipped Allah until death came to him.

Thus, those among creation who enjoy the greatest happiness and bliss and hold the highest rank are those who follow him most closely and conform most fully to his example in knowledge and practice. As for the speculative theologians who do not follow him, the discussion concerns their syllogisms, which constitute their arguments

---

## Page 1349

and proofs for their knowledge and learning. This includes all speculative theologians and philosophers who oppose anything of the Sunnah and hadith.

The discussion of this subject is too extensive to encompass here. What is known in general, however, is that philosophers and speculative theologians are among the worst of humankind for empty verbiage, asserting falsehood, and denying the truth in their propositions and proofs. Hardly a single proposition of theirs—Allah knows best—is free of this.

I remember once saying to one of their devoted admirers who defended them—when I was still young and had only recently reached puberty: “Everything these people say contains something false, either in their proofs or in their propositions. Either they state a true proposition but offer weak proofs for it, or the proposition itself is false.” That admirer began expressing great dismay at this and mentioned “the question of Allah’s oneness.”

I said: “Allah’s oneness is true. But mention whichever of their proofs you know, so that I may point out its flaws to you.” He quoted some of them word for word, until he understood the error. He then went to his son—who was also one of their partisan supporters—and related this to him. He said that Alī then began expressing great dismay at this. I replied: “I do not doubt Allah’s oneness, but I do doubt this particular proof.”

Several things demonstrate this to you:

- First: You find them to be the most doubtful and unsettled of people, and the weakest in knowledge and certainty. They experience this within themselves, and others observe it in them. The evidence for this is too abundant to recount here.

Their only distinction lies in their ability to raise objections, attack arguments, and debate. It is well known that raising objections and attacking arguments is not knowledge and brings no benefit, and the best

---

## Page 1350

condition of someone who engages in it is to be on a par with a layman. Knowledge lies only in answering the question. This is why you find that most of their arguments carry equal weight, since each of them undermines the other’s proofs.

It has been said that al-Ashʿarī—although he was among those closest to the Sunnah and hadith, and most knowledgeable about them—composed a book towards the end of his life on the equal weight of competing proofs, meaning the proofs of speculative theology, for that was the discipline in which he was proficient. Their leading scholars have continually acknowledged the absence of proofs and guidance in their approach, as we have related from Abū Hāmid and others. Abū Hāmid al-Ghazālī even said:

“The people who have the most doubts at the time of death are the speculative theologians.”

Abū ʿAbd Allāh al-Rāzī is among the most notable examples in this regard—the realm of bewilderment, doubt, and instability. Yet he goes to excess in this: he has an insatiable appetite for raising doubts rather than establishing the truth. Others, by contrast, establish something and hold firmly to some element of truth. Yet some people may hold firmly to pure falsehood—rather, it must contain some element of truth.

Among the distinguished later scholars, and those most accomplished in philosophy and speculative theology, was Ibn Wāsil al-Hamawī. He used to say:

“I lie on my back and draw the covering over half my face. Then I review the doctrines, the arguments of this group and that, and the objections of this group and that, until dawn breaks, without any view having gained greater weight in my judgment.”

For this reason, al-Khattābī recited:

Arguments shatter like glass: you take them for truth,
Yet each breaks another and is itself broken.

If this is the state of their arguments, what futile talk, falsehood, and empty verbiage could be greater than this?

---

## Page 1351

And how can it be fitting for people such as these to accuse the people of hadith and the Sunnah of empty verbiage? These are the people greatest in knowledge, certainty, reassurance, and tranquillity. They know, and they know that they know; they are certain of the truth, neither doubting nor wavering.

As for the certainty, knowledge, and guidance granted to the scholars of hadith and the most distinguished among them, this is beyond description. Yet even their ordinary followers possess certainty and beneficial knowledge of which the leading speculative theologians who engage in philosophy have acquired nothing. This is evident and plain for everyone to see.

The most any of them can say is that these people have reached firm convictions without evidence and held resolutely to them without proof, merely accepting the views of others. This may indeed be true of many ordinary people. But conviction grounded in knowledge differs from conviction grounded in desire. Someone who is certain without knowledge recognizes within himself that he does not know what he is certain of, whereas someone whose certainty rests on knowledge recognizes within himself that he knows.

For knowing or not knowing is like hearing and seeing, or not hearing and seeing: a person recognizes this within himself, just as he recognizes that he loves or hates, desires or dislikes, is happy or sad, experiences pleasure or suffering, and so forth. Someone who doubts that he knows when he does know is like someone who is certain that he knows when he does not. This resembles someone who doubts that he has heard and seen, or who is certain that he has heard and seen something he has neither heard nor seen.

A person may err or lie in either denying or affirming. Yet such incidental error or falsehood does not prevent him from having firm, undoubting certainty about these matters, just as he is certain of the tastes and smells he perceives, even though a disturbance may arise that makes something sweet taste bitter to him.

---

## Page 1352

Thus, the incidental causes of error in the inward or outward senses and in reason are like illnesses that affect the activity of the body and soul: soundness is the normal state, both in perception and in activity. For Allah created His servants with an innate disposition. Errors in these matters are recognized through their particular causes, such as yellow bile affecting taste, misalignment of the eyes, and similar conditions. Otherwise, anyone who examines his own firm convictions will find that most people who are certain of things that do not warrant certainty derive their conviction from some form of desire. As Allah Most High says:

“Indeed, many lead others astray through their desires without knowledge.”

And He says:

“And who is more astray than one who follows his desire without guidance from Allah?”

This is why you find the Jews standing firmly by and persisting in their falsehood, because of the arrogance, envy, hardness of heart, and other desires within them. As for the Christians, they are more deeply misguided, although they are generally less evil in their habits and character. Yet they are not certain of most of their misguided beliefs. Rather, upon examination, you find that anyone from either group who abandons desire and gives the matter some consideration comes to recognize that Islam is true.

The point here is that a person’s awareness of whether he knows or does not know rests on his finding within himself that he knows. Thus, against someone who denies knowledge, we offer no argument other than that we find within ourselves that we know. Likewise, scholars argued against those who deny mass-transmitted reports by saying that we find within ourselves that we know these things and are certain of them, just as we know and are certain of what we have perceived through our senses.

Scholars who have examined the matter rigorously have made the attainment of knowledge through a report the criterion for establishing mass transmission. They did not delimit it by any number or characteristic; rather, whenever knowledge is attained, that is what counts. A person finds within himself that he knows, and this is true.

---

## Page 1353

For a person cannot establish that he knows by means of a proof: his knowledge of that proof’s premises requires him to find within himself that he knows them. If knowing that he knows required a proof, this would lead to circular reasoning or an infinite regress. For this reason, a person does not perceive the presence of knowledge when its cause is present, if that knowledge is self-evident, or when he knows the two premises, if it is inferential. This argument has been used against those who deny that reasoning yields knowledge, although this question requires distinctions that are beyond the scope of the present discussion.

The point is that whoever considers a proof that yields knowledge finds within himself that he knows upon understanding that proof, just as he finds himself hearing and seeing when listening to a sound or looking for the sun, the crescent moon, or something else. Knowledge arises in the soul, as do all other perceptions and movements, through the causes Allah appoints. Most of this occurs through the angels of Allah Most High. Through them, Allah, glorified is He, sends down upon His servants’ hearts whatever knowledge, strength, and other things He wills. Hence the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said concerning Hassān:

“O Allah, strengthen him with the Holy Spirit.”

Allah Most High says:

“He has inscribed faith in their hearts and strengthened them with a spirit from Him.”

And the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever seeks judicial office and seeks assistance in obtaining it is left to bear it unaided. But whoever neither seeks judicial office nor seeks assistance in obtaining it, Allah sends down an angel to guide him aright.”

ʿAbd Allāh ibn Masʿūd said:

“We used to say that tranquillity speaks through ʿUmar’s tongue.”

Ibn Masʿūd also said:

“The angel has a prompting, and Satan has a prompting. The angel’s prompting is a promise of good and an affirmation of the truth. Satan’s prompting is a promise of evil and a denial of the truth.”

This statement made by Ibn Masʿūd is reliably transmitted

---

## Page 1354

from him, and some narrators have at times traced it back to the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. It is a comprehensive statement encompassing the foundations of the servant’s knowledge and action: awareness and will.

The explanation is this: the servant possesses a faculty of awareness, sensation, and perception, and a faculty of will and movement. The first is the foundation of the second and entails it; the second entails the first and completes it. Through the first, he affirms what is true and rejects what is false; through the second, he loves what benefits and suits him, and hates what harms him and is incompatible with him.

Allah, glorified is He, created His servants with a natural disposition that includes recognizing and affirming the truth, recognizing and rejecting falsehood, recognizing and loving what is beneficial and congenial, and recognizing and hating what is harmful and incompatible. All this belongs to their natural disposition. Whatever is true and existent, the natural disposition affirms. Whatever is true and beneficial, it recognizes, loves, and finds reassurance in. This is what is right. Whatever is false and nonexistent, the natural disposition rejects as false, hates, and repudiates.

Allah Most High says:

“He enjoins upon them what is right and forbids them what is wrong.”

The human being is as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, described him when he said:

“The most truthful names are Hārith [one who works] and Hammām [one who continually intends].”

Thus, he is always intending and acting, but he acts only in the hope of gaining benefit or averting harm. Yet this hope may rest on a false belief, either concerning the intended object itself—in which case it is neither beneficial nor harmful—or concerning the means, which do not lead to it. This is ignorance.

He may know that something harms him and still do it, and know that something benefits him yet leave it undone, because that knowledge is opposed by his inward pursuit of another pleasure or his desire to avert another pain. He acts ignorantly and unjustly in giving one precedence over the other.

For this reason, Abū al-‘Āliyah said:

“I asked the Companions of Muhammad

---

## Page 1355

may Allah bless him and grant him peace, concerning the words of Allah Most High:

‘Allah only undertakes to accept repentance from those who do evil out of ignorance and then repent soon afterwards.’

They replied: ‘Everyone who disobeys Allah is ignorant, and everyone who repents before death has repented soon afterwards.’”

A person acts only with hope—even when he is fearful and afraid, he strives only for deliverance and flees only from what he fears. Hope arises only through the promise of good instilled in his soul: attaining what he loves or escaping what he dislikes. Every human being therefore holds beliefs, affirming some things and rejecting others. He also has an intention and a will directed towards what he hopes for—something he considers desirable and attainable—or towards having what he loves with him, or warding off what he dislikes.

Allah created the servant to seek good and hope to attain it through his deeds. If he denies the truth instead of affirming it, and does not hope for good so as to seek it and work towards it, he is a loser through his failure to affirm the truth and pursue good. How much worse, then, if he denies the truth and dislikes the very desire for good? And how much worse if he believes falsehood and desires evil?

Thus, ‘Abd Allah ibn Mas‘ūd mentioned that the human heart receives a prompting from the angel and a prompting from Satan. The angel’s prompting is an affirmation of truth, which does not belong to the category of corrupt belief. Satan’s prompting is a denial of truth and a promise of evil. This belongs to the category of desiring evil and expecting it to occur: either with hope for it, if it accords with the soul’s desires, or with fear of it, if the soul does not love it. Hope and fear each necessarily entail the other.

---

## Page 1356

Thus, true knowledge and righteous intention originate in the angel’s prompting, while false belief and corrupt intention originate in Satan’s prompting. Allah Most High says:

“Satan threatens you with poverty and commands you to commit immorality, whereas Allah promises you forgiveness from Him and bounty.”

He Most High also says:

“It is only Satan who frightens you with his allies.”

That is, he makes you fear his allies. And He Most High says:

“And when Satan made their deeds seem attractive to them and said, ‘No one among mankind can overcome you today, and I am your protector.’”

Satan is a whisperer who withdraws: when the servant remembers his Lord, he withdraws; when the servant neglects His remembrance, he whispers. Thus, neglecting the remembrance of Allah is a cause and starting point for false belief and corrupt intention entering the heart. Remembrance of Allah Most High includes reciting and understanding His Book and discussing knowledge, as Mu‘ādh ibn Jabal said: “And discussing it is glorification of Allah.”

The speculative theologians have disputed how knowledge arises in the heart after examining evidence. Some said that this occurs through causal generation. Those who deny causal generation said: “Rather, it occurs through the action of Allah Most High. Examining evidence either contains knowledge within it or necessarily entails it.” This view is supported by those speculative theologians who affiliate themselves with the sunnah, and by the jurists who agree with them among the followers of Mālik, al-Shāfi‘ī, Ahmad and others.

The philosophers said: “Rather, this occurs through emanation from the Active Intellect when the soul is ready to receive that emanation.” They may claim that the Active Intellect is “Gabriel.”

As for the statement of those who say, “This occurs through Allah’s action,” it is correct on the basis that Allah is the teacher of all knowledge and the Creator of all things. Yet this is a general statement that does not explain the cause itself

---

## Page 1357

in its specific form. As for the view of those who affirm causal generation, some of it is true and some false. If they claim that the knowledge thus generated arises solely through the servant’s power, that is certainly false. Rather, it arises through two factors: the servant’s power and another cause, such as the force in an arrow and the receptivity of what it strikes. There is no doubt that examining evidence occurs through a cause; the question, however, is what completes the acquisition of knowledge.

As for the philosophers’ claim that this occurs through the Active Intellect, it is an unfounded myth. Still more false is their claim that this is Gabriel. Their further claim that all bodily forms and their perfections arising in the world of the elements proceed from its emanation and are caused by it is among the most utterly false claims.

Nevertheless, their attribution of this to spiritual factors is broadly correct. Allah, glorified and exalted, governs the affairs of the heavens and the earth through His angels, who are the emissaries of His command. The very word malak (angel) indicates this. The prophets have reported this, and the Quran and sunnah attest to it with more evidence than can be mentioned here. This includes what the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said about the angels entrusted with the formation of the embryo and other matters. But singling out one spirit connected to the lunar sphere as the lord of this world is false.

This is not the place to examine that exhaustively. Yet it must be understood that the initiating influence upon the soul’s awareness and activity comes from angels or devils. The angel prompts affirmation of the truth and commands good, while the devil prompts denial of the truth and commands evil. Affirmation and denial accompany a person’s examination of evidence, just as commands and prohibitions accompany his will.

---

## Page 1358

Thus, if one examines evidence that guides—such as the Quran—and is free from Satan’s interference, that examination brings knowledge and guidance. For this reason, the servant is commanded to seek refuge from Satan, the accursed, when reciting. If, however, one examines a misleading argument while believing it to be sound—whether because both its premises, or one of them, contain falsehood, or because its premises are sound but their combination is not—this produces a corrupt belief in the heart. Such is the nature of most of the specious arguments advanced by those who uphold falsehood and oppose the Book and the sunnah, including philosophers, speculative theologians, and their like.

If, then, someone examining evidence must necessarily have something to examine, and examining the conceived matter whose judgment is sought does not itself yield knowledge—indeed, such examination may bring various specious arguments to mind, which he mistakes for evidence because of the heart’s intense thirst to know the judgment concerning that question and to affirm what he has conceived—then the examination that yields knowledge is examination of evidence that guides.

The evidence that guides, in the broadest and unqualified sense, is “the Book of Allah” and “the sunnah of His Prophet.” What the revealed Law has brought concerning the two forms of examination is that which is beneficial, useful, and conducive to guidance: remembering Allah and the truth He has sent down. If someone wishes to examine and reflect upon evidence in general, without specifying a particular question to be answered, this consists in examining the Book of Allah and pondering it, as Allah Most High says:

“There has come to you from Allah a light and a clear Book.”

“Through it Allah guides those who seek His pleasure to the ways of peace, brings them out of darkness into light by His permission, and guides them to a straight path.”

And Allah Most High says:

“And thus We have revealed

---

## Page 1359

to you a revelation by Our command. You did not know what the Book was, nor what faith was, but We made it a light by which We guide whom We will of Our servants. And indeed, you guide to a straight path.”

“The path of Allah, to whom belongs whatever is in the heavens and whatever is on earth. Surely, to Allah all matters return.”

As for examining a particular question or proposition in order to determine the judgment concerning it and affirm the truth about it, when the servant does not know what would guide him to either conclusion, such examination alone brings no benefit. He may arrive at convictions that he considers true, although they are false; these come from Satan’s suggestions. He may also arrive at convictions that are true; these come from an angel’s suggestions.

Likewise, when examining the evidence that guides—namely, the Quran—he may understand the words in their proper context and grasp the intended meaning of the evidence, and thus be guided by the Quran. Or he may fail to understand it, or distort the words by taking them out of their proper contexts, and thus go astray through it; this comes from Satan. As Allah Most High says:

“We send down of the Quran that which is healing and mercy for the believers, but it increases the wrongdoers only in loss.”

And He says:

“By it He leads many astray and guides many, but He leads none astray by it except the defiantly disobedient.”

And He says:

“As for those who believe, it has increased them in faith, and they rejoice.”

“But as for those in whose hearts is disease, it has added impurity to their impurity.”

And He says:

“Say: It is guidance and healing for those who believe. But those who do not believe have deafness in their ears, and it is blindness for them.”

And He says:

“This is a clear explanation for mankind, and guidance and admonition for the God-fearing.”

Thus, one who examines evidence is like someone looking for the crescent moon: he may see it, or he may fail to see it because his eyesight is impaired. The same is true of one whose heart is blind.

---

## Page 1360

As for someone examining a particular question, he needs two things: to find evidence that guides, and to be guided by it and benefit from it.

The revealed Law therefore commands him to do what brings the causes of guidance down upon his heart and turns away the causes that obstruct it. This concerns remembrance of Allah Most High and heedlessness of Him. Satan is a whisperer who withdraws: when the servant remembers his Lord, Satan retreats; when he neglects the remembrance of Allah, Satan whispers. “Remembrance of Allah” imparts faith and is the foundation of faith.

Allah, glorified be He, is the Lord and Sovereign of everything, and He teaches and bestows all knowledge. Just as He Himself is the source of everything that exists, remembrance of Him and knowledge of Him are the foundation of all knowledge; and remembrance of Him is in the heart. The Quran provides detailed knowledge and thus increases faith, as Jundub ibn ʿAbd Allāh al-Bajalī and other Companions said:

“We learned faith, then we learned the Quran, and so our faith increased.”

For this reason, the first revelation Allah sent down to His Prophet was:

“Read in the name of your Lord who created.”

He commanded him to read in Allah’s name. This included the command to remember Allah and the truth that had been revealed. He said:

“In the name of your Lord who created.”

“He created man from a clinging substance.”

“Read, and your Lord is the Most Generous.”

“Who taught by the pen.”

“Taught man what he did not know.”

Thus He, glorified be He, speaks of His creation in general and, in particular, of man, the noblest of existing beings; and of His teaching knowledge in general and to man in particular. He mentions teaching by the pen, which is the final stage, thereby necessarily implying the teaching of speech and the imparting of knowledge within the heart.

---

## Page 1361

The reality of the matter is that the servant needs the knowledge and guidance he asks for: he seeks and supplicates. Through remembering Allah and acknowledging his need for Him, Allah guides him and shows him the way, as He said:

“My servants, all of you are astray except those whom I have guided. So seek My guidance, and I will guide you.”

Likewise, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, used to say:

“O Allah, Lord of Gabriel, Michael, and Isrāfīl, Originator of the heavens and the earth, Knower of the unseen and the seen! You judge between Your servants concerning that over which they differ. Guide me, by Your permission, to the truth concerning which I differ. Indeed, You guide whom You will to a straight path.”

This is further clarified by the fact that someone seeking knowledge through inquiry, inference, reflection, and contemplation cannot acquire it unless he examines evidence that gives him knowledge of what it indicates. Whenever knowledge is acquired through inquiry, the person conducting that inquiry must already possess some knowledge held in mind and firmly established in his heart, whose acquisition requires no inquiry. What is already known thus becomes the foundation and means for the reflection through which he seeks to know something else.

For this reason, remembrance concerns Allah, for He, glorified be He, is the known Truth, whereas reflection concerns His creation, as Allah Most High said:

“Those who remember Allah while standing, sitting, or lying on their sides, and reflect on the creation of the heavens and the earth.”

A report also states:

“Reflect on what is created, and do not reflect on the Creator.”

For thought and assessment proceed through illustrative examples and analogies, and these concern things that resemble one another—namely, created things.

---

## Page 1362

As for the Creator—majestic is His glory, glorified and exalted is He—He has neither likeness nor equal. Reflection based on analogy therefore cannot apply to Him. Rather, He is known through the innate disposition, and the servant remembers Him.

Through remembrance and what He has revealed about Himself, the servant gains profound knowledge of Him that cannot be attained through thought and assessment alone. I mean knowledge of Him Himself, for He Himself is not an object of reflection. As for understanding the meanings of what He has revealed and similar matters, thought and assessment do have a place, as taught by the Quran and the Sunnah.

For this reason, many of those devoted to worship and Sufism urge constant remembrance and regard it as the gateway to reaching the Truth. This is good if they combine it with contemplation of the Quran and the Sunnah and adherence to them. Many practitioners of rational inquiry and speculative theology urge reflection and inquiry, regarding this as the path to knowledge of the Truth. Inquiry is sound when directed toward truth and evidence, as explained earlier.

Each of these two paths contains truth, but each needs the truth found in the other. Each must also be cleared of the falsehood that has entered it. All this is accomplished by following what the Messengers brought.

We have discussed this at length elsewhere, explaining the paths of those devoted to worship, spiritual discipline, and remembrance, and the path of those devoted to speculative theology, rational inquiry, and inference, as well as what is acceptable and unacceptable in each. We have also explained the complete path brought by the prophetic message, which encompasses every truth. This is not the place to elaborate on that.

---

## Page 1363

The point here is simply that a person is conscious of possessing knowledge: he experiences this and knows it without anyone’s mediation, just as he is conscious of other things. Knowledge in the heart is like food in the body. The body senses food and drink; likewise, hearts sense the knowledge that descends upon them, which is their food and drink. As the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Everyone who hosts a feast wishes people to attend it, and Allah’s feast is the Quran.”

And as Allah Most High said:

“He sends down water from the sky, and valleys flow according to their capacity. The torrent carries swelling foam, and a similar foam rises from what they heat in the fire to make ornaments or utensils.”

In the two Sahīh collections, Abū Mūsā reports that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“The guidance and knowledge with which Allah has sent me are like rain that falls upon a land. One part of it absorbed the water and produced abundant pasture and grass.
