Another part retained the water, which people used for watering and cultivation. Another part was merely level ground that neither retained water nor produced pasture. This is the likeness of one who gains understanding of Allah’s religion and benefits from the guidance and knowledge with which Allah has sent me, and the likeness of one who pays no heed to it and does not accept Allah’s guidance with which I was sent.”

Thus, he compared the guidance and knowledge that descend upon hearts to the water that descends upon the earth. Just as Allah has angels entrusted with clouds and rain, He has angels entrusted with guidance and knowledge. The latter are the provision and nourishment of hearts, while the former are the provision and nourishment of bodies. Al-Hasan said

---

## Page 1364

al-Basrī, commenting on the saying of the Most High,

“and spend from what We have provided for them,”

said: “Among the greatest forms of giving is the sharing of knowledge”—or words to that effect.

Another report states:

“What an excellent gift and what an excellent present: a good word that a man hears and then offers to a Muslim brother of his.”

Another report from Abū al-Dardā’ states:

“No servant gives better charity than an admonition with which he exhorts his believing brothers, who then depart having benefited from it through Allah”—or words to that effect.

It is reported from Ka‘b ibn ‘Ujrah:

“‘Shall I offer you a gift?’ He then mentioned invoking blessings upon the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace.”

Ibn Mājah narrated in his Sunan from Abū Hurayrah that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“The best charity is for a man to acquire knowledge and then teach it to his Muslim brother.”

Mu‘ādh ibn Jabal said:

“Devote yourselves to knowledge, for seeking it is worship; learning it for Allah’s sake is a good deed; imparting it to its proper recipients draws one near to Allah; teaching it to those who do not know it is charity; investigating it is striving in Allah’s cause; and discussing it is glorification of Allah.”

For this reason, everything—even the fish in the sea—seeks forgiveness for the one who teaches goodness. Allah and His angels send blessings upon the one who teaches people goodness, because of the benefit this brings to all creatures.

The opposite holds for those who conceal knowledge: Allah curses them, and those who curse also curse them. Some of the early generations said:

“When people conceal knowledge and sins are committed, rain is withheld. The animals then say: ‘O Allah, [curse] (*) the disobedient among the children of Adam, for we have been deprived of rain because of their sins.’”

If a person’s knowledge that he possesses knowledge rests upon his experiencing it and sensing it within himself—and this is a self-evident fact—then they have no right to make claims about what

> **Footnotes:**
> (*) The text in square brackets is absent from the printed edition; the correction is taken from Siyānat Majmū‘ al-Fatāwā (p. 255).

---

## Page 1365

is within people’s souls, declaring without proof that it is not knowledge. Their failure to experience it within themselves does not imply that others have not experienced it—especially when those reporting it describe the certainty within themselves, reporting from someone whose knowledge, truthfulness, and understanding of what he says they do not doubt.

Such is the case with the leading scholars of the Muslims, the early generations of this community, and the bearers of authoritative proof. They describe the certainty, assurance, and necessary knowledge they possess. This is illustrated by the account preserved about Najm al-Dīn al-Kubrā, when two speculative theologians visited him: one was Abū ‘Abd Allāh al-Rāzī, and the other was a Mu‘tazilī theologian.

They said, “Shaykh, we have heard that you possess certain knowledge.”

He replied, “Yes, I possess certain knowledge.”

They asked, “How is that possible? We have been debating since the beginning of the day until now, yet neither of us has been able to establish a proof against the other.” —I think the account concerned establishing the truth of Islam.—

He replied, “I do not know what you two are talking about, but I possess certain knowledge.”

They said, “Describe certain knowledge to us.”

He replied, “Certain knowledge, as we understand it, consists of impressions that come upon souls and that souls are unable to repel.”

They began repeating, “Impressions that come upon souls and that souls are unable to repel,” expressing their admiration for this answer.

This is because speculative theologians divide knowledge into necessary and acquired, or self-evident and inferential. Inferential, acquired knowledge must ultimately rest upon necessary or self-evident premises. These require no proof; otherwise, circular reasoning or an infinite regress would follow.

Necessary knowledge is that which

---

## Page 1366

takes hold of the created being’s soul so inescapably that he cannot free himself from it. Thus, what makes it necessary is his inability to dispel it from his soul.

The shaykh explained that their knowledge was necessary and came upon souls in a way they could not repel. The two asked him, “How can one attain that?”

He replied, “Abandon what you are engaged in, and practise the remembrance and worship that Allah has commanded you.”

Al-Rāzī said, “I am too occupied for this.”

The Mu‘tazilī said, “My heart has been scorched by doubts, and I long for these impressions.” He remained with the shaykh for a time, then came out of his place of worship, saying, “By Allah, my master, the truth lies only in what these people who liken Allah to His creation say!” He meant those who affirm the divine attributes, for the Mu‘tazilah call those who affirm the attributes people who liken Allah to His creation.

This was because he had come to know, with necessary knowledge that he could not dispel from his heart, that the Lord of the universe must be distinct from the universe and separate from it, possessing attributes peculiar to Him, and that the Lord described by the Jahmiyyah is nothing but sheer nonexistence.

The well-known account of what the shaykh Abū Ja‘far al-Hamdānī, a man with deep knowledge of Allah, said to Abū al-Ma‘ālī al-Juwaynī is relevant here. When the latter began saying from the pulpit, “Allah existed when there was no Throne,” Abū Ja‘far said, “Master, let us leave aside mention of the Throne”—meaning that this is known only through revelation—“and tell us about this inescapable impulse we find in our hearts. No one with knowledge of Allah has ever said ‘O Allah’ without finding in his heart an irresistible impulse that seeks what is above, turning neither right nor left. How, then, can we dispel this impulse from our hearts?”

He related: Abū al-Ma‘ālī slapped his head and said, “Al-Hamdānī has bewildered me! Al-Hamdānī has bewildered me!” Then he stepped down.

---

## Page 1367

This is because His rising over the Throne after creating the heavens and the earth in six days is known through the revelation brought by the messengers, as Allah has related in the Quran and the Torah.

As for His being above His creatures and separate from them, this is known necessarily through the innate disposition shared by all the children of Adam. The better a person knows Allah, the more devotedly he worships Him, the more frequently he calls upon Him, and the more his heart remembers Him, the stronger and more complete his necessary knowledge of this becomes.

Thus, the innate disposition is perfected by its revealed counterpart: the innate disposition makes the matter known in general terms, while the revealed law explains and clarifies its details and attests to what the innate disposition cannot establish on its own. Such is the matter. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 1368

Section

In sum, everyone firmly entrenched in his religious innovation considers his analogical reasoning consistently applicable, since it treats alike what he regards as equivalent—even if this entails repeatedly contradicting the revealed texts.

This occurs both in doctrinal questions concerning revealed reports and in practical questions concerning voluntary acts. A speculative theologian may apply his analogy with unbroken consistency, thus appearing more accomplished than someone who departs from it. Meanwhile, a follower of the Sunnah who shares that analogy may make statements that contradict it in certain cases, sometimes recognizing the inconsistency and sometimes failing to recognize it—the latter being more common. He may also be misled by tenuous distinctions. By undermining his own underlying rationale and distinguishing between cases that are equivalent in that respect, he appears inferior to the first in knowledge, understanding, and consistency in applying his position. Yet this is not so; rather, he is better than the first.

The analogy they shared was unsound from the outset, because it contradicted the revealed text and sound analogy. Thus, the one who applies it consistently is more unsound and inconsistent than the one who departs from it.

This is the situation whenever two people agree upon an analogy that is not actually valid, while one of them has revealed texts that contradict that analogy in certain cases. In many instances, jurists call this istihsān (juristic preference). Thus, those who adopt istihsān by setting aside an analogy in favour of a revealed text are better than those who apply the analogy consistently and set aside the text.

---

## Page 1369

For this reason, it is reported that Abū Hanīfah said:

“Do not follow Zufar’s analogies, for if you follow them, you will declare the lawful unlawful and the unlawful lawful.”

Zufar frequently applied what he considered sound analogies consistently, despite his limited knowledge of the revealed texts. Abū Yūsuf’s approach was the opposite: he was more knowledgeable about hadith than Zufar. Thus, most questions in which Zufar disagreed with his fellow jurists rested on analogical reasoning, which, upon examination, proves to be nothing but weak analogy. By contrast, in most questions in which Abū Yūsuf disagreed with Abū Hanīfah and Muhammad followed him, he followed the revealed texts and sound analogies. After Abū Hanīfah’s death, Abū Yūsuf travelled to the Hijaz and benefited from their knowledge of Sunnah reports that were not widely known in Kūfah. He used to say:

“Had my companion seen what I have seen, he would have changed his position as I have.”

He knew that his companion intended only to follow the Sharia, but that others might possess knowledge of Sunnah reports that had not reached him.

This is also the situation of many jurists in relation to one another concerning an analogy on which they have agreed, although its validity has not been established by reliable evidence. Agreement on it entails applying it consistently; yet those versed in the revealed texts may depart from it, while those unfamiliar with the texts apply it consistently.

Likewise, this is the situation of most speculative theologians on the side of affirmation in relation to those on the side of negation, in questions concerning the divine attributes, divine decree, and other matters. They may agree with them on an analogy involving negation; the latter then apply it consistently, using it to deny what the revealed texts affirm, whereas those who affirm do not do so

---

## Page 1370

but rather hold that what the text entails must be affirmed. They may therefore accept part of the texts’ meaning, or draw a weak distinction.

The root of this is their agreement with those others on a weak analogy, as in questions concerning body, substance, and similar matters. You find the same situation with someone who assists an oppressor in his actions, for actions arise only from a will. The oppressor carries his will through consistently, harming the very person who helped him or committing an injustice that his helper does not approve of. The helper then seeks to interrupt this consistent application and restrict the scope of its underlying rationale. Hence the saying:

“Whoever helps an oppressor will be afflicted by him.”

This applies to all wrongdoers, whether in word or deed, including those given to religious innovation and wickedness. Whoever goes against the Quran and Sunnah in what he asserts, commands, or does is a wrongdoer. Allah sent His messengers so that people might uphold justice, and Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, is the best of them. Allah, glorified be He, explained justice to him in ways He did not explain to others, and enabled him to accomplish what He did not enable others to accomplish. Thus, he did and commanded things that others neither commanded nor did.

This is because, in many circumstances, human beings may neither know what justice truly is nor be able to achieve it. Rather, the closer something comes to justice and the more it resembles it, the better it is: this is the best course. We have discussed this at length elsewhere. Allah Most High says:

“And establish weight with justice.”

And He says:

“Allah does not burden a soul beyond its capacity.”

And He says:

“So fear Allah

---

## Page 1371

as much as you are able.” And he, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“When I command you to do something, do as much of it as you are able.”

The point is that the knowledge, certainty, tranquility, sound conviction, steadfast speech, and absolute confidence in their beliefs possessed by ordinary believers and their scholars among Ahl al-Sunnah wa-l-Jamāʿah are denied only by someone whom Allah has deprived of reason and faith.

Even supposing that the opponent does not concede this, there is no doubt that they testify to this about themselves and say that they experience it. He and his group, however, testify to the opposite about themselves and find within themselves nothing but doubt. Which of the two groups, then, more deserves to have its discourse described as empty verbiage? Which is more deserving of being charged with ignorance, misguidance, falsehood, and deceit?

The statements on this subject by the shaykhs and leading scholars of the Sunnah, jurisprudence, and knowledge are far too extensive to recount here.

---

## Page 1372

The second point:

You find that the practitioners of speculative theology are more prone than anyone else to shift from one position to another: they affirm a position categorically in one place, then categorically affirm its opposite elsewhere and declare anyone who holds the former position an unbeliever. This is evidence of a lack of certainty. For faith is as Caesar described it when he asked Abū Sufyān about those who had embraced Islam with the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“Does any of them renounce his religion out of dissatisfaction with it after embracing it?”

He replied, “No.”

Caesar said, “Such is faith: once its joy permeates the hearts, no one becomes dissatisfied with it.”

This is why one of the early generations—ʿUmar ibn ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz or someone else—said:

“Whoever makes his religion a target for disputation will frequently shift from one position to another.”

As for the adherents of the Sunnah and hadith, none of their scholars or righteous ordinary followers is known ever to have abandoned his position or belief. Rather, they are the most steadfast of people in adhering to it, even when subjected to all manner of ordeals and trials. Such was the condition of the prophets and their followers in earlier times, such as the people of the Trench and others like them, and of the early generations of this community—the Companions, the Successors, and other leading scholars. Indeed, Mālik, may Allah have mercy on him, used to say:

“Do not envy anyone who has suffered no affliction in this matter.”

His point was that Allah will inevitably test the believer; if he remains patient, He raises his rank, as the Most High said:

“Alif-Lām-Mīm. Do people think that they will be left to say, ‘We believe,’ without being tested? We certainly tested those before them, so Allah will surely make evident those who are truthful

---

## Page 1373

and He will surely make evident those who lie.” The Most High also said:

“And We made from among them leaders guiding by Our command when they were patient and were certain of Our signs.”

And the Most High said:

“By time, indeed, mankind is in loss, except for those who believe and do righteous deeds and encourage one another to truth and encourage one another to patience.”

If an adherent of misguided doctrines remains steadfast in his position, this is because of the truth it contains. For every religious innovation embraced by a large group must contain enough of the truth brought by the Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and affirmed by the adherents of the Sunnah and hadith, to account for its acceptance. Pure falsehood is never accepted under any circumstances.

In short, the steadfastness and stability found among the adherents of hadith and the Sunnah exceed many, many times over those found among the practitioners of speculative theology and philosophy. Indeed, the philosopher is more unsettled and bewildered in his position than the speculative theologian, because the latter possesses truth received from the prophets that the philosopher does not possess. Thus, you find someone like Abū al-Husayn al-Basrī and others like him more steadfast than someone like Ibn Sīnā and others like him.

You also find that the adherents of philosophy and speculative theology are more divided and at odds than anyone else, although each claims that what he says is incontrovertibly true and established by demonstrative proof. By contrast, the adherents of the Sunnah and hadith are the most united and harmonious of people. The closer any group is to them, the closer it is to unity and harmony. Thus, the Muʿtazilah are more united and harmonious than the philosophers, for the philosophers hold so many different positions concerning divine matters, the afterlife, and prophethood—indeed, even concerning the natural sciences, mathematics, and the properties of the celestial spheres—that none but the Lord of Majesty can enumerate them.

---

## Page 1374

Those who compiled the doctrines of the ancients—such as “Abū al-Hasan al-Ashʿarī” in his Kitāb al-Maqālāt (Book of Doctrines), and Judge “Abū Bakr” in his Kitāb al-Daqāʾiq (Book of Subtleties)—recorded many times more of their doctrines than al-Fārābī, Ibn Sīnā, and others like them did.

Among speculative theologians, those who affirm the divine attributes—such as the Kullābiyyah, Karrāmiyyah, and Ashʿariyyah—are more united and harmonious than the Muʿtazilah. The disagreements among the Muʿtazilah and their declarations that one another are unbelievers—even a pupil declaring his teacher an unbeliever—are of the same kind as those found among the Khawārij. Those who have written about the failings of the Muʿtazilah have recounted instances of this too numerous to describe at length. You will find unity and harmony only through following the teachings transmitted from the prophets in the Quran and hadith, and what follows from these. You will find division and disagreement only among those who abandon these teachings and give precedence to something else. The Most High said:

“But they will not cease to differ,”

“Except those to whom your Lord has given mercy, and for that He created them.”

He thus tells us that those who receive His mercy do not differ. They are those who follow the prophets in word and deed: the adherents of the Quran and hadith within this community. Whoever opposes them in any matter misses out on mercy to that extent.

Thus, because the philosophers were further removed from following the prophets, their disagreements were greater. Likewise, because the Khawārij, Muʿtazilah, and Rāfidah were further removed from the Sunnah and hadith, their divisions in this respect were greater—especially the Rāfidah. Indeed, it is said that they are the most divided of all groups, because they are the furthest removed from the Sunnah and the united community. The Muʿtazilah, by contrast, are closer to these than they are.

---

## Page 1375

At the beginning of his Kitāb Mukhtalif al-Hadīth (Book on Conflicting Hadiths), Abū Muhammad ibn Qutaybah discusses the adherents of hadith and their leading authorities, alongside the speculative theologians and theirs. Simply naming the authorities of both groups and describing their statements and deeds suffices to make clear to everyone that the adherents of hadith are the people of truth and guidance, while others are more deserving of being associated with misguidance, ignorance, empty verbiage, and falsehood.

Moreover, those who oppose the adherents of hadith are prone to corrupt conduct, whether through unsound belief and hypocrisy or through sickness of the heart and weakness of faith. Their neglect of obligations, transgression of prescribed limits, disregard for the rights of others, and hardness of heart are plain for all to see. Most of their teachers are accused of grave offenses. Although some among them are known for asceticism and worship, the asceticism and worship of some ordinary adherents of the Sunnah outweigh theirs.

It is well known that knowledge is the foundation of action, and that sound foundations entail sound consequences. A person’s corrupt conduct arises from one of two things: need or ignorance. Someone who knows that a thing is reprehensible and has no need of it will not do it—unless, of course, his desires have overcome his reason and sins have gained mastery over him. That, however, is a different matter and a separate category.

Furthermore, no speculative theologian is known who does not hold some doctrine concerning Islam whose proponent is declared an unbeliever by Muslims at large—even by his own associates. A general statement suffices without naming individuals. Which group, then,

---

## Page 1376

is more deserving of being associated with empty verbiage and misguidance than these people? This implies that apostasy occurs among them, just as hypocrisy is widespread among them.

If this concerns obscure doctrines, one may say that a person is mistaken and misguided regarding them, but that the proof on the basis of which he would be judged an unbeliever has not been established against him. Yet among some groups of them, this occurs in evident matters that ordinary Muslims and learned Muslims alike know to belong to the religion of Islam. Indeed, even Jews and Christians know that Muhammad, peace and blessings be upon him, was sent with these teachings and declared those who opposed them to be unbelievers. These include his command to worship Allah alone, without any partner, and his prohibition against worshipping anyone or anything besides Allah—angels, prophets, the sun, the moon, stars, idols, or anything else. This is the most manifest hallmark of Islam. They also include his command to perform the five daily prayers, his making them obligatory, and his emphasis on their great importance; his enmity toward Jews, Christians, polytheists, Sabians, and Magians; and his prohibition of shameful deeds, usury, wine, gambling, and the like.

You then find that many of their leaders have fallen into these things and thus become apostates, although they may repent of them and return to Islam. It has been related of al-Jahm ibn Safwān that he abandoned prayer for forty days, not believing it to be obligatory. They resemble tribal chiefs such as al-Aqra' ibn Hābis, Uyaynah ibn Hisn, and others who apostatized from Islam and subsequently returned to it. Some of these were suspected of hypocrisy and sickness of the heart, while others were not.

Or one may say that, on account of their learning, they resemble Abd Allāh ibn Abī Sarh, who was

---

## Page 1377

a scribe of revelation. He then apostatized and joined the polytheists, so the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, declared his life forfeit in the year of the conquest. Then Uthmān brought him to the Prophet, who accepted his pledge of allegiance to Islam.

Thus, whoever writes works on the doctrines of the polytheists and their like is, at best, a Muslim. Many of these leaders are like this: at times, you find them openly apostatizing from Islam, and at other times returning to it with sickness and hypocrisy in their hearts. They may also enter a third state, in which faith prevails over hypocrisy. Yet they seldom escape some form of hypocrisy, and accounts of this concerning them are well known. Ibn Qutaybah mentioned some of these accounts at the beginning of Mukhtalif al-hadīth (Conflicting Hadiths). Writers on religious doctrines have also recorded some such accounts concerning certain individuals, as reported by others. These are mentioned by Abū Īsā al-Warrāq, al-Nawbakhtī, Abū al-Hasan al-Ash'arī, Judge Abū Bakr ibn al-Bāqillānī, Abū Abd Allāh al-Shahrastānī, and others who record the doctrines of the speculative theologians.

Even more serious than this is that some of them write works on the religion of the polytheists and apostasy from Islam. Thus al-Rāzī wrote his book on the worship of heavenly bodies and idols, presented arguments for its goodness and benefit, and encouraged it. This constitutes apostasy from Islam by the consensus of the Muslims, although he may have repented of it and returned to Islam.

What is remarkable is that the speculative theologians claim that the adherents of hadith and the Sunnah are followers of received authority, not practitioners of rational inquiry and argument, and that they reject reason as a source of proof. Sometimes a rejection of rational inquiry is attributed to certain imams of the Sunnah, and this is among the things for which they reproach them.

---

## Page 1378

They may be told in reply:

“This is not true. The adherents of the Sunnah and hadith do not reject what the Quran teaches; this is a principle upon which they agree. Allah has commanded examination, drawing lessons, reflection, and contemplation in numerous verses. None of the early generations of this community, nor any of the imams and scholars of the Sunnah, is known to have rejected this. Rather, they all agree that people should undertake the examination, reflection, drawing of lessons, contemplation, and other activities prescribed by the revealed law.

“However, ambiguity has arisen in the use of the expressions ‘rational inquiry and argument’ and ‘discourse.’ They rejected the false inquiry, discourse, and arguments introduced by the speculative theologians, who then assumed that rejecting these necessarily meant rejecting rational inquiry and argument altogether. Similarly, some speculative theologians call what they have devised ‘the foundations of religion.’ This is an exalted designation, yet what it designates contains corruption of religion whose extent Allah alone knows.

“When the adherents of truth and the Sunnah reject this, the proponent of falsehood says: ‘They have rejected the foundations of religion.’ Yet they have not rejected anything that deserves to be called the foundations of religion. They have only rejected what this person has called the foundations of religion. These are names that they and their forefathers have devised, using designations for which Allah has sent down no authority. Religion is what Allah and His Messenger have prescribed, and its foundations and subsidiary teachings have been explained. It is inconceivable that the Messenger should have explained the subsidiary teachings of religion but not its foundations, as we have explained elsewhere. The same applies to the terms ‘rational inquiry,’ ‘drawing lessons,’ and ‘argument.’”

“Generally, these forms of misguidance only find their way to those who do not hold fast to the Book and the Sunnah, as was

---

## Page 1379

al-Zuhrī wont to say:

“Our scholars used to say: ‘Holding fast to the Sunnah is salvation.’”

Mālik said: “The Sunnah is Noah’s ark: whoever boards it is saved, and whoever stays behind drowns.”

This is because the Sunnah, the revealed law, and the prescribed way constitute the straight path that leads Allah’s servants to Him. The Messenger is the expert guide who leads them along this path, as Allah Most High says:

“Indeed, We have sent you as a witness, a bearer of good tidings, and a warner, and as one who calls to Allah by His permission, and as an illuminating lamp.”

Allah Most High also says:

“And indeed, you guide to a straight path—the path of Allah, to whom belongs whatever is in the heavens and whatever is on earth. Surely, to Allah all matters return.”

And Allah Most High says:

“And this is My path, which is straight, so follow it; and do not follow other ways, for they will separate you from His way.”

‘Abd Allāh ibn Mas‘ūd said: “The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, drew a line, then drew other lines to its right and left. He then said: ‘This is Allah’s way, and these are other ways. On each of them is a devil calling people to it.’ Then he recited: ‘And this is My path, which is straight, so follow it; and do not follow other ways, for they will separate you from His way.’”

If a person of sound judgment who hopes to meet Allah reflects on this illustration and considers all the various groups—the Khawārij, then the Mu‘tazilah, then the Jahmiyyah and the Rāfidah, and those speculative theologians closer to the Sunnah than these, such as the Karrāmiyyah, the Kullābiyyah, the Ash‘ariyyah, and others—and considers how each has a way that departs from what the Companions and the adherents of hadith follow, while claiming that its own way is correct, one will find that they are the very people intended by this illustration given by the one protected from error, who does not speak from his own desire. It is nothing but revelation revealed.

What is astonishing is that some of these people explicitly state that, if a hadith conflicts with their reason—especially

---

## Page 1380

in reports concerning Allah’s attributes—they interpret the hadith to conform to their reason, explicitly give their reason precedence over the hadith, and make it the standard by which the hadith is judged. I would ask: does the law revealed to Muhammad explicitly prescribe giving precedence to this reason of theirs, so that it belongs to the way we are commanded to follow? Or is it the reason of an ignorant, misguided, bewildered innovator who has departed from that way? There is no power nor strength except through Allah.

These Ittihādiyyah (who identify Allah with creation) and others like them have gone astray only because of their deficient knowledge of, and faith in, Allah’s attributes, by which He is distinct from created beings, and their failure to follow the Sunnah and the way of the early generations in this matter. Indeed, they may hold Jahmī beliefs that contradict the Sunnah, having received them from someone given to philosophizing or speculative theology. Those beliefs then obstruct their path to Allah. Whenever their hearts seek to draw near to their Lord, follow the straight path to Him, and worship Him—in accordance with their innate disposition and what the messengers conveyed to them concerning His being on high and His greatness—those misleading obstacles turn them away from this. Thus, you find many who follow the Jahmiyyah uncritically agreeing with them in speech, while their hearts remain true to their innate disposition and the Sunnah. Most of them do not understand the negation they utter; rather, they regard it as an unqualified, general declaration of Allah’s transcendence. Some do not understand the Jahmiyyah’s position at all. Instead, they understand the negation in a sound sense, believe that those who affirm the divine attributes affirm its opposite, and hear some people say so.

For example, they may understand the statement, “He is not in any direction, has no place, and is not in heaven,” to mean that He is not within the heavens. This is a sound meaning, and their belief in it is correct, but

---

## Page 1381

they assume that those who made this negation meant no more than that, whereas this is not so. Rather, they mean that there is nothing whatsoever above the Throne, and nothing above the heavens but sheer nonexistence: no God there to be worshipped, no Lord to be invoked and petitioned, no Creator who created all creatures, and that the Prophet was never taken up to his Lord at all. This is what they mean.

This is what led the Ittihādiyyah to say, “He is the existing things themselves.” For their hearts recognize nothing that exists apart from these existing things, if there is nothing else above them. This is among the innate truths concerning existence that are directly apprehended: either there is only this created existence, or there is another existence separate and distinct from it. This is especially so when they know that the celestial spheres are round and that what is highest is what encompasses the rest. They know that there is only this created existence, or a being above it. If, along with this, they believe that there is no other existence and nothing above the world, they must consequently say, “He is this created existence,” as the Ittihādiyyah have said. This is precisely the argument of the Ittihādiyyah.

This very same outlook is shared by the early and later Jahmiyyah, as when they say, “He is everywhere, yet He is nowhere, and is not particular to anything.” They constantly combine these two contradictory statements because they wish to affirm a being, yet in their view there is nothing above the world. He must therefore be either the world itself or within it. Then they wish to affirm something other than what is created;

---

## Page 1382

so they say, “He is not within the world, just as He is not outside it.” Or they say, “He is the existence of created things, not the things themselves.” Or they say, “He is absolute existence.” Thus they affirm Him within whatever they affirm, when their hearts are alike in negation and denial—namely, denying a real being distinct from created things and exalted above them.

They differ only in what they affirm, forcing their innate dispositions and their reason to accept what is impossible and contradictory. They say, “He is within the world, yet He is not within it,” or, “He is the world, yet He is not the world.” Alternatively, they give precedence to affirmation and say, “Rather, He is existence itself”; or to negation and say, “He is neither within the world nor outside it.” Or they adhere to affirmation in one state and negation in another: when reason predominates in one of them, he gives precedence to negation—that He is not within the world; when spiritual feeling and worship predominate, he favors affirmation—that He is within this existence, or is this existence itself. You will find no Jahmī except in one of these four positions. Although they differ in what they affirm—as I have explained to you—they share this denial.

I have seen among them and in their books, and heard from them and from those who report about them, as much of this as Allah has willed. In all these states, they have lost their way concerning the One they worship, their God and their Creator. Then I read the statements of the early Muslim generations and the imams, all of whom describe them in similar terms. Allah has therefore blessed us to follow the path of the believers, and we have believed in Allah and His Messenger.

Every one of these people finds himself unsettled by this belief because it is inherently contradictory. His unease is only partly soothed by some form of unquestioning adherence to someone he reveres, by his fear of disagreeing with his companions, or by his claim that this is a judgment of fancy and imagination rather than reason.

---

## Page 1383

This contradiction in affirming a being who is neither outside the world nor the world itself—an affirmation rejected by their innate dispositions, direct awareness, and reason—is distinct from the innate acknowledgment of a Maker above the world. The latter is the innate disposition’s acknowledgment of recognized truth; the former is its rejection of manifest falsehood.

An example of this is the well-known account related by Muhammad ibn Tāhir al-Maqdisī. Shaykh Abū Jaʿfar al-Hamdānī once attended a gathering in which the teacher Abū al-Maʿālī was saying from the pulpit, “Allah existed when there was no Throne,” and denying His rising over the Throne—as was well known of his teaching, although toward the end of his life he abandoned this belief and died upon the faith of his mother and the elderly women of Nishapur.

He related that Shaykh Abū Jaʿfar said, “Teacher, let us set aside mention of the Throne”—meaning that knowledge of it comes only through revelation—“and tell us about this inescapable awareness we find in our hearts: no one who knows Allah has ever said, ‘O Allah,’ without finding in his heart an impulse directed upward, turning neither right nor left. How, then, can we remove this inescapable awareness from our hearts?”

Abū al-Maʿālī cried out, placed his hand on his head, and said, “Al-Hamdānī has confounded me!”—or words to that effect—and descended from the pulpit.

This shaykh spoke on behalf of all the children of Adam. He explained that knowledge of the Throne and of Allah’s rising over it comes only through revelation and the reports of the Quran and Sunnah. This differs from acknowledging that Allah is above creation, without specifying a Throne or a rising over it. This is something innate and self-evident that we, and everyone who calls upon Allah Most High, find in our hearts. How, then, can we remove this inescapable awareness from our hearts?

---

## Page 1384

{And the slave woman whom the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, asked, “Where is Allah?” She replied, “In heaven.” He said, “Set her free, for she is a believer.”} was a non-Arab slave woman. Who, do you think, had taught her and informed her of what she said? She was simply expressing the innate disposition upon which Allah Most High had created her. The Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, affirmed her answer and testified to her faith.

Let anyone endowed with reason reflect on this: he will find that it guides him to know his Lord and acknowledge Him as he should—not the innovations of those given to excessive speculation and pretentious speech, whom Satan has beguiled and lulled with false hopes.

One example is that leading speculative theologians who have mixed theology with philosophy count certain things among carefully guarded secrets and hidden sciences. Yet anyone with even a little reason and religious commitment who examines these things will find such ignorance and error in them that he would never have imagined these men falling into them. Indeed, he may even refuse to believe that such things came from them.

An example is the commentary on the hadith of the Ascension composed by Abū ʿAbd Allāh al-Rāzī, in which he followed the example of Ibn Sīnā and ʿAyn al-Qudāt al-Hamdānī. He related the hadith of the Ascension in a lengthy narrative, with peculiar names and a sequence found nowhere in the books of the Muslims—not in the authentic, good, or weak hadiths transmitted among the scholars. It was merely fabricated by some beggars and storytellers, some devilish preachers, or some heretics.

Then, despite his ignorance of the hadith of the Ascension—the account found in the books of hadith, Quranic commentary, and the Prophet’s biography—and his turning away from what these books contain to something never heard from a scholar and not found

---

## Page 1385

in any trace of transmitted knowledge—he interpreted it according to the misguided Sabian astrologers’ interpretation. He made the Messenger’s Ascension an ascent through thought to the celestial spheres, and identified the prophets he saw with the heavenly bodies: Adam was the moon, Idrīs was the sun, and the four rivers were the four elements. He also held that the Messenger had come to know absolute necessary existence. He then extolled this interpretation, treating it as secret knowledge that must be kept beyond the understanding of believers and their scholars. Indeed, some who had held him in high esteem were utterly astonished when they saw it. One of his partisan defenders continued to deny it until they showed him a copy in the handwriting of a well-known scholar familiar with his circumstances. He had included it in his book entitled “al-Matālib al-ʿĀliya” (The Lofty Pursuits), in which he collected the principal views of the philosophers and speculative theologians.

You also find Abū Hāmid al-Ghazālī—who surpassed those men in his knowledge of jurisprudence, Sufism, speculative theology, legal theory, and other fields, as well as in asceticism, worship, sincerity of intention, and mastery of the Islamic sciences—referring in “al-Arbaʿīn” (The Forty) and similar works to his book “al-Madnūn bihi ʿalā Ghayr Ahlihi” (That Which Is Withheld from Those Unqualified for It). If you seek out that book, believing it to contain the secrets of reality and the ultimate objects of inquiry, you will find precisely the teachings of the philosophizing Sabians, with their wording and arrangement altered. Anyone unfamiliar with the true nature of people’s doctrines and those of the various religious communities will believe that this is the secret shared by the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, and Abū Bakr, and that it is what is disclosed to those who attain unveiling and apprehend realities through a divine light. For Abū Hāmid frequently refers in his books to that divine light and to what he believes

---

## Page 1386

Sufis and devout worshippers attain through their spiritual discipline and religious devotion in apprehending realities and having them unveiled to them, to the point that they use this as a standard for judging what the revealed Law has brought. The reason for this was that, through his intelligence and sincere pursuit, he had recognized the inconsistencies in the approach of speculative theologians and philosophizing thinkers. Allah had granted him faith in broad outline—as he himself related—and he began yearning for a detailed understanding of that outline. In the words of the spiritual masters and Sufis, he found something closer to the truth and more firmly grounded than the discourse of philosophers and speculative theologians. His assessment was correct. Yet he had not learned of the prophetic inheritance of knowledge and spiritual states possessed by the foremost members of the Muslim community, or of the knowledge and worship attained by the earliest forerunners, through which they achieved unveilings of knowledge and acts of devotion that those others had not attained.

He therefore came to believe that a detailed understanding of that outline could be attained through that path alone, since he knew no other path. The distinctively Sunni, prophetic path had been closed to him by his limited knowledge of it and by the specious arguments he had adopted from philosophizing thinkers and speculative theologians, who thereby stood between him and that path. For this reason, he frequently condemned these barriers and the path of knowledge. But this concerned only the knowledge he had pursued, which had veiled him from truly following the prophetic message. It is not knowledge at all, but merely philosophical and theological doctrines. As the early Muslim generations said:

“Knowledge of speculative theology is ignorance.”

And as Abū Yūsuf said:

“Whoever seeks knowledge through speculative theology becomes a heretic.”

---

## Page 1387

For this reason, some who recognize his merit and religious devotion have come to deny that these books are his. The jurist Abū Muhammad ibn ‘Abd al-Salām even denied—in the notes taken from him—that “The Beginning of Guidance” was his work, saying: “It is merely something falsely attributed to him.” Yet the acceptable material in these books exceeds the objectionable material many times over. The objectionable material consists of general statements, and these books contain neither creeds nor the fundamentals of religion.

As for “That Which Is Withheld from Those Unqualified for It,” another group of scholars denied its attribution to him. Those thoroughly familiar with him and his circumstances, however, know that all this is his own discourse, because they know the sources underlying it and recognize the resemblance between its various parts. Nevertheless, he and others like him were—as I have already explained—unsettled, unable to hold consistently to a firm position. Their intelligence and eagerness to learn made them aspire to the path of the foremost among mankind, but it was not decreed for them to follow the path of the foremost members of this community, who inherited knowledge and faith from the Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. These are the people who possess the realities of faith and the Quran—as we have already explained—who understand the Book of Allah, know and understand the hadith of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and accompany this knowledge with the corresponding spiritual states and deeds, in accordance with what the prophetic message brought.

For this reason, Shaykh Abū ‘Amr ibn al-Salāh said—in a passage I saw in his own handwriting:

“Much has been said about Abū Hāmid, and he himself has said much. As for these books”—meaning those that contradict the truth—“no heed should be paid to them. As for the man himself, one should remain silent about him and entrust his affair to Allah.”

---

## Page 1388

His meaning is that he should not be spoken of ill, for Allah’s pardon of those who forget or err, and the sinner’s repentance, can efface every sin. Such pardon and repentance are highly likely in his case and in similar cases. Moreover, Allah’s forgiveness through good deeds—his own and those of others—and His expiation of sins through afflictions extend even to cases where sins are established. Thus, one should not presume the absence (*) of such forgiveness in the case of a particular individual without clear insight, especially when he has done much good, possesses sound knowledge, performs righteous deeds, and has good intentions.

He inclined toward philosophy, but presented it in the guise of Sufism and Islamic expressions. For this reason, Muslim scholars refuted him—even his closest associate, Abū Bakr ibn al-‘Arabī, who said:

“Our shaykh Abū Hāmid became deeply immersed in the philosophers’ teachings, then wished to extricate himself, but could not.”

He has also been reported to have espoused doctrines of the Bātiniyya (esoteric sects), and corroboration of these reports can be found in his books. Abū ‘Abd Allāh al-Māzarī refuted him in a work devoted specifically to that purpose, as did Abū Bakr al-Turtūshī. His companion Abū al-Hasan al-Marghīnānī refuted his statements in The Niche of Lights and similar works. Shaykh Abū al-Bayān and Shaykh Abū ‘Amr ibn al-Salāh also refuted him. Ibn al-Salāh, Abū Zakariyyā al-Nawawī, and others warned against his statements on these matters. Ibn ‘Aqīl, Ibn al-Jawzī, Abū Muhammad al-Maqdisī, and others likewise refuted him.

This is a broad subject, for those who depart from the way of the earliest forerunners among

> **Footnotes:**
> (*) Shaykh Nāsir ibn Hamad al-Fahd (p. 255) states: “Perhaps the reading should be ithbāt (affirmation).”

---

## Page 1389

the Emigrants and the Helpers, and those who followed them in righteousness, adopt three approaches to the Messenger’s words: imaginative representation, reinterpretation, and attributing ignorance.

Those who adopt imaginative representation are the philosophers and the Bātiniyya. They say that he conjured up images of things that have no underlying reality, and that imaginative representation is the distinctive characteristic of prophethood.

(The approach of reinterpretation)
This is the approach of the speculative theologians among the Jahmiyya, the Muʿtazila, and their followers. They say that his statements have interpretations contrary to what their wording indicates and what is understood from it. Although he did not explain his intended meaning or clarify the truth that must be believed, his purpose, they maintain, was to prompt rational inquiry, so that people would discover the truth through their own reason and strive to reinterpret his words to accord with their own views, thereby earning a reward. Thus, his purpose was not to provide them with clarification, guidance, direction, and instruction, but rather to obscure and confuse. He did not acquaint them with the truth, so that they would arrive at it through their own reason and then recognize that his words were not intended to clarify it. They therefore regard themselves as better off in terms of knowledge without his words than with them.

Those mentioned earlier, such as Ibn Sīnā and others like him, object to this group’s position. They say that his expressions are numerous and explicit, and cannot admit reinterpretation; rather, his intention was imaginative representation and to make people believe things to be otherwise than they actually are.

As for the third group, who claim to be followers of the early generations, they say that the Messenger did not know the meaning of these verses revealed to him, nor did his Companions

---

## Page 1390

know their meaning. Indeed, their position necessarily implies that he himself did not know the meaning of the hadiths he uttered concerning the divine attributes, but spoke words whose meaning he did not know. Those who claim to follow the way of the early generations say that those generations did not know the meanings of the scriptural texts; indeed, they say this even of the Messenger. This is among the most untenable positions.

Among the things they rely upon is their understanding of the words of Allah Most High:

“And none knows its interpretation except Allah.”

They assume that ta’wīl (interpretation) means what they themselves call reinterpretation: a meaning contrary to the apparent meaning.

These people may then say: “The texts are to be understood according to their apparent meaning, while their ta’wīl is known only to Allah.” Yet by ta’wīl they mean something contrary to the apparent meaning. This is a contradiction on their part. One group understands “the apparent meaning” to refer solely to the wording of the texts. Both groups misunderstand the verse.

This is because the term “ta’wīl” has acquired three meanings through differences in terminological usage.

The first is the reality to which the words ultimately refer, even when this accords with their apparent meaning. This is the sense intended by the term ta’wīl in the Quran and Sunnah, as in the words of Allah Most High:

“Are they waiting for anything but its fulfilment? On the Day its fulfilment comes, those who had forgotten it before will say, ‘The messengers of our Lord certainly came with the truth.’”

Another example is the statement of ‘Ā’isha:

“The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, would frequently say while bowing and prostrating: ‘Glory be to You, O Allah, our Lord, and to You belongs all praise. O Allah, forgive me,’ thereby putting the Quran into practice.”

---

## Page 1391

The second meaning intended by the term ta’wīl is “explanation,” as it is used by many Quran commentators. Thus Mujāhid, a leading authority in Quran commentary, said that “those firmly grounded in knowledge” know the interpretation of the ambiguous passages. By this he meant explaining them and clarifying their meanings, which those firmly grounded in knowledge do know.

The third meaning intended by ta’wīl is to divert an expression from the apparent meaning it indicates to a contrary meaning, on the basis of separate evidence that requires this. Such interpretation necessarily departs from what the wording indicates and makes clear. Calling this ta’wīl was not the usage of the early generations. Rather, a group of later scholars who delved into jurisprudence, its principles, and speculative theology restricted the term ta’wīl to this meaning. They assumed that this was what Allah Most High intended by His words:

“And none knows its interpretation except Allah.”

They then followed two approaches regarding this interpretation: one group said that none knows it except Allah, while another said that those firmly grounded in knowledge know it. Both groups are mistaken.

For in many instances—or in most, indeed the vast majority of them—this interpretation amounts to distorting words from their proper places, like the interpretations of the Qarāmitah and the Bātiniyyah. This is the interpretation that the early generations of this community and its leading scholars unanimously condemned. They denounced its adherents from every corner of the earth, and meteors were hurled in their wake.

Imam Ahmad wrote a book refuting these people, which he entitled: “Refutation of

---

## Page 1392

the Heretics and the Jahmiyyah concerning the Ambiguous Passages of the Quran about Which They Entertained Doubts and Which They Interpreted Contrary to Their True Meaning.” Ahmad thus reproached them for explaining the Quran in ways contrary to its meaning.

Neither Ahmad nor any of the leading scholars said that the Messenger did not know the meanings of the verses and hadiths concerning the divine attributes. Nor did they say that the Companions and those who followed them in righteousness did not know the explanation and meanings of the Quran. How could they, when Allah commanded reflection upon His Book? He Most High said:

“This is a blessed Book which We have revealed to you, that they may reflect upon its verses.”

He did not say, “some of its verses.” He also said:

“Then do they not reflect upon the Quran?”

And He said:

“Then have they not reflected upon the word?”

There are other such texts which make clear that Allah loves people to reflect upon the entire Quran, and that He has made it a light and guidance for His servants. It is impossible for something whose meaning cannot be understood to serve that purpose.

Abū ‘Abd al-Rahmān al-Sulamī said: “Those who used to teach us the Quran—‘Uthmān ibn ‘Affān and ‘Abd Allāh ibn Mas‘ūd—told us: ‘Whenever we learned ten verses from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, we would not move beyond them until we had learned the knowledge and practice they contained.’ They said: ‘Thus we learned the Quran, knowledge, and practice together.’” These matters are discussed at length elsewhere.

The point here is this: if what someone says about the Messenger and his explanations to people belongs to the teachings of the heretics, what can his position concerning the early generations be, that he should claim to follow them? Even by his own reckoning and that of his group, he is at odds with the Messenger and the early generations. For he has openly proclaimed teachings of the deniers which, in his view, the Messenger considered best left undisclosed because they would corrupt people. But this is not so in the judgment of those possessed of knowledge and faith.

---

## Page 1393

The teachings of the deniers are false both inwardly and outwardly, and the Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and his followers are free of such things. Rather, he died leaving us upon a clear, bright path, whose night is like its day; none deviates from it except one who is doomed to perish.

He informed us that:

“Every new matter introduced after him is an innovation, and every innovation is misguidance.”

Some practitioners of speculative theology may recite this verse by Majnūn of Banū ‘Āmir:

“Each claims a bond with Laylā,
Yet Laylā acknowledges none of their claims.”

If someone composes poetry containing wisdom, or quotes a verse to illustrate something he has recognized as true, he is close to the mark. But if he seeks to establish a claim merely through words arranged in verse or otherwise, he should be told: “You must demonstrate that the early generations do not acknowledge those with whom you claim affiliation.”

This is evident in what he and others have mentioned: they attribute to the early generations statements they never made, and which no one knowledgeable about them and upright in his reporting has transmitted from them. For a transmitter must possess both knowledge and integrity.

If we suppose that someone has reported the position of the early generations as he describes it, then either he has little knowledge of their transmitted accounts—like Abū al-Ma‘ālī, Abū Hāmid al-Ghazālī, Ibn al-Khatīb, and others like them. Their knowledge of hadith was insufficient for them to be counted even among ordinary practitioners of that discipline, let alone its specialists. Each of these men knew al-Bukhārī, Muslim, and their hadiths only by hearsay, much as ordinary people speak of them. They do not distinguish between authentic, mass-transmitted hadith

---

## Page 1394

as recognized by hadith scholars, and fabricated, falsely attributed hadith. Their books bear the clearest witness to this, for they contain astonishing things. You will find that most of these speculative theologians and Sufis who depart from the way of the early generations acknowledge this, either at death or before it. The accounts of this are numerous and well known.

Take Abū al-Hasan al-Ash‘arī: he spent forty years in Mu‘tazilism, arguing in its defence. Then he renounced it, explicitly declared the Mu‘tazilites misguided, and refuted them at length.

Likewise, Abū Hāmid al-Ghazālī, despite his extraordinary intelligence, devotion, knowledge of speculative theology and philosophy, and pursuit of asceticism, spiritual discipline, and Sufism, ultimately suspends judgment on these questions and remains bewildered. Towards the end, he refers people to the approach of those who practise spiritual unveiling—even if he subsequently returned to the approach of the hadith scholars and composed Iljām al-‘Awāmm ‘an ‘Ilm al-Kalām (Restraining the Common People from Speculative Theology).

Similarly, Abū ‘Abd Allāh Muhammad ibn ‘Umar al-Rāzī said in the book he composed on the categories of pleasure:

“I have reflected upon the methods of speculative theology and the approaches of philosophy, and I have not found them to heal the ailing or quench the thirsty. I have found the closest path to be the way of the Quran. For affirmation, I read:

‘The Most Merciful rose over the Throne.’

‘To Him ascends good speech, and righteous work raises it.’

And for negation, I read:

‘There is nothing like Him.’

‘And they do not encompass

---

## Page 1395

Him in knowledge.’

‘Do you know of any equal to Him?’

Then he said: ‘Whoever has undergone an experience like mine will have come to know what I know.’”

He would often recite:

“Reason’s bold advance ends in fetters,
And most of mankind’s striving goes astray.
Our souls feel estranged within our bodies;
Our worldly life yields only harm and misery.
From a lifetime of inquiry we have gained
Nothing but a collection of ‘he said’ and ‘they said.’”

Here, too, is Imām al-Haramayn, who abandoned the views he had espoused and defended, and chose the way of the early generations. He would say:

“My companions, do not occupy yourselves with speculative theology. Had I known that it would bring me to this, I would never have pursued it.”

At his death, he said:

“I plunged into the vast sea, leaving behind the people of Islam and their sciences, and ventured into what they had forbidden me to pursue. Now, unless my Lord comes to my aid with His mercy, woe to Ibn al-Juwaynī! Here I am, dying upon my mother’s creed”—or he said, “the creed of the elderly women of Nishapur.”

Likewise, Abū ‘Abd Allāh Muhammad ibn ‘Abd al-Karīm al-Shahrastānī stated:

“He reported that he had found among the philosophers and speculative theologians nothing but bewilderment and regret.”

He would recite:

“By my life, I have travelled through all the seats of learning,
And cast my gaze among their landmarks;
Yet I saw only a bewildered man, his hand upon his chin,
Or a regretful man, gnashing his teeth.”

And Ibn al-Fārid—one of the later proponents of the doctrine that Creator and creation are one, and author of the celebrated poem in t-rhyme entitled “Nazm al-Sulūk” (The Poem of Spiritual Wayfaring), in which he expressed this doctrine in elegantly worded verse, yet it is more vile than the flesh of

---

## Page 1396

a pig on a golden platter. How fitting it would be to call it “Nazm al-Shukūk” (The Poem of Doubts)! Allah knows best about it and what it contains. It gained wide currency, and people of the time went to great lengths in praising it and esteeming the doctrine that Creator and creation are one which it contains—recited as death approached him:

“If what I have suffered marks my rank in love with you,
Then I have wasted my days.
A wish my soul attained for a time—
Today I deem it a jumble of dreams.”

Among the foundations of faith is that Allah makes His servant steadfast through the firm word in this worldly life and in the Hereafter, as the Most High says:

“Have you not seen how Allah sets forth a parable: a good word is like a good tree, whose root is firmly fixed and whose branches reach into the sky?”

“It yields its fruit at all times by its Lord’s permission. And Allah sets forth parables for people so that they may take heed.”

“And a bad word is like a bad tree, uprooted from the surface of the earth, with no stability.”

“Allah makes those who believe steadfast through the firm word in this worldly life and in the Hereafter. And Allah leads the wrongdoers astray, and Allah does whatever He wills.”

The word is the foundation of belief, for belief is the word that a person holds to be true. The best of words and beliefs is the declaration of Allah’s oneness and the belief that there is no deity worthy of worship except Allah. The worst of words and beliefs is the declaration of associating partners with Allah, which means taking another deity alongside Allah. This is false and has no reality. Hence He, glorified be He, says:

“With no stability.”

Thus, the more a researcher investigates and a person acts upon these corrupt words and beliefs, the more he increases only in misguidance, distance from the truth, and knowledge of their falsity. As the Most High says:

“As for those who disbelieve, their deeds are like a mirage on a plain, which a thirsty person thinks is water, until, when he reaches it, he finds it to be nothing. But he finds Allah there, who pays him his account in full; and Allah is swift in

---

## Page 1397

reckoning.”

“Or like layers of darkness in a deep sea, covered by waves, above which are waves, above which are clouds—darkness upon darkness. When one stretches out his hand, he can hardly see it. And whoever Allah does not grant light has no light.”

He, glorified be He, thus sets forth two parables:

The first is the parable of disbelief and compound ignorance, which the person possessing it thinks has substance, whereas in reality it is an illusion with no existence, like a mirage. The heart thirsts for truth just as the body thirsts for water. When he seeks what he thought was water, he finds it to be a mirage, and he finds Allah there, who pays him his account in full; and Allah is swift in reckoning. This is how you will find most of those who depart from the Sunnah and the community.

The second is the parable of disbelief and simple ignorance, in which a person neither discerns any truth nor sees any guidance. Compound disbelief necessarily entails simple disbelief, and every instance of disbelief must involve compound ignorance. Allah, glorified be He, set forth these two parables to illustrate the condition of holding a false belief and the condition of not knowing the truth—which resembles the condition of those who have incurred wrath and those who are astray—the condition of one who obstinately clings to falsehood until punishment befalls him, and the condition of a person astray who cannot see the path of guidance.

We therefore ask Allah the Almighty to make us steadfast through the firm word in this worldly life and in the Hereafter, and to enable us to hold fast to the Book and the Sunnah.

---

## Page 1398

What many followers of shaykhs and Sufis attribute to truthful shaykhs may, for example, be lies and impossibilities. Or it may consist of ambiguous statements of theirs that these followers have misinterpreted; mistakes and lapses made by some shaykhs; or their sins and errors, such as the many religious innovations and immoral acts that some commit on the basis of a defensible interpretation or on an unjustifiable basis. The person may be pardoned for this, repent of it, have good deeds through which he is forgiven, or suffer afflictions through which his sins are expiated. Alternatively, such statements may come from people who imitate Allah’s allies through ascetic practices, acts of worship, and spiritual attainments, yet are not among Allah’s God-fearing allies. Rather, they are among the ignorant, the unjust, the transgressors, the hypocrites, or the disbelievers.

This is widespread and has filled the world. You find every group claiming exclusive access to secrets and realities that even the messengers do not claim, asserting that these are possessed by its select few and must be met only with unquestioning acceptance. They support these claims with fabricated hadiths and false interpretations. One example is the statement they attribute to ‘Umar:

“The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and Abū Bakr used to converse, while I was like a black African between them.”

Thus they portray ‘Umar, in the company of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and his friend, as being like a black African, although he was present and listening to their words.

Then one of them claims to have acquired this knowledge through something cast into his heart, and each claims that the lies and falsehood he himself utters constitute that knowledge. Were I to enumerate the various kinds of false claims in this matter, the discussion would be lengthy.

Some of them ascribe poems to the shaykh, calling them “the Quran’s Companion.” These poems stir him more deeply, and their contents bring him greater joy, than the Quran does, although they contain various lies and errors.

---

## Page 1399

Others compose poems for him proclaiming union with God, asserting that he is the creator of all creation, that he created the heavens and the earth, and that they prostrate before him and worship him.

Others describe their Lord in their poems using accounts transmitted in fabricated reports that liken Him to created beings, claim to describe how He is, and ascribe a body to Him. These are fabricated lies and outright disbelief: sharing meals and drinks with Him, walking alongside Him, embracing Him, His descending to the earth and sitting in one of its gardens, and the like. Each of them treats these things as hidden secrets and closely guarded knowledge reserved for the elect among Allah’s God-fearing allies.

One example is what you find among the Rāfidah, adherents of Shiism, and those who have learned from them: claims to knowledge of secrets and realities supposedly received from the Prophet’s household, concerning either religious knowledge or knowledge of events. They regard these as among the most exalted matters, which they must urge one another to conceal, while believing whatever of them they do not truly understand. Yet all of it is fabricated lies and invented falsehood. This group—the “Rāfidah”—is among those most given to lying and claiming possession of concealed knowledge. This is why the Bātiniyyah and the Qarāmitah have affiliated themselves with them.

The first of these people appeared in the time of the Commander of the Faithful, ‘Alī ibn Abī Tālib, may Allah be pleased with him. They began claiming that he had been singled out to receive secret knowledge and a special bequest. His closest companions would therefore ask him about this, and he would deny it. When word reached him that such claims were being made, he would address the people and deny that this was true of him.

---

## Page 1400

The compilers of the authentic hadith collections have transmitted these words of ‘Alī through several routes. For example, one authentic report relates that Abū Juhayfah said:

“I asked ‘Alī, ‘Do you have anything that is not in the Quran?’ He replied, ‘No, by Him who splits the grain and creates the soul! We have nothing except what is in the Quran, apart from an understanding of His Book that Allah grants a man, and what is in this document.’ I asked, ‘What is in the document?’ He replied, ‘Blood money, the release of captives, and that a Muslim is not to be killed for killing a disbeliever.’”

Al-Bukhārī’s wording is:

“‘Do you have any revelation other than what is in Allah’s Book?’ He replied, ‘No, by Him who splits the grain and creates the soul! I know of nothing except an understanding of the Quran that Allah grants a man.’”

The two authentic hadith collections also relate, through Ibrāhīm al-Taymī, from his father—and this is one of the soundest chains of transmission on earth—that ‘Alī said:

“We have nothing except Allah’s Book and this document from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace: ‘Medina is a sanctuary between ‘Ayr and Thawr.’”

In a narration recorded by Muslim:

“‘Alī ibn Abī Tālib addressed us, saying: ‘Whoever claims that we have a book that we read, other than Allah’s Book and what is in this document’—the narrator said, ‘His document was hanging in the sheath of his sword’—‘has lied. It contains the ages of camels and certain matters concerning wounds, and in it the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said: “Medina is a sanctuary.”’” The hadith continues.

As for the lies and secrets they claim to have received from Ja‘far al-Sādiq, these are among the greatest fabrications. It is even said that no one has had as many lies attributed to him as Ja‘far, may Allah be pleased with him. Among these works attributed to him is the book al-Jafr, in which they claim he wrote

---

## Page 1401

events. A jafr is a young goat; they claim that he wrote this on its skin. The same applies to the book “al-Bitāqa” (The Card), which Ibn al-Hillī and others like him from the Maghrib claim, and to works such as “al-Jadwal” (The Table), concerning the crescent moon, “al-Haft,” attributed to Ja‘far, and much Quran commentary and other material.

Another example is the “Epistles of the Brethren of Purity,” compiled by a group in Baghdad under Buyid rule. They were Sabian adherents of philosophy who professed adherence to the upright monotheistic faith. They claimed to have combined the religion of the Sabians who had altered their religion with the upright monotheistic faith, bringing together philosophical discourse and elements of the Sharia. The work contains much disbelief and ignorance. Nevertheless, some people—including certain prominent judges in the provinces—claim that it consists of the words of Ja‘far al-Sādiq. This is a heretic’s assertion and an ignorant man’s slander.

[Another example is what some ordinary people recount of the predictions of future battles attributed to “Ibn Ghandab.” They claim that he taught al-Hasan and al-Husayn. The scholars agree that no such thing ever existed. These predictions attributed to “Ibn Ghandab” were merely composed by certain ignorant people during the reign of Nūr al-Dīn or thereabouts. They are defective verse that reveals their composer’s ignorance.]

Likewise, most of these predictions of future battles transmitted in verse and similar forms are lies. In our own time, judges and shaykhs have fabricated more than one such work. I made one of these men acknowledge this after he had claimed that they were ancient, and I said to him: “Rather, you composed them yourself and passed them off

> **Footnotes:**
> (*) Shaykh Nāsir ibn Hamad al-Fahd stated (p. 29):
> “Ibn Ghandab” is a textual corruption; the correct reading is “Ibn ‘Aqb,” as also appears in al-Minhāj, 7:182–183, and as Khalīfa mentions in Kashf al-Zunūn, 2:1818:
> “Malhamat Ibn ‘Aqb (The Prophecy of Ibn ‘Aqb): He is Yahyā ibn ‘Aqb, the teacher of al-Hasan and al-Husayn—may Allah be pleased with them both (!). It is a poem rhyming in lām, beginning:
> ‘I have seen a wondrous state of affairs,
> For reasons that my verse records.’”

---

## Page 1402

on one of the Muslim rulers when the Muslims were besieging Acre.” Other judges and others likewise deceived rulers other than this one.

Falsehood concerning events in the world is more prevalent than falsehood concerning religious matters, because those who give worldly interests precedence over religion are more eager for such knowledge. Although religious people also have an interest in it, their interest in religion is stronger. The former do not possess the same light for distinguishing truth from falsehood as religious people do. Hence liars have proliferated in this field, many of their falsehoods have gained currency, vast sums have been wrongfully acquired through them, and many people aspiring to kingship and similar ambitions have been killed as a result.

For this reason, they employ various methods of deception in this field and deliberately lie about it. Sometimes they appeal to physical movements and configurations brought about by Allah, such as the movements of the celestial spheres and heavenly bodies, meteors, thunder, lightning, winds, and other such phenomena. At other times, they rely on movements and configurations that they themselves produce, such as divining with sand, pebbles, and barley, drawing lots by hand, and similar practices belonging to the same category as divination by arrows. They seek knowledge of events through this practice of divination, whether using arrows, pebbles, or other objects mentioned by scholars of Quranic exegesis.

Thus, whenever someone physically alters any material object in order to discover what the future holds for him, this belongs to the same category—unlike the good omen sanctioned by the Sharia, which used to

---

## Page 1403

please the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. This is when a person sets out trusting in Allah and hears a good word:

“He liked good omens and disliked taking ill omens.”

A good omen reinforces what a person has undertaken with Allah’s permission and in reliance upon Him, whereas taking an ill omen runs counter to this. It is therefore disliked for a person to take ill omens. Such omens harm only those who heed them, because they have harmed themselves; they do not harm those who rely upon Allah. The purpose here is not to discuss these matters or explain why they sometimes prove correct and repeatedly prove wrong.

Rather, the point is that people deliberately tell many lies concerning these practices, even when the practices have given no indication of what they claim. Likewise, many people deliberately lie about dreams, although these include righteous dreams, which constitute one of the forty-six parts of prophethood. Similarly, the jinn would mix a hundred lies with a word they heard from heaven and then convey it to the soothsayers. Thus, it is established in Sahīh Muslim:

“Mu‘āwiyah ibn al-Hakam al-Sulamī reported: I said, ‘O Messenger of Allah, I have only recently left the time of ignorance, and Allah has brought Islam. Some men among us visit soothsayers.’

“He replied, ‘Do not visit them.’

“I said, ‘Some men among us take ill omens.’

“He replied, ‘That is something they feel in their hearts, but it must not deter them.’

“I said, ‘Some men among us draw lines.’

“He replied, ‘One of the prophets used to draw lines; whoever matches his lines is right.’”

If abundant lies may be deliberately introduced even into things that are parts of prophethood or reports from the angels, what, then, of something inherently inconsistent, with no firm foundation? This is why you find that most people whose religion is corrupt engage in falsehoods concerning events in the world, such as those who hold that the Creator and creation are one. For Ibn ‘Arabī, in his book “‘Anqā’ Mughrib” and elsewhere, foretold many future events

---

## Page 1404

most of which were false. The same applies to Ibn Sab‘īn, and likewise to those who calculated how long this Muslim community would endure through the numerical values of alphabetic letters—a method inherited from the Jews—and through the movements of the heavenly bodies—a method inherited from the Sabians. This was done by Abū Nasr al-Kindī and other philosophers, by some followers of al-Rāzī who wrote on Quranic interpretation, and by some with Shiite leanings who interpreted the experiences of ascetics.

I have encountered groups among their followers who claim that these matters belong to hidden secrets and closely guarded knowledge. I discussed this with several groups of them, swearing to them that it was fabricated falsehood and that none of these things would come to pass. I challenged some of them to join me in invoking Allah’s curse upon whichever of us was lying, because the matter concerned the foundations of religion. They belonged to those who hold that the Creator and creation are one, whose claims would take a long time to describe.

They claimed that their shaykh, whom they regarded as the foremost spiritual knower and ascetic of his age, was the Messiah who would descend. They claimed that this meant the descent of the spiritual essence of Jesus, peace be upon him; that his mother was named Mary; that he would unite the three religions; and that he would appear in a manifestation more perfect than that of Muhammad and the other messengers. They hold views that rank among the gravest abominations, whose enumeration and description would take a long time.

Another astonishing thing is that these theologians, who claim to know the realities of matters of knowledge and religion while opposing the Sunnah and the Muslim community, each cite as evidence whatever hadith they happen to encounter

---

## Page 1405

that is fabricated or too ambiguous for its meaning to be understood. Whenever one of them finds a report containing ambiguity, he interprets it according to his own opinion. Some of them thus cite fabrications, such as the statement falsely attributed to ‘Umar, “I was like a Zanj man (a black African),” or what they narrate about “the secret of the Ascension,” or their account that:

“The people of the Suffah heard the intimate discourse without the Messenger being aware of it. When the Messenger descended, they informed him. He asked, ‘From where did you hear it?’ They replied, ‘We were listening to the address.’”

Indeed, when I explained to a group who had assumed the role of shaykhs and become examples for people to follow that this was a lie, and that Allah had never caused any such thing to happen, I said: “What makes this clear to you is that the Ascension took place in Mecca, according to the explicit text of the Quran and the consensus of the Muslims, whereas the Suffah existed only in Medina. How, then, could there have been people of the Suffah in Mecca?”
