Likewise, they cite the claim that the people of the Suffah fought against the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and his Companions alongside the polytheists when the latter prevailed, claiming that they were with Allah. They use this to justify following whatever actually happens, whether it constitutes obedience to Allah or disobedience, and to equate whatever happens with what His religion prescribes. This resembles the words of those who associated partners with Allah:

“If Allah had willed, we would not have associated partners with Him, nor would our fathers.”

There are many fabrications of this kind.

As for ambiguous reports, an example is their appeal to certain Companions’ prohibition against disclosing some obscure matters of knowledge, such as the statement of ‘Alī, may Allah be pleased with him:

“Speak to people about what they understand, and leave aside what they cannot comprehend. Would you like Allah and His Messenger to be disbelieved?”

And the statement of ‘Abdullāh ibn Mas‘ūd:

---

## Page 1406

“No man relates to a people an account beyond their understanding without its becoming a trial for some of them.”

And the statement of ‘Abdullāh ibn ‘Abbās concerning the interpretation of the verses:

“What assurance do you have that, if I explained them to you, you would not disbelieve? Your disbelief in them would consist in denying them.”

These reports are true. Yet each of them identifies what was left unspoken with his own claims of secrets and realities which, when disclosed, prove to be falsehood, disbelief, and hypocrisy. Even Abū Hāmid al-Ghazālī, in “Minhāj al-Qāsidīn” (The Path of the Seekers) and elsewhere, and others like him, cited these lines attributed to ‘Alī ibn al-Husayn:

“How many a jewel of knowledge, were I to disclose it,
Would bring the charge: ‘You are an idol-worshipper!’
And Muslim men would deem my blood lawful to shed,
Regarding their foulest deed as something good.”

If, then, such are the methods of those who claim a depth of understanding and knowledge of secrets that has led them outside the Sunnah and the Muslim community—who allege that this knowledge concerning religion or the created world belongs exclusively to them, believe its general and ambiguous statements, and claim to have been granted an understanding of the realities of worship and purity of religious devotion that was not granted to the earliest generation, the guardians of Islam and luminaries of the faith, yet do not dare reject or deny these claims, despite their falsehood sometimes being apparent and sometimes hidden—then it is evident that reason and religion require us to recognize that prophethood and messengership have the stronger claim to all deep understanding, knowledge, insight, and comprehensive understanding of the secrets of things and their inner realities. No believer disputes this. We are now addressing those whose hearts contain faith.

---

## Page 1407

Since this is so, those who know these matters best are those most closely connected with the Messenger: those who know best his words and deeds, his movements and stillness, his comings and goings, his inward and outward life; those most knowledgeable about his Companions, his life and its events; those most diligent in studying all this and those who transmitted it; and those most devoted to following him and taking him as their example.

These are the people of the Sunnah and hadith. They preserve his hadith, distinguish its sound reports from its unsound ones, comprehend it, and possess an understanding of its meanings that Allah grants them. They believe and affirm it, obey and submit, and follow his example. Alongside this, they possess powerful intellects, sound analogical reasoning and discernment, and profound spiritual unveilings and communications. Their reflection, reasoning and judgment are the soundest, and their dreams and unveilings the truest.

Does not anyone with even the slightest reason and religious commitment know that these people have a stronger claim to truthfulness, knowledge, faith and sound understanding than those who oppose them? That they possess knowledge which the ignorant and the religious innovator deny? That what they possess is the manifest truth, whereas those who are ignorant of their ways and oppose them are themselves burdened with empty verbiage and misguidance?

This is a subject that would require a lengthy explanation. For human souls produce words and deeds that none but the Lord of Majesty can fully encompass. Utterances include informative statements and performative expressions, such as commands and prohibitions. The finest and truest discourse is the Book of Allah: its reports are the truest reports, its exposition the clearest exposition, and its commands the wisest commands.

“Then in what statement after Allah and His verses will they believe?”

And everyone

---

## Page 1408

who follows discourse or an utterance put forward in opposition to the Quran—of which it is said that its speaker receives it through inspiration and revelation, or that he composes and originates it—is among the gravest wrongdoers. Thus, when Allah, glorified be He, mentioned the statement of those who failed to give Him His due esteem by denying that revelation was sent down to human beings, He mentioned the three categories of those who imitate him and claim to match him.

For someone claiming to match him either says, “Allah has revealed to me”; or says, “It was revealed to me,” “It was imparted to me,” or “I was told,” without naming the speaker; or attributes it to himself and states that he himself composed it.

These possibilities exhaust the alternatives because he either leaves the agent unnamed or names him. If he names him, he presents it either as Allah’s speech or as his own. If he presents it as the speech of devils, it will not be accepted from him. Whatever he presents as the speech of angels falls within what he attributes to Allah or what he expresses without naming its agent. Allah Most High therefore said:

“Who does greater wrong than someone who fabricates a lie against Allah, or says, ‘Revelation has come to me,’ when nothing has been revealed to him, or says, ‘I will send down the like of what Allah has sent down’?”

Reflect on how He grouped the first two within the category of someone who presents it as revelation from Allah without naming the revealer. Both belong to the same class in claiming to receive communications, whereas He placed the other in the category of someone who claims to produce its like. Thus He said:

“Someone who fabricates a lie against Allah,”

then said:

“Or says, ‘I will send down the like of what Allah has sent down.’”

The fabricator of lies and the person who says, “Revelation has come to me,” when nothing has been revealed to him, both fall under the first designation, to which the other designation is conjoined. These, then, are the three who lay claim to a semblance of prophethood. Before them, the denier of prophethood had already been mentioned.

---

## Page 1409

Thus, this encompasses all the foundations of disbelief: denying the messengers or seeking to rival them, as did Musaylimah the Liar and others like him. These are the “foundations of innovation” that we refute here, for whoever opposes the Sunnah rejects some of what the Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, brought, or counters the Messenger’s words with something he treats as their equal: an opinion, an unveiling, or the like.

It has therefore become clear that those who call these people and their leaders Hashwiyyah are themselves more deserving of every blameworthy description they mention. The leaders of those they disparage have a stronger claim to every form of beneficial knowledge, rigorous scholarship, the uncovering of truths, and specialized knowledge unknown to these ignorant detractors who deny Allah and His Messenger.

If they disparage them as Hashwiyyah because they narrate hadiths without discrimination, then their opponents are the foremost purveyors of worthless opinions and discourse whose soundness is unknown—indeed, whose falsity is known. If it is because they include ordinary people who lack discernment, then every one of those sects has among its followers some of the most ignorant and unbelieving people. Yet the ordinary followers of those they disparage are the ones who frequent the mosques for prayer, remember Allah and supplicate to Him, perform pilgrimage to the Ancient House, strive in Allah’s cause, and embody honesty, trustworthiness, and every kind of good in the world.

It has thus become clear to you that their opponents are more deserving of these grounds for censure, while these people are further removed from them. People must therefore turn to them concerning the prophetic inheritance with which Allah has singled them out, and which is found only among them.

---

## Page 1410

Moreover, we should consider both those given this label and those who apply it to them: which of the two is more deserving of it? It is well known that this label became prevalent among those who deny the divine attributes and are suspected of heresy. Scholars such as Abū Hātim and others have stated that a hallmark of heretics is their calling the people of hadith Hashwiyyah.

We shall use terms whose application is not disputed, such as “affirmation” and “negation.” We therefore say: it is well known that some people apply this label to the people of hadith who accept hadith according to its apparent meaning. The further someone is removed from hadith, the more vehemently he disparages its adherents with this label: the Qarmatians, then the philosophers, then the Muʿtazilah. They use it to disparage the speculative theologians who affirm the divine attributes—among the Kullābiyyah, Karrāmiyyah, and Ashʿariyyah—as well as jurists, Sufis, and others.

They apply this label to anyone who follows and accepts the scriptural texts. Those who affirm attributes established through reason, such as knowledge and power, but not those known through revelation, and others of similar views, call those who affirm the attributes known through revelation Hashwiyyah. Such is the practice of Abū al-Maʿālī al-Juwaynī, Abū Hāmid al-Ghazālī, and others like them.

Abū Muhammad followed Abū al-Maʿālī’s approach in jurisprudence and speculative theology. Yet Abū Muhammad was more knowledgeable about hadith and more faithful to it than Abū al-Maʿālī, and better versed in the schools of jurisprudence. Abū al-Maʿālī adhered more closely to speculative theology, while the two were comparable in their command of Arabic.

These people criticize their opponent either for collecting hadith filler without distinguishing sound reports from weak ones, or because they regard following hadith in matters of theological foundations as belonging to the doctrine of

---

## Page 1411

the Hashwiyyah, because these are matters requiring certain knowledge, which hadith does not provide; following scriptural texts as a general principle in foundational inquiries of speculative theology is mere filler, because those texts do not suffice for that purpose.

The issue thus comes down to one of two things: doubt concerning either the chain of transmission or the text. Either they attribute to the Messenger something he is not known to have said—as with solitary reports—and maintain that these reports yield certain knowledge; or they regard their understanding of the wording as certain knowledge when it is not, given the possible interpretations of textual evidence.

There is no doubt that this is the mainstay of every heretic and hypocrite: it nullifies knowledge of the message with which Allah sent His Messenger. Sometimes he says, “We do not know that they said this,” and sometimes, “We do not know what they meant by these words.” Once knowledge of their statements or their meaning is denied, no knowledge can be gained from them. He can then advance whatever doctrines he wishes, secure against being challenged with reports from the prophets, for he has entrusted that frontier to those two guards, who repel the Messenger’s troops from him and assail anyone who invokes those reports as evidence.

This very position constitutes an attack on prophethood itself, even if he professes reverence for the prophets and acknowledges their perfection: his is the acknowledgment of someone who receives no knowledge from them. In his eyes, the Messenger is thus like a caliph accorded recognition on coins and in sermons—merely in form and wording, in writing and speech—without any command or prohibition of his being obeyed. He possesses the outward form of leadership through the coinage and sermons assigned to him, but not its reality.

As for this arrangement—even if many kings have deemed it permissible because some caliphs were unable to

---

## Page 1412

discharge the duties of rule, such as jihad and governance, as many governors’ deputies act when those who appointed them are weak and incapable, so that sound administration results from combining the precedence of the man of office and noble lineage with his deputy’s strength; or even if a ruler does this out of personal desire and ambition for leadership for himself and his faction, excluding someone more entitled to it than he is, and follows the path of those who seize power through aggression—it is well known that a believer in Allah and His Messenger cannot deem it permissible to say of the prophetic message that it is incapable of establishing and explaining knowledge, such that acknowledging it while obtaining knowledge of divine matters elsewhere would ensure the soundness of religion. Nor can he deem it permissible to transgress against it by putting himself forward before Allah and His Messenger, placing his own knowledge and statements above the Messenger’s knowledge and statements. Nor can he deem it permissible to subject it to rational reinterpretations and claim that this perfects the religion, and that the religion cannot be complete without it.

At best, he claims that the Messenger knew that what he conveyed had interpretations and explanations other than what the apparent sense and understood meaning of his words indicated; that he left these unexplained only because he could not explain them among those Bedouins and others like them; and that he entrusted this task to the intellects of later generations. This is indeed what they do.

For the adherents of philosophy say that the messengers were unable to explain the truths because disclosing them would corrupt people, whose minds could not bear them. They may then say that the messengers knew these truths. Some of them may instead say, “They did not know them,” or, “I know them better than they did.” They then explain these truths themselves through the methods of inference at their disposal.

Yet they have failed to understand that if knowledge of these truths is possible, then it is possible for the messengers, just as they claim it is possible for themselves. Otherwise, by their own admission, they have no way of knowing them. The same applies to expressing

---

## Page 1413

and explaining knowledge through speech and writing. If that is impossible, then neither can you do it—yet you speak and record your knowledge in books. If it is possible, then your claim that “the messengers were unable to do so” cannot be valid.

If you say, “These truths can be communicated to a select few, but not to people at large”—which is indeed what they say—then it is well known that the messengers’ knowledge would be possessed by their closest followers, just as your knowledge is possessed by yours. It is also well known that the more thoroughly someone knows a leader’s words, circumstances, and affairs, both private and public, and the more faithfully he upholds them, the stronger his claim to a close association with that leader.

There is no doubt that the people of hadith are the most knowledgeable members of the Muslim community and the most intimately acquainted with the knowledge of the Messenger and his closest followers: the Rightly Guided Caliphs and the rest of the Ten; Ubayy ibn Kaʿb, ʿAbd Allāh ibn Masʿūd, Muʿādh ibn Jabal, ʿAbd Allāh ibn Salām, Salmān al-Fārisī, Abū al-Dardāʾ, ʿUbādah ibn al-Sāmit, Abū Dharr al-Ghifārī, ʿAmmār ibn Yāsir, and Hudhayfah ibn al-Yamān; and Saʿd ibn Muʿādh, Usayd ibn Hudayr, Saʿd ibn ʿUbādah, ʿAbbād ibn Bishr, Sālim, the freedman of Abū Hudhayfah, and others. These were among those closest to the Messenger, most knowledgeable about his private affairs, and most faithful in following his example in them.

The scholars of hadith know these people and their private affairs better than anyone else and follow their example most faithfully. Thus, they possess this knowledge—the knowledge of the Messenger’s closest followers and inner circle—just as the closest followers of the philosophers possess the knowledge of

---

## Page 1414

their leaders, and the closest followers of the speculative theologians possess the knowledge of their leaders, as do the closest followers of the Qarmatians and the Bātiniyyah. The same applies to the leading scholars of Islam: those closest to each imam know his private affairs best. Take Mālik ibn Anas, for example.

Since Ibn al-Qāsim was the closest to him and knew his private affairs best, Mālik’s followers relied on his transmission. Indeed, even the “private questions” transmitted by Ibn Abī al-Ghamr are accepted on his authority, although some have challenged them. The same applies to Abū Hanīfah: Abū Yūsuf, Muhammad, and Zufar knew him best. Likewise with other imams. A scholar may write a book or make a statement, and someone who did not hear it directly from him may understand his intent better than someone who did. As the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Someone to whom a message is conveyed may understand it better than someone who heard it directly.”

In any case, however, the transmitter must be among the close associates who know the circumstances of the person from whom he transmits. Similarly, among the followers of the imams are those who understand their texts better than some of their direct companions.

It is firmly established in the minds of Muslims that the heirs of the messengers and the successors of the prophets are those who uphold the religion through knowledge, action, and calling people to Allah and the Messenger. These are the Messenger’s true followers. They are like a tract of good, fertile land that receives water and produces abundant pasture and grass: it flourishes in itself, and through it people flourish.

These are the people who combine insight into the religion with the strength to call others to it. For this reason, they are the heirs of the prophets, concerning whom Allah Most High said:

“And remember Our servants Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob, possessors of strength and vision.”

---

## Page 1415

Thus, “strength” means strength in carrying out Allah’s command, and “vision” means insight into Allah’s religion. Through insight, the truth is grasped and recognized; through strength, one is able to convey it, put it into practice, and call others to it. This group possessed powers of memorization and understanding, deep knowledge of religion, discernment, and interpretation.

They caused rivers of knowledge to flow from the revealed texts, unearthed their treasures, and were granted a special understanding of them. Thus, when the Commander of the Faithful, ʿAlī ibn Abī Tālib, may Allah be pleased with him, was asked, “Did the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, give you anything exclusively, to the exclusion of others?” he replied:

“No, by Him who splits the grain and creates the soul, except for an understanding of His Book that Allah grants a servant.”

This understanding is like the pasture and grass produced by fertile land. It is what distinguishes this group from the second group: those who preserved the texts and devoted their attention to memorizing and accurately retaining them. People came to them and accepted these texts, drew inferences from them, extracted their treasures, put them to profitable use, sowed them in soil suited to cultivation and growth, and watered them, each according to his capacity.

“Each group knew its own drinking place.”

These are the people concerning whom the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“May Allah make radiant the person who hears my words, retains them, and then conveys them just as he heard them. For many a bearer of religious knowledge has no deep understanding of it, and many a bearer of religious knowledge conveys it to someone with greater understanding than his own.”

Consider ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAbbās, may Allah be pleased with them both, the great scholar of this community and interpreter of the Quran. What he heard from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, amounts to fewer than twenty or so hadiths in which he

---

## Page 1416

says, “I heard and saw.” He also heard much from the Companions, and his understanding and ability to draw inferences were blessed, until he filled the world with knowledge and religious understanding. Abū Muhammad ibn Hazm said that his fatwas were collected in seven large volumes. Yet this collection contained only what had reached its compiler; otherwise, Ibn ʿAbbās’s knowledge was like an ocean. His religious understanding, his deductions, and his understanding of the Quran were of such a calibre that he surpassed others, although they had heard what he heard and memorized the Quran just as he had. His soil, however, was among the richest and most receptive to cultivation. He sowed the revealed texts in it, and it brought forth every noble kind of growth. That is Allah’s bounty, which He grants to whom He wills, and Allah is the Possessor of great bounty.

What comparison is there between Ibn ʿAbbās’s fatwas, his interpretation, and his deductions, and the fatwas and interpretation of Abū Hurayra? Yet Abū Hurayra had a stronger memory than he did; indeed, he was the foremost memorizer of hadith in the entire community. He conveyed hadith just as he had heard it and diligently reviewed it at night. His efforts were devoted to memorizing and conveying what he had memorized exactly as he had heard it. Ibn ʿAbbās’s efforts, by contrast, were devoted to gaining deep understanding, drawing inferences, opening up the wellsprings of the revealed texts, making rivers flow from them, and extracting their treasures.

So too did their heirs after them: in their religion, they relied on drawing inferences from the revealed texts, not on philosophical imaginings, analogical opinion, or other innovated views. Inevitably, victory, true praise, and reward both now and hereafter belonged to the heirs of the prophets, who follow them in this world and the Hereafter. For a person follows the religion of his close friend.

“Say, ‘If you love Allah, then follow me; Allah will love you.’”

---

## Page 1417

In any case, they are the most knowledgeable people in the community about the Messenger’s hadith, his life, his purposes, and his circumstances.

By the people of hadith, we do not mean those who confine themselves to hearing, writing down, or transmitting it. Rather, we mean all those best qualified in memorizing it, knowing it, understanding it outwardly and inwardly, and following it inwardly and outwardly. The same applies to the people of the Quran.

The least of their qualities is their love for the Quran and hadith, their study of both and their meanings, and their practice of what they know both require. Thus, their jurists know the Messenger better than other jurists; their Sufis follow the Messenger more closely than other Sufis; their rulers are better qualified than others to govern according to the Prophet’s example; and their ordinary people are more faithful in their allegiance to the Messenger than others.

It is well known that those who exalt philosophy and speculative theology and believe in their teachings are further removed than these people from knowing hadith and following it. This is an observable fact. Indeed, if you examine their circumstances, you will find them among the most ignorant people concerning the sayings of the Messenger—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—his circumstances, and the inward and outward aspects of his affairs. You will even find many ordinary people more knowledgeable about these matters than they are. You will find that they do not distinguish between what the Messenger said and what he did not say. They may not even distinguish between a mass-transmitted hadith from him and a fabricated hadith falsely attributed to him.

In seeking agreement with him, they rely only on whatever accords with their own views, whether fabricated or not. They therefore turn away from hadiths which those intimately acquainted with the Messenger know with certain, self-evident knowledge to be his words, in favour of hadiths which those same people know with certain, self-evident knowledge to have been falsely attributed to him, while they

---

## Page 1418

do not know what he meant. Indeed, most of these people do not understand the meanings of the Quran, let alone hadith. Many of them have not even memorized the Quran at all.

How, then, can someone who has neither memorized the Quran nor understood its meanings, and who knows neither hadith nor its meanings, know the truths learned from the Messenger? Anyone who reflects rationally will find that, among all groups, the closer a group is to Allah and His Messenger, the greater its knowledge of, and attention to, the Quran and hadith. The further it is from Allah and His Messenger, the further removed it is from both. You will even find among their leading scholars those who cannot distinguish the Quran from other speech. A verse may be mentioned in someone’s presence, and he will say, “We do not accept that this hadith is authentic.” Or he may say, “Because he—peace be upon him—said such-and-such,” when the words are actually a verse from the Book of Allah. Astonishing examples of this have reached us, and those that have not reached us are more numerous still.

A trustworthy man told me that one of the leading speculative theologians, a man called Shams al-Dīn al-Asbahānī, the teacher of al-Aykī, took charge of the school at the shrine of al-Husayn in Egypt. They handed him a volume from a multivolume Quran, and he read, “In the name of Allah, the Most Compassionate, the Most Merciful,” pronouncing the letters alif, lām, mīm and sād as a word, until someone told him, “Alif, lām, mīm, sād.”

Reflect on this fair judgment, so that it becomes clear to you that those who disparage the people of hadith and turn away from their way are undoubtedly ignorant heretics and hypocrites. Thus, when Imam Ahmad learned that the people of hadith had been mentioned in Mecca in the presence of Ibn Abī Qutayla, who said, “They are evil people,” Imam Ahmad stood up, shaking out his garment and saying, “A heretic! A heretic! A heretic!” Then he entered his house, for he understood what the man was driving at.

---

## Page 1419

The hypocrites’ disparagement of those learned in what the Messenger brought goes back to the hypocrites who lived in the Prophet’s time, may Allah bless him and grant him peace.

The people of knowledge, however, used to say that they are the “abdāl” (substitutes), because they take the place of the prophets and truly stand in their stead. They are not among those lacking substance, whose reality is unknown. Each of them stands in the prophets’ place within the sphere in which he represents them: one in knowledge and exposition, another in worship and spiritual state, and another in both.

They also used to say that they are the victorious group who will prevail, upholding the truth, until the Hour comes. For with them are the guidance and the religion of truth with which Allah sent His messengers. It is this religion that Allah has promised to make prevail over all other religions, and Allah is sufficient as a witness.

---

## Page 1420

Section

The essential point may be summarized as follows: the messengers either knew the descriptive and prescriptive truths or did not know them. If they knew them, they either could or could not explain them through speech and writing. If they could explain them, their explanation could be accessible either to ordinary people and the elite alike, or to the elite alone.

If someone says that they did not know these truths, and that the philosophers and speculative theologians knew them better and explained them more effectively, then this is undoubtedly the position of hypocritical heretics. We shall address them later. The purpose of the present discussion, then, is to show that this is the position of heretics, and that only a hypocrite or an ignorant person would maintain it.

If, on the other hand, he says:

The messengers’ purpose is to set humanity at large aright. People in general cannot understand these inner truths, so the messengers addressed them through parables so that they might benefit, presenting rational truths in sensory forms.

Their discourse concerning Allah and the Last Day thus included imaginative representations and depictions of intelligible realities in perceptible forms, through which people in general could benefit in matters of faith in Allah and the Hereafter. This instils in their souls a sense of Allah’s greatness and the momentousness of the Last Day, prompting them to worship Allah and to hope and fear; thus they benefit

---

## Page 1421

from this and attain happiness according to their capacities and dispositions; for what the messengers did represents the utmost possible disclosure of truths to humanity at large. The messengers’ aim is to preserve humanity and secure its welfare in this life and the Hereafter.

It is well known that this is the position of accomplished philosophers such as al-Fārābī, Ibn Sīnā, and others. It is also the position of every accomplished and distinguished speculative theologian insofar as he differs from the people of hadith. Al-Fārābī says:

“The distinctive quality of prophethood is excellence in imaginatively representing intelligible realities in perceptible forms,”

or words to that effect. Ibn Sīnā mentions this meaning in several places and says:

“It was not possible for Moses son of Imrān, dealing with those Hebrews, nor for Muhammad, dealing with those uncouth Arabs, to explain truths to them as they really are. They would have been incapable of understanding them; and even if they understood them as they really are, they would lose their resolve to follow them, because they would see in them no knowledge that calls for action.”

This meaning is also found in the writings of Abū Hāmid al-Ghazālī, others like him, and those who came after him. Some of it appears in al-Ihyā’ and elsewhere, and likewise in the writings of al-Rāzī.

As for the proponents of the unity of being and similar speculative theologians, this is the central principle around which their thought revolves. It also forms the foundation of the discourse of the Bātiniyya (esotericists) and the Qarmatians. These latter, however, deny the outward aspects of practical matters

---

## Page 1422

and matters of knowledge alike. Others, however, do not deny the outward practices established through mass transmission, though they may regard them as intended for the general public rather than the elite, just as they do with matters conveyed in revealed reports.

Their discourse revolves around the claim that the prophetic message serves the welfare of the general public in knowledge and practice, but not that of the elite. This is the central premise of the authors of “Rasā’il Ikhwān al-Safā” (Epistles of the Brethren of Purity) and the other distinguished proponents of philosophy. Some of them nevertheless hold that compliance with the practical prescriptions of religious law is obligatory. Such people are numerous among their jurists, Sufis, and judicious philosophers. This was the extent of Ibn Sīnā’s understanding when he repented and committed himself to fulfilling the obligations prescribed by religious law. The ancient philosophers held that the laws laid down by the foremost sages of their lands had to be followed; all the more reason, then, for them to require adherence to the laws of the messengers. For, as Ibn Sīnā said:

“The philosophers of the world agree that no law superior to this law of Muhammad has ever come into the world.”

All judicious philosophers agree that he is the most perfect and excellent member of the human race, and that the messengers as a class are superior to the celebrated philosophers as a class. They may then claim that the messengers and prophets are great sages, while the philosopher-sages are lesser prophets; or they may regard them as two distinct classes. This is not the place to explain that, for we have discussed it elsewhere.

The point is simply that the furthest these leading philosophers and speculative theologians go

---

## Page 1423

is to make this claim. We have presented the matter through an exhaustive rational classification, leaving no category out, to show that whoever opposes the hadith scholars in knowledge and practice is either ignorant or a hypocrite. A hypocrite is ignorant and worse besides, as we shall explain, if Allah wills. The ignorant person in question has an element of hypocrisy, even if he is unaware of it. Thus, whoever denies this is both ignorant and a hypocrite.

We therefore said: Whoever claims that he and the leading figures of his group know realities better than the messengers and explain them more clearly is a heretic and a hypocrite if he outwardly professes faith in them, by the agreement of the believers. We shall address him later.

If, however, he says, “The messengers possessed greater knowledge and clarity of exposition, but these realities cannot be known, or cannot be explained at all, or both are possible only for the elite,” we reply: “Then you cannot attain the knowledge and clarity of exposition that the messengers were unable to attain.”

If you say, “These realities cannot be known,” we reply: “Then you and your leading figures are all the more incapable of knowing them.”

If you say, “The messengers cannot explain them,” we reply: “Then neither you nor your leading figures can explain them.”

If you say, “That is possible for the elite, but not for the general public,” we reply: “Then it is possible for the messengers to convey that to the elite, though not to the general public.”

---

## Page 1424

If they claim that none of the elite among the messengers’ companions could understand these realities, they place the first and foremost believers below later generations in knowledge and faith. This is one of the claims of the heretics, for it ranks certain ancient peoples—the Greeks, Indians, and others—as superior to this community in intellect and in their understanding of divine matters and matters of worship. Such are the claims of hypocritical heretics, since Muslims agree that this community is the best and most accomplished of all communities, and that its best and most accomplished members are its earliest believers.

Once this is conceded, those who know these earliest believers best and follow them most closely are the scholars of hadith and the adherents of the sunnah. Hence Imam Ahmad said in the epistle of ‘Abdūs ibn Mālik:

“The fundamental principles of the sunnah, according to us, are to hold firmly to the way of the companions of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, to follow their example, and to abandon religious innovations. Every religious innovation is misguidance. The sunnah, according to us, consists of the reports from the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace.”

The sunnah explains the Quran and provides evidence concerning it—that is, indications of its meaning.

For this reason, scholars have stated that the Rāfidī doctrine is the foundation of heresy, and that the first person to introduce it was a hypocritical heretic, ‘Abd Allāh ibn Saba’. For whoever impugns the first and foremost believers thereby impugns their transmission of the message, their understanding of it, or their adherence to it. The Rāfidīs sometimes impugn their knowledge of the message and sometimes their adherence to it—and attribute these instead to the Prophet’s household and to an infallible figure who has no existence whatsoever.

The heretics among the philosophers, the Nusayrīs, and others sometimes impugn the transmission of the message; this is

---

## Page 1425

the view of their ignorant members. At other times, they impugn the understanding of the message. This is the position of their more adept members, including the leading philosophers, the Ittihādiyya, and others like them.

Once, when al-Tilimsānī was ill, a man visited him accompanied by some students of hadith. Al-Tilimsānī began explaining his principle concerning thought: that it is a veil, and that everything hinges on unveiling. What he meant was the unveiling of absolute existence. One of those students asked, “What, then, does Umm al-Dardā’s statement mean: ‘Abū al-Dardā’s finest deed was reflection’?”

Annoyed that someone like this had been admitted to his presence, he said to the man who had brought him, “How can someone like this be allowed to visit me?” Then he said:

“Do you know, my son, what Abū al-Dardā and others like him resemble? They resemble people who heard certain words and preserved them for us, so that we would be the ones to understand them and know what their speaker intended. They also resemble a courier who carried a letter from the ruler to his deputy.”

Or he said something to that effect, for it has been a long time since I heard this account. The man who visited him related it to me; he is trustworthy and knows what he is talking about in this matter. He had explored these fields extensively.

Likewise, Ibn Sīnā and others express disparagement of the Companions inherited from his father and his Qarmatian associates. Thus, when they discuss humanity’s need for the imamate toward the end of their treatment of philosophy, you find them alluding to the views of the misguided Rāfidīs. The Rāfidīs, however, are more explicit in their vilification than these philosophers.

For this reason, you find an association and resemblance between the “Rāfidīs,” the “Qarmatians,” and the “Ittihādiyya.” Several things unite them. Among these are attacks on the best members of this community, on what the adherents of the sunnah and the community uphold, and on what

---

## Page 1426

is firmly established among the fundamental tenets of the faith and the foundations of religion. They also claim to possess something esoteric that distinguishes them from everyone else and is exclusive to them. Yet they curse one another, hate one another, and disagree with one another. I have seen and heard countless instances of this, just as Allah said concerning the Christians:

“And from those who say, ‘We are Christians,’ We took their covenant, but they forgot a portion of what they had been reminded of. So We stirred up enmity and hatred among them until the Day of Resurrection.”

And He said concerning the Jews:

“And We cast enmity and hatred among them until the Day of Resurrection. Whenever they kindle a fire for war, Allah extinguishes it.”

The same applies to the theologians who muddle together different positions. At times they side with the Muslims—even if they are innovators—and at times with the Sabian philosophers. At other times they side with the polytheistic unbelievers. Sometimes they weigh the different factions against one another and wait to see which side will prevail; sometimes they remain bewildered between them. This last group has become numerous among those who profess Islam, including scholars, rulers, and others, especially when the polytheistic Turks gained ascendancy over the lands of Islam in the East during the seventh century AH.

Many of those who professed Islam harbored such hypocrisy and apostasy that the polytheists and the People of the Book were given power over Muslim lands. Thus, you find Abū Abd Allāh al-Rāzī questioning whether proofs expressed in words establish certainty and whether transmitted reports yield knowledge. These are the two premises underlying heresy, as we explained earlier.

Then, in those matters of Islam that he does affirm, he relies on what is necessarily known to belong to the religion of Islam, such as acts of worship and manifest prohibitions. Likewise, regarding the affirmation of bodily resurrection—after examining Quran commentaries and hadiths—he regards knowledge of it as derived from many sources. Thus he does not engage in negation in the manner of

---

## Page 1427

the Sabian philosophers; nor does he affirm matters as do the learned believers devoted to pure monotheism.

His position concerning “the Companions” is similar. Although he acknowledges their integrity in what they transmitted and their knowledge in general, he claims in some places that they did not know the philosophers’ specious arguments or the matters they explored, because he found no transmitted account of the Companions speaking in the philosophers’ terminology. He uses this as an argument in refuting those who claimed . . . (1).

Likewise, such assertions concerning “the Companions” are found only among the most ignorant and unjust of those who speak on scholarly matters: these speculative theologians, would-be philosophers, Shia partisans, and the Ittihādiyya. These assertions resemble the words of many scholars and rulers: “I am braver than they were. They did not fight an enemy like the one we fought, nor engage directly in warfare as we have, nor govern as we have.” Such talk is found only among the most ignorant and unjust kings.

If he means that the Companions did not know the philosophers’ particular expressions and the means by which they explain their intended meanings, this does not detract from them. Messengers and their companions are not required to know the languages of other nations; they are required to know only what is indispensable to conveying the message. The interpreters who mediate between them know each party’s expressions and their meanings. If the two meanings are identical—as with words denoting the sun and the moon—they convey the shared meaning; otherwise, they know where the two meanings coincide and differ, and convey to each party what the other intends. In doing so, they explain the concepts and clarify the identity, similarity, and closeness between the two meanings.

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) A blank space in the original, approximately three words long.

---

## Page 1428

Thus, the Companions knew what the Messenger had brought. What he brought contained a demonstration of the falsity of every unbeliever’s disbelief, establishing this through sound reasoning that was more valid and clearer in its exposition than those unbelievers’ arguments. As Allah Most High says:

“And they do not come to you with an argument except that We bring you the truth and the best explanation.”

He, glorified be He, informs us that whenever the unbelievers present the Messenger with a rational argument supporting their falsehood, Allah brings him the truth, together with explanations, evidence, and examples that explain, reveal, and clarify the truth better than their reasoning does. Every judgment or argument advanced by the Sabians, the would-be philosophers, and other unbelievers falls within the scope of what the Companions knew.

Allah Most High mentions this verse after His words:

“And the Messenger said, ‘My Lord, indeed my people have abandoned this Quran.’”

“And thus We have appointed for every prophet an enemy from among the criminals. But sufficient is your Lord as a guide and a helper.”

He thus makes clear that whoever abandons the Quran is among the Messenger’s enemies, and that this enmity is inevitable and inescapable. Do you not consider His words, Most High:

“And on the Day the wrongdoer bites his hands, he will say, ‘Oh, I wish I had taken a path with the Messenger!

“‘Woe to me! I wish I had not taken so-and-so as a close friend!

“‘He led me away from the Reminder after it had come to me.’ And Satan always forsakes man.”

Allah Most High sent His Prophet Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, to all the worlds, and set forth examples for them all in the message with which He sent him. As He Most High says:

“And We have certainly set forth for people in this Quran every kind of example, so that they may take heed.”

He thus informs us that He has set forth every kind of example in this Quran for all people.

---

## Page 1429

There is no doubt that the language used in addressing people varies according to need, just as weapons do in warfare. If the Muslims’ enemies differ in their fortifications and armaments from the Persians and Byzantines, then fighting them must accord with what the Sharia requires. Its foundation is to seek what best fulfils obedience to Allah and most benefits His servant—what is best in this world and the Hereafter.

Someone familiar with their warfare may be better able to fight them than someone who is not, not because he possesses greater strength or courage, but because of his affinity with them. Likewise, a non-Arab who emulates the Arabs—such people being the best of the non-Arabs—knows better how to address his own non-Arab people than an Arab does. And an Arab who emulates non-Arabs—such people being the lowest among the Arabs—knows better how to address Arabs than a non-Arab does. It is reported in a hadith:

“The best of your non-Arabs are those who emulate your Arabs, and the worst of your Arabs are those who emulate your non-Arabs.”

Thus, when the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, besieged Tā’if, he bombarded its people with a catapult. He fought them in a manner unlike that in which he fought others in direct battle, as at Badr and elsewhere. Likewise, when the Muslims were besieged in the year of the Trench, they dug a trench, something they had not needed outside a siege. It is said that Salmān advised them to do this, and they accepted his advice because it was a means of carrying out what Allah and His Messenger had commanded.

We have already established in our treatise on “Sunnah and Innovation” that an innovation in religion is something that has not been prescribed by

---

## Page 1430

Allah and His Messenger—that is, anything not commanded as either obligatory or recommended.

As for anything commanded as obligatory or recommended, where that command is established by evidence from the Sharia, it belongs to the religion Allah has prescribed, even if those in authority disagree over some of these matters. This applies whether or not it was practised during the lifetime of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Whatever was done after his death at his command—such as fighting the apostates, the renegade Kharijites, the Persians, the Byzantines and the Turks, expelling the Jews and Christians from the Arabian Peninsula, and other such acts—is part of his Sunnah.

For this reason, ‘Umar ibn ‘Abd al-‘Azīz used to say:

“The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, established practices whose observance affirms the Book of Allah, brings obedience to Allah to completion, and strengthens adherence to Allah’s religion. No one may change them or entertain the opinion of anyone who opposes them. Whoever follows their guidance is rightly guided; whoever seeks victory through them is granted victory. Whoever opposes them and follows a path other than that of the believers, Allah will leave him to what he has chosen and cause him to enter Hell—an evil destination.”

The Sunnah of his rightly guided caliphs is therefore among the things commanded by Allah and His Messenger. There is detailed evidence from the Sharia for this, which is not appropriate to discuss here.

Thus, just as Allah has explained in His Book how to address the People of the Book and establish the proof against them through the signs He has made clear of Muhammad’s messengership, may Allah bless him and grant him peace; through what their own scriptures contain concerning this and what they have distorted and altered in their religion; and through confirmation of what the messengers before him brought—so that, when a learned and fair-minded person from the People of the Book hears this, he finds it all among the clearest arguments and soundest proofs.

---

## Page 1431

And debate and argument are beneficial only when accompanied by justice and fairness. Otherwise, an unjust person either denies the truth he knows—as do the sophist and the one who distorts revelation through esoteric interpretation—or refuses to listen and examine the means of attaining knowledge, turning away from reflection and reasoning. Just as outward sensory perception is not attained by one who turns away, and carries no weight with one who denies it, so inward perception is not attained by one who turns away from reflection and inquiry. Rather, the seeker of knowledge strives to acquire it through its proper avenues.

For this reason, he is called a diligent striver, just as someone who strives diligently in worship or other pursuits is given that description. As one of the early generations said:

“One who strives diligently among you is but as one at play among them.”

Ubayy ibn Ka‘b and Ibn Mas‘ūd said:

“Moderation in following the Sunnah is better than strenuous effort in religious innovation.”

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“When a judge strives to reach the correct ruling and succeeds, he has two rewards; when he strives but errs, he has one reward.”

Mu‘ādh ibn Jabal said—this is also narrated as a statement traced back to the Prophet, though it is preserved as a statement of Mu‘ādh:

“Devote yourselves to knowledge. Teaching it is a good deed; seeking it is worship; discussing it is glorification of Allah; investigating it is striving in His cause; teaching it to someone who does not know it is charity; and imparting it to those suited to receive it is a means of drawing near to Allah.”

Thus, one who investigates knowledge is described as striving in Allah’s cause.

Since argument is beneficial only when accompanied by justice, Allah Most High said:

“And do not argue with the People of the Scripture except in a way that is best, except for those who commit injustice among them.”

We are therefore not obliged to argue in the best manner with someone who acts unjustly. When knowledge is obtained from those among the People of the Book who have embraced Islam, who know what they possess in their own language and translate it for us into Arabic, it is useful in debating with them and addressing them, as was the practice of ‘Abd Allāh ibn Salām

---

## Page 1432

and Salmān al-Fārisī, Ka‘b al-Ahbār, and others, who would relate the knowledge they possessed. Their knowledge could then be cited as corroborating what the Messenger brought, serving as evidence against them in one respect and against others in another, as we have explained elsewhere.

Hebrew words bear some resemblance to Arabic words, just as words resemble one another through derivation based on phonetically related consonants. I have heard the Torah’s wording in Hebrew from people of the Book who embraced Islam, and found the two languages so remarkably close that I came to understand much of their Hebrew speech merely through my knowledge of Arabic. Its sound meanings are either close to those of the Quran, like them, or identical with them, although the Quran possesses exceptional qualities of expression and meaning.

If someone among them engaged in argument wishes to challenge the Quran on the basis of transmitted evidence or reason—for example, by citing something attributed to the prophets in their scriptures that contradicts what Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, brought, or something contrary to what Allah has stated in their scriptures, as when they claimed before the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, that Allah had commanded them to blacken the adulterer’s face with charcoal rather than stone him—then the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and the believers could request the Torah and trustworthy interpreters to read it in Arabic and translate it, such as ‘Abd Allāh ibn Salām and others like him. This was the case when he said to their rabbi:

“Lift your hand from the verse concerning stoning.”

There it was, clearly visible. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, stoned the two adulterers from among them after establishing the proof against them from their own scripture. This was because it accorded with what Allah had revealed to him concerning stoning. He said:

“O Allah, I

---

## Page 1433

am the first to revive Your command after they had put it to death.”

For this reason, Ibn ‘Abbās said, concerning Allah’s words:

“We sent down the Torah, containing guidance and light, by which the prophets who submitted to Allah judged.”

He said: “Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, is among the prophets who submitted to Allah.” He judged only by what Allah had revealed to him, as Allah said:

“And judge between them by what Allah has revealed.”

Likewise, one may read a copy translated into Arabic by trustworthy translators, using Arabic script and wording. Through these, one can learn what their scriptures contain, with the assistance of trustworthy Muslim translators or those among us who know their script, such as Zayd ibn Thābit and others like him. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, instructed him to learn it. This hadith is well known in the Sunan collections, and al-Bukhārī cited it as evidence in the chapter entitled “Translation for the Judge, and Whether an Interpreter Is Permissible.” He wrote:

“Khārija ibn Zayd ibn Thābit related from Zayd ibn Thābit that the Prophet instructed him to learn the Jews’ script: ‘I then wrote the Prophet’s letters for him, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and read their letters to him whenever they wrote to him.’”

Corresponding in their script and conversing in their language are activities of the same kind. Although they may occur together, either may also occur independently of the other—for example, writing Arabic words in Hebrew script or other non-Arabic scripts, or writing non-Arabic words in Arabic script. It has also been said that this suffices.

For this reason, Allah, glorified be He, said:

“All food was lawful for the Children of Israel, except what Israel had forbidden himself before the Torah was revealed. Say, ‘Bring the Torah and recite it, if you are truthful.’”

Thus, we were commanded to ask them to bring the Torah and recite it, if they were truthful in reporting

---

## Page 1434

what contradicted this. For they would twist their tongues when reciting Scripture so that you would think it was part of Scripture, though it was not. They would write scripture with their own hands and then say, “This is from Allah,” and they would lie in their speech and writing. For this reason, a translation is accepted only from someone trustworthy.

If one of them argues against the Quran by citing a report from the earlier messengers, such as the statement attributed to Moses:

“Observe the Sabbath as long as the heavens and the earth endure,”

we can say to them: “In which book is this? Bring it.” We know that this is not in their books; it is merely a fabricated lie. They possess the prophetic writings, numbering two hundred and twenty, and (the book al-Mathnawī, whose name means al-muthannāh (a secondary text). This is what Abd Allāh ibn Amr counted among the signs of the Hour that would arise among us, saying:

“The Hour will not come until al-muthannāh is read among them, with no one changing it.” He was asked, “What is al-muthannāh?” He replied, “What is commissioned to be written other than the Book of Allah.”)

Likewise, if they are asked about the mentions of Allah’s names and attributes in Scripture, so that the agreement of the earlier prophets with Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, may establish the proof against them and others, and they then distort words from their proper places, this can be detected, as explained above.

If they present a rational argument, it too can be understood in light of what is in the Quran by referring it back to the Quran. An example is their rejection of abrogation on rational grounds, to the point that they say: “What He has forbidden cannot be abrogated, nor can what He has commanded be prohibited.” Allah Most High said:

“The foolish among the people will say, ‘What has turned them away from the direction of prayer they used to face?’”

---

## Page 1435

Al-Barā’ ibn Āzib said—as reported in the two Sahīh collections—“They are the Jews.” Then Allah, glorified be He, said:

“Say, ‘To Allah belong the east and the west. He guides whom He wills to a straight path.’”

He thus mentioned that abrogation depends on the divine will and that the later command may be more beneficial and conducive to good. His words, “He guides whom He wills to a straight path,” explain what is more beneficial and conducive to good, while “whom He wills” refers the matter back to His will.

All the speculative theologians rely on some aspect of this verse when they say that religious obligations depend either on divine will alone, as some maintain, or on benefit, as others maintain. On either view, abrogation is possible.

Then He, glorified be He, demonstrated that abrogation had occurred through the Torah’s prohibition of things previously lawful: He had made certain things lawful for Israel, then prohibited them in the Torah. This permission had been established by divine law through a pronouncement. They had not merely deemed those things permissible by retaining their original status, such that withdrawing that permission would not constitute abrogation, as some of them claim. He commanded that the Torah be brought concerning this matter. This is indeed what we have found in it, as converts to Islam from among the People of the Book have related to us on more than one occasion.

The same applies to debating Sabian philosophers, polytheists, and others like them. For if a Sabian philosopher cites the statements of the ancient Sabian philosophers—which were rendered and translated into Arabic—and presents them either unaltered or in the form their later followers gave them through additions or omissions, expansion or abridgment, rejection of some of their content, and the introduction of ideas

---

## Page 1436

other than those originally found in them, and the like—then, if he discusses matters unrelated to religion, such as questions of “medicine” and pure “arithmetic” treated in their works, and those who learned from them—such as Muhammad ibn Zakariyyā al-Rāzī, Ibn Sīnā, and other heretical physicians—write material that amounts merely to benefiting from the works of unbelievers and hypocrites in worldly affairs, this is permissible.

This is just as it is permissible to live in their houses, wear their clothes and bear their weapons, or enter into agreements with them to cultivate land, as the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, did with the Jews of Khaybar. Likewise, when the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, and Abū Bakr left Mecca as emigrants, they hired “Ibn Urayqit”—a man from Banū al-Dīl—as an expert guide, meaning someone skilled in finding the way. They entrusted him with their lives and their mounts, and arranged to meet him at the cave of Thawr on the third morning. Khuzāʿah, both its Muslims and its unbelievers, were trusted confidants and sincere advisers to the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, and he accepted their advice. All this is reported in the two Sahīh collections. Abū Tālib also supported and defended the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, despite his polytheism. Such examples are numerous.

Among the polytheists and the People of the Book are trustworthy individuals, as Allah Most High said:

“Among the People of the Book are some who, if you entrust them with a great amount of wealth, will return it to you; and among them are some who, if you entrust them with a single dinar, will not return it to you unless you keep standing over them.”

For this reason, it is permissible to entrust one of them with property, and a Muslim may seek treatment from an unbeliever if he is trustworthy. The leading scholars, including Ahmad and others, expressly stated this, since it involves accepting their accounts of worldly matters within their knowledge and trusting them in those matters. This is permissible provided it does not entail an overriding harm, such as his exercising authority over Muslims, gaining ascendancy over them, or the like. Thus, acquiring medical knowledge from their books is like relying on an unbeliever to find the way or seeking medical treatment from him.

---

## Page 1437

Indeed, this is even better, for they did not write their books for any particular Muslim, such that betrayal might enter into them. There is no need here to rely on any of them and risk betrayal; rather, this is simply benefiting from what they have produced, like clothing, dwellings, farmland, weapons, and the like.

If they discuss matters concerning “religion,” then, if they transmit them from the prophets, they are like the People of the Book in this respect, but worse off. If they make knowledge of these matters dependent on rational inference, then whatever agrees with the Quran is true. Whatever contradicts it, however, is shown to be false by the examples set forth in the Quran, as Allah Most High says:

“They do not bring you any argument but We bring you the truth and the best explanation.”

Thus, the Quran contains both the truth and clear reasoning that demonstrates the invalidity of the reasoning they present. If their statements are undifferentiated but contain truth—as is generally the case with the Sabians who altered their religion, such as “Aristotle” and his followers, and with others who followed them—then the truth is to be accepted and falsehood rejected. Yet even the truth they contain is not set forth as clearly as the truth is in the Quran. This matter therefore depends on knowing the Quran, its meanings, its explanation, and its translation.

Translation and explanation have “three levels”:

The first is translating the wording alone, such as replacing an expression with a synonymous expression. In this kind of translation, you seek to establish that what one group means by its expression is precisely what another group means by theirs. This is useful knowledge, for many people tie a meaning to a particular expression and do not consider it independently of both expressions.

---

## Page 1438

The second is translating and explaining the meaning by giving the listener a clear conception of it. Making the meaning clear to him and enabling him to understand it goes beyond translating the wording. This is like explaining an Arabic book to an Arabic speaker who has heard its Arabic words but has neither grasped nor understood their meanings. A meaning can be made clear by describing the thing itself or something comparable to it: attributes are combined from individual concepts that the listener understands, and this combination represents the meaning, either precisely or approximately.

(The third level is demonstrating its truth and establishing it by mentioning the night and the reasoning that establishes that meaning, either through evidence alone or through evidence that explains the cause of its existence. Here, examples and comparisons may be needed to enable him to accept that meaning as true, just as, at the “second level,” examples are needed to give him a clear conception of it. Merely conceiving it may itself yield knowledge of its truth. If understanding its meaning suffices to accept it as true, no analogy, example, or further evidence is needed.

Once a person knows the Quran in this way, the statements of the People of the Book, the Sabians, and the polytheists—whether they agree with it or contradict it—must likewise be translated in both wording and meaning.

The Quran contains a detailed explanation of everything, as Allah Most High says:

“It is not a fabricated account, but a confirmation of what came before it and a detailed explanation of everything.”

And He says:

“We have sent down to you the Book as an explanation of everything.”

It is well known that the Muslim community is commanded to convey the Quran, both its wording and its meaning, just as this was enjoined upon

---

## Page 1439

the Messenger. Allah’s message can only be conveyed in this way, and conveying it to non-Arabs may require translation for them; it is therefore translated for them as far as possible. Translation may require examples to give a clear conception of the meanings, and these then form part of a complete translation.

If, as is well known, most Muslims—indeed, most of those among them who claim scholarly learning—do not carry out the translation, interpretation, and explanation of the Quran, then others are all the more likely to be unable to translate and explain what they possess. For Muslims have a more complete understanding, their Book is more upright in its statements and finer in its discourse, and their language is more expansive. This is especially so when those meanings are not established as true, but contain much falsehood. Translating false meanings and giving a clear conception of them is difficult, because nothing true corresponds to them in every respect.

Thus, if we are asked about something they say, “Is it true or false? And how can the truth and falsehood within it be discerned?” we give a verbal reply supported by argument and evidence, just as the polytheists and the People of the Book used to ask the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, about various questions or debate with him, and just as nations used to dispute with their messengers. For many people claim that the revealed Law agrees with philosophy.

An example of this is when they mention “the ten intellects” and “the nine souls,” and say that the first intellect is the first emanation from the Being whose existence is necessary in itself; that it is a necessary consequence of His essence and an effect caused by Him; that the second likewise emanates from the first; and that every celestial sphere has an intellect and a soul.

---

## Page 1440

It is said in reply: Your expression “intellect and soul” belongs to your own terminology and must therefore be translated. Even if the words are Arabic, their meaning must still be translated.

They then say: “Intellect” is the spirit abstracted from matter—that is, the body—and its attachments. They call it an intellect, and also call it “separate.” They call such spirits “entities separate from matter” because they are separate from bodies, just as the human spirit, when it leaves the body, becomes separate from the matter that is the body. “Soul” is the spirit that governs the body, like the human soul while it is in the body. So long as it is in the body, it moves it. When it leaves the body, it becomes pure intellect: that is, it apprehends knowledge without moving any body. These, then, are the intellects and souls.

The account we have given is among the best translations of what is meant by intellect and soul, yet most of them do not grasp this.

They say: We have established a soul for every celestial sphere because its motion is voluntary, and voluntary motion can only proceed from a soul. We have also established an intellect for every soul because the intellect is perfect and has no need of motion, whereas that which moves seeks perfection. There must therefore be something above it on which it is modeled, and something that is its cause.

Thus, our souls move in order to emulate the intellects above us. All of this is an emulation of the Being whose existence is necessary, insofar as this is possible. Only an intellect emanates from the First, because a soul entails a body, and a body contains

---

## Page 1441

multiplicity, whereas what emanates from Him can only be one. They differ greatly concerning emanation, but this is not the place to discuss those differences.

They are told in reply:

As for your assertion that there are spirits in heaven, this resembles what is found in the Quran and Allah’s other scriptures. Yet these spirits are not “the angels,” as those among you claim who profess to believe in what was revealed to the Messenger and what was revealed before him. They say: “We intended nothing but good and reconciliation between religious law and philosophy.” They have thus claimed that what the philosophers call intellects and souls are what the prophets call angels. This is not so, although they resemble them in certain respects.

For the terms “angels” and “angel” imply that they are Allah’s messengers, as He, Most High, says:

“Who makes the angels messengers.”

And as He says:

“By those sent forth in succession.”

The angels are Allah’s messengers in carrying out His command governing creation, through which He regulates the heavens and the earth. As He, Most High, says:

“Until, when death comes to one of you, Our messengers take his soul, and they never neglect their duty.”

And as He says:

“Yes indeed, and Our messengers are with them, recording.”

They are also His messengers concerning His religious command, with which the angels descend, for He says:

“He sends down the angels with revelation by His command upon whomever He wills of His servants.”

And He, Most High, says:

“It is not for any human being that Allah should speak to him except through revelation, or from behind a veil, or by sending a messenger who reveals, by His permission, whatever He wills. Indeed, He is Most High, All-Wise.”

And He, Most High, says:

“Allah chooses messengers from among the angels and from among mankind.”

None but Allah can count the number of His angels, as He, Most High, says:

“We have not appointed the keepers of

---

## Page 1442

Hell except angels, and We have made their number only a trial for those who disbelieve, so that those given the Scripture may be certain, and those who believe may increase in faith, and those given the Scripture and the believers may have no doubts, and so that those in whose hearts is disease and the disbelievers may say, ‘What does Allah mean by this example?’ Thus Allah leaves astray whom He wills and guides whom He wills. And none knows the forces of your Lord except Him.”

They are also told:

The references in the Quran and Sunnah to the angels and their multitude are beyond enumeration. Indeed, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“The heaven creaks, and rightly should it creak: there is not a space four fingers wide in it without an angel standing, sitting, bowing, or prostrating.”

Allah, Most High, says:

“The heavens almost break apart from above them, while the angels glorify their Lord with praise and seek forgiveness for those on earth. Indeed, Allah is the All-Forgiving, the Most Merciful.”

Whoever limits their number to ten or nineteen, or claims that the nineteen appointed over Saqar are the intellects and souls, does so out of ignorance of what has come from Allah and His Messenger.

His error in this is evident if the names do not agree in the attributes or magnitude of what they designate, as synonymous terms do. The two things designated agree only in that each is a spirit connected with the heavens. This is merely one of the attributes of the angels of the heavens. Thus, what they have affirmed is only some of

---

## Page 1443

the attributes of some angels. Compared with the angels—their attributes, their magnitude, and their numbers—what they have affirmed is exceedingly little: an even smaller proportion than the prophets acknowledged by the Samaritans represent among all the prophets, for they believe in no prophet after Moses and Joshua. How could it be otherwise, when the only attributes they have affirmed for the angels are what they know from their own selves: mere knowledge for the intellects and voluntary motion for the souls?

It is well known that the angels possess knowledge, states, wills, and deeds that none but the Lord of Majesty can enumerate. The Quran’s descriptions of their glorification and worship of Allah are too numerous to mention here, as in His account of addressing the angels and commanding them to prostrate to Adam. The Most High also says:

“But if they are arrogant, those who are with your Lord glorify Him night and day, and they never grow weary.”

The Most High says:

“Indeed, those who are with your Lord are not too proud to worship Him; they glorify Him and prostrate to Him.”

The Most High says:

“And they say, ‘The Most Merciful has taken offspring.’ Glory be to Him! Rather, they are honored servants. They do not precede Him in speech, and they act at His command. He knows what is before them and what is behind them, and they intercede only for those of whom He approves, and they stand in fearful awe of Him. Whoever among them says, ‘I am a god besides Him,’ We will recompense with Hell. Thus do We recompense the wrongdoers.”

The Most High says:

“Allah chooses messengers from among the angels and from among humankind.”

The Most High says:

“Those who bear the Throne and those around it glorify their Lord with praise and believe in Him

and seek forgiveness for those who believe.”

The Most High says:

“Each believes in Allah, His angels, His Books, and His messengers.”

The Most High says:

“When you said to the believers, ‘Is it not enough for you that your Lord should reinforce you with three thousand angels sent down? The Most High says:

The Most High says:

The Most High says:

The Most High says:

The Most High says:

He says:

The Most High says:

The Most High says:

The Most High says:

“By those lined up in ranks, those who drive forcefully, and those who recite the Reminder.”

The Most High says:

“So ask them: Does your Lord have

---

## Page 1445

daughters, while they have sons?”

“Or did We create the angels as females while they were witnesses?”

“Indeed, it is out of their falsehood that they say, ‘Allah has begotten,’ and indeed they are liars.”

—up to His words, Most High—

“Indeed, we are those who stand in ranks, and indeed, we are those who glorify Allah.”

In the two Sahīh collections, Jābir ibn Samura reports that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Will you not form rows as the angels form rows before their Lord?” He said: “They complete the first row and stand closely together in the row.”

In the two Sahīh collections, Qatāda reports from Anas, from Mālik ibn Sa‘sa‘a, in the hadith of the Ascension, that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace—after mentioning his ascent to the seventh heaven—said:

“Then the Much-Frequented House was shown to me, and I asked Gabriel about it. He said: ‘This is the Much-Frequented House. Seventy thousand angels pray in it every day. Once they leave, they never return; that is their final visit.’”

Al-Bukhārī said: Hammām reported from Qatāda, from al-Hasan, from Abū Hurayra, that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“When the reciter says ‘Āmīn,’ say ‘Āmīn,’ for whoever’s ‘Āmīn’ coincides with that of the angels will have his previous sins forgiven.”

In another narration in the two Sahīh collections:

“When he says ‘Āmīn,’ the angels in heaven say ‘Āmīn.’”

Also in the Sahīh, Abū Sālih reports from Abū Hurayra that the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“When the imam says, ‘Allah hears whoever praises Him,’ say, ‘O Allah, our Lord, to You belongs all praise,’ for whoever’s words coincide with those of the angels will have his previous sins forgiven.”

And in

---

## Page 1446

the Sahīh, ‘Urwa reports from ‘Ā’isha, the wife of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, that she heard the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, say:

“The angels descend into al-‘anān—that is, the clouds—and speak of what has been decreed in heaven. The devils eavesdrop and overhear it, then convey it to the soothsayers, who add to it a hundred lies of their own.”

In the two Sahīh collections, Abū Hurayra reports that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Allah has angels who roam, free of other duties, seeking gatherings of remembrance. When they find a gathering in which Allah is remembered, they sit with those present and surround one another with their wings until they fill the space between themselves and the lowest heaven. When those gathered disperse, the angels ascend and rise to heaven. Allah asks them—though He knows best—‘Where have you come from?’

“They say, ‘We have come from servants of Yours on earth who glorify You, proclaim Your greatness, declare that none has the right to be worshipped but You, praise You, and ask of You.’

“He says, ‘What do they ask of Me?’

“They say, ‘They ask You for Your Paradise.’

“He says, ‘Have they seen My Paradise?’

“They say, ‘No, O Lord.’

“He says, ‘What would it be like if they saw My Paradise?’

“They say, ‘They also seek Your protection.’

“He says, ‘From what do they seek My protection?’

“They say, ‘From Your Fire.’

“He says, ‘Have they seen My Fire?’

“They say, ‘O Lord, no.’

“He says, ‘What would it be like if they saw My Fire?’

“They say, ‘They also ask Your forgiveness.’

“He then says, ‘I have forgiven them, granted them what they asked for, and protected them from what they sought protection against.’

“They say, ‘O Lord, among them is so-and-so, a servant who frequently sins. He was merely passing by and sat with them.’

“He says, ‘I have forgiven him too. They are people whose companion will not be wretched.’”

---

## Page 1447

In the two Sahīh collections, ‘Urwa reports that ‘Ā’isha told him that she asked the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, “Have you ever experienced a day more difficult than the day of Uhud?”

He replied:

“I have suffered much at the hands of your people. The worst I suffered from them was on the day of al-‘Aqaba, when I presented myself to Ibn ‘Abd Yālīl ibn ‘Abd Kulāl, but he did not grant what I sought. I went on my way, weighed down by sorrow, and did not come to myself until I reached Qarn al-Tha‘ālib. I raised my head and saw a cloud shading me. When I looked, I saw Gabriel within it. He called to me and said, ‘Allah has heard what your people said to you and how they responded to you. He has sent you the angel of the mountains so that you may command him to do whatever you wish to them.’

“The angel of the mountains called to me and greeted me with peace. Then he said, ‘O Muhammad, it is as you wish. If you wish, I will crush them between al-Akhshabayn, the two mountains.’”

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said, “Rather, I hope that Allah will bring forth from their descendants people who worship Allah alone, associating nothing with Him.”

There are many similar authentic hadiths mentioning the angels in the heavens, the angels of the air and the mountains, and others.
