The same applies to the angels who attend to the affairs of the children of Adam. For example, in the hadith agreed upon by al-Bukhārī and Muslim—the hadith of the truthful one whose truthfulness is confirmed—the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, says:

“Then the angel is sent to him and commanded to record four matters. He is told, ‘Write down his provision, his lifespan, and whether he will be wretched or blessed.’ Then the soul is breathed into him.”

In the Sahīh, al-Barā’ ibn ‘Āzib reports:

“The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said to Hassān, ‘Satirize them—or exchange satire with them—and Gabriel is with you.’”

Also in the Sahīh, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said to him:

“Reply on my behalf. O Allah, strengthen him

---

## Page 1448

with the Holy Spirit.”

In the Sahīh, Anas reports:

“It is as though I can still see the dust rising in the lane of Banū Ghunm from Gabriel’s procession.”

In the two Sahīh collections, ‘Ā’isha reports that al-Hārith ibn Hishām asked, “O Messenger of Allah, how does revelation come to you?”

He replied:

“Sometimes it comes to me like the ringing of a bell, and this is the most difficult form for me. Then it ceases, and I have retained what he said. At other times, the angel appears to me in the form of a man and speaks to me, and I retain what he says.”

The accounts of Gabriel coming to the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace—sometimes in the form of a Bedouin and sometimes in the form of Dihya al-Kalbī—and repeatedly speaking to him and teaching him to recite are too numerous to recount here.

In the two Sahīh collections, Abū Hurayra reports that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Angels take turns among you by night and by day, and they gather at the dawn and afternoon prayers. Then those who spent the night among you ascend, and their Lord asks them—though He knows better than they do—‘In what state did you leave My servants?’ They reply, ‘We left them praying, and we came to them while they were praying.’”

In the two Sahīh collections, ‘Ā’isha reports:

“I stuffed a pillow bearing images for the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace; it resembled a cushion. He came and stood there, and the expression on his face began to change. I asked, ‘What is the matter, O Messenger of Allah?’

“He said, ‘What is this pillow?’

“I replied, ‘It is a pillow I made for you to recline on.’

“He said, ‘Did you not know that the angels do not enter a house containing an image? Those who make images will be punished on the Day of Resurrection and told, “Give life to what you have created.”’”

And in the two Sahīh collections,

---

## Page 1449

Ibn ‘Abbās reports:

“I heard Abū Talha say, ‘I heard the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, say, “Angels do not enter a house containing a dog or an image depicting figures.”’”

Likewise, in the two Sahīh collections, ‘Abd Allah ibn ‘Umar reports:

“Gabriel made a promise to the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and said, ‘We do not enter a house containing a dog or an image.’”

In the two Sahīh collections, Abū Hurayra reports that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“The angels pray for each of you as long as he remains in the place where he prayed, saying, ‘O Allah, forgive him; O Allah, have mercy on him,’ provided he does not invalidate his ablution.”

These and similar texts describe the categories of angels, their attributes, and their actions in ways that rule out their being the “intellects and souls” these people describe, or Gabriel being the “active intellect,” or the angels associated with human beings being good faculties and the devils being corrupt faculties, as these people claim.

Moreover, their claim that the intellects and souls—which they identify as angels—are effects caused by Allah, emanating from His Essence as an effect proceeds from its cause, amounts to saying that they are begotten of Allah and that Allah has begotten the angels.

This is something Allah has rejected and declared Himself free of. He has declared its proponent a liar and exposed the falsehood of this claim through His words:

“He neither begets nor was begotten, and there is none comparable to Him.”

The Most High also says:

“Indeed, it is out of their falsehood that they say, ‘Allah has begotten,’ and they are surely liars. Has He chosen daughters over sons? What is the matter with you? How do you judge? Will you not take heed? Or do you have clear authority? Then bring your scripture, if you are truthful.”

And through His words: “And they have assigned partners to Allah

---

## Page 1450

—the jinn—although He created them. And without knowledge they have falsely attributed sons and daughters to Him. Glory be to Him! He is exalted above what they describe.”

And the words of the Most High:

“They say, ‘The Most Merciful has taken a child.’ Glory be to Him! Rather, they are honored servants. They do not speak before He speaks, and they act at His command. He knows what is before them and what is behind them. They intercede only for those with whom He is pleased, and they stand in awe of Him.”

The Most High also says:

“The Messiah would never disdain to be a servant of Allah, nor would the angels brought near to Him.”

And the Most High says:

“They say, ‘The Most Merciful has taken a child.’ You have indeed uttered something monstrous! The heavens almost rupture at it, the earth splits apart, and the mountains collapse in ruins, because they attribute a child to the Most Merciful. It is not fitting for the Most Merciful to take a child. Everyone in the heavens and the earth will come to the Most Merciful as a servant. He has accounted for them and counted them precisely. Each of them will come to Him alone on the Day of Resurrection.”

He thus declares that they are subjected to Him—that is, subdued, directed, held in servitude, and subjugated. They are not like an effect generated by necessity, whose condition cannot conceivably change. He also declares that they are servants of Allah, not to be likened to Him as an effect is likened to its cause or a child to its parent, as these Sabians claim.

The Most High says:

“They say, ‘Allah has taken a child.’ Glory be to Him! Rather, to Him belongs whatever is in the heavens and the earth; all are obedient to Him. He is the Originator of the heavens and the earth. When He decrees a matter, He only says to it, ‘Be,’ and it is.”

He thus declares that He brings about everything by His word “Be,” not through an effect being generated from Him.

Likewise, He says, glorified is He:

“They have assigned partners to Allah—the jinn—although He created them. And without knowledge they have falsely attributed sons and daughters to Him. Glory be to Him! He is exalted above what they describe. He is the Originator of the heavens and the earth. How could He have a child when He has no consort? He created all things, and He has knowledge of all things.”

---

## Page 1451

He thus declares that generation occurs only from two sources, just as a conclusion follows from two premises. The same applies to all other known effects: an effect arises only through the conjunction of whatever completes its cause. No single thing by itself can ever be a cause or a parent. Nothing in this world arises except from two sources, even if these are the agent and that which receives its action, as with fire and wood, or the sun and the earth. From one thing alone, however, nothing proceeds or is generated. The Quran thus makes clear that they have erred in their analogical reasoning concerning causation and generation, in holding that the world proceeds from Him through causation and generation.

Likewise, He says:

“And of all things We created pairs, so that you may take heed”?

This contradicts their assertion that only one thing proceeds from Him.

This fulfills what Allah Most High declares in His words:

“They never bring you an argument without Our bringing you the truth and the best explanation.”

For He has undertaken to do this regarding everyone who departs from following the Messenger. The Most High says:

“Blessed is He who sent down the Criterion to His servant, so that he may be a warner to the worlds.”

(He thus mentions) divine oneness and messengership, continuing up to His words:

“On the Day when the wrongdoer will bite his hands, saying, ‘If only I had taken a path with the Messenger! Woe to me! If only I had not taken so-and-so as a close friend! He led me away from the Reminder after it had come to me.’ And Satan ever forsakes man.”

Everyone who departs from following the Messenger is therefore a wrongdoer to that extent. One who introduces religious innovations is a wrongdoer to the extent that he departs from the Messenger’s sunnah.

“And the Messenger will say, ‘My Lord, my people have indeed abandoned this Quran.’ Thus We have appointed for every prophet an enemy from among the evildoers. But your Lord is sufficient as a guide and a helper.”

“And those who disbelieve say, ‘Why has there not been sent down

---

## Page 1452

to him the Quran all at once?’ Thus it is, so that We may strengthen your heart thereby, and We have recited it in measured stages.”

“They never bring you an argument without Our bringing you the truth and the best explanation.”

These Sabians have indeed brought an argument, namely their assertion:

“From one, only one can proceed and be generated. The Lord is one, so only one can proceed from Him and be generated from Him.”

Allah therefore brought the truth and the best explanation. He made clear that nothing at all proceeds from or is generated from a single thing, and that nothing has been generated from Him or proceeded from Him. Rather, He created everything, and He created two of every kind, forming a pair. Hence Mujāhid said regarding the even and the odd—as al-Bukhārī records in his Sahīh:

“The even is creation, for every created thing has a counterpart; the odd is Allah, who has no likeness.”

Thus He said:

“How could He have a son when He has no consort?”

For effects proceeding from causes, and things generated among existing beings, necessarily involve two things: one acts like the father, and the other like the receptive mother. They may call these the agent and the recipient, as with the sun and the earth, or fire and wood. As for a single thing proceeding from a single thing, this has no existence whatsoever in reality. Their analogy with rays in relation to the sun, and with sound—such as ringing—in relation to motion and striking, likewise constitutes an argument against them in support of Allah, His Messenger, and the believers.

This is because, if the rays

---

## Page 1453

are understood to mean what inheres in the sun itself, then that is one of its attributes. The Creator’s attributes are not created, nor are they part of the world under discussion.

If, however, the rays are understood to mean what is reflected onto the earth, then this necessarily involves two things: the sun, which acts as the active father, and the earth, which acts as the receptive mother. The earth is thus the sun’s consort. Likewise, sound is generated only from two bodies, one striking the other or pulling away from it. Thus, the sound found in the bodies of this world is generated from two sources, one striking the other or pulling away from it. Whatever analogy they invoke as evidence, what Allah has brought is the truth and the best explanation—the clearest and most illuminating exposition of the truth.

Moreover, they regard them as a complete cause of what lies beneath them, reinforcing and necessitating it. They even regard them as our originating principles and as our fathers and mothers. Sometimes they make the intellect the father and the soul the mother.

Some of them sometimes say that “the two parents” are the intellect and nature, as the author of al-Fusūs (The Bezels) said concerning Noah’s words:

“Forgive me and my parents.”

That is, those of whom I was the product, namely the intellect and nature. They even call them lesser lords and gods and worship them. This is disbelief, contrary to what the messengers brought.

---

## Page 1454

It was in this sense that some of the early generations described the Sabians as worshipping angels. Likewise, the Arabic translations of their ancient authorities’ writings state that they called them lesser gods and lords, just as they also worshipped the stars. The Quran denies that they are lords or gods, or that they possess anything beyond what belongs to a messenger who acts only upon the command of the one who sent him and intercedes only after being granted permission to intercede.

Allah refuted those among the Arabs, Romans, and other nations who made such claims. He, Most High, said:

“Nor would he command you to take the angels and prophets as lords. Would he command you to disbelieve after you have submitted to Allah?”

And He, Most High, said:

“They say, ‘The Most Merciful has taken a son.’ Glory be to Him! Rather, they are honoured servants.”

“They do not speak before He speaks, and they act by His command.”

And He, Most High, said:

“Say, ‘Call upon those whom you claim to be gods besides Allah. They do not possess even an atom’s weight in the heavens or on earth, nor do they have any share in either, nor does He have any helper among them.’”

“Intercession is of no benefit with Him except for one whom He permits. Then, when fear is lifted from their hearts, they say, ‘What did your Lord say?’ They reply, ‘The truth. And He is the Most High, the Most Great.’”

Some hadiths have already been cited concerning the angels falling unconscious when Allah issues a decree concerning creation or a religious revelation.

And He, Most High, said:

“How many angels there are in the heavens whose intercession avails nothing, except after Allah has given permission for whom He wills and is pleased with.”

And He, Most High, said:

“Rather, they are honoured servants”—and the rest of the verse.

---

## Page 1455

And He, Most High, said:

“We descend only by the command of your Lord. To Him belongs whatever is before us, whatever is behind us, and whatever lies between. Your Lord is never forgetful.”

And He, Most High, said:

“Say, ‘Call upon those whom you claim besides Him. They have no power to remove harm from you or to shift it elsewhere.’”

“Those whom they invoke seek a means of approach to their Lord—which of them will be nearest—and hope for His mercy and fear His punishment. Indeed, your Lord’s punishment is to be feared.”

The verse was revealed concerning those who invoke angels and prophets. This is not the place for an exhaustive discussion of that matter. For Allah, glorified be He, sent Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, “with concise, comprehensive speech.” The expressions in the Quran are comprehensive and all-encompassing, conveying in universal and general terms what is scattered throughout the speech of others. Moreover, He names each thing in a way that indicates the attribute relevant to the stated and explained judgment, and makes clear how that designation conveys its meaning.

His declaring Himself free of offspring, begetting, and taking a child is more comprehensive and sounder than expressing the denial through the term ʿilla (cause). The original sense of ʿilla concerns change, as with an illness that alters the body’s healthy condition; ʿalīl (ill) is the opposite of healthy.

It has been said that “maʿlūl” is used only in connection with drinking. One says, “He drank water ʿallan baʿda nahalin (a second time after the first),” and ʿallaltuhu means that you gave him a second drink.

As for using the term “ʿilla” for something that necessitates or entails another thing, this belongs to the conventional usage of the scholars of speculative theology. Although these two senses are related to the ordinary linguistic meaning of ʿilla through the notion of change, that relationship is more evident in the term “tawallud” (generation). Hence, it is more common in ordinary speech. People say:

---

## Page 1456

“Such-and-such results from this matter”; “This generates such-and-such”; and “This or that has resulted from that matter”—speaking of any cause that produces an effect in speech and action. Even natural philosophers speak of “the elements and generated things,” meaning the minerals, plants, and animals generated from the four basic elements: earth, water, air, and fire.

Thus, His denial—glorified be He—that He begets anything entails that nothing is generated from Him. His denial that He takes a child entails that He has not done so with any of His creatures as a mark of honour, and that it is not fitting for Him to accord any of His servants the status of a child.

This refutes the claims of those who assert such things concerning the Messiah and others, those who say:

“We are the children of Allah,”

and those who say, “Philosophy is becoming like God.” A child is of the same kind as its parent and is its counterpart, even though it is an offshoot of it. Hence, those who affirm these ideas are among the most extreme of all people in asserting resemblance and equivalence to Him, assigning Him rivals, and equating others with Him and placing them on a par with Him.

Thus, the philosophers who maintain that intellects and souls emanate from Him through generation and causation make them His rivals and take them as gods and lords. Indeed, they may worship only them and invoke none besides them, regarding them as the originators of everything else beneath them.

So praise be to Allah, who has not taken a child and has no partner in His dominion. And:

“Blessed is He who sent down the Criterion to His servant, so that he may be a warner to all the worlds.”

“He to whom belongs the dominion of the heavens and the earth

---

## Page 1457

and who has taken no child and has no partner in His dominion, and who created everything and determined it precisely.” (1).

For these people have made the jinn partners with Allah, although He created them, and have falsely attributed sons and daughters to Him without knowledge. It has been said that the term “jinn” includes the angels, as has been said concerning His words:

“They have established a kinship between Him and the jinn.”

Although it has been said, in explaining this, that some Arab polytheists claimed that Allah had entered into marriage ties with the jinn, who then bore the angels, they also worshipped the angels, just as the Sabaean philosophers worshipped them. As the Exalted says:

“They have made the angels, who are servants of the Most Merciful, female. Did they witness their creation? Their testimony will be recorded, and they will be questioned.”

And the Exalted says:

“And on the Day when He gathers them all together, He will then say to the angels, ‘Was it you that these people used to worship?’”

“They will say, ‘Glory be to You! You are our Protector, not they. Rather, they used to worship the jinn; most of them believed in them.’”

This means that the angels did not command them to do so. Rather, the jinn commanded them to do so, so that they would worship the devils that appear to them in visible forms, just as idols have devils associated with them.

Likewise, devils descend upon some of those who worship the celestial bodies and observe them until an apparition descends upon one of them and speaks to him. It is, in fact, one of the devils.

For this reason, the Exalted says:

“Did I not enjoin upon you, O children of Adam, that you should not worship Satan? Indeed, he is a clear enemy to you.”

“And that you should worship Me? This is a straight path.”

“And he has indeed led astray great multitudes among you

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) A space of five lines has been left blank here in the margin of the original manuscript. He wrote in the draft: “The small sheet follows this.” We could not find it.

---

## Page 1458

; did you not use reason?” And He says:

“Will you then take him and his descendants as allies instead of Me, while they are enemies to you? What an evil substitute for the wrongdoers!”

Although they do not intend to worship Satan or ally themselves with him, in reality they worship him and ally themselves with him. It has thus become clear that these Sabaean philosophers, who are proponents of religious innovation, believe in only a little of what the messengers brought concerning the angels—their attributes and their stature.

This is because these people pursued only the path of reasoning from the motions of the celestial spheres and drawing analogies with their own souls, while denying and remaining ignorant of aspects of Allah’s creation and His bringing things into existence.

The reason for this is what a number of those who compiled accounts of them have mentioned: their early leading figures, such as Pythagoras, Socrates and Plato, used to travel to the land of the prophets in Syria and receive teachings from Luqmān the Wise and, after him, from the companions of David and Solomon. Aristotle, however, did not travel to the land of the prophets, nor did he possess the knowledge of the surviving prophetic teachings that his predecessors possessed. He had only a small measure of sound Sabaean belief, so he invented these syllogistic teachings for them. They became a framework followed by his adherents. At times, he happened to speak correctly about the natures of bodies or the form of logic.

“As for the earlier thinkers,” they are not known to have had a complete, innovated doctrinal system comparable to those of the proponents of innovation among Muslim speculative theologians, such as Abū al-Hudhayl, Hishām ibn al-Hakam and others like them who devised a doctrinal system

---

## Page 1459

in “the various subjects of fundamental religious doctrine,” and a group followed him in this. For the leading scholars of the Muslims—such as Mālik, Hammād ibn Zayd, al-Thawrī and others like them—spoke only of what the revealed message brought, wherein lie guidance and healing. Those who have no knowledge of the Muslims’ path substitute the teachings of these people for it. This is what causes religious innovations to emerge in every community: the ways of the messengers become obscured among them, and destruction ensues.

For this reason, they used to say: Holding firmly to the Sunnah is salvation. Mālik, may Allah have mercy on him, said:

“The Sunnah is like Noah’s Ark: whoever boards it is saved, and whoever stays behind perishes.”

This is true. Only those who believed the messengers and followed them boarded Noah’s Ark, while those who did not board it had rejected the messengers. Following the Sunnah means following the message that came from Allah. Thus, one who follows it is, inwardly and outwardly, like one who boarded the Ark with Noah. Whoever fails to follow the message is like one who failed to follow Noah, peace be upon him, and board the Ark with him.

Likewise, when a knowledgeable believer reflects on the various doctrines containing error and disbelief held by philosophers and other peoples, he finds that the Quran and Sunnah reveal their true condition, clarify whatever truth they contain, and distinguish their truth from their falsehood.

The Companions were the most knowledgeable of all people concerning this, just as they were the most steadfast in waging jihad against the disbelievers and hypocrites. As Abd Allāh ibn Mas‘ūd said of them:

“Whoever among you wishes to follow an example should follow those who have died, for no living person can be considered safe from trials. Those were the Companions of Muhammad: their hearts were the most righteous in this community, their knowledge the deepest, and they were the least given to affectation—a people whom Allah chose

---

## Page 1460

to accompany His Prophet and uphold His religion. So acknowledge their due rights and hold fast to their guidance, for they followed the straight path.” Thus, he described them as combining perfect righteousness of heart with perfect depth of knowledge.

This combination is rare among later generations, as reflected in the saying, “Among the rarities are a jurist who is a Sufi and a scholar who is an ascetic,” and similar remarks. Those whose hearts are righteous, whose intentions are good, and whose aims are sound are praised because their hearts are free of blameworthy desires. Yet they often lack knowledge and an understanding of people’s actual circumstances—an understanding that calls for condemning evil, forbidding it, and waging jihad in Allah’s cause. Those who delve deeply into the sciences, on the other hand, may acquire such knowledge of evils and specious arguments that it leads them into various forms of error and misguidance. The Companions of Muhammad, however, had the most righteous hearts and the deepest knowledge of all people.

Moreover, among most of those in later generations who delve deeply into knowledge, whether speculative theologians or devotees, this depth is accompanied by blameworthy affectation: speaking and acting without knowledge, and pursuing what cannot be attained. The Companions of Muhammad, despite being the most accomplished of all people in beneficial knowledge and righteous deeds, were the least given to affectation. One of them would utter a word or two of wisdom or insight through which Allah would guide an entire community. This is among Allah’s favors to this community. Others, by contrast, fill pages with contrived statements and extravagant utterances that rank among the worst needless innovations and invented opinions. Their only precedent in this was the follies of the soul inherited from those whose intentions in religion were corrupt.

---

## Page 1461

It is related that Allah, glorified be He, said to the Messiah:

“I shall create a community that I shall favor above every other community, though it has neither knowledge nor forbearance.” The Messiah said, “My Lord, how will You favor them above all other communities when they have neither knowledge nor forbearance?” He said, “I shall bestow upon them from My knowledge and My forbearance.”

This is among the distinctive blessings of following the Messenger. The more closely a person follows him, the more fully he attains this blessing. As Allah Most High says:

“O you who believe, fear Allah and believe in His Messenger. He will grant you a double portion of His mercy, provide you with a light by which you may walk, and forgive you. And Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful.”

“So that the People of the Scripture may know that they have no control over any of Allah’s bounty, and that bounty is in Allah’s hand: He grants it to whom He wills. And Allah is the Possessor of great bounty.”

Likewise, the two Sahīh collections contain the reports narrated by Abū Mūsā and ‘Abd Allāh ibn ‘Umar:

“The parable of us and the nations before us is that of a man who hired workers. He said, ‘Who will work for me until midday for one qīrāt each?’ The Jews worked. Then he said, ‘Who will work for me until the afternoon prayer for one qīrāt each?’ The Christians worked. Then he said, ‘Who will work for me until sunset for two qīrāts each?’ The Muslims worked. The Jews and Christians became angry and said, ‘We have done more work for less pay!’ He said, ‘Have I withheld anything that was your due?’ They said, ‘No.’ He said, ‘Then this is my bounty, which I grant to whom I will.’”

Thus, the Quran and the Sunnah show that Allah grants the followers of this Messenger bounty that He did not grant the adherents of the two Scriptures before them. What, then, of those among the Sabians who rank below them, let alone the innovators among the Sabians—the philosophizers and their like?

---

## Page 1462

It is well known that the people of hadith and the Sunnah have the closest ties to the Messenger and his followers. They therefore enjoy Allah’s favor, His singling them out for knowledge and forbearance, and multiplied rewards in a measure not granted to others. As one of the early generations said:

“The people of the Sunnah stand within Islam as the people of Islam stand among the religions.”

These remarks draw attention to what ignorant and misguided people imagine to be the Companions’ shortcomings in knowledge and exposition, or in strength and prowess in battle. This is not the place for a detailed treatment of the matter.

The point is to emphasize that anyone who claims, through his conduct or his words, that a group other than the people of hadith has grasped hidden, unseen realities that the people of hadith have not—concerning creation and resurrection, the beginning and the final return, faith in Allah and the Last Day, knowledge of the Necessary Existent and the rational soul, and the knowledge and moral qualities through which souls are purified, reformed, and perfected—is, if he believes in the messengers, an ignorant person with a pronounced trait of hypocrisy. Otherwise, he is an outright hypocrite, among those described in the words:

“When it is said to them, ‘Believe as the people have believed,’ they say, ‘Should we believe as the foolish have believed?’ Indeed, it is they who are the foolish, but they do not know.”

He may also be among “those who dispute concerning Allah’s signs without any authority having come to them,” and among those described in the words:

“As for those who argue concerning Allah after He has been acknowledged, their argument is invalid before their Lord; wrath is upon them, and a severe punishment awaits them.”

This can also be demonstrated through sound rational reasoning that admits of no doubt—even though it is evident by natural disposition to everyone whose disposition is sound. For if the Messenger is the most perfect of all creation and the most knowledgeable

---

## Page 1463

of realities and the most upright in speech and conduct, it follows that those who know him best are the most knowledgeable of all creation concerning those realities, and that those who conform most closely to him and follow his example are the best of all creation.

One should not object: “This natural disposition is altered by the negligence and transgression found among those who identify with the Sunnah and hadith.” The answer is that these failings are more prevalent among others. What is required is to compare each group as a whole with the other, considering both praiseworthy and blameworthy qualities. This is the just comparison. What alters this natural disposition is limited knowledge of hadith and the Sunnah and insufficient adherence to them, coupled with the degree of rigor in certain areas of knowledge and excellence in certain actions found among those who oppose them. These merits then become a misleading basis for accepting a person’s other views and giving him preference. We have no interest in naming individuals. Abū Muhammad ibn Qutaybah, at the beginning of his book Mukhtalif al-Hadīth (Conflicting Hadith Reports), and other scholars have mentioned countless matters in this connection that illustrate what we have said.

The aim is simply to describe the method of knowledge and action itself: the method by which the realities of matters addressed through reports and theoretical inquiry are understood, and the realities of matters involving will and action are attained. Whenever someone other than the Messenger possesses knowledge of these matters, the ability to explain them, or a desire to impart that knowledge, the Messenger knows more about them, is more eager to provide guidance, and is better able to explain them than that person. The same applies to his Companions after him and to their followers.

These are the qualities of perfection: knowledge, will, doing good, and the ability to do so, as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said in the supplication for seeking Allah’s guidance in making a choice:

---

## Page 1464

“O Allah, I seek Your guidance in making a choice by Your knowledge, and I seek ability from You by Your power, and I ask You of Your immense bounty. For You have power and I do not; You know and I do not; and You are the Knower of all that is unseen.”

He, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, thus taught us to seek Allah’s guidance by His knowledge, so that He teaches us from His knowledge what enables us to recognize what is good, and to seek ability from Him by His power, so that He makes us able.

The Arabic form istif‘āl expresses a request for an action, as in the authentic hadith in which Allah Most High says:

“O My servants, all of you are hungry except those whom I have fed. So ask Me to feed you, and I shall feed you. O My servants, all of you are astray except those whom I have guided. So ask Me for guidance, and I shall guide you.”

Seeking Allah’s guidance means asking Him to guide us, and seeking food from Him means asking Him to feed us. The former is nourishment for the hearts, and the latter nourishment for the bodies. The same applies to seeking His guidance in making a choice by His knowledge and seeking ability from Him by His power.

Then he said:

“And I ask You of Your immense bounty.”

This is a request for His generosity, favor, gifts, and kindness, which He bestows through His will, mercy, and compassion.

That is why he said:

“For You have power and I do not; You know and I do not.”

He did not say, “I have no compassion for myself,” because a person seeking guidance in making a choice desires good for himself and asks for it. Yet he neither knows what that good is nor has the ability to attain it unless Allah makes it known to him and enables him to attain it.

If, then, the Messenger is the most knowledgeable of all creation about realities pertaining to what is reported and what is sought, the most eager of all creation to teach, guide, and impart knowledge, and the most capable of all creation in explanation and expression, then it is impossible that anyone below him should have imparted to his own close followers a greater knowledge of these realities than the Messenger imparted to his own close followers;

---

## Page 1465

so it is impossible for any of the groups to possess knowledge of realities that the scholars of hadith do not possess.

If no one among these groups is more knowledgeable about these realities or explains them more clearly than he does, then whatever ignorance among some of their members they are criticized for must be more prevalent in the group of the opponent who criticizes them. Thus, their critic is ignorant and unjust, with a trait of hypocrisy—if he is a believer. This is the point at issue.

Moreover, what we have explained is perceived by the heart. I know this in detail concerning everyone with whom I am acquainted. This is a general statement that could be elaborated in many ways, but this is not the place for that.

---

## Page 1466

Section

As for the statement, “The Hashwiyya are of two kinds: one does not shrink from hashw, likening Allah to creation, or ascribing a body to Him; the other conceals itself behind the way of the early generations. Yet the way of the early generations consists only of affirming Allah’s oneness and declaring Him free of imperfection, not likening Him to creation or ascribing a body to Him”—all proponents of innovation likewise claim this for themselves. As the poet said:

Each claims a bond of love with Laylā,
Yet Laylā acknowledges none of their claims.

This statement contains both truth and falsehood. Among its true elements is the condemnation of those who liken Allah to His creatures and regard His attributes as being of the same kind as theirs.

Allah Most High has said:

“There is nothing like Him.”

And He Most High has said:

“Nor is there to Him any equivalent.”

And He has said:

“Do you know of any equal to Him?”

We have discussed this at length and presented the rational proofs indicated by the Book of Allah that negate such likeness. We have explained aspects of this that the deniers of the divine attributes, who present themselves as upholding Allah’s freedom from imperfection, have not mentioned—arguments neither found in their books nor heard from their leading authorities. Indeed, most of the arguments they offer are weak, because they seek to establish both truth and falsehood; no consistently valid proof can therefore be established for that

---

## Page 1467

—a proof free of flaws—unlike the case of someone who is measured in what he says and strives to speak rightly. Allah will set his deeds right, as He Most High says:

“O you who have believed, fear Allah and speak words of appropriate justice.”

“He will amend for you your deeds and forgive you your sins.”

Another element of truth in his statement is its indication that those who claim to follow the way of the early generations should be refuted when they are ignorant of their teachings or depart from them through additions or omissions. Likening Allah to His creation and falsely attributing statements to the early generations are reprehensible, whether or not this is called hashw. This applies to many extremists among those who affirm the divine attributes and transmit fabricated hadiths concerning them, such as the report about “the sweat of horses”; “His descent late on the day of ‘Arafah upon a dark-grey camel, to shake hands with those on foot and embrace those riding”; “His manifesting Himself to His Prophet on earth”; “his seeing Him on a chair between heaven and earth”; “his seeing Him during circumambulation”; or “in some of the lanes of Medina”—and other fabricated hadiths of this kind.

I have seen examples of this that rank among the gravest abominations and acts of disbelief. Several people have brought me booklets and books containing such fabrications against Allah and His Messenger. Chains of transmission have been fabricated for these hadiths. Indeed, one of them took a book written by Shaykh Abū al-Faraj al-Maqdisī concerning the criteria by which a follower of the Sunnah is distinguished from an adherent of innovation, and presented it as something Allah had revealed to His Prophet on the night of the Ascension, commanding him to test people by it: whoever accepted it was a follower of the Sunnah, and whoever did not was an adherent of innovation. They also added to it things that Shaykh Abū al-Faraj had never said, nor would any rational person say.

A well-known figure may say, regarding questions

---

## Page 1468

and arguments, something that is true or contains a semblance of truth. When ignorant people take this and alter it, it comes to contain errors amounting to the gravest falsehood and deceit.

The point is that his words contain some truth, but also several falsehoods.

First: His statement, “He does not shy away from hashw and corporealism,” condemns people using labels for which Allah has revealed no authority. The One whose praise confers honor and whose condemnation brings disgrace is Allah. Labels that carry religious praise or blame must be those for which Allah has revealed His authority and which are established by the Quran, the Sunnah, or scholarly consensus, such as believer and disbeliever, learned and ignorant, moderate and deviant.

As for these “three expressions,” they occur neither in Allah’s Book nor in any hadith from Allah’s Messenger. Nor did any of the early generations of this community or its leading scholars use them, whether to deny or affirm them. The first to introduce their use as terms of condemnation were the “Muʿtazilah,” who parted from the main body of Muslims. Following the path of the Muʿtazilah rather than that of the community’s early generations means abandoning the upright speech required in religion and following the path of misguided innovators.

Of these expressions, the only one whose condemnation is reported from some of the early generations is “likening Allah to His creation.” Had he confined himself to that expression, he would have had a precedent among the righteous early generations. Had he mentioned the descriptions that Allah negates in the Quran, such as “equal, rival, and namesake,” and said, “Some of them do not shy away from asserting a likeness between Allah and His creation, or the like,” he would have condemned a statement that Allah negates in His Book and whose proponent the Quran shows to be blameworthy. It would then remain to consider whether the person making that statement actually deserves the blame attributed to him.

---

## Page 1469

As for labels whose bearers are neither condemned nor praised by Islamic law, two matters must be established: first, what these labels mean; and second, that those so labelled are blameworthy under Islamic law. The person being criticized may challenge both points, saying: “We do not concede that the people you mean fall under these labels that you condemn, when no evidence from revelation establishes that they warrant condemnation. Even if they do fall under them, we do not concede that everyone to whom these labels apply is blameworthy under Islamic law.”

Second: This group, of whom you said, “They do not shy away from hashw, likening Allah to His creation, and corporealism,” either includes those who affirm the attributes attested by the Quran and the Sunnah, or it does not. If you include them, you are condemning everyone who affirms these attributes. Yet it is well known that this is the position of the early generations generally and of the leading scholars of the religion. Indeed, even the leading scholars of speculative theology affirm these scripturally attested attributes in general, although their approaches differ. These include Abū Saʿīd ibn Kullāb, Abū al-Hasan al-Ashʿarī, and the leading scholars among his followers, such as Abū ʿAbd Allāh ibn Mujāhid, Abū al-Hasan al-Bāhilī, Judge Abū Bakr ibn al-Bāqillānī, Abū Ishāq al-Isfarāyīnī, Abū Bakr ibn Fūrak, Abū Muhammad ibn al-Labbān, Abū ʿAlī ibn Shādhān, Abū al-Qāsim al-Qushayrī, Abū Bakr al-Bayhaqī, and others. For every one of these scholars

---

## Page 1470

affirms such scripturally attested attributes as Allah Most High wills. The foundation of the doctrine transmitted from them is to affirm every attribute mentioned in the Quran. As for the attributes mentioned in hadith, some of them affirm these, while others do not.

If you condemn all those who affirm the attributes, including your predecessors and others, you are left with none but the Jahmiyya: the Muʿtazila and those among the later Ashʿarīs and others who agree with them in denying the scripturally attested attributes. Yet you have offered no argument that can be relied upon. How can people be censured for not shying away from the position held by the early generations of the Muslim community, its leading scholars, and the very scholars whom their critic follows?

If, however, you do not include those who affirm the scripturally attested attributes under the label “al-Hashwiyya,” this statement does not help you. Indeed, you yourself have espoused this position elsewhere. If a person’s words leave him no alternative but to condemn either himself or his predecessors—whose standing as leading scholars he acknowledges, and whom he recognizes as superior to their followers—then, in either case, this condemnation falls upon him.

He would also share something of the character of the Khawārij, to the first of whom the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“You would indeed be ruined and at a loss if I did not act justly.”

That is to say: if you acknowledge that I am Allah’s Messenger, yet claim that I act unjustly, then you are ruined and at a loss. Likewise, anyone who condemns those whom he acknowledges to be the finest and best members of the Muslim community, and from whom his own group received its knowledge and faith, is ruined and at a loss through this condemnation. Such is the condition of the Rāfida in their condemnation of the Companions.

---

## Page 1471

The third point concerns his statement:

“The other hides behind the doctrine of the early generations.”

If by “hiding” you mean concealing adherence to the doctrine of the early generations, the response is this: that doctrine is kept concealed only in lands dominated by people of religious innovation, such as the lands of the Rāfida and the Khawārij. An oppressed believer there may conceal his faith and his adherence to the Sunnah, just as the believer from Pharaoh’s household concealed his faith, and as many believers concealed theirs while living in the territory of war. If these people are in a land where you wield authority, and they have concealed their adherence to the doctrine of the early generations, then you have condemned yourself, since you belong to a group among whom that doctrine must be concealed. If you yourself are among the oppressed who conceal their adherence to it, there is no sense in condemning yourself. If you belong neither to them nor to the ruling elite, there is no justification for condemning people by describing them as “hiding.”

If, however, by “hiding” you mean that they use it as a shield, protect themselves from others through it, and openly profess it, so that when one of them is addressed, he says, “I follow the doctrine of the early generations”—and this is what he intended, and Allah knows best—then the response to him is this:

There is no fault in someone openly professing the doctrine of the early generations, identifying with it, and declaring his allegiance to it. Rather, by unanimous agreement, this must be accepted from him, for the doctrine of the early generations can only be true.

If he conforms to it both inwardly and outwardly, he is like the believer who follows the truth both inwardly and outwardly. If he conforms to it only outwardly, but not inwardly, he is like the hypocrite: his outward profession is accepted, and his inner state is left to Allah. We have not been commanded to probe people’s hearts or cut open their bellies.

---

## Page 1472

As for his statement:

“The doctrine of the early generations consists solely of affirming divine oneness and transcendence, not attributing a body to Allah or likening Him to creation.”

The response to him is this: the terms “divine oneness,” “divine transcendence,” “likening Allah to creation,” and “attributing a body to Allah” have acquired multiple meanings because dialectical theologians and others use different technical conventions. Each group means something by these terms that others do not.

The Jahmiyya among the Mu'tazila and other groups mean by “divine oneness and transcendence” the denial of all attributes, and by “attributing a body to Allah and likening Him to creation” the affirmation of any of them. Thus, anyone who says, “Allah sees,” or “He possesses knowledge,” is, in their view, someone who likens Allah to creation and attributes a body to Him.

Many dialectical theologians who affirm the divine attributes mean by divine oneness and transcendence the denial of all or some of the attributes known from scriptural reports, and by attributing a body to Allah and likening Him to creation the affirmation of all or some of those attributes.

The philosophers mean by divine oneness what the Mu'tazila mean, and more besides. They even say that He has only an attribute of negation, an attribute of relation, or one combining the two.

The Ittihādiyya (proponents of the unity of existence) mean by divine oneness that He is absolute existence. Others have still other technical usages for this term.

As for the divine oneness with which Allah sent the messengers and revealed the scriptures, it includes none of these technical usages. Rather, Allah commanded His servants to worship Him alone and associate no partners

---

## Page 1473

with Him whatsoever. Thus, no one besides Him may have any share in the worship and its attendant acts that belong exclusively to Him. This concerns deeds. As for words, it consists in believing in the attributes with which He has described Himself and with which His Messenger has described Him.

If you mean that the doctrine of the early generations is divine oneness in the sense conveyed by the Quran and Sunnah, then this is true. Those who affirm the divine attributes known from scriptural reports do not disagree with this.

But if you mean that the doctrine of the early generations is divine oneness and transcendence as certain groups understand them, then anyone who examines the reliably transmitted statements of the early generations, preserved in collections of their reports, knows this to be false. Not a single statement from any of the early generations agrees with the distinctive positions of these groups, nor does a single statement deny the divine attributes known from scriptural reports.

It is well known that if the doctrine of the early generations is to be established through transmission from them, then one must refer to the reports transmitted from them. But if it is to be established solely through inference, whereby anyone who considers a view correct says:

“This is the view of the early generations, because the early generations say only what is correct, and this is correct,”

then this is precisely what emboldens each of the proponents of religious innovation to claim that he follows the doctrine of the early generations. Whoever makes this statement has exposed his own fault: he has claimed adherence to their doctrine without transmitting anything from them, relying instead on his assertion that his own view is the truth.

As for the scholars of hadith, they describe the doctrine of the early generations only through mass-transmitted reports

---

## Page 1474

, citing the Muslim scholars who transmitted their doctrine, and at other times relating their actual words on this subject, as we did in our response to the request for a fatwa.

When we sought to explain the doctrine of the early generations, we presented two methods:

First, we cited as many of their statements as were available, identifying the scholars who transmitted them through accepted chains of transmission.

Second, we identified those who transmitted the doctrine of the early generations from all the various Muslim groups: the four schools of jurisprudence, the scholars of hadith, the Sufis, and the speculative theologians, such as al-Ashʿarī and others. Thus, the doctrine of the early generations was transmitted unanimously by these groups and through mass transmission. We did not establish it merely by claiming that we were right and our opponents were wrong, as the proponents of religious innovation do.

Furthermore, the term “tajsīm” (corporealism) does not occur in the statements of any of the early generations, whether in denial or affirmation. How, then, can it be permissible to say that the doctrine of the early generations consists in denying or affirming corporealism, when neither this term nor its meaning has been reported from them?

Likewise, the term “divine oneness,” in the sense of denying any of the attributes, does not occur in the statements of any of the early generations. Nor does the term “transcendence,” in the sense of denying any of the divine attributes known from scriptural reports, occur in the statements of any of the early generations.

---

## Page 1475

The term “likening” does, however, occur in the statements of some of them, together with their explanation of it, as we have recorded from them. By “likening,” they meant likening Allah to His creation, not denying the attributes found in the Quran and hadith. Furthermore, even if this statement were true in itself, it would still lack any supporting proof that ought to be followed. It is merely an assertion made in the course of a dispute—something easily offered by anyone who considers it permissible and commendable to speak without knowledge or justice.

It also reveals little familiarity with the views of either the people of the Sunnah or the proponents of religious innovation, for he said:

“Likewise, all proponents of religious innovation claim that they follow the doctrine of the early generations.”

This is not the case. Rather, groups notorious for religious innovation, such as the Kharijites and the Rafidites, do not claim to follow the doctrine of the early generations. Indeed, they declare most of the early generations to be disbelievers. The Rafidites disparage Abū Bakr, ʿUmar, the majority of the earliest pioneers among the Emigrants and the Helpers, those who followed them in righteousness, and the other leading scholars of Islam. How, then, can they claim to follow the doctrine of the early generations? Instead, they falsely and slanderously claim adherence to the doctrine of the Prophet’s household. Likewise, the Kharijites declared ʿUthmān, ʿAlī, and the majority of Muslims among the Companions and their successors to be disbelievers. How, then, can they claim to follow the doctrine of the early generations?

(The fourth point)

This designation is not mentioned in the Book of Allah, the Sunnah of His Messenger, or the statements of any of the Companions, their successors, the leading scholars of Islam, or any shaykh or scholar

---

## Page 1476

accepted by the Muslim community at large. Since that is not the case, condemning people by this designation has no basis in a scriptural text, scholarly consensus, or any authority whom laypeople may legitimately follow. When such condemnation has no foundation for either scholars exercising independent judgment or those who follow scholarly authority generally, it is utterly unsound and unjust. Even if someone whom certain laypeople could legitimately follow were to condemn others by this designation, that would not constitute proof, since another layperson following an authority whom he may legitimately follow cannot be condemned on that basis. Moreover, Abū Muhammad and others like him did not consider it permissible to pronounce on many subsidiary questions of jurisprudence merely by following scholarly authority. How, then, could he permissibly pronounce on the fundamentals of religion on that basis?

The crux of the matter is this:

Anyone who condemns others by this designation is either exercising independent scholarly judgment or following scholarly authority. The former must have a scriptural text, scholarly consensus, or evidence derived from either, for condemnation and praise are judgments governed by the revealed law. We have already explained this and stated that praise and condemnation, love and hatred, promises of reward and threats of punishment, allegiance and enmity, and similar religious judgments are valid only when based on designations for which Allah has revealed authority. Making such judgments dependent on invented designations is impermissible; indeed, it amounts to instituting a religion for which Allah has given no permission. We also explained that one must know the limits of what Allah has revealed to His Messenger.

The Muʿtazilites likewise declare groups among the Companions and their successors to be sinners. They disparage many of them and the hadiths they transmitted that contradict their views and desires. Indeed, they also declare to be disbelievers those among the earlier and later generations who oppose the principles they have adopted. Thus, their disparagement extends to the scholars of

---

## Page 1477

the early generations and their knowledge to a degree not found among the people of the Sunnah and the community. Professing adherence to the doctrine of the early generations is not one of their distinguishing marks, even though they affirm the caliphate of the four caliphs. They also revere leading scholars of Islam, and the majority of them, in a way that those other groups do not. Yet their disparagement of many of these scholars is beyond the scope of this discussion.

“As for al-Nazzām,” his disparagement of the Companions is likewise beyond the scope of this discussion.

Even if these proponents of religious innovation disparaged the early generations partly because of shortcomings and transgressions among those claiming adherence to them, and because some of them exercised independent scholarly judgment in matters where the correct view lay elsewhere, such occurrences became a trial for their opponents, leading them far astray.

The point here is that the groups commonly known among the people of the Sunnah and the community for religious innovation do not profess adherence to the early generations. Indeed, the group most notorious for religious innovation is the Rafidites, to the extent that ordinary people recognize no distinguishing mark of religious innovation other than Rafidism. In their usage, a Sunni is anyone who is not a Rafidite. This is because, more than any other group, the Rafidites oppose the Prophetic hadiths and the meanings of the Quran, disparage the early generations of the Muslim community and its leading scholars, and attack the majority of the community. Since they are furthest from following the early generations, they are the most notorious for religious innovation.

Thus, it is clear that the distinguishing mark of the proponents of religious innovation is their failure to profess adherence to the way of the early generations. Hence Imam Ahmad said in the epistle transmitted by ‘Abdūs ibn Mālik:

“The foundations of the Sunnah, according to us, are to adhere to the way of the Companions of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace.”

---

## Page 1478

As for the dialectical theologians who affirm the divine attributes—the Kullābīs, Karrāmīs, and Ashʿarīs—along with the jurists, Sufis, and hadith scholars, these generally do not disparage the early generations. Indeed, they may agree with them on most of the broad outlines of their teachings. Yet the more knowledgeable any of them is about hadith, the better he understands the doctrine of the early generations and the more closely he follows it. Each group’s reverence for the early generations is proportionate to its adherence to the Sunnah and the scarcity of religious innovation within it.

As for the claim that professing adherence to the early generations is a distinguishing mark of the proponents of religious innovation, this is categorically false. Such a situation is possible only where ignorance is widespread and knowledge scarce.

This is illustrated by the fact that many of Abū Muhammad’s associates among the followers of Abū al-Hasan al-Ashʿarī explicitly acknowledge their disagreement with the early generations—for example, concerning faith and the reinterpretation of Quranic verses and hadiths. They say:

“The doctrine of the early generations is that faith consists of speech and action, and that it increases and decreases. As for the dialectical theologians among our associates, their doctrine is such-and-such.”

Likewise, they say:

“The doctrine of the early generations is that these verses and hadiths concerning the divine attributes are not to be reinterpreted. The dialectical theologians, however, advocate reinterpreting them, regarding this as either obligatory or permissible.”

They mention the disagreement between the early generations and their associates who practise dialectical theology. This is what their tongues declare and their books record. Is there no discerning person who will take heed, no deluded person who will desist?

That position is established as belonging to the early generations, even by the explicit admission of their opponents. Then a doctrine arises that departs from their position. Does this not explicitly imply that the early generations had gone astray concerning monotheism and Allah’s freedom from imperfection, while later generations came to understand these matters? This is necessarily false in light of sound knowledge and firmly grounded religion.

---

## Page 1479

Moreover, dialectical theologians sometimes defend the positions of the early generations and sometimes those of the dialectical theologians, as several of them do, including Abū al-Maʿālī al-Juwaynī, Abū Hāmid al-Ghazālī, al-Rāzī, and others. Yet whichever doctrine they defend on a given occasion is, by implication, the one to be relied upon. Thus, they do not remain steadfast upon a single religious position, and doubts overwhelm them. This is Allah’s customary way with those who turn away from the Quran and Sunnah.

At other times, they regard their later brethren as more adept and knowledgeable than the early generations, saying:

“The way of the early generations is safer, but the way of these later scholars is more knowledgeable and wiser.”

They thus credit their brethren with superiority in knowledge, exposition, rigorous inquiry, and understanding, while attributing to the early generations deficiency and shortcomings in these matters, or error and ignorance. The most they will do for the early generations is offer excuses for their shortcomings and neglect.

There is no doubt that this is a form of Rāfidī rejectionism. Although it does not amount to declaring the early generations unbelievers—as some of the Rāfidīs and Khārijites do—or declaring them morally corrupt—as some of the Muʿtazilīs, Zaydīs, and others do—it does amount to declaring them ignorant, mistaken, and misguided, and attributing sins and acts of disobedience to them. Even if this falls short of imputing moral corruption, it still amounts to claiming that the people of generations deemed inferior by the revealed Law are more knowledgeable and virtuous than those of the generations deemed superior.

For anyone who reflects upon the Quran and Sunnah and upon the consensus of all the groups belonging to Ahl al-Sunnah wa-l-Jamāʿah, it is necessarily known that the best generation of this community—in deeds, words, beliefs, and every other virtue—is the first generation, then

---

## Page 1480

those who follow them, then those who follow them—as has been established through multiple reports from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace—and that they surpass the later generations in every virtue: knowledge, deeds, faith, intellect, religion, exposition, and worship, and are better qualified to explain every difficult matter. No one denies this except someone who obstinately disputes what is necessarily known to be part of Islam and whom Allah has led astray with knowledge. As Abdullāh ibn Masūd, may Allah be pleased with him, said:

“Whoever among you wishes to follow an example should follow those who have died, for the living are not safe from trials. Those are the Companions of Muhammad: those of this community with the most righteous hearts, the deepest knowledge, and the least affectation. They are people whom Allah chose to accompany His Prophet and establish His religion. So recognize their due and hold fast to their guidance, for they followed upright guidance.”

Another said:

“Adhere to what has been handed down from those who came before, for they brought what suffices and heals. No hidden good unknown to them has emerged since their time.”

Moreover, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“No time comes but that the one after it is worse, until you meet your Lord.”

How, then, could an age come upon us that is better with regard to the greatest object of knowledge—namely, knowledge of Allah Most High? This can never happen.

How well al-Shāfiī, may Allah have mercy on him, expressed it in his Epistle:

“They surpass us in all knowledge, intellect, religion, virtue, and every means by which knowledge is attained or guidance grasped. Their judgments for us are better than our own judgments for ourselves.”

Furthermore, these Jahmī Kullābīs—such as Abū Muhammad, the author of this statement, and others like him—may be asked: How can you abandon the way of the early generations when the highest attainment of the early generations is to be

---

## Page 1481

in agreement with the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace?

Indeed, most of the knowledge and faith possessed by the early generations came from their Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, through whom Allah brought them out of darkness into the light and guided them to the path of the Almighty, the Praiseworthy. Concerning him, Allah said:

“It is He who sends down clear verses upon His servant to bring you out of darkness into the light.”

And He Most High said:

“O you who believe, fear Allah and believe in His Messenger. He will grant you a double portion of His mercy, provide you with a light by which to walk, and forgive you. Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful.”

“So that the People of the Book may know that they have no power over any of Allah’s bounty.”

And He Most High said:

“Allah has certainly bestowed a favor upon the believers by sending among them a Messenger from among themselves, reciting His verses to them, purifying them, and teaching them the Book and wisdom, although before that they were clearly astray.”

And He Most High said:

“And thus We have revealed to you a spirit by Our command. You did not know what the Book was, nor what faith was, but We have made it a light by which We guide whom We will of Our servants. Indeed, you guide to a straight path.”

“The path of Allah, to whom belongs whatever is in the heavens and whatever is on earth.”

Abū Muhammad and others like him have followed the path of the heretics who say that the Messenger did not explain the truth concerning monotheism, nor explain to people how things truly are. Rather, he presented to people the opposite of the truth; as for the truth itself, he either concealed it or did not know it.

For these heretics among the philosophers, and those who follow their path in opposing what the Messenger brought concerning matters of knowledge—such as monotheism, the Hereafter, and the like—say that the Messenger perfected practical matters relating to ethics and to household and civic governance

---

## Page 1482

and brought a practical religious law that is the finest of all the world’s laws. They acknowledge that the world has never known a law better or more perfect than his. For they have seen how well he governed the world, the principles of justice he established, and the injustice he eradicated. As for the matters of knowledge he conveyed—concerning the Lord’s attributes and names, His angels, His books, His messengers, the Last Day, Paradise, and Hell—when they saw that these contradicted their own beliefs, they divided into two groups regarding the Messenger.

The most extreme among them say that he did not possess this knowledge; his excellence lay only in practical matters: worship and ethics. As for matters of knowledge, they claim that the philosophers knew more about them than he did—indeed, more than the other prophets as well.

These people say that Alī was a philosopher and knew more about matters of knowledge than the Messenger, and that Aaron was a philosopher and knew more about them than Moses. Many of them exalt Pharaoh, calling him “Plato the Copt.” They claim that the man of Midian whose daughter Moses married—whom some people identify as Shuayb—was Plato, Aristotle’s teacher. They also say that Aristotle was al-Khidr, along with other such statements whose ignorance and misguidance are known in their full extent only to the Lord of Majesty. At the very least, these statements betray their ignorance of the chronology of the prophets. For Aristotle, by their own unanimous admission, was a minister

---

## Page 1483

to Alexander, son of Philip of Macedon, from whom the Jews and Christians reckon the Roman era. He lived about three hundred years before Christ. They may suppose that he was “Dhū al-Qarnayn” (the Two-Horned One) mentioned in the Quran, and that Aristotle was a minister to the Dhū al-Qarnayn mentioned in the Quran. This is ignorance. For this Alexander, son of Philip, did not reach the lands of the Turks or build the barrier; he reached only the lands of the Persians.

Dhū al-Qarnayn, who is mentioned in the Quran, reached the eastern and western reaches of the earth and lived before this Alexander. It is said that his name was Alexander, son of Dārā. He was a believer who worshipped Allah alone, whereas the other was a polytheist: he and his people worshipped the stars and idols and practised sorcery. Likewise, Aristotle and his fellow Greeks were polytheists who worshipped idols and practised sorcery. They wrote works on these subjects; the accounts of them are well known, and the traces they left plainly attest to this. What comparison can there be between the two?

The purpose here is to explain what these esoteric philosophers say about what the Messenger brought.

And (the second group among them say that the Messenger knew the actual truth concerning divine oneness and the resurrection. They say he knew that the Lord has no affirmative attribute, that He is not seen and does not speak; that the celestial spheres are eternal and beginningless, having always existed and continuing forever; that bodies will not rise again; and that Allah has no angels who are living, speaking beings descending with revelation

---

## Page 1484

from Him and ascending to Him; rather, he inwardly held the beliefs of these esoteric sects, but could not disclose them to the common people. For, if these beliefs were made known, their minds and hearts would not accept them; instead, they would reject them and recoil from them. He therefore presented them with imagery and similitudes that would benefit them in their religion, even though this involved misleading them, keeping them ignorant, and causing them to believe things contrary to their actual reality, because of the benefit this would bring them.

They take as imams the leaders of the esoteric sects, such as the descendants of Ubayd ibn Maymūn al-Qaddāh, who claimed descent from Muhammad ibn Ismā‘īl ibn Ja‘far. They were not his descendants; rather, their ancestor was a Jew who was the stepson of a Magian, and they professed Shiism outwardly. In reality, they did not adhere to the creed of any Shia group: neither the Imāmīs nor the Zaydīs, nor even the extremists who believe in Alī’s divinity or prophethood. Rather, they were worse than all these groups. Hence Muslim scholars wrote numerous works exposing their secrets and tearing away their veils, and Muslims undertook many military campaigns against them. Their history is well known. Ibn Sīnā and his household were followers of these people during the reign of their Egyptian ruler. This was why Ibn Sīnā embraced philosophy.

These people regard Muhammad ibn Ismā‘īl as the concealed imam and maintain that he abrogated the religious law of Muhammad ibn Abd Allāh ibn Abd al-Muttalib. They say that these Ismā‘īlīs were infallible imams. Indeed, they may say that they are superior to the prophets, and they may say that they are gods to be worshipped. This was why al-Hākim sent his servant, “Hashtakīr” al-Durzī, to Wādī Taym Allāh ibn Tha‘laba

---

## Page 1485

in Syria; he led the people of that region astray, and remnants of his following remain among them to this day, professing the divinity of al-Hākim. He led them out of Islam, so they do not recognize the obligation of the five daily prayers, fasting the month of Ramadan, or pilgrimage to the Sacred House, nor do they acknowledge the prohibition of what Allah and His Messenger have forbidden, such as carrion, blood, pork, wine, and other things. These people initially invite anyone who responds to their call to embrace Shiism, observe what the Rāfidīs deem obligatory, and regard as forbidden what they forbid.

They then lead him, step by step, until they finally bring him to abandon Islam altogether and to believe that the aim is to learn their secrets—the knowledge through which the soul attains perfection, as the heretical philosophers maintain. Whoever acquires this knowledge has reached the ultimate goal: the acts of worship incumbent upon ordinary people, such as the five daily prayers, fasting Ramadan, and pilgrimage to the House, are no longer obligatory for him, and forbidden things that remain unlawful for others become lawful for him.

Thus, when these people exalt the Messenger—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—and say that his knowledge was perfect, they place him in the same category as their heretical leaders, claiming that he presented the common people with teachings contrary to those he concealed for the elite. Elsewhere we have explained the falsity of their claims in a discussion beyond the scope of the present occasion.

The point here is that those who deny Allah’s being above creation and His attributes known through revelation, such as the author of al-Lumʿa and others like him, make claims about the Messenger similar to theirs: what he openly taught was not the actual truth, because he could not disclose that truth to the common people. Thus, if

---

## Page 1486

they say this about the Messenger himself, what, then, do they say about his followers “among the early generations of the Muslim community”—the Companions and the Successors?

Anyone whose fundamental position concerning the Messenger and the earliest forerunners among the Emigrants and the Helpers is of this kind is opposing them, not agreeing with them. This is especially so if he openly professes the denial which, in his view, the Messenger and his closest Companions concealed rather than proclaimed: in this respect too, he would be opposing them. This approach is adopted by most of those who negate the divine attributes, such as Ibn Rushd the Younger and others. A substantial amount of this also appears in the writings of Abū Hāmid al-Ghazālī. Ibn ʿAqīl and others like him sometimes say this as well. However, when Ibn ʿAqīl departed from the Sunnah early in his life, his predominant tendency was toward Jahmism and Muʿtazilism. His later position was different, for he came to embrace the pure Sunnah.

Abū Hāmid inclined toward philosophy, but presented it in the guise of Sufism and Islamic expressions. For this reason, Muslim scholars refuted him, including even his closest disciple, Abū Bakr ibn al-ʿArabī, who said:

“Our shaykh, Abū Hāmid, became deeply immersed in the philosophers’ thought, then tried to break free of them, but could not.”

He has also been reported to have professed doctrines of the Bātiniyya (esotericists), and corroboration of these reports can be found in his books. The scholars mentioned earlier refuted him.

---

## Page 1487

Section

The objector then said:

Abū al-Faraj ibn al-Jawzī said in his refutation of the Hanbalīs:

“They have affirmed for Allah, glorified be He, an eye, a form, a right and a left, a face in addition to His essence, a forehead, a chest, two hands, two legs, fingers, a little finger, a thigh, a shin, a foot, a side, a waist, a back and a front, ascending, descending, hurrying, and 'ujb. They have indeed completed the whole bodily form, yet they say: ‘This is to be understood in its apparent sense, but these are not bodily organs.’ People like these should not be addressed, for they defy reason, and it is as though they were speaking to children.”

I say:

This statement must be examined from several perspectives:

First: exposing the partisanship marked by ignorance and injustice that it contains, before discussing the substantive scholarly question.

Second: showing that it is a refutation entirely devoid of argument or evidence.

Third: showing the weakness of its transmitted material and its reasoning.

As for the “first” point: Abū al-Faraj did not compose the work from which his words were quoted

---

## Page 1488

as a refutation of the Hanbalīs, as this objector stated. Rather, he intended it—as he claimed—to refute some of them, specifically Abū ‘Abd Allāh ibn Hāmid, Qādī Abū Ya‘lā, his teacher Abū al-Hasan ibn al-Zāghūnī, and their followers. Abū al-Faraj did not undertake to refute the Hanbalīs as a whole, nor did he attribute to them what he condemned. Indeed, in opposing these men, he cites the statements of many Hanbalīs as evidence, including members of the Tamīmī family, such as Rizq Allāh al-Tamīmī, and Abū al-Wafā’ ibn ‘Aqīl.

Rizq Allāh inclined toward the approach of his predecessors, such as his grandfather Abū al-Hasan al-Tamīmī and his uncle Abū al-Fadl al-Tamīmī. Al-Sharīf Abū ‘Alī ibn Abī Mūsā was a companion of Abū al-Hasan al-Tamīmī. It is reported of him that he said:

“Qādī Abū Ya‘lā has indeed shat on the Hanbalīs—filth that water cannot wash away.”

We shall discuss this as Allah enables us, striving to speak with knowledge and justice. There is no power nor strength except through Allah.

There have always been Hanbalīs who incline toward a form of affirmation that another group among them rejects, while others refrain from both negation and affirmation. Thus, they experience the same kind of disagreement found among all other groups. Their disagreements, however, concern subtle points; as for the major principles, they agree upon them. For this reason, they have had less disagreement and division than other groups, owing to their firm adherence to the Sunnah and transmitted reports. In matters concerning the foundations of religion, Imam Ahmad has statements clarifying disputed questions that no one else possesses. His statements are supported by the Quran and Sunnah and by following the path of the righteous predecessors. Hence, all groups within the Muslim community that profess adherence to the Sunnah—its jurists, speculative theologians, and Sufis—profess adherence to him.

---

## Page 1489

Yet these people may disagree over certain questions, for this is inevitable in the world. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, foretold that this would inevitably occur, and that when he asked his Lord not to let them turn their violence against one another, his request was denied. Thus, some disagreement is inevitable among groups professing adherence to the Sunnah and the community. Nevertheless, there must be among them a group that holds firmly to the Quran and Sunnah. Likewise, although disputes and differences inevitably arise among Muslims, there will always remain within this nation a group upholding the truth, unharmed by those who oppose or abandon them, until the Hour comes.

Accordingly, since Abū al-Hasan al-Ash‘arī and his followers professed adherence to the Sunnah and the community, he professed allegiance to Imam Ahmad, stating that he took him as his example and followed his path. The agreement and harmonious relations between his leading followers and many followers of Imam Ahmad are well known. Indeed, Abū Bakr ‘Abd al-‘Azīz cited Abū al-Hasan’s arguments in his discussions just as he cited those of his own fellow adherents, because he regarded him as one of their speculative theologians.

Among those most strongly inclined toward them were members of the Tamīmī family: Abū al-Hasan al-Tamīmī, his son, his grandson, and others like them. The affection and companionship between Abū al-Hasan al-Tamīmī and Qādī Abū Bakr ibn al-Bāqillānī were well known and widely recognized. For this reason, when the hadith scholar Abū Bakr al-Bayhaqī discussed Imam Ahmad’s creed in the book he compiled on his merits, he relied on what he transmitted from the statements of Abū al-Fadl ‘Abd al-Wāhid ibn Abī al-Hasan al-Tamīmī.

Abū al-Fadl also wrote a work on this subject, setting out Ahmad’s creed as he understood it. He did not reproduce Ahmad’s own words, but instead presented the broad statements of his creed in his own wording, repeatedly using the phrase, “And Abū ‘Abd Allāh was.” He is like someone who writes

---

## Page 1490
