If you say: “This argument rests on an implication drawn by the opposing party, yet you oppose and dispute it.”

---

## Page 1678

The reply to you is:

Singling out the many for mention does not imply a different judgment concerning the rest, whether affirmative or negative. Moreover, its implication is that they have not been favored above those outside the many. If they have not been favored above them, they may be equal to them, or those others may be superior to them. There are three possibilities: either they are superior to those who remain, those others are superior to them, or they are equal to them.

He said:

Differences in essential natures and essences necessarily affect the judgments and attributes pertaining to them. If the essential natures of humans and angels differ, one of the two must be superior, for it is impossible for them to be both identical and unequal in excellence. Once this rational proposition establishes that one is superior, and that divine statement establishes that humans are not superior, the superiority of angels is established—which is the point to be proved.

Several of those who affiliate themselves with the Sunnah have stated that the prophets and righteous human beings are superior to the angels. The Mu‘tazilah hold that angels are superior to humans, while the followers of al-Ash‘arī hold two positions: some consider the prophets and Allah’s friends superior, while others suspend judgment and make no categorical assertion as to which of the two is superior. It has been reported that some of their later scholars inclined toward the position of the Mu‘tazilah. This view has also sometimes been attributed to some who profess adherence to the Sunnah and support it.

It was related to me that someone who discussed deeds of the heart said:

“As for the angels who govern the heavens, the earth, and what lies between them, and those entrusted with the children of Adam, these are superior

---

## Page 1679

than these angels. As for the karūbiyyūn (cherubim), who stand above such tasks, no one is superior to them.” Some of them may make an exception for our Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. His exclusion from the general judgment concerning humanity means deeming him superior either to every individual angel or to those angels who govern the affairs of the world.

This is what has reached me of others’ statements on this question. I had thought that discussion of it was a later development, until I discovered that it was grounded in transmitted reports, going back to the early generations and the Companions. This prompted me to investigate the matter thoroughly, whereupon we adopted the position of the early generations.

Abū Ya‘lā al-Mawsilī related in his well-known Kitāb al-Tafsīr (Book of Quranic Commentary) a report from ‘Abd Allāh ibn Salām. He was knowledgeable about both the earlier and the later Scripture, having been one of the People of the Book. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, had testified that his life would end well; Mu‘ādh’s counsel at his death also identified him as one of the four scholars from whom knowledge should be sought. He said:

“Allah has created no creature more honored by Him than Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace.” This is the report related from him.

I asked, “Not even Gabriel or Michael?”

He replied, “My nephew, do you know what Gabriel and Michael are? Gabriel and Michael are merely creatures subjected to Allah’s command, like the sun and the moon. Allah Most High has created no creature more honored by Him than Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace.”

‘Abd Allāh also related in al-Tafsīr (Quranic Commentary) (*) and elsewhere, from Ma‘mar, from Zayd ibn Aslam, that he said:

“The angels said, ‘Our Lord, You have given the children of Adam this world, where they eat and drink; so grant us the Hereafter.’

“He replied, ‘By My might, I will not make the righteous among the descendants of the one whom I created with My two hands like those to whom I said, “Be,” and they came into being.’”

> **Footnotes:**
> (*) Shaykh Nāsir ibn Hamad al-Fahd (p. 256) said: “Perhaps this should read al-Sunna.”

---

## Page 1680

Likewise, there is the account of the angels, all of them without exception, prostrating to Adam, and of the curse upon the one who refused to prostrate to him. This bestowed honor and dignity upon Adam.

Some foolish people have said: “The prostration was only to Allah, and Adam was made their direction of prayer, so that they prostrated toward him just as one prostrates toward the Ka‘bah. This does not establish his superiority over them, just as prostrating toward the Ka‘bah does not establish its superiority over a believer in Allah’s sight. Indeed, a believer’s sanctity is greater in Allah’s sight than that of the Ka‘bah.” They also said: “Prostrating to anyone other than Allah is forbidden; indeed, it is disbelief.”

The answer is that the prostration was to Adam, by Allah’s command and as an obligation He imposed, according to the consensus of all whose opinions carry weight. Several points demonstrate this:

First, He said “to Adam,” not “toward Adam.” Each preposition has its own meaning, and the language distinguishes between saying “I prostrated to him” and “I prostrated toward him.” As Allah Most High says:

“Do not prostrate to the sun or to the moon, but prostrate to Allah, who created them, if it is Him that you worship.”

And He says:

“To Allah prostrate all those in the heavens and the earth.”

Muslims unanimously agree that prostrating to anyone other than Allah is forbidden. As for the Ka‘bah, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, used to pray toward Jerusalem, then prayed toward the Ka‘bah. He would pray toward a short spear—not ‘to a short spear’—and toward a tree trunk—not ‘to a trunk’ or ‘to a tree.’ One who prostrates to something submits and humbles himself before it in his heart. By contrast, one who prostrates toward something merely turns his face and body toward it outwardly, just as he turns his face toward some

---

## Page 1681

direction when he heads toward it, as He said:

“So turn your face toward the Sacred Mosque, and wherever you are, turn your faces toward it.”

Second, if Adam had merely been a direction of prayer, Iblīs would not have refused to prostrate or claimed that he was better than Adam. A direction of prayer may consist of stones, without this making them superior to those who pray toward them. A man may pray toward a short spear, a camel, or another man, without imagining that this makes any of them superior. What, then, was Satan trying to avoid? This is truly astonishing!

Third, if He had made Adam the direction of prayer for a single prostration, then the direction of prayer and Jerusalem would have been many thousands of times superior to him, since they were made permanent directions for every kind of prayer. Thus, this lengthy account—which has been made a mark of his distinction and one of the greatest blessings bestowed upon him, and which has conveyed to the world that Allah elevated him through it and favored him—would amount to no more than His making him like the Ka‘bah on certain occasions. Yet even some of the faith, knowledge, and closeness to the Most Merciful that he was granted are far superior to the Ka‘bah; indeed, the Ka‘bah was established for him and his descendants. Is this, then, to be counted among the great blessings bestowed upon him, or is he to be likened to it in some exceedingly small and insignificant respect? No rational person would say this.

As for their statement, “It is not permissible to prostrate to anyone other than Allah,” the answer is: if this is stated in general terms, it is a general statement whose scope excludes the permissibility of prostrating to Adam. Yet specific evidence establishes that they prostrated to him, and a general statement does not override the specific evidence that qualifies it.

Second, they claim that prostrating to anyone other than Allah is forbidden both to us and to the angels. As for the former, there is no evidence; as for the latter, what proof is there for it?

---

## Page 1682

Third, do they mean that it is forbidden even when Allah commands it, or that it is forbidden when He has not commanded it? The latter is true, but it does not resolve the issue. As for the former, how could it be forbidden after Allah the Exalted has commanded it?

Fourth, Joseph’s father and brothers fell down in prostration to him, and it is said that this was their form of greeting. How, then, can prostration be said to be forbidden without qualification? Animals also used to prostrate to the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, yet animals do not worship Allah. How, then, can it be said that prostrating to something necessarily entails worshipping it? The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“If I were to command anyone to prostrate to anyone else, I would command a wife to prostrate to her husband.”

This is because of the greatness of his right over her. It is well known that he did not say, “If I were to command anyone to worship.”

Seventh (1), the explanation here is as follows: submission and heartfelt devotion, together with acknowledgment of lordship and servitude, can, in an absolute sense, be directed only to Allah—glorified and exalted be He—alone. Directing them to anyone else is impossible and invalid. Prostration, however, is one of the ordinances of revealed law, for Allah the Exalted has commanded us to prostrate to Him. Had He commanded us to prostrate to any of His creatures other than Himself, we would have prostrated to that creature in obedience to Allah, Mighty and Majestic, since He would have wished us to honor the one to whom we prostrated. Had He not made prostration obligatory for us, performing it would not have been obligatory at all.

Thus, the angels’ prostration to Adam was worship of Allah, obedience to Him, and an act through which they drew near to Him; for Adam, it conferred distinction, honor, and reverence. Joseph’s brothers’ prostration to him was a greeting and salutation. Do you not see that, had Joseph prostrated to his parents as a greeting, this would not have been deemed reprehensible for him?

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) Thus in the original.

---

## Page 1683

Nor has it been reported that Adam prostrated to the angels. Rather, Adam and his descendants were commanded to prostrate only to Allah, Lord of all worlds. Perhaps this—and Allah knows best the realities of all things—is because they are the noblest kind of beings, namely, the righteous descendants of Adam. There is no one above them to whom prostration would be fitting except Allah, Lord of all worlds. They are equals among themselves, and none possesses such an advantage over the others as would warrant prostration to him. As for other beings, they have prostrated to them: the angels to the most upright father, and the animals to the noblest son.

As for their claim, “Adam had done nothing beforehand to warrant being honored with prostration,” this is empty talk babbled by some of those who withdrew from the community. Allah the Exalted’s blessings, favors, and bounties upon His servants are not bestowed because of anything on their part. Even if they were bestowed because of something on their part, He Himself would be the One who had bestowed that very cause. Thus, He bestows it upon them and then shows them appreciation for His own blessings. Their claim is also invalid according to their own principles, though we need not explain that here.

As for His saying:

“And to Him they prostrate,”

even if it is conceded that this conveys exclusivity, its purpose—and Allah knows best—is to show their superiority to those human beings who associate partners with their Lord and worship others besides Him. He therefore informs them that the angels worship none besides Him. Moreover, this statement is general, whereas that verse is specific, so Adam is excepted. It may then be said: prostration is of two kinds—prostration purely as worship, and prostration to confer honor. The first can be directed only to Allah. As for the second, why do you say that the same applies to it? The verse is understood as referring to the first kind, thereby reconciling the evidence.

As for the second question, it has been related from some of the early generations that the angels who

---

## Page 1684

prostrated to Adam were only angels on earth, not the angels of the heavens.

Others say they were the angels of the heavens, excluding the cherubim. A later writer adopted this view and found it inconceivable that the most exalted angels should prostrate to Adam, given that they pay no heed to anything other than Allah. They related in support of this:

“Among Allah’s creatures are beings who do not know whether Adam was created or not.”

He also appealed to Allah’s words:

“Were you arrogant, or were you among the exalted?”

He took “the exalted” to mean the angels of heaven, maintaining that the angels of heaven were not commanded to prostrate to Adam.

Know, then, that this claim has, in the first place, no support warranting its acceptance, whether from transmitted evidence or rational argument. It rests on nothing but passing thoughts, fleeting notions, and insinuations whose source is the throne of Iblīs. He incites them with his voice to deprive them of the blessing he strove to withhold from their father long ago. Or else it is a statement made by someone who speaks both truth and falsehood. We, however, have grounds for rejecting it on several counts.

First, it contradicts the position held by the general body of scholars versed in the Book and the Sunnah. If one must follow others on their authority, these scholars are more deserving of being followed.

Second, it contradicts both the apparent meaning of the Noble Book and its explicit wording. A plural noun made definite by the definite article encompasses the entire class it denotes. Allah the Exalted says:

“And when We said to the angels, ‘Prostrate to Adam.’”

Thus, the angels’ prostration includes all the angels. This is what the language in which the Quran was revealed entails. Departing from the scope of a general statement by restricting it to particular members requires adequate evidence, and no such evidence exists.

---

## Page 1685

Third, He says:

“So the angels prostrated, all of them without exception.”

Even if the noun “angels” did not itself encompass every member of the class, reinforcing it with “all” would necessarily convey that meaning. Even if this did not suffice, “without exception” would provide yet further emphasis and confirmation. Even those who dispute the implications of general terms do not dispute them once they are reinforced by expressions conveying universality. Indeed, such emphatic expressions are introduced precisely to exclude the possibility of restriction and similar interpretations.

It has reached me that one of the early generations said:

“No people introduce an innovation without there being something in the Quran that refutes it, though they do not know it.”

Perhaps His words, “all of them without exception,” were included to answer anyone who might claim that only some of the angels prostrated to him, not all of them. This expression would thus refute their claim.

Anyone who still inwardly entertains a restricted interpretation after such confirmation and emphasis should offer himself condolences over his understanding and his ability to draw evidence from the Quran, for he can have no confidence in anything derived from it. I wonder: if all the angels had prostrated and Allah wished to inform us of that, what wording could be more complete and comprehensive? What other statement could be made? Is this not among the clearest possible expressions?

Fourth, this expression occurs repeatedly in the Quran. In the hadith of intercession, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“And He made His angels prostrate to you.”

The same occurs in the account of the debate between Moses and Adam. Some maintain that when a general statement is accompanied by something specific, its clarification must accompany it and may not be delayed, lest the listener be led into an erroneous belief. None of these statements is accompanied by evidence restricting its scope, so their universality must be affirmed with certainty. Others maintain—and this is the more correct view—that clarification may be delayed beyond the time the statement is made

---

## Page 1686

but only after searching for evidence that restricts its scope—and Allah knows best. Its general scope must therefore be affirmed. If the account has been repeated without anything indicating a restricted scope, then claiming that its scope is restricted is not a falsehood.

Their denial that the cherubim prostrated is groundless, for they prostrated in obedience to and worship of their Lord. The proponent of this view further claimed that they are superior to Adam if it is established that they did not prostrate. Unsupported accounts neither establish truth nor overthrow falsehood. Their interpretation of “the exalted ones” as the cherubim amounts to speaking about the Book of Allah, glorified and exalted is He, without knowledge. This interpretation is not known to have come from any recognized imam, nor does the wording provide any evidence for it.

It has also been said that “Have you become arrogant?” means: Did you seek to become great only from this moment, or were you exalted before that? We have no need to interpret Allah’s words according to our own opinions, and Allah knows best their interpretation.

Here arises (a third question: those who prostrated to him may have done so despite being superior to him, for a superior may serve an inferior.

We reply: Know that there is nothing objectionable in a superior benefiting an inferior, for the leader of a people is their servant. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, is the best of people and the one who benefits people most. In reality, however, the reward for the benefit he provides returns to him, and drawing near to Allah is brought to completion by benefiting His creation. This can be raised as an objection against anyone who argues from the angels’ management of our affairs that they are superior to us because of the many benefits they bring us.

As for prostration itself, it brings no benefit to its recipient beyond exaltation, distinction, and honor. It is utterly inappropriate for someone superior to be beneath someone inferior to him and lower than him in actual—not merely imagined—honor. Understand this, for a subtle truth lies beneath it.

---

## Page 1687

The second proof:

Allah’s words, recounting what Iblīs said:

“Do You see this one whom You have honored above me?”

This explicitly states that Adam was honored above Iblīs when Iblīs was commanded to prostrate to him.

The third proof:

Allah Most High created Adam with His Hand, as mentioned in the Book and the Sunnah, whereas He created the angels not with His Hand but by His word. This is affirmed by all who profess Islam, Sunnis and adherents of innovation alike—indeed, the People of the Scripture also affirm it. People hold three views concerning Allah’s two Hands.

As for Ahl al-Sunnah, they say:

Allah’s two Hands are two attributes of His Essence. The same principle applies to them as to all His attributes, including His life, knowledge, power, will, and speech. They therefore affirm every attribute with which He has described Himself and with which His prophets have described Him, even when the names of His attributes are shared with the attributes of others.

Likewise, He has names that may also be applied to others, such as compassionate, merciful, knowing, hearing, seeing, forbearing, patient, appreciative, powerful, giver of security, exalted, mighty, and great. Yet any actual resemblance or equivalence is denied, as in His words, Most High:

“There is nothing like Him, and He is the All-Hearing, the All-Seeing.”

This verse combines affirmation with declaring Him free of any likeness. The relation of His attributes to Him is like the relation of His creation to Him: one relation and attribution resembles the other. It is in this respect that His names and the names of His attributes are shared with others. Likewise, seeing Him has been compared to seeing the sun and moon—a comparison between the acts of seeing, not between the objects seen. Similarly, He has drawn an analogy between His relationship with His servants, whom He owns, and the relationship of some of His creatures with their slaves. His is the highest description in the heavens. So reflect on

---

## Page 1688

this, for it dispels doubts and filters away confusion. Thus, all the names and attributes we hear ascribed and attributed to Him are understood in a manner befitting Allah and appropriate to His Essence.

As for the other two groups: among those who liken Allah to His creation and equate Him with it are some who say, “A hand like my hand”—exalted is Allah above that! Those who deny and negate His attributes say, “The two Hands are the two blessings and the two powers.” Allah is indeed supremely great! In any event, all these groups agree that Adam possesses an excellence and distinction that no one else possesses, since Allah created him with His Hand.

(The third point:

This is counted among the blessings Allah bestowed upon Adam, as when Moses said to him:

“Allah created you with His Hand.”

The same will be said to him on the Day of Resurrection. They mention this among the blessings Allah bestowed uniquely upon him, distinguishing him from other created beings, rather than among those he shared with others. This is a clear explanation and proof of his superiority over all other created beings. As Zayd ibn Aslam related, Allah Most High said to the angels:

“I will not treat the righteous descendants of the one I created with My two Hands like those to whom I said, ‘Be,’ and they came into being.”

(The fourth proof:

Some of our companions have argued for the superiority of the prophets over the angels on the basis of His words:

“Indeed, Allah chose Adam and Noah, the family of Abraham and the family of Imrān above all beings.”

And His words:

“We chose them, with knowledge, above all beings.”

The expression “all beings” encompasses angels, jinn, and humans. Yet this calls for scrutiny, for, as regards the categories of beings, the term may refer to

---

## Page 1689

all categories of created beings, as in His words, Most High:

“Praise be to Allah, Lord of all beings.”

It may also refer only to human beings, in all their different categories, as in His words, Most High:

“Do you approach males among all beings?”

“Do you commit an obscenity that no one among all beings has ever committed before you?”

For they did not have intercourse with animals or jinn.

“All beings” may also refer to the people of a particular time, as in His words:

“We chose them, with knowledge, above all beings.”

Thus, His words:

“Indeed, Allah chose Adam and Noah, the family of Abraham and the family of Imrān”—to the end of the verse—

may encompass all categories of created beings, or may refer only to the descendants of Adam.

Someone citing this verse as evidence may say: The expression “all beings” encompasses all categories of created beings through which Allah is known, for they are signs of Him and indications pointing to Him—especially those among them who possess knowledge, such as the angels. The expression must therefore be understood in its general scope unless evidence requires restricting it.

His words have also been cited as evidence:

“We have certainly honoured the descendants of Adam”—to the end of the verse.

This, however, is a weak argument; rather, the verse indicates the opposite, as we have established.

(The fifth proof:

His words:

“I am placing a khalīfah on earth.”

This provides evidence for the superiority of the khalīfah in two respects:

“First”: the khalīfah is superior to those over whom he is appointed, and there were angels on earth. At most, however, this establishes his superiority over the angels who were on earth.

“Second”: the angels asked Allah Most High that

---

## Page 1690

the office of khalīfah be assigned to them and the khalīfah be one of them, as they said:

“Will You place in it someone who will spread corruption there and shed blood?”—to the end of the verse.

Had this office not been an exalted rank above their own ranks, they would not have sought it or wished to share the distinction of its holder.

The seventh proof (1): The descendants of Adam were shown to be superior to them through knowledge. When Allah, Mighty and Majestic, asked them about the names, they did not answer Him and acknowledged that they did not know them; Adam then informed them of those names. Allah Most High has said:

“Are those who know equal to those who do not know?”

The eighth proof (2), the first drawn from hadith, is what Hammād ibn Salamah narrated from Abū al-Muhazzim, from Abū Hurayrah, from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, who said:

“The passing away of this world is indeed of less consequence to Allah than the killing of a believing man, and a believer is more honoured in Allah’s sight than the angels who are with Him.”

This explicitly states that believers are more honoured in Allah’s sight than the angels brought near to Him. He then mentioned what al-Khallāl narrated:

“Abū Hurayrah said: The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, addressed us.” He related his words, at the end of which he said: “‘Come closer and make room for those behind you.’ The people came closer and gathered tightly together. A man asked, ‘Are we to make room for the angels or for people?’

“He replied, ‘For the angels. When they are with you, they are neither in front of you nor behind you, but to your right and left.’

“They asked, ‘Why are they not in front of us or behind us? Is it because we are superior to them, or because they are superior to us?’

“He replied, ‘Yes. You are superior to the angels.’”

> **Footnotes:**
> (1, 2) Thus in the original.

---

## Page 1691

Al-Khallāl narrated it, and it explicitly affirms the superiority of human beings over angels. However, the status of its chain of transmission is unknown, so its acceptance depends on the soundness of that chain.

Abdullāh ibn Ahmad narrated in Kitāb al-Sunnah (The Book of the Sunnah), from Urwah ibn Ruwaym, who said: “Al-Ansārī informed me, on the authority of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, that the angels said:

‘Our Lord, You created us and created the descendants of Adam. You enabled them to eat, drink, wear clothes, have relations with women, ride animals, sleep, and rest, while granting us none of these things. So grant them this world and grant us the Hereafter.’”

He related the hadith as traced back to the Prophet, whereas it was cited earlier through Zayd ibn Aslam, from his father, without being traced back to the Prophet.

Zayd ibn Aslam grew in knowledge, understanding of religion, and scrupulous piety, to the point that Alī ibn al-Husayn would leave the gatherings of his own people to attend his gathering. Al-Zuhrī reproached him for this, and he replied: “One sits only where one benefits”—or he said, “where one finds what sets one’s heart right.”

Some four hundred seekers of knowledge would attend his gathering. Their least virtue was to give away whatever worldly possessions they had, and none would keep things for himself at the expense of the others. Thus, he would make such a statement only on the basis of . . . (1) that was clear. Lying about Allah, Mighty and Majestic, is graver than lying about His Messenger.

At the very least, these reports show that the earliest generations of the righteous predecessors used to transmit among themselves that righteous human beings are superior to angels, without any objection from them, and no one disagreed

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) A blank in the original.
> Shaykh Nāsir ibn Hamad al-Fahd (p. 42) writes: “The wording appears to be illā ‘an ‘ilm bayyin (except on the basis of clear knowledge), or something similar.”

---

## Page 1692

among them on this point. Disagreement arose only after people became divided by their inclinations and their opinions diverged; this had been virtually settled among them.

The eleventh proof (1): Hadiths in which Allah boasts of His servants, such as:

“Allah Most High descends every night to the lowest heaven, and on the afternoon of Arafah, when He boasts to His angels of the pilgrims. Likewise, He boasts to them of those who pray, saying: ‘Look at My servants: they have fulfilled one obligation and are awaiting another.’”

Both hadiths are in Sahīh Muslim. Such boasting can only be about those who are superior.

If it is objected that these reports were transmitted by individual narrators who are not well known, and that the reports themselves are not sufficiently widespread to establish knowledge, whereas this is a matter concerning knowledge, we reply:

First, who says that the absolute standard in this matter is certainty whose opposite is impossible? Rather, a strong presumption is sufficient here, and that has been attained.

Furthermore, what is meant by saying that it “concerns knowledge”? Do you mean that there is no knowledge? That is conceded. Yet every sound understanding founded on evidence constitutes knowledge, even though some people reserve the term “knowledge” for certainty that cannot be overturned. Allah Most High has said:

“Then if you know them to be believers.”

This has been discussed fully elsewhere. If, however, calling it a matter “concerning knowledge” means that certainty is required, this is an empty assertion unsupported by evidence. Were it true, one would have to refrain from discussing any matter not established as knowledge except on the basis of certainty—a manifest inconsistency.

Furthermore, we say: these reports, taken as a whole, with some reinforcing others, and transmitted through

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) Thus in the original.

---

## Page 1693

different chains of transmission, may produce certainty for those experienced in the study of chains of transmission and well versed in hadith and its narrators. This is knowledge particular to their field, just as each group has its own field of knowledge. Their attaining knowledge does not entail that others attain it, unless those others acquire the knowledge by which they distinguish authentic hadiths from weak ones.

Knowledge, with its diverse kinds and differing characteristics, need not be shared by all rational people, especially knowledge conveyed through transmitted reports. Although some disputants claim that necessary knowledge must be shared by all, this is true of some instances of necessary knowledge, not all. Indeed, we allow that any instance of necessary knowledge could fail to be shared by all. In practice, however, some of it is commonly shared. Certain people mistakenly concluded that all of it must therefore be shared, and consequently denied much of the knowledge possessed specifically by others.

Furthermore, we say: even if we suppose that these reports do not yield knowledge, but only a strong presumption, or that what is required is certainty, our answer is that the requisite conclusion is established independently of these hadiths. They merely confirm and support it, so that different kinds of evidence converge in support of this position.

The twelfth proof (1): The early Muslim generations used to narrate hadiths indicating the superiority of righteous human beings over the angels, and these were narrated publicly. Had this been objectionable, they would have objected to it; this therefore indicates that they held that belief. Even if this does not yield conclusive certainty, some presumptions are at least strong

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) Thus in the original.

---

## Page 1694

and predominant; this may vary from person to person and according to their circumstances.

The thirteenth proof (1)—an inquiry that reveals the true nature of the issue—is as follows:

When comparing two things in respect of superiority, one must first identify the excellence in question, then consider which of them possesses the greater claim to it. Moreover, we are speaking only of the superiority of righteous human beings once they have attained perfection and reached their ultimate goal and fullest development. This occurs only when they enter Paradise, attain nearness to Allah, dwell in the highest ranks, and are greeted by the Most Merciful, who grants them special nearness to Himself and reveals Himself to them. They delight in gazing upon His noble Face, while the angels attend to their service by their Lord’s permission. Whoever investigates this matter should consider this carefully. Most of those who have erred, when comparing the two kinds of beings, have viewed the angels in their completeness and perfection, while viewing human beings in this lowly, troubled life, which is not worth a mosquito’s wing in Allah’s sight. This is not a fair comparison.

I therefore say: the superiority of one being over another lies only in its nearness to Allah, exalted is He, and in the greater distinction of His choosing and the grace of His selecting us, even though we do not comprehend the reality of this.

This is the general account. Superiority is also assessed according to qualities that are, in themselves, pure report and unalloyed perfection, such as life, knowledge, power, purity, cleanliness, wholesomeness, and freedom from deficiencies and faults. We shall therefore discuss these two forms of excellence:

(As for the first: Allah, exalted is He, created the Garden of Eden and planted it with His Hand, and He has not disclosed

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) Thus in the original.

---

## Page 1695

what it contains to any angel brought near or any prophet sent as a messenger. He said to it, “Speak,” and it replied, “The believers have indeed succeeded.”

This is reported in numerous hadiths, as is the statement that He looks upon it each night before dawn and that it is His abode. This, then, is the honour Allah, exalted is He, has prepared for His believing servants, which none of the angels has seen. It is known that those of higher rank can see those below them, but not the reverse. Nor can one object, “This also applies to the messengers,” for it was built specifically for them, although the time for them to dwell in it has not yet come. It stands prepared for them: they are advancing towards perfection and ascending to loftier ranks, and this is their recompense and reward. As for the angels, their present condition resembles their condition thereafter, for their reward is continuous, and Paradise was not created [for them] (*).

This is confirmed by His words, exalted is He:

“No soul knows what joy is kept hidden in store for them.”

The reality of what Allah has prepared for His close servants is therefore hidden from the angels. Earlier, He also concealed from them Adam’s condition at his initial creation and in other respects.

The superiority of Allah’s righteous servants demonstrates the superiority of an individual of their kind. For an individual of their kind, once their superiority over all beings and persons is established, the superiority of their kind over all kinds is established. It is impossible for a member of an inferior kind to rise so far as to surpass all individuals and all superior kinds, for this would alter their essential realities and divest beings of their intrinsic attributes. Nevertheless, such an individual may surpass some members of the superior kind, while

> **Footnotes:**
> (*) Shaykh Nāsir ibn Hamad al-Fahd states (p. 42):
> It appears that the wording should read: “Paradise was not created for them.”

---

## Page 1696

the latter retains the distinction conferred by the superiority of his kind and essential nature, just as some horses are better than other horses without being better than all horses.

With this understood, the scholars held in esteem and Allah’s accepted close servants have related that Muhammad, the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, will be seated by his Lord on the Throne with Him. Muhammad ibn Fudayl narrated this from Layth, from Mujāhid, in his interpretation of:

“It may be that your Lord will raise you to a praiseworthy station.”

This has also been related through other chains, some traced back to the Prophet and others not. Ibn Jarīr said: This does not contradict the widely reported hadiths stating that the praiseworthy station is intercession. According to the consensus of the imams, no one among those who profess and claim Islam holds that seating him on the Throne is objectionable (*)—only some of the Jahmiyyah have denied it, and mentioning it in the interpretation of the verse is not objectionable. If the superiority of the eminent individual among us over the eminent individual among them is established, then the superiority of one kind over the other is established: I mean the superiority of our righteous ones over them.

“As for the beings themselves”

Allah created Adam himself with His hand, created him in His image, and breathed into him of His spirit. This has not been established for any other being. This is a sea in which even a swimmer may drown; only someone strengthened by the light of guidance can venture into it. Otherwise, he falls into either likening Allah to His creation or denying His attributes. Let the person of understanding recognize that beyond the limits of his knowledge lies a far-distant goal, and above everyone possessed of knowledge is one more knowledgeable. Let him be utterly certain that what the prophetic traditions convey is true, both outwardly and inwardly, even if his intellect falls short of grasping it and his knowledge does not encompass it:

“By the Lord of heaven and earth, it is indeed true, just as surely as you speak.”

So do not enter the door of denial, approaching with withheld assent and closed eyes—in rejection

> **Footnotes:**
> (*) Shaykh Nāsir ibn Hamad al-Fahd states (p. 42):
> The correct reading is munkar (objectionable).

---

## Page 1697

of its outward meaning and in astonishment at its inner meaning—all to preserve the rules you devised through your own faculties and systematized according to principles that have kept you away from your Lord. Beware of asserting Allah’s transcendence in ways contrary to those of the earlier scholars, and guard against equating Him with His creation or likening Him to it. By my life, this is the straight path, sharper than a sword and finer than a hair. Whoever Allah does not grant light has no light.

As for the qualities in which they differ in excellence, these include everlasting life and eternal existence in the Hereafter; an angel has no more than this. Although this life of ours is marred by death, I have already explained that the comparison is made only after both natures have attained perfection, when nothing remains but enduring existence. Other such qualities include the knowledge that distinguishes the angels.

To this we say: There is no denying that each kind possesses knowledge that the other does not, for revelation reaches the messengers in various ways, as Allah Most High says:

“It is not for a human being that Allah should speak to him except through revelation, or from behind a veil, or by sending a messenger to reveal, by His permission, whatever He wills.”

He thus explains that He speaks to human beings in three ways: one involves an angel as intermediary, while in the other two the angel conveys no revelation. What part did the angel have in the night of the Ascension, the day at the Mount, the teaching of the names, and so much else? Even if it were established that human knowledge in this world comes only through the angels—which, by Allah, is false—what would they make of the Day of Resurrection, when the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, has said:

---

## Page 1698

“Allah will then grant me forms of praise and glorification of Him, inspiring me with words He has never granted anyone before me.”

Once this is clear—that knowledge is apportioned by Allah, rather than conveyed exclusively through the angels in all cases, as this fool claims without knowledge; that, instead, the Quran shows that Allah Most High singled out Adam for knowledge the angels did not possess, namely knowledge of the names, which is the noblest of all knowledge; and that He declared Adam superior to them because of his greater knowledge—why turn away from this clear point into side paths?

Another such quality is power. Some have claimed that an angel is stronger and more capable, citing the account of Gabriel: that he is mighty in strength and carried the town of Lot’s people on a single feather of his wing. Yet Allah has granted some of His servants something greater than that: He drowned all the inhabitants of the earth in response to Noah’s supplication. The Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“Among Allah’s servants are those who, if they swore an oath by Allah, He would fulfil it for them.”

“Many a dishevelled, dust-covered man, turned away from people’s doors, would have his oath fulfilled by Allah if he swore by Him.”

This applies generally to all things. Reports explaining this state that among Allah’s servants are those who, if they swore by Allah that He would remove a mountain—or the mountains—from their places, He would remove them; and if they swore that He would not bring about the Resurrection, He would not bring it about. This is hyperbole.

Nor should it be said that the former excels through strength created within him, whereas the latter does so through a supplication he makes. In reality, both ultimately amount to the same thing: the intended outcome of power, the object of strength, and that on account of

---

## Page 1699

which the strong is preferred over the weak. Suppose, then, that this holds in this world; how will you account for the Hereafter?

It is related in a report:

“O My servant, I say to a thing, ‘Be,’ and it is. Obey Me, and I will enable you to say to a thing, ‘Be,’ and it will be. O My servant, I am the Living One who never dies. Obey Me, and I will make you live without ever dying.”

Another report states:

“The believer receives gifts from Allah: ‘From the Living One who never dies to the living one who never dies.’”

This is the ultimate goal, beyond which there is nothing to aspire to. How could it be otherwise, when through Allah he hears, sees, strikes, and walks? No strength can withstand his.

As for purity, blamelessness, sanctity, freedom from shortcomings and faults, and complete obedience devoted exclusively to Allah—unaccompanied by disobedience, inadvertence, or heedlessness, with all their deeds and words conforming to His command—someone has asked: “How can human beings possess these qualities?” In truth, these qualities are the grounds of superiority, as has been said:

“I prize nothing as highly as soundness.”

The answer has several aspects.

First: If we consider these qualities in the Hereafter, believers will possess them there in their most perfect and complete form. We have already explained that the discussion concerns their superiority not merely in this life, but when they attain perfection and completeness and settle in the abode of true life. This provides a conclusive answer to every argument of this kind. How, then, do they compare with people whose faces are like the moon and like the sun, who neither urinate, blow their noses, nor spit, and who have not the slightest fault or imperfection?

Second: This very fact is itself proof of the superiority of the human being, whereas the angels

---

## Page 1700

are created with a single disposition and a fixed nature from which they cannot depart, whereas human beings are otherwise.

(Third aspect:

The slips and lapses committed by righteous human beings become a means of raising their ranks and turning their evil deeds into good deeds. Allah loves those who repent and those who purify themselves. Some commit an evil deed that becomes a cause of their entering Paradise. Were pardon not dearer to Him, those of His creation whom He most honors would not have been tried with sin. The same applies to His joy at His servants’ repentance and His laughter when a servant knows that none but Allah forgives sins. Understand this, for it is among the mysteries of divine lordship, and through it the reason why those brought near to Him fall into sin becomes clear.

(Fourth aspect:

It is related:

“When the angels regarded the sins of the children of Adam as grievous, Allah Most High instilled desire in some of them, and they fell into sin.”

This is an argument Allah Most High presents against the angels. As for worship, it has been argued that the angels are continually engaged in worship and glorification: some stand and never sit; some sit and never stand; some bow and never prostrate; and some prostrate and never bow.

“They glorify Him night and day without growing weary.”

The answer is that superiority depends on the deed itself and its quality, not its extent or quantity, as Allah Most High says:

“We have made whatever is on the earth an adornment for it, so that We may test them as to which of them is best in deeds.”

He also says:

“We do not let the reward of anyone who does good deeds go to waste.”

A single utterance of glorification by a human being may be better than an earth full of another’s deeds. Each day, deeds were raised on Idrīs’s behalf equivalent to the deeds of all the inhabitants of

---

## Page 1701

the earth; indeed, two men may stand in the same prayer row, yet the difference in the reward for their prayers may be as great as the distance between heaven and earth.

It has also been related:

“The groaning of sinners is dearer to Me than the resounding voices of those who glorify Me.”

They have also said that scholars among human beings are better and more excellent despite the presence of opposing and countervailing factors. Moreover, in this worldly life and in the Hereafter, they are inspired to glorify Allah just as they are inspired to breathe. As for benefits extending to others, benefiting creation, and governing the world, they have argued that people’s provisions pass through the angels’ hands; that the angels descend with knowledge and revelation, guard and uphold, and perform their other activities.

The answer is that righteous human beings have the same merits and more. Sufficient evidence of this is the intercession of the one whose intercession is accepted: his intercession for sinners, his intercession for humankind so that they may be brought to account, and his intercession for the people of Paradise so that they may enter it.

Only after this does the angels’ intercession take place. How can they compare with the one addressed in His words:

“We have sent you only as a mercy to the worlds”?

And how can they compare with those who:

“Give others preference over themselves, even though they themselves are in need”?

How can they compare with those who call to guidance and the religion of truth, or with those who establish a good practice? How can they compare in light of his saying, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“Among my community are those who will intercede for more people than Rabīʿah and Mudar”?

And how can they compare with the aqtāb, awtād, aghwāth, abdāl, and nujabāʾ? (1).

This, then—may Allah guide you—is the basis for superiority according to the known grounds; we have offered an example

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) Thus in the original.

---

## Page 1702

by which we have marked out the path and opened the door to understanding the virtues of the righteous. Whoever reflects on this and is granted a share of it will discern beyond it what none but Allah can enumerate. Only those whose grasp of discourse and knowledge is confined to outward appearances, and whose grasp of realities extends no further than their outlines, have turned away from this. They have fallen into innovations and specious arguments, and lost their way among positions and pathways. We shall now set out the arguments they have advanced.

(The first argument:

His words, Most High:

“The Messiah will never disdain to be a servant of Allah, nor will the angels brought near to Him.”

Someone wishing to demonstrate the submission of the mighty and the obedience of the great begins with those of lower rank, progressively ascending to those of higher rank, so that the listener may understand, step by step, the greatness of the one to whom they submit and whom they obey. Otherwise, if the listener were confronted at the outset with the submission of the greatest, the successive degrees of greatness would not become clear. Mentioning those of lesser rank afterward would be pointless; indeed, it would be a backward step and a diminution.

Thus, people naturally say: “So-and-so does not come to me, yet so-and-so does”—meaning: “How can he disdain to come to me, when someone nobler and greater than he comes to me?” One does not say: “So-and-so does not refuse to honor you, nor does someone above him.” The progression from the Messiah to the angels therefore proves their superiority, especially since they are described as being near to Allah, which is the very essence of excellence.

The answer:

The judge maintained that this is not a case of conjoining the higher-ranking with the lower-ranking; rather, it is simple conjunction.

He said:

This is because some people worshipped the Messiah and claimed that he was the son of Allah—glory be to Him—while others worshipped the angels and claimed that they were Allah’s daughters, as Allah Most High related

---

## Page 1703

concerning both groups. Allah Most High thus made clear here: “Those whom you worshipped besides Me are My servants; they will never disdain to worship Me. Were they both to disdain to worship Me, I would subject them both to a painful punishment.” The Messiah is the visible one, and he belongs to humankind. This explanation, however, is open to question. Allah knows best its true meaning.

We then say:

If this is what is intended, there is nothing to dispute. But if the intended meaning is a progression from the lower to the higher, then know—may Allah illuminate your heart and open your breast to Islam—that the angels possess distinctive qualities which human beings do not, especially in this world. No discerning person doubts this: at present, they occupy an exalted station, are closer to Allah, more prominent in bodily form, greater in character, more beautiful in appearance, longer-lived, and more blessed in their effects, besides other praiseworthy qualities, some of which we know and others we do not. Human beings, too, possess distinctive qualities and advantages.

The question, however, concerns these two sets of advantages considered as a whole: which is superior?

This approach lays the groundwork for understanding this verse and what follows it. Yet the question goes beyond this. Wherever something entails the superiority of angels, it is because of matters in which they are distinguished and which are peculiar to them. Those beneath them in such matters should not claim superiority over them on grounds connected with those very distinctions.

Thus, if the Messiah were hypothetically to disdain to worship Allah, this could only be on account of the signs with which Allah supported him—such as healing those born blind and those afflicted with leprosy, raising the dead, and other such things—and because his creation differed from that of the other descendants of Adam, and he was granted asceticism in his turning away from this world and all it contains. Yet there is not one of these qualities in which the angels do not excel him, for they were all created from

---

## Page 1704

no parents, nor from a mother. The earth over which Gabriel’s horse passed would come to life through it, and knowing what people store in their homes is easy for the angels. In the hadith concerning a leper, a bald man, and a blind man, it is related: “The angel passed his hand over them, and they were healed.”

Thus, the angels possess a fuller, higher, and greater share than the Messiah of these qualities on account of which he was worshipped and declared the son of Allah, Mighty and Majestic. Yet they do not disdain to worship Him, so he has all the more reason, as a created being, not to disdain it. As for nearness to Allah and an honoured place with Him, these are matters beyond such miraculous signs. Moreover, at most, the verse establishes their superiority to the Messiah while he is in this worldly life. But once he has taken his place in the Hereafter, and what I shall leave unmentioned has come to pass, on what grounds can it be said that they are superior to him there?

(The second argument:

Allah Most High says to His Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“Say, ‘I do not tell you that I possess Allah’s treasuries, nor do I know the unseen, nor do I tell you that I am an angel.’”

A similar statement occurs in Surah Hūd. This is adduced as evidence in several ways:

First: He grouped possession of Allah’s treasuries and knowledge of the unseen with the denial that he claimed to be an angel, disclaiming all three in a single sequence. If the condition of one who knows the unseen and has power over the treasuries is superior to that of one who does not, then the condition of an angel must be superior to that of someone who is not an angel—even if that person is our Prophet, as in this verse.

Second: He denied himself only a condition greater than the condition he actually possessed, and did not deny himself a condition

---

## Page 1705

lower than his own; for whoever possesses the higher quality is more capable of what lies below it. This therefore indicates that an angel’s condition is superior to his condition of being an angel, which is the point to be proved.

Third: Al-Qādī mentioned that, were it not for the settled conviction of those addressed that an angel is greater, it would not have been appropriate to address them by disclaiming something beneath his rank. They were allowed to retain this belief, and he did not challenge it; it is therefore established as true.

The answer has several aspects:

(First: He denied knowing the unseen and possessing Allah’s treasuries, and denied being an angel who neither eats nor drinks nor enjoys pleasures. His denial of these things does not necessarily mean that an angel is superior to him. Do you not see that, had he said, “Nor am I a writer or a reader,” this would not indicate that someone who writes and reads is superior to someone who does neither? Thus, the verse provides no proof for this argument.

There is also what Al-Qādī said: they demanded the attributes of divinity—knowledge, power, and self-sufficiency—that is, knowing all things, having power over all things, and needing nothing. He therefore disclaimed the attributes of divinity. This is why they said:

“What is the matter with this messenger, who eats food and walks in the marketplaces?”

Allah Most High said in his defence:

“We did not send any messengers before you but that they ate food and walked in the marketplaces.”

It is as though they wanted him to possess the characteristic of the angels—that he should be

---

## Page 1706

endowed with that characteristic. For angels have no hollow interiors and neither eat nor drink, whereas human beings have hollow interiors and eat and drink. Thus, the matter concerned this characteristic. This is clear, Allah willing.

(Second: The other is more accomplished in a particular respect, so he denied possessing an angel’s condition in that respect. This does not necessarily mean that the angel possesses a distinguishing superiority over him. Something similar has already been discussed concerning the angel’s condition and greatness, and the fact that human beings possess nothing comparable of the same kind. But why not say that human beings possess something of a different kind that is superior to it?

Thus, when someone is asked to do something beyond his ability, he may say, “I am not an angel,” even though a believer is superior in standing to the jinn and to a king among kings.

(Third: At most, this establishes the angel’s superiority in that particular state. Even if this were conceded, it would not rule out his becoming superior to the angel thereafter. Hence his ability, knowledge, and freedom from need increase in the Hereafter. This is like a child saying, “I do not claim to be an elder, nor do I claim to be a scholar,” although he may advance to those ranks and beyond them.

(The third argument: The words of Iblīs to Adam and Eve:

“Unless you become angels or become immortal.”

The implied meaning is “out of aversion to your becoming so” or “lest you become so.” Had their being angels not been a more perfect state than their being human, he would not have tempted them with it, nor would they have thought it the higher state. For this reason, he coupled it with immortality. One who lives forever is superior to one who perishes, and an angel lives longer than a human being; thus, his worship would be greater, and he would be superior to a human being.

---

## Page 1707

The answer has several aspects:

First: As al-Qādī explained, his words,

“Unless you become angels,”

expressed his assumption that angels were better than the two of them, just as he assumed that he was better than Adam—and he was mistaken. His words,

“Or become immortal,”

were based on his assumption that they would prefer immortality because it entails freedom from illnesses, sicknesses, pains, afflictions, and death. Such is the condition of anyone who lives eternally in Paradise. This was not a statement of superiority over the prophets. Do you not see that the maidens and youths created in Paradise live there eternally, yet are not superior to the prophets?

Second: An angel is superior in certain respects, and immortality was likewise more desirable to them, so they inclined towards it.

Third: Their condition at that time was an initial state, not their final state. For in their final state they have attained immortality, with no restriction inherent in it or accompanying it, and no cessation thereafter. Likewise, in their final state they reach a condition superior and more perfect than the angelic condition they initially sought. This is clear.

The fourth argument: Allah Most High says:

“Allah chooses messengers from among the angels and from among humankind.”

He mentioned the angels first, and one begins only with the best and noblest, followed by the next best and noblest, just as He did in His words:

“Those will be with the ones upon whom Allah has bestowed favour: the prophets, the utterly truthful, the martyrs, and the righteous.”

Thus, He began with the most perfect and the best.

---

## Page 1708

The answer is that what is mentioned first is often not the best. Rather, something may be mentioned first for various reasons, as in the words of Allah Most High:

“And when We took from the prophets their covenant, and from you, and from Noah and Abraham.”

This does not indicate that Noah is superior to Abraham, while the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, is superior. Likewise, His words:

“Indeed, the Muslim men and Muslim women, the believing men and believing women”

do not indicate that a Muslim is superior to a believer. Perhaps—and Allah knows best—He mentioned the angels first because they preceded humankind in creation and in being sent as messengers, for they were sent to both jinn and humankind. Thus, He mentioned them in their successive order of creation and mission, according to the order in which they came into existence.

Allah Most High also says:

“He grants daughters to whom He wills, and grants sons to whom He wills.”

Yet males are superior to females. He also says:

“By the fig and the olive,”

and:

“By the sun and its brightness,”

and the verses that follow; and:

“In both are fruit, date palms, and pomegranates,”

among other examples. In none of these passages does being mentioned first indicate the superiority of what is mentioned first. It is therefore clear that precedence in mention does not necessarily imply superiority.

(The fifth argument: His words, Most High:

“When they saw him, they were astounded by him and cut their hands, and said, ‘Allah forbid! This is no human being; this is none other than a noble angel.’”

This indicates that an angel is superior to a human being. They only intended to identify a condition greater than that of human beings.

Two answers have been given to this argument. The first is that they did not believe that angels were more beautiful than all the prophets, even though they had not seen them, on the word of an informant

---

## Page 1709

who had informed them and whose report they had accepted. When his beauty overwhelmed them, they said:

“This is no human being; this is none other than a noble angel.”

For such beauty is not a human attribute.

The second answer is that they believed angels to be better than the prophets, and this belief of theirs was mistaken. It cannot be argued that, because it was not accompanied by a rebuke, it must be true. Their statement,

“This is no human being,”

was mistaken. Their statement,

“This is none other than a noble angel,”

was also mistaken, in their denying (*) that he was human and asserting that he was an angel. Even if it was not accompanied by a rebuke, this indicates that it is true and that their statement,

“This is no human being; this is none other than a noble angel,”

was mistaken in denying his humanity and asserting that he was an angel. Even if it was not accompanied by a rebuke, this was because they had lost their senses upon seeing him, and so they were not blamed for it in that state.

I also say that the women did not mean that he was a prophet, nor even that he was among the righteous at that time. They did not attest to his superiority over other human beings in righteousness and religion; they attested only to his superiority in beauty and loveliness. His beauty captivated them, so they likened him to the angels. This has nothing to do with superiority in the sense we intend.

We then say: If superiority in beauty is indeed established, it has been established that the first group of the inhabitants of Paradise will enter with faces like the sun, and those who follow them with faces like the moon—as the hadith states. Such is the ultimate condition of the blessed. If there is any superiority in beauty and angelic nature, it is only in this worldly life, according to the knowledge possessed by the women and most people.

> **Footnotes:**
> (*) Shaykh Nāsir ibn Hamad al-Fahd states (p. 42, footnote):
> “Thus in the text. This is a scribal error; the correct reading is nafyihinna (their denial).”

---

## Page 1710

As for the distinction Allah has bestowed upon His righteous servants and the honor He has prepared for them, most people are far removed from these matters and give them no consideration. The same applies to the knowledge Allah bestowed upon them from the very beginning of their creation—knowledge the angels wished to share, and one of the grounds of their superiority. This is the answer, together with the preceding one.

(The sixth argument:
Allah Most High says:

“Indeed, this is the word of a noble messenger,
endowed with strength, established in honor with the Lord of the Throne,
obeyed there and trustworthy.”

These are descriptions of Gabriel. Then He says:

“Your companion is not mad.”

He described Gabriel as noble, entrusted with a message, strong, established in honor with Him, obeyed, and trustworthy. Having described him with these excellent attributes, He followed this with His words:

“Your companion is not mad.”

He identified the human messenger as our companion, denied that he was mad, affirmed that he had seen Gabriel, and cleared him of both withholding and suspicion. There is a vast disparity here between human beings and angels, and between their attributes and the favors bestowed upon them. This argument was advanced by one of the Muʿtazilites, and through it he strayed from the straight path.

The answer:

First, what does he make of His words:

“Have We not opened your breast for you?”

—and the rest of the surah—and His words:

“By the morning brightness,
and by the night when it grows still”?

And His words:

“Indeed, We have granted you a clear victory”

—and the verses that follow—and:

“Your Lord may raise you to a station of praise”?

And what does he make of the account of the Night Ascension, in which Gabriel remained behind while the Prophet advanced to his station? Then what does he make of intimate friendship with Allah, which is being drawn near to Him? This is the contention of someone who has not accorded the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, his rightful esteem.

---

## Page 1711

Secondly, we say:

Gabriel was the one who brought the message and conveyed the revelation, yet he was unseen by the people: they had neither seen him with their eyes nor heard his speech with their ears. Some claimed that the one who came to the Prophet was a devil teaching him what to say, or that a human being was teaching it to him. Allah therefore told His servants about the messenger who brought it, describing him in the finest terms and explaining his standing with the utmost clarity. All of this honored Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, refuted their allegations against him, and affirmed his message. For Gabriel was his companion who brought him the revelation. Thus Allah said:

“Indeed, this is the word of a noble messenger.”

That is, the human messenger did not utter it of his own accord; he merely conveyed it, saying what he had been told. The designation “messenger” thus indicated that his role was solely that of an intermediary acting on another’s behalf. Allah then described Gabriel with attributes that ruled out every flaw: strength, an established position, trustworthiness, and nearness to Allah, glorified be He. Once the standing of the angelic messenger had been established, He made clear that he came from Him and brought nothing but good.

The human messenger’s outward character was already known to them, and he was the one who conveyed the message to them. Were it not for these human messengers, they would have been unable to receive the message from the angelic messenger.

He said:

“Your companion”

to indicate that he had lived among them for years before this, with no history of the things they now alleged and accused him of—madness, sorcery, and the like. It also indicated that, had it not been for his prior life among them and his companionship with them, they would have been unable to receive from him.

Do you not hear Him say:

---

## Page 1712

“Had We made him an angel, We would have made him a man”

—as a distinction—among the messengers. He then established the truth of his message by affirming that he had seen Gabriel and was trustworthy concerning what he received from him. These two qualities thus established his mission in the most effective, complete, and fitting manner. They have also cited verses whose intended meanings have already been explained, describing the angels’ glorification of Allah, obedience, worship, and other qualities.

(The seventh argument:

The well-known, authentic hadith in which Allah, Almighty and Majestic, says:

“Whoever remembers Me within himself, I remember him within Myself; and whoever remembers Me in an assembly, I remember him in an assembly better than it.”

The assembly in which Allah mentions the one who remembers Him consists of:

(the angels. The hadith explicitly states that they are superior to, and better than, the assembly in which the servant remembers his Lord. Some have said: How many assemblies have there been in which Allah was remembered while the Messenger was present! Indeed, this occurred in the gatherings of all the messengers. How, then, can this authentic hadith be set aside?

The answer:

This hadith is authentic, and it is the best and strongest evidence they have cited. Two answers have been offered to it, one weaker than the other.

The weaker answer is that “better” may refer to the remembrance rather than to those among whom the mention is made. The intended meaning would then be: “I mention him with a remembrance better than his remembrance,” because Allah’s remembrance is His speech.

This argument has no merit, however, for khayr (“better”) is a genitive adjective qualifying “assembly.” Moreover, He attached to it the expression minhum (“than them”), not minhu (“than it”). Had that meaning not been intended, the wording would have been: “I mention him in an assembly with a remembrance better

---

## Page 1713

than it,” with “better” in the accusative and the pronoun referring to the remembrance. This is perfectly clear to anyone who understands Arabic. We seek refuge in Allah from pedantic excess.

The second answer takes the hadith to mean an assembly better than a gathering in which no prophet is present. The hadith, however, is deliberately general and all-inclusive. How could it be otherwise, when the prophets and the righteous friends of Allah are people of remembrance, and their gatherings are gatherings of mercy? How, then, could they be excluded?

There are, however, several plausible explanations here:

First: “the highest assembly,” among whom Allah mentions those who remember Him, consists of the choicest and finest angels. Moreover, it is Allah who mentions the servant among them. It may be said that the comparison should be drawn with the finest descendants of Adam gathered in the assembly of His Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Although he is the finest of humankind, those around him are not the finest of all the other virtuous human beings, for the messengers and prophets are superior to them.

Second: even if an earthly gathering includes a group of prophets among whom the servant remembers his Lord, Allah Most High mentions the servant among groups of angels more numerous than those present. Their being better would thus be due to their overwhelming numbers, against which nothing can compare; for the more numerous a group is, the better it is than a smaller one.

Third: perhaps the highest assembly includes a group of prophets among whom Allah mentions the servant, for their souls are there.

---

## Page 1714

Fourth: some people distinguish between “better” and “superior,” using “better” to mean more beneficial.

Fifth: the hadith indicates that the highest assembly is superior to these people who remember Allah only in this world and in their present condition. They have not yet attained perfection or become fit to surpass the highest assembly. Thus, the highest assembly is better than them in this condition, just as a wise elder is better than children generally, because he then possesses virtues that the children lack. Yet among those children there may be someone who will eventually far surpass him. Our discussion concerns only the ultimate outcome and final state. Let this be carefully considered, for it is a sound answer, if Allah wills.

Allah, glorified is He, knows best the true nature of His creatures and which of them are most excellent, and He is wisest in His governance of them. There is no power nor strength except through Allah. These are the remarks I have been able to set down in the time available, while in haste. Allah is the One whose help is sought, and it is He whom we ask to guide our hearts and direct our tongues and hands aright. All praise is due to Allah, Lord of all worlds.

---

## Page 1715

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked:

“Which of the two Mothers of the Believers, Khadījah and ‘Ā’ishah, is superior?”

He replied:

Khadījah’s precedence, her contribution in the early days of Islam, and her support and service to the religion were qualities in which neither ‘Ā’ishah nor any other Mother of the Believers shared. ‘Ā’ishah’s contribution in the later period of Islam, her preservation of the religion and its transmission to the Muslim community, and her attainment of knowledge in which neither Khadījah nor anyone else shared were among the qualities that distinguished her from others.

---

## Page 1716

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—also said:

Section

The best women of this community are “Khadījah,” “‘Ā’ishah,” and “Fātimah.” There is disagreement over their relative superiority, and the matter involves distinctions that this is not the place to discuss. Khadījah and ‘Ā’ishah were among his wives.

If, on this basis, it is said that his “wives,” taken collectively, are superior to his “daughters,” taken collectively, that is correct: his wives were more numerous, and there were more women of distinction among them than among his daughters.

---

## Page 1717

Shaykh al-Islām also said:

Section

As for “the wives of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace,” no one except Abū Muhammad ibn Hazm claimed that they were superior to the ten. This is an anomalous view in which no one preceded him. Those leading scholars to whom it was reported rejected it, and the texts of the Quran and Sunnah refute it. The argument he advanced is unsound.

He argued that a woman shares her husband’s rank in Paradise, and that the rank of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, is the highest of all ranks; therefore, his wives would share his rank. Yet his argument would necessarily imply that his wives are superior to all the prophets; that the wife of every man among the people of Paradise is superior to anyone equal to her husband; and that the youths who attend upon the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and the wide-eyed maidens married to him are superior to the prophets and messengers. All believers know these conclusions to be false.

It is established in an authentic hadith that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“‘Ā’ishah’s superiority over other women is like the superiority of tharīd (bread soaked in broth) over other foods.”

He thus mentioned her superiority over women alone. It has also been established

---

## Page 1718

in an authentic report from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, that he said:

“Many men have attained perfection, but only a few women have attained it—either two or four.”

Most of his wives are not among those few. The hadiths establishing the superiority of the Companions, such as his saying, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“If I were to take an intimate friend from among the people of the earth, I would take Abū Bakr as an intimate friend,”

indicate that no one on earth, whether man or woman, is superior to Abū Bakr in his estimation. Likewise, it is authentically reported that ‘Alī said: “The best of this nation after its Prophet is Abū Bakr, then ‘Umar.” Other texts also establish this, but there is not enough space here to cite them.

In short, this is an anomalous view that none of the early generations held. Despite Abū Muhammad’s extensive knowledge, profound learning, and the immensely valuable insights he offers, some of his objectionable, anomalous opinions are as astonishing as his excellent and outstanding views. Examples include his assertions that Mary was a prophetess, that Āsiyah was a prophetess, and that the mother of Moses was a prophetess.

Judge Abū Bakr, Judge Abū Ya‘lā, Abū al-Ma‘ālī, and others have reported a consensus that no woman has been a prophetess. The Quran and Sunnah both indicate this, as in His words:

“We sent before you only men, to whom We revealed, from among the people of the towns.”

And His words:

“The Messiah, son of Mary, was only a messenger; messengers had passed away before him. His mother was a woman of consummate truthfulness.”

He stated that the highest rank his mother attained was that of consummate truthfulness. This has been discussed at length elsewhere.

---

## Page 1719

Shaykh al-Islām said:

Section:

As for Abū Bakr and al-Khidr, their relative merit depends on whether al-Khidr was a prophet. Most scholars hold that he was not a prophet. This is the view adopted by Abū ‘Alī ibn Abī Mūsā and other scholars. On this view, Abū Bakr and ‘Umar are superior to him.

The second view is that he was a prophet. Abū al-Faraj ibn al-Jawzī and others adopted this view. On this view, he is superior to Abū Bakr. However, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and Jesus, son of Mary, are both superior to him by unanimous agreement. Muhammad is at the beginning of this nation, and Jesus at its end.

---

## Page 1720

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was also asked about two men who disagreed. One said:

“Abū Bakr al-Siddīq and ‘Umar ibn al-Khattāb—may Allah be pleased with them both—are more learned and have a deeper understanding of religion than ‘Alī ibn Abī Tālib—may Allah be pleased with him.”

The other said:

“Rather, ‘Alī ibn Abī Tālib is more learned and has a deeper understanding of religion than Abū Bakr and ‘Umar.”

Which of these two views is more correct? And are these two hadiths authentic: the saying of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace,

“The best judge among you is ‘Alī,”

and his saying,

“I am the city of knowledge, and ‘Alī is its gate”?

If they are authentic, do they establish that ‘Alī was more learned and had a deeper understanding of religion than Abū Bakr and ‘Umar—may Allah be pleased with them all?

If someone claims that the Muslims unanimously agree that ‘Alī—may Allah be pleased with him—was more learned and had a deeper understanding of religion than Abū Bakr and ‘Umar—may Allah be pleased with them all—is his claim correct or mistaken?

He replied:

All praise is due to Allah.

None of the recognized Muslim scholars has said that ‘Alī was more learned and had a deeper understanding of religion than Abū Bakr and ‘Umar, or even than Abū Bakr alone. Anyone who claims a consensus on this is among the most ignorant and dishonest of people. Indeed, several scholars have reported a scholarly consensus that Abū Bakr al-Siddīq was more learned than ‘Alī. Among them was Imam Mansūr ibn ‘Abd al-Jabbār al-Sam‘ānī al-Marwadhī, one of the leading Sunni scholars among the followers of al-Shāfi‘ī, who mentioned in his book, “Taqwīm al-adillah ‘alā al-imām,”

---

## Page 1721

the consensus of Sunni scholars that Abū Bakr was more learned than ‘Alī. I know of none of the renowned imams who disputes this. How could it be otherwise, when Abū Bakr al-Siddīq would issue fatwas, give commands and prohibitions, pass judgments, and deliver addresses in the presence of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace? He did so when he and the Prophet went out to call people to Islam, when they emigrated together, on the day of Hunayn, and on other occasions. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, remained silent, approving his actions and accepting what he said. No one else held this position.

When the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, consulted those of his Companions who possessed knowledge, religious understanding, and sound judgment, he gave precedence to Abū Bakr and ‘Umar.
