These two took precedence over all his other Companions in speaking and in matters of knowledge in the Messenger’s presence, peace be upon him. This was the case, for example, when he consulted them about the captives of Badr: Abū Bakr and ‘Umar were the first to speak. The same was true on other occasions.

It is reported in a hadith that he said to them:

“If you both agree on a matter, I will not oppose you.”

For this reason, their joint opinion constitutes authoritative evidence according to one of the two scholarly views, which is also one of the two positions reported from Ahmad. This is not the case with the opinion of ‘Uthmān and ‘Alī. In the Sunan collections, it is reported that he said:

“Follow the example of the two who come after me: Abū Bakr and ‘Umar.”

He did not accord this distinction to anyone else. Rather, it is authentically established that he said:

“Adhere to my sunnah and the sunnah of the rightly guided caliphs after me. Hold fast to it and bite firmly upon it with your molars, and beware of newly introduced

---

## Page 1722

matters, for every innovation is misguidance.”

He thus commanded that the sunnah of the rightly guided caliphs be followed, which includes all four caliphs. He singled out Abū Bakr and ‘Umar, however, as examples to be followed. One whose example is followed both in his actions and in the practices he institutes for Muslims holds a higher standing than one followed only in the practices he institutes.

In Sahīh Muslim, it is reported that the Prophet’s Companions were travelling with him when he said:

“If the people obey Abū Bakr and ‘Umar, they will be rightly guided.”

It is authentically established that Ibn ‘Abbās would issue fatwas according to the Book of Allah. If he found no answer there, he would turn to the sunnah established by the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. If he found no answer there either, he would issue a fatwa according to the opinion of Abū Bakr and ‘Umar. He did not do this with the opinions of ‘Uthmān and ‘Alī. Ibn ‘Abbās was the great scholar of this community and, in his time, the most knowledgeable of the Companions and the most learned in religious law. Yet he issued fatwas according to the opinions of Abū Bakr and ‘Umar, giving their opinions precedence over those of the other Companions. It is authentically established that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“O Allah, grant him understanding of the religion and teach him interpretation.”

Furthermore, Abū Bakr and ‘Umar enjoyed greater closeness to the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, than anyone else, and Abū Bakr was the closer of the two. He would spend much of the night conversing with him about knowledge, religion, and the interests of the Muslims. As Abū Bakr ibn Abī Shaybah reported: Abū Mu‘āwiyah narrated to us, from al-A‘mash, from Ibrāhīm, from ‘Alqamah, from ‘Umar, who said:

“The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, would converse at night at Abū Bakr’s home about matters concerning the Muslims, and I would be with him.”

In the two Sahīh collections, it is reported from ‘Abd al-Rahmān ibn Abī Bakr that the Companions of the Suffa were

---

## Page 1723

poor people, and that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“‘Whoever has enough food for two should take a third person with him, and whoever has enough for four should take a fifth or a sixth.’ Abū Bakr brought three people home, while the Prophet of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, took ten. Abū Bakr ate supper with the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, then stayed until I had prayed ‘Ishā’. He then returned and stayed until the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, became drowsy. He came home after as much of the night had passed as Allah willed. His wife asked, ‘What kept you from your guests?’ He replied, ‘Did you not give them supper?’ She said, ‘They refused to eat until you came. Supper was offered to them, but they would not yield.’”

He then related the rest of the hadith. Another version states:

“He would converse with the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, into the night.”

On the journey of emigration, no one accompanied him except Abū Bakr. On the day of Badr, no one remained with him in the shelter except Abū Bakr. He said:

“No one has been more generous to us with his companionship and wealth than Abū Bakr. If I were to take anyone on earth as an intimate friend, I would take Abū Bakr.”

This is among the most authentic, widely transmitted hadiths, reported through numerous chains in the authentic collections.

In the two Sahīh collections, Abū al-Dardā’ said:

“I was sitting with the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, when Abū Bakr approached, holding up the edge of his garment so that his knee was exposed. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said, ‘Your companion has taken the initiative.’ Abū Bakr greeted him and said, ‘Something happened between me and Ibn al-Khattāb, and I spoke hastily to him. Then I regretted it and asked him to forgive me, but he refused, so I have come to you.’ He said, ‘May Allah forgive you,’ three times. Then ‘Umar regretted what he had done and went to Abū Bakr’s house, but did not find him. He therefore came to the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. The Prophet’s expression began to change, and he grew angry, until

---

## Page 1724

Abū Bakr grew apprehensive and said twice, ‘I was more in the wrong, O Messenger of Allah.’ The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said, ‘Allah sent me to you, and you said, “You are lying,” while Abū Bakr said, “You are telling the truth.” He supported me with his person and his wealth. Will you not leave my companion alone for my sake? Will you not leave my companion alone for my sake?’” He was never mistreated after that. Al-Bukhārī said: “Ghāmara means ‘he took the lead in doing good.’”

In the two Sahīh collections, Ibn ‘Abbās said:

“‘Umar was placed on his bier, and people gathered around him, supplicating, praising him, and praying over him before he was carried away. I was among them. I was startled by a man who grasped my shoulder from behind. I turned around and found that it was ‘Alī. He invoked Allah’s mercy upon ‘Umar and said:

‘You have left behind no one whose deeds I would rather have when meeting Allah, Mighty and Majestic, than yours. By Allah, I certainly used to think that Allah would place you with your two companions. That was because I so often heard the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, say, “Abū Bakr, ‘Umar, and I came,” “Abū Bakr, ‘Umar, and I entered,” and “Abū Bakr, ‘Umar, and I went out.” So I used to hope—or think—that Allah would place you with them.’”

In the two Sahīh collections and elsewhere, it is reported that:

“On the day of Uhud, after the Muslims had suffered casualties, Abū Sufyān said, ‘Is Muhammad among the people? Is Muhammad among the people? Is Muhammad among the people?’ The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said, ‘Do not answer him.’ Then he said, ‘Is Ibn Abī Quhāfah among the people? Is Ibn Abī Quhāfah among the people? Is Ibn Abī Quhāfah among the people?’ The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said, ‘Do not answer him.’ Then he said, ‘Is Ibn al-Khattāb among the people? Is Ibn al-Khattāb among the people? Is Ibn al-Khattāb among the people?’ The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said, ‘Do not answer him.’

Then he said to his companions, ‘As for these men, indeed

---

## Page 1725

you have been rid of them.’ ‘Umar could not restrain himself and said, ‘You lie, enemy of Allah! Those you named are indeed alive, and what will distress you still awaits you.’” The hadith continues.

Thus, the leader of the unbelievers at that time asked only about the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, Abū Bakr, and ‘Umar—not about anyone else—because he knew that they were the leaders of the Muslims: the Prophet and his two chief advisers.

For this reason, al-Rashīd asked Mālik ibn Anas about their standing in relation to the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, during his lifetime. He replied, “Their standing in relation to him during his lifetime was like their standing in relation to him after his death.” Their exceptionally close association and companionship with him, together with their perfect affection, harmony, and love, and their shared knowledge and religious commitment, mean that they have a greater claim to that distinction than others. This is plain and evident to anyone familiar with their circumstances.

As for Abū Bakr al-Siddīq, although he mastered matters of knowledge and jurisprudence that others could not grasp until he explained them, no statement contradicting a scriptural text has been recorded from him. This demonstrates the utmost mastery. By contrast, numerous statements contradicting scriptural texts have been recorded from others because those texts had not reached them. ‘Umar’s recorded agreement with the scriptural texts is more extensive than ‘Alī’s. This is known to anyone familiar with scholarly questions and the scholars’ views on them.

One example is maintenance for a woman whose husband has died: ‘Umar’s view agreed with the scriptural text, unlike the other view. Likewise, regarding the issue of declaring one’s wife forbidden to oneself, the view of ‘Umar and others is more consistent with the scriptural texts than the other view.

It is also established in the two Sahīh collections that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said: “There were among the nations

---

## Page 1726

before you men who received inspiration; if there is any such man in my community, it is ‘Umar.” It is also established in the two Sahīh collections that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“I saw myself being given a cup of milk. I drank until I could see the satisfaction of my thirst flowing from my nails, then I handed what remained to ‘Umar.” They asked, “How did you interpret it, Messenger of Allah?” He replied, “Knowledge.”

It is reported in al-Tirmidhī and elsewhere that he said:

“Had I not been sent among you, ‘Umar would have been sent.”

Moreover, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, appointed al-Siddīq to lead the “prayer,” which is the pillar of Islam, and to oversee the “pilgrimage rites,” whose rulings are the most complex among matters of worship. He conducted the pilgrimage rites before the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, himself performed the pilgrimage. He proclaimed that no polytheist was to perform the pilgrimage after that year, and that no one was to circumambulate the House naked. The Prophet then sent ‘Alī ibn Abī Tālib after him to notify the polytheists that their treaty was being terminated. When ‘Alī caught up with him, Abū Bakr asked, “Are you in command, or under orders?” He replied, “Under orders.”

Thus, he placed Abū Bakr in command over ‘Alī ibn Abī Tālib. ‘Alī was among those whom the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, ordered to listen to and obey Abū Bakr concerning the pilgrimage, the rulings governing travellers, and other matters. This occurred after the expedition to Tabūk, during which he had left ‘Alī in charge of Medina. No men remained in Medina except hypocrites, those with valid excuses, or sinners. ‘Alī caught up with him and asked, “Are you leaving me behind with the women and children?” He replied, “Are you not content to stand in relation to me as Aaron stood in relation to Moses?”

By this, he explained that leaving ‘Alī in charge of Medina did not imply any diminution of his standing, for Moses had left Aaron in charge. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, regularly appointed men to take his place, but on those occasions other men remained there. In the year of Tabūk, however, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, set out with all the Muslims and permitted no one to remain behind rather than join the expedition, because the enemy was formidable and the journey was

---

## Page 1727

long. During this expedition, Allah revealed Surah Barā’ah. Abū Bakr’s written instructions on zakat are the most comprehensive and concise of such documents, which is why the majority of jurists followed them. Other such documents contain earlier provisions that had been abrogated, indicating that he was more knowledgeable about the Sunnah that superseded them.

In the two Sahīh collections, Abū Sa‘īd is reported to have said:

“Abū Bakr knew the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, better than any of us.”

Furthermore, during Abū Bakr’s time, whenever the Companions disputed an issue, he settled it between them and their disagreement ended. Not a single issue is known over which they disputed during his time without his intervention bringing their disagreement to an end. Examples include their disagreements concerning the death of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, his burial place, his inheritance, the preparations for Usāmah’s army, fighting those who withheld zakat, and other major issues.

Indeed, he served among them as the successor to the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace: teaching them, correcting them, and providing explanations that dispelled their doubts. Thus, while he was among them, they did not disagree. After him, no one’s knowledge and excellence equalled his, so they began to dispute certain issues, such as inheritance involving a grandfather and siblings, declaring one’s wife forbidden to oneself, triple divorce, and other well-known matters over which they had not disputed during Abū Bakr’s time. They disagreed with ‘Umar, ‘Uthmān, and ‘Alī on many of their views, whereas they are not known to have disagreed with Abū Bakr on any matter in which he issued a legal opinion or judgment. This demonstrates the utmost degree of knowledge.

He took the place of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and upheld Islam without neglecting any part of it. Rather, he brought people back through the very door by which they had left, despite the many opponents among the apostates and others, and the many who failed to support him. Through him, their knowledge and religion were brought to such completeness that he could not be matched in this by

---

## Page 1728

anyone, until the religion was established as it had been before. They called Abū Bakr the successor to the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Thereafter, they called ‘Umar and others the Commander of the Faithful.

Al-Suhaylī and other scholars said:

“The words, ‘Do not grieve; indeed, Allah is with us,’ found expression in Abū Bakr’s case in language, just as they did in reality. Thus, they would say: ‘Muhammad is the Messenger of Allah, and Abū Bakr is the successor to the Messenger of Allah.’ This verbal connection ended with his death, for they did not call anyone after him ‘the successor to the Messenger of Allah.’”

Furthermore, “‘Alī ibn Abī Tālib” learned some of the Sunnah from Abū Bakr, whereas Abū Bakr did not learn from ‘Alī ibn Abī Tālib. This is illustrated by the well-known hadith in the Sunan collections concerning the prayer of repentance. ‘Alī said:

“Whenever I heard a hadith from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, Allah would benefit me through it as much as He willed. When someone else narrated a hadith to me, I would ask him to swear an oath; if he swore an oath to me, I would believe him. Abū Bakr narrated to me—and Abū Bakr spoke the truth—that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said: ‘No Muslim commits a sin, then performs ablution properly, prays two units of prayer, and seeks Allah’s forgiveness, without Allah forgiving him.’”

Further evidence of this is that the leading scholars of Kūfah who accompanied ‘Umar and ‘Alī, such as ‘Alqamah, al-Aswad, Shurayh the judge, and others, would give preference to ‘Umar’s opinion over ‘Alī’s. Among the Successors of Medina, Mecca, and Basra, this was too evident and well known to require mention. It was in Kūfah that ‘Alī’s jurisprudence and knowledge became particularly prominent, owing to his residence there during his caliphate. None of ‘Alī’s followers who accompanied him is known to have preferred him over Abū Bakr

---

## Page 1729

and ‘Umar—in jurisprudence, knowledge, or anything else. Rather, all his “followers” who fought his enemy alongside him joined the rest of the Muslims in giving precedence to Abū Bakr and ‘Umar, except those whom ‘Alī rebuked and condemned. These people were few and obscure during ‘Alī’s time. They comprised three groups:

One group went to extremes regarding him, such as those who claimed that he was divine. ‘Alī burned these people with fire.

Another group reviled Abū Bakr. Their leader was ‘Abd Allāh ibn Saba’. When news of this reached ‘Alī, he sought to have him killed, but he fled from him.

A third group preferred him over Abū Bakr and ‘Umar. ‘Alī said: “If I learn that any of you has preferred me over Abū Bakr and ‘Umar, I will flog him with the prescribed punishment for a slanderer.”

It has been transmitted from ‘Alī through some eighty or more chains of transmission that he said from the pulpit of Kūfah:

“The best of this community after its Prophet are Abū Bakr and ‘Umar.”

This is also authentically recorded in Sahīh al-Bukhārī and elsewhere through narrators specifically from Hamdān—the tribe of which ‘Alī said:

Were I the gatekeeper at the gate of Paradise,
I would say to Hamdān, “Enter in peace.”

It is narrated by Sufyān al-Thawrī from Mundhir al-Thawrī, both of whom were from Hamdān. Al-Bukhārī narrated it from Muhammad ibn Kathīr, who said that Sufyān al-Thawrī narrated to them, saying that Jāmi‘ ibn Shaddād narrated to them, saying that Abū Ya‘lā Mundhir al-Thawrī narrated to them, from Muhammad ibn al-Hanafiyyah, who said:

“I said to my father, ‘Father, who is the best of people after the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace?’

“He replied, ‘My son, do you not know?’

“I said, ‘No.’

“He said, ‘Abū Bakr.’

“I asked, ‘Then who?’

“He said, ‘Then ‘Umar.’”

---

## Page 1730

And he said this to his son, whom he did not fear, and to his close associates; he also ordered that anyone who preferred him over those two be punished. A person acting out of humility is not permitted to order the punishment of everyone who speaks the truth, nor may he call such a person a slanderer. Knowledge is the foremost virtue: whoever is superior to another—whether among the prophets, the Companions, or others—is more knowledgeable than him. Allah Most High says:

“Are those who know equal to those who do not know?”

The evidence for this is abundant, as are the scholars’ discussions of it. As for the statement, “‘Alī is the most skilled of you in judging,” none of the compilers of the Six Books or the well-known hadith collections arranged by Companion—neither Ahmad nor anyone else—narrated it with an authentic or even a weak chain of transmission. It is narrated only through someone known for lying. ‘Umar ibn al-Khattāb did, however, say: “Ubayy is our best reciter, and ‘Alī is our most skilled judge.” He said this after Abū Bakr’s death.

What is recorded in al-Tirmidhī and elsewhere is that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“The most knowledgeable of my community concerning the lawful and the unlawful is Mu‘ādh ibn Jabal, and the most knowledgeable concerning the laws of inheritance is Zayd ibn Thābit.”

There is no mention of ‘Alī in it. Even the hadith that mentions ‘Alī, despite its weakness, states that Mu‘ādh ibn Jabal is the most knowledgeable concerning the lawful and the unlawful, and that Zayd ibn Thābit is the most knowledgeable concerning the laws of inheritance.

If this hadith were assumed to be authentic, the person most knowledgeable concerning the lawful and the unlawful would have broader knowledge than the person most knowledgeable concerning judicial rulings. This is because what is specific to judicial rulings is merely the settlement of disputes on the basis of what is apparent, while the underlying reality may be otherwise. As the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“You bring your disputes before me, and some of you may be more adept at presenting their case than others. I judge only according to what I hear. If I award anyone something that rightfully belongs to his brother, he must not take it, for I am only allotting him a piece of the Fire.”

Thus, the foremost of judges explained that his judgment

---

## Page 1731

does not make the unlawful lawful. Rather, a Muslim is forbidden to take, on the strength of his judgment, anything awarded to him that rightfully belongs to someone else.

Knowledge of the lawful and the unlawful encompasses both outward appearances and underlying realities. Thus, whoever is most knowledgeable concerning it is more knowledgeable about the religion.

Moreover, judicial adjudication is of two kinds. The first is adjudication when the two litigants contradict one another—for example, when one makes a claim that the other denies. Judgment is then given on the basis of evidence and the like. The second concerns matters on which they do not contradict one another—they agree on the facts—but do not know what each is entitled to. Examples include disputes over the division of an inheritance, what each spouse owes the other, or what each partner is entitled to, and similar matters. This category falls within knowledge of the lawful and the unlawful. If someone whose opinion they both accept gives them a legal ruling, that suffices, and they have no need for someone to adjudicate between them. They need a judge only when they contradict one another, which generally involves wrongdoing, though it may also arise from forgetfulness.

Knowledge of the lawful and the unlawful, however, is needed by everyone, righteous and wicked alike, whereas what is specific to judicial adjudication is needed by only a few of the righteous. This is why, when Abū Bakr instructed ‘Umar to judge between the people, an entire year passed without two people bringing any dispute before him. If all the cases of this kind adjudicated by the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, were counted, they would not amount to ten. How can this compare with his teachings concerning the lawful and the unlawful, which are the mainstay of the religion of Islam and are needed by the elite and ordinary people alike?

---

## Page 1732

His saying, “The most knowledgeable of them concerning the lawful and the unlawful is Mu‘ādh ibn Jabal,” is, by the agreement of hadith scholars, more likely to be authentic than his saying, “The best of you in judicial judgment is ‘Alī”—even supposing the latter were admissible as evidence. Since the former has a sounder chain of transmission and is clearer in its import, it follows that anyone who cites the latter as evidence that ‘Alī was more knowledgeable than Mu‘ādh ibn Jabal is ignorant. How much more so if he claims that ‘Alī was more knowledgeable than Abū Bakr and ‘Umar, both of whom were more knowledgeable than Mu‘ādh ibn Jabal! This is despite the fact that some scholars grade the hadith mentioning Mu‘ādh and Zayd as weak, while others grade it as good. As for the hadith mentioning ‘Alī, it is weak.

The hadith, “I am the city of knowledge,” is weaker and still more unsound. For this reason, it is counted among fabricated, falsely attributed reports, even though al-Tirmidhī narrated it. Accordingly, Ibn al-Jawzī included it in al-Mawdū‘āt (Fabricated Reports) and demonstrated that it is fabricated through all its chains of transmission. Its falsehood can be recognized from its very wording, without examining its chain of transmission.

For if the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, were “the city of knowledge,” this city would have only one gate. Yet his teachings cannot have been conveyed by just one person. Rather, they must have been conveyed by transmitters numerous enough for their reports to establish certain knowledge for those who were not present. A single transmitter’s report does not yield certain knowledge unless accompanied by corroborating indications. Those indications may be absent, or they may be unknown to many or most people; consequently, those people would not attain certain knowledge of the Quran and the mass-transmitted Sunnah. This contrasts with mass transmission, which yields certain knowledge for the elite and ordinary people alike.

This hadith was fabricated by either a heretic or an ignorant person who thought it constituted praise. Yet it opens the way for heretics to impugn knowledge of the religion—since, on this account, only one Companion conveyed it.

---

## Page 1733

Moreover, this contradicts what is known through mass transmission. Knowledge from the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, reached all the Muslim cities through channels other than ‘Alī, may Allah be pleased with him. This is obvious in the case of the people of Medina and Mecca, and likewise those of Syria and Basra, who transmitted very little from ‘Alī. Most of his knowledge circulated among the people of Kūfa. Yet even they had learned the Quran and Sunnah before ‘Uthmān assumed the caliphate, let alone before ‘Alī’s caliphate.

The people of Medina most learned in jurisprudence and religious knowledge had learned the religion during ‘Umar’s caliphate and earlier. None of them had learned anything from ‘Alī except those who learned from him while he was in Yemen, just as they learned at that time from Mu‘ādh ibn Jabal. Mu‘ādh ibn Jabal stayed longer among the people of Yemen and taught them more than ‘Alī did. Thus, the people of Yemen transmitted more from Mu‘ādh than they did from ‘Alī. It was under Mu‘ādh that Shurayh and other leading Successors learned jurisprudence.

When ‘Alī arrived in Kūfa, Shurayh was already serving there as a judge. During his caliphate, ‘Alī found Shurayh and ‘Ubayda al-Salmānī serving as judges, both of whom had learned jurisprudence under others, not under him.

Thus, if knowledge of Islam had spread throughout the “cities of Islam”—in the Hijāz, Syria, Yemen, Iraq, Khurāsān, Egypt, and the Maghreb—before he arrived in Kūfa, and if, when he came to Kūfa, most of the knowledge that had reached him had also been conveyed by other Companions, and if ‘Alī conveyed no knowledge exclusively without others conveying still more knowledge that was exclusive to them,

---

## Page 1734

then Abū Bakr, ‘Umar, and ‘Uthmān accomplished more than ‘Alī in the “general dissemination” of knowledge afforded by holding authority.

As for “particular dissemination,” Ibn ‘Abbās issued more legal opinions than he did, and Abū Hurayra transmitted more reports than he did, although ‘Alī was more learned than both of them, just as Abū Bakr, ‘Umar, and ‘Uthmān were also more learned than both. For the Rightly Guided Caliphs disseminated knowledge generally that people needed more urgently than the particular knowledge conveyed by others.

As for the reports spread by liars and the ignorant claiming that ‘Alī possessed knowledge exclusive to him among the Companions, they are all false. It is established in the authentic collection that he was asked:

“Do you possess anything from the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace?” He replied: “No, by Him who splits the seed and creates the soul, except an understanding of His Book that Allah grants a servant, and what is in this document.” It contained the prescribed blood-money payments—that is, the ages of the camels required as blood money—as well as the freeing of captives and the rule that a Muslim is not to be put to death for killing an unbeliever.

In another wording:

“Did the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, entrust you with anything that he did not entrust to the people?” He denied this.

There are other reports from him indicating that anyone who claims that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, singled him out for special knowledge has lied about him.

What some ignorant people say—that he drank some of the water used to wash the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and thereby inherited the knowledge of the first and the last generations—is among the most vile and feeble lies. Drinking the water used to wash the deceased is not prescribed, nor did ‘Alī drink any of it. Had this conferred knowledge, everyone present would have shared it with him. No scholar has transmitted this report.

---

## Page 1735

The same applies to the claim that he possessed esoteric knowledge that distinguished him from Abū Bakr, ‘Umar, and others. This is among the assertions of the heretical Bātiniyya (esotericists) and others like them, whose unbelief is greater than theirs. Indeed, they hold forms of unbelief not found among Jews and Christians—for example, those who believe in his divinity and prophethood, that he was more learned than the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and that he secretly taught the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Such assertions are made only by those who go to extremes in unbelief and heresy. And Allah, glorified and exalted be He, knows best.

---

## Page 1736

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked about a man who adheres to the sunnah but has doubts about ranking the three above ‘Alī, because of the Prophet’s words to him:

“You are of me, and I am of you”; and his words, “Your position in relation to me is like that of Aaron in relation to Moses”; and his words, “I shall certainly give the banner to a man who loves Allah and His Messenger,” and so on; and his words, “For whomever I am an ally, ‘Alī is also an ally. O Allah, be an ally to whoever allies himself with him, and an enemy to whoever is his enemy,” and so on; and his words, “I remind you of Allah regarding my household.”

Also because of the words of Allah, glorified be He, “Then say: Come, let us call our sons and your sons,” to the end of the verse; His words, exalted be He, “Has there come upon man,” to the end of the verse; and His words, “These are two adversaries who have disputed concerning their Lord,” to the end of the verse.

He replied:

First, it must be understood that superiority is established when the person of greater merit is shown to possess distinctive qualities whose like are not found in the person of lesser merit. If the two are otherwise equal and one possesses distinctive qualities not shared by the other, then he is superior. Qualities shared by both, however, do not establish his superiority over the other. Accordingly, the virtues that distinguished al-Siddīq (the Truthful)—may Allah be pleased with him—were not shared with him

---

## Page 1737

by anyone else, whereas ‘Alī’s virtues are shared by others. For his words:

“If I were to take an intimate friend from among the people of the earth, I would take Abū Bakr as an intimate friend”; his words, “Every small doorway into the mosque must be blocked up, except Abū Bakr’s doorway”; and his words, “The person most generous to me in his companionship and wealth is Abū Bakr”—these establish three distinctions that no one else shared with him.

First: None of them had supported him through companionship and wealth as Abū Bakr had.

Second: His words, “Every small doorway into the mosque…” single him out to the exclusion of all the others. Some liars sought to transmit a similar report concerning ‘Alī, but a fabricated report cannot stand against an authentic one.

Third: His words, “If I were to take an intimate friend,” explicitly establish that no human being but him deserved this intimate friendship, had it been possible. If anyone else had been superior to him, that person would have been more entitled to it, were such a friendship to occur.

Likewise, his command that he lead the people in prayer throughout his illness was one of his distinctive merits. So too was his appointment of him in Medina to lead the pilgrimage, to establish the sunnah and eradicate the vestiges of pre-Islamic ignorance. This was another distinction unique to him. Likewise, his words in the authentic hadith:

“Call your father and your brother, so that I may write a document for Abū Bakr.”

There are many such hadiths showing that none of the Companions was his equal.

As for his words, “You are of me, and I am of you,” he said them to others as well: he also said them to Salmān and the Ash‘arīs. Allah Most High said:

“They swear by Allah that they are indeed of you, but they are not of you.”

And his words—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—“Whoever cheats us is not of us, and whoever bears arms against us is not of us,” imply that whoever refrains from

---

## Page 1738

these major sins is one of us. Thus every believer whose faith is complete is of the Prophet, and the Prophet is of him. Likewise are his words concerning Hamza’s daughter:

“You are of me, and I am of you.”

His words to Zayd, “You are our brother and our client,” are not exclusive to Zayd; rather, all his clients are likewise.

Similarly, his words, “I shall certainly give the banner…” constitute the most authentic hadith narrated concerning ‘Alī’s virtues. Some liars added that Abū Bakr and ‘Umar had taken the banner and then fled. Yet an authentic report records that ‘Umar said, “I never desired command except on that day.” This hadith therefore refutes those hostile to ‘Alī who disparage him. Nevertheless, this is not a distinction unique to him: every believer whose faith is complete loves Allah and His Messenger, and is loved by Allah and His Messenger. Allah Most High said:

“Allah will bring forth a people He will love and who will love Him.”

These were the people who fought the apostates, and their leader was Abū Bakr. An authentic report also relates that he was asked, “Whom do you love most?” He replied, “‘Ā’isha.” The questioner asked, “And among men?” He replied, “Her father.” This was a distinction unique to him.

As for his words:

“Are you not content to stand in relation to me as Aaron stood in relation to Moses?”

He said this during the expedition to Tabūk, when he appointed ‘Alī as his deputy in Medina. It was alleged that he had left him behind because he disliked him. Whenever the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—went on a military expedition, he would appoint a man from his community as his deputy, while able-bodied believers remained in Medina. On the expedition to Tabūk, however, he gave no one permission to remain behind; no one stayed except someone with a valid excuse or someone acting disobediently. Thus this deputation carried little weight, and the hypocrites used it to disparage him. He therefore explained to him: “I did not appoint you as my deputy because I consider you deficient. Moses appointed Aaron as his deputy, although Aaron shared his prophetic mission. Are you not content with that?” It is well known that he had appointed others as deputies before him, and that they stood in this same

---

## Page 1739

position in relation to him. This was therefore not a distinction unique to him. Had this appointment as deputy been superior to the others, it would not have escaped ‘Alī’s notice; yet he followed him in tears. Further evidence of this is that, afterward, he placed Abū Bakr in command over him in the year 9 AH. Nor was his being sent to announce the termination of treaties a distinction unique to him. Since established custom required that only a man from his household conclude or terminate treaties, the purpose would have been fulfilled by any of his kindred who announced their termination. However, he was the best of Banū Hāshim after the Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—and thus had the strongest claim to precedence over the rest of them.

When he placed Abū Bakr in command after saying, “Are you not content…” and so forth, we know that these words do not indicate that ‘Alī stood in Aaron’s position in every respect. He likened him to Aaron specifically in being appointed as deputy, and that was not a distinction unique to him. The Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—also likened Abū Bakr to Abraham and Jesus, and ‘Umar to Noah and Moses—may blessings and peace be upon them—when the two offered their advice concerning the captives. This is greater than likening ‘Alī to Aaron, yet it did not entail that the two occupied the same position as those messengers. Likening one thing to another because they resemble each other in certain respects is common in the Quran, the Sunnah, and Arabic speech.

As for his words:

“Whoever has me as his ally has ‘Alī as his ally. O Allah, be an ally to whoever allies himself with him…” and so forth,

this does not occur in any of the principal hadith collections except al-Tirmidhī’s, which contains only:

“Whoever has me as his ally has ‘Alī as his ally.”

As for the additional wording, it is not part of the hadith. Imam Ahmad was asked about it and said, “An addition from Kufa.” There is no doubt that it is a fabrication, for several reasons:

---

## Page 1740

First: truth does not invariably follow any particular individual except the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Were this otherwise, that individual would have to be followed in everything he said. Yet it is well known that the Companions and ‘Alī’s own followers disagreed with him on matters in which scriptural evidence supported those who opposed his view, as in the case of a pregnant widow.

The wording:

“O Allah, grant victory to whoever supports him…” and so forth,

contradicts what actually occurred. Some people fought alongside him at Siffīn but were not victorious, while others did not fight alongside him yet were not forsaken. Sa‘d, for example, who conquered Iraq, did not fight alongside him. Likewise, the supporters of Mu‘āwiya and the Umayyads who fought against him conquered many lands of the unbelievers, and Allah granted them victory.

Similarly, the wording:

“O Allah, be an ally to whoever allies himself with him, and an enemy to whoever is hostile to him,”

contradicts a fundamental principle of Islam. The Quran makes clear that believers remain brothers even when they fight one another and some commit aggression against others.

As for the wording:

“Whoever has me as his ally has ‘Alī as his ally,”

some hadith scholars, such as al-Bukhārī and others, have challenged its authenticity, while others have graded it as sound. If he did say it, he did not mean an allegiance exclusive to ‘Alī, but rather a shared allegiance: the bond of faith belonging to all believers. Allegiance is the opposite of hostility, and there is no doubt that believers must be given allegiance over others. Thus, it refutes the Nawāsib (those hostile to the Prophet’s household).

The hadith about “giving a ring in charity during prayer” is a fabrication according to the unanimous agreement of scholars versed in this field. This has been demonstrated in numerous ways, explained at length elsewhere.

As for his words on the day of Ghadīr Khumm:

“I remind you of Allah concerning my household,”

this is not a distinction unique to him

---

## Page 1741

; rather, he shares this equally with all members of the household. Those furthest from observing this counsel are the Rāfida (rejectionists), for they are hostile to al-‘Abbās and his descendants. Indeed, they are hostile to most members of the household and assist the unbelievers against them.

As for the verse of mubāhala (mutual invocation of Allah’s curse), it does not establish a distinction unique to him. Rather, the Prophet summoned ‘Alī, Fātima, and their two sons—not because they were the best members of the Muslim community, but because they were the closest members of his household, as in the hadith of the cloak:

“O Allah, these are the members of my household, so remove impurity from them and purify them completely.”

He thus supplicated for them and singled them out. The word “selves” can refer to people of the same kind, as in His words:

“The believing men and women thought well of themselves.”

And He said:

“So kill yourselves,”

meaning that some of you should kill others. Likewise, the Prophet’s words:

“You are of me, and I am of you,”

do not mean that ‘Alī is part of his very being. There is no doubt that ‘Alī holds the highest standing among the Prophet’s relatives: he possesses a distinction through kinship and faith that none of the other relatives possesses. He was therefore included in the mubāhala. This does not preclude someone outside the Prophet’s relatives from being superior to him, since the mubāhala involved his relatives.

As for His words:

“These are two adversaries,”

and the rest of the verse, it applies equally to ‘Alī, Hamza, and ‘Ubayda. Indeed, all the others who fought at Badr share in its application.

As for the surah beginning:

“Has there come upon man…,”

the claim that it was revealed concerning him, Fātima, and their two sons is false, because it is a Meccan surah, whereas al-Hasan and al-Husayn were born in Medina. Even supposing that claim were true, nothing in it establishes that someone who feeds a needy person, an orphan, and a captive is the best of the Companions. Rather, the verse is general and applies equally to anyone who does this. It indicates that such a person deserves a reward for this deed, although other deeds—such as faith in Allah, prayer at its appointed time, and jihad—are superior to it.

---

## Page 1742

He was asked about someone who says, “I do not consider ‘Alī superior to others,” yet invokes blessings upon ‘Alī alone whenever he is mentioned: “Is it permissible for him to single him out for blessings to the exclusion of others?”

He replied:

No one may single out anyone other than the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—for invocations of blessings: neither Abū Bakr, nor ‘Umar, nor ‘Uthmān, nor ‘Alī. Whoever does so is an innovator. Rather, he should either invoke blessings upon them all or refrain from invoking blessings upon any of them.

What is prescribed is to say:

“O Allah, send blessings upon Muhammad and the family of Muhammad, as You sent blessings upon Abraham. Indeed, You are Praiseworthy, Glorious. And bless Muhammad and the family of Muhammad, as You blessed Abraham among all peoples. Indeed, You are Praiseworthy, Glorious.”

Whoever says, “I do not consider ‘Alī superior to others,” is mistaken and contradicts the scriptural evidence. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 1743

He was asked about the statement of Shaykh “Abū Muhammad ‘Abd Allāh ibn Abī Zayd” at the end of his creed:

“The best generation is that of those who saw the Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—and believed in him, then those who followed them, then those who followed them. The best of the ‘Companions’ are the rightly guided caliphs: Abū Bakr, ‘Umar, ‘Uthmān, and ‘Alī.”

What is the evidence for the superiority of Abū Bakr over ‘Umar, of ‘Umar over ‘Uthmān, and of ‘Uthmān over ‘Alī? Once this has been established, must someone who ranks the less virtuous above the more virtuous be punished, or not? Explain this to us in detail; may you be rewarded, if Allah Most High wills.

He replied:

All praise is due to Allah, Lord of all worlds. As for the superiority of Abū Bakr, followed by ‘Umar, over ‘Uthmān and ‘Alī, this is agreed upon by the Muslim imams renowned as leading authorities in knowledge and religion, among the Companions, the Successors, and their successors.

This is the position of Mālik and the people of Medina; al-Layth ibn Sa‘d and the people of Egypt; al-Awzā‘ī and the people of Syria; and Sufyān al-Thawrī, Abū Hanīfah, Hammād ibn Zayd, Hammād ibn Salamah, and others like them among the people of Iraq.

It is also the position of al-Shāfi‘ī, Ahmad, Ishāq, Abū ‘Ubayd, and other imams of Islam who enjoy an honourable reputation in the Muslim community. Mālik reported the consensus of the people of Medina on this, saying: “I never encountered anyone whom I took as an example who doubted that Abū Bakr and ‘Umar should be given precedence.”

---

## Page 1744

This is widely transmitted from the Commander of the Faithful, ‘Alī ibn Abī Tālib. In Sahīh al-Bukhārī, Muhammad ibn al-Hanafiyyah relates that he said to his father, ‘Alī ibn Abī Tālib:

“Father, who is the best of people after the Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace?”
He replied, “My son, do you not know?”
I said, “No.”
He replied, “Abū Bakr.”
I asked, “Then who?”
He replied, “‘Umar.”

This is narrated from ‘Alī ibn Abī Tālib through some eighty chains of transmission, and it is reported that he used to say it from the pulpit in Kufa. Indeed, he said: “If anyone is brought before me who ranks me above Abū Bakr and ‘Umar, I will flog him with the prescribed punishment for a slanderer.” Thus, according to his statement—may Allah be pleased with him—anyone who ranks him above Abū Bakr and ‘Umar is to receive eighty lashes. Sufyān used to say: “Whoever ranks ‘Alī above Abū Bakr has disparaged the Emigrants. I do not think any deed of his ascends to Allah while he persists in that view.”

In al-Tirmidhī and elsewhere, this ranking in virtue is reported from the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—who said:

“‘Alī, these two are the leaders of the mature men among the people of Paradise, from the first generations and the last, except for the prophets and messengers.”

It is widely reported in the two Sahīh collections and elsewhere, through several chains—from Abū Sa‘īd, Ibn ‘Abbās, Jundub ibn ‘Abd Allāh, Ibn al-Zubayr, and others—that the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

“If I were to take anyone on earth as an intimate friend, I would take Abū Bakr as an intimate friend. But your companion is the intimate friend of Allah.”

By “your companion,” he meant himself. It is also reported in the Sahīh that he said from the pulpit:

“No one has been more generous to me in his companionship and wealth than Abū Bakr. If I were to take anyone on earth as an intimate friend, I would take Abū Bakr as an intimate friend. But your companion is the intimate friend of Allah. Let every small doorway into the mosque be sealed, except the small doorway of

---

## Page 1745

Abū Bakr.”

This explicitly shows that, in his estimation, no one on earth except Abū Bakr deserved his intimate friendship, had such friendship with a created being been possible for him. Thus, in his estimation, no one was superior to Abū Bakr or dearer to him. Likewise, it is reported in the Sahīh that ‘Amr ibn al-‘Ās asked him:

“Who is dearest to you?”
He replied, “‘Ā’ishah.”
He asked, “And among men?”
He replied, “Her father.”

It is likewise reported in the Sahīh that he said to ‘Ā’ishah:

“Call your father and your brother for me, so that I may write a document for Abū Bakr, leaving no room for people to disagree about him after me.”

Then he said:

“Allah and the believers will accept none but Abū Bakr.”

It is also reported in the Sahīh that a woman said to him, “Messenger of Allah, what if I come and do not find you?”—as though she meant his death. He replied:

“Then go to Abū Bakr.”

In the Sunan, he is reported to have said:

“Follow the example of the two who come after me: Abū Bakr and ‘Umar.”

It is reported in the Sahīh that, while on a journey, he said:

“If the people obey Abū Bakr and ‘Umar, they will be rightly guided.”

In the Sunan, he is reported to have said:

“I saw myself as though I had been placed in one pan of a balance and the community in the other, and I outweighed the community. Then Abū Bakr was placed in one pan and the community in the other, and Abū Bakr outweighed them. Then ‘Umar was placed in one pan and the community in the other, and ‘Umar outweighed them.”

It is reported in the Sahīh that Abū Bakr and ‘Umar had an exchange of words. Abū Bakr asked ‘Umar to seek forgiveness for him, but he did not do so. Abū Bakr then came to the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—and told him what had happened. He said:

“Sit down, Abū Bakr. May Allah forgive you.”

‘Umar regretted what he had done and went to Abū Bakr’s home, but did not find him there. He then came to the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace. The Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—became angry and said:

“People, I came to you and said, ‘I am the Messenger of Allah,’ but you said, ‘You are lying,’ while Abū Bakr said, ‘You are speaking the truth.’ Will you then leave my companion alone for my sake? Will you then leave my companion alone for my sake? Will you then, for my sake, leave my

---

## Page 1746

companion alone?”

After that, he was never mistreated again.

It is also established by mass-transmitted reports in the Sahīh and Sunan that, when the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—fell ill, he said:

“Tell Abū Bakr to lead the people in prayer.”

He repeated this twice or three times, until he said:

“You are indeed like the women in Joseph’s story! Tell Abū Bakr to lead the people in prayer.”

By singling him out, repeating his instruction, and emphatically giving him precedence over all the other Companions in leading the prayer—even though ‘Umar, ‘Uthmān, ‘Alī, and others were present—he made clear to the community that Abū Bakr held precedence over the others in his estimation—may Allah bless him and grant him peace.

It is reported in the Sahīh that, when ‘Umar’s body was laid out for the funeral, ‘Alī ibn Abī Tālib came through the rows and said:

“I hope that Allah will place you with your two companions, for I often heard the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—say, ‘I entered with Abū Bakr and ‘Umar; I went out with Abū Bakr and ‘Umar; I went with Abū Bakr and ‘Umar.’”

This shows how constantly the two accompanied the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—when he entered, went out, and went about. Thus, when al-Rashīd asked Mālik, “Abū ‘Abd Allāh, tell me about the standing of Abū Bakr and ‘Umar in relation to the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace,” he answered:

“Commander of the Faithful, their position beside him during his lifetime was like their position beside him after his death.”

He replied, “You have set my mind at rest, Mālik!”

This makes clear the exceptional closeness of their companionship with him, their support for his mission, and their relationship with him as confidants. These are facts necessarily known to everyone familiar with the circumstances of the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—his words, his deeds, and his conduct with his Companions. Hence, no one knowledgeable about his life, Sunnah, and character disputed this. Rather, only

---

## Page 1747

those who lack knowledge of the reality of the Prophet’s affairs—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—deny this or withhold judgment on it, even if they have some knowledge of speculative theology, jurisprudence, arithmetic, or other subjects. The same applies to those who have heard fabricated hadiths contradicting these facts, which specialist scholars know with necessary certainty, and have consequently withheld judgment or preferred someone other than Abū Bakr.

This is like other matters known with necessary certainty to those versed in the Sunnah of Allah’s Messenger—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—even though others doubt or deny them. These include the hadiths established among them through mass transmission concerning his intercession, his Cistern, and the emergence of those guilty of major sins from the Fire; and those similarly established concerning Allah’s attributes, divine decree, His exaltedness, the vision of Him, and other fundamental principles upon which those versed in his Sunnah agree, as these have been transmitted to them from him through mass transmission—even if others are unaware of this.

Likewise, specialist scholars have received through mass transmission from him the rulings concerning the right of pre-emption, requiring the defendant to swear an oath, stoning an adulterer who has consummated a valid marriage, and requiring a minimum value for stolen property, together with similar rulings disputed by some adherents of religious innovation. For this reason, the leading scholars of Islam agreed that anyone who opposed such fundamental principles was to be deemed an adherent of religious innovation.

This differs from disagreement over matters of independent juristic reasoning in which the transmitted practices of the Prophet have not reached this degree of mass transmission. Examples include their disagreements over adjudicating on the basis of one witness and an oath, qasāma (the oath procedure in homicide cases), drawing lots, and other matters that have not reached this degree.

As for “ ‘Uthmān and ‘Alī,” the question of their relative merit is less firmly established than the preceding one, for disagreement had arisen over it

---

## Page 1748

for Sufyān al-Thawrī and a group of scholars from Kufa considered ‘Alī superior to ‘Uthmān, though Sufyān and others later retracted that view. Some scholars of Medina withheld judgment concerning ‘Uthmān and ‘Alī; this is one of the two positions reported from Mālik. The other position reported from him, however, gives precedence to ‘Uthmān over ‘Alī, as do the other leading scholars, including al-Shāfi‘ī, Abū Hanīfa and his followers, Ahmad ibn Hanbal and his followers, and other leading scholars of Islam. Indeed, these scholars disagreed over whether someone who gave precedence to ‘Alī over ‘Uthmān should be regarded as an adherent of religious innovation. They held two views, both of which are reported from Ahmad.

Ayyūb al-Sakhtiyānī, Ahmad ibn Hanbal, and al-Dāraqutnī said: “Whoever gives precedence to ‘Alī over ‘Uthmān has disparaged the Emigrants and the Helpers.” This Ayyūb was the imam of Ahl al-Sunnah and of the people of Basra. Mālik narrated from him in al-Muwatta’, although Mālik did not narrate from the people of Iraq. It is reported that, when asked about narrating from him, Mālik said: “Everyone from whom I have narrated to you was inferior to Ayyūb.”

Abū Hanīfa mentioned him, saying: “I saw him sitting in the mosque of Allah’s Messenger—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—and whenever I recall him, my body shudders.”

The evidence for this is the report recorded by the two authors in their Sahīh collections and elsewhere:

{Ibn ‘Umar said: “During the lifetime of Allah’s Messenger—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—we used to rank people according to merit. We would say: ‘Abū Bakr, then ‘Umar, then ‘Uthmān.’” Some versions add: “This would reach the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—and he would not disapprove of it.”}

Furthermore, it is established through an authentic report in Sahīh al-Bukhārī and elsewhere that when the Commander of the Faithful, ‘Umar ibn al-Khattāb, entrusted the choice of the caliph to consultation among six men: ‘Uthmān, ‘Alī

---

## Page 1749

, Talha, al-Zubayr, Sa‘d, and ‘Abd al-Rahmān ibn ‘Awf—Sa‘īd ibn Zayd was not included among them, although he was one of the ten promised Paradise and belonged to Banū ‘Adī, ‘Umar’s tribe—he said of his son ‘Abd Allāh: “ ‘Abd Allāh will attend your deliberations, but he will have no part in the decision.” He also instructed that Suhayb should lead the prayers after his death until they agreed upon one man.

When ‘Umar died and they gathered by the pulpit, Talha said: “Whatever claim I have in this matter, I relinquish to ‘Uthmān.” Al-Zubayr said: “Whatever claim I have in this matter, I relinquish to ‘Alī.” Sa‘d said: “Whatever claim I have in this matter, I relinquish to ‘Abd al-Rahmān ibn ‘Awf.” Thus three withdrew and three remained.

They then met, and ‘Abd al-Rahmān ibn ‘Awf said: “Let one of us withdraw and appoint one of the others.” ‘Uthmān and ‘Alī remained silent, so ‘Abd al-Rahmān said: “I will withdraw.” It is also reported that he said he was bound by Allah’s covenant and solemn pledge to appoint the better of the two.

‘Abd al-Rahmān ibn ‘Awf then spent three days and nights consulting the Emigrants, the Helpers, and those who followed them in righteousness. He also consulted the Mothers of the Believers and the governors of the provinces, who were in Medina, having performed Hajj with ‘Umar and been present at his death. ‘Abd al-Rahmān ibn ‘Awf eventually said: “For three days I have not closed my eyes in sleep.”

On the third day, he said to ‘Uthmān: “Do you bind yourself by Allah’s covenant and solemn pledge to rule justly if I appoint you, and to listen and obey if I appoint ‘Alī?” He replied: “Yes.”

He said to ‘Alī: “Do you bind yourself by Allah’s covenant and solemn pledge to rule justly if I appoint you, and to listen and obey if I appoint ‘Uthmān?” He replied: “Yes.”

Then he said: “I have found that the people regard no one as equal to ‘Uthmān.”

So ‘Alī, ‘Abd al-Rahmān, and all the other Muslims pledged allegiance to him willingly and by their own choice, without any inducement he offered them or any threat with which he frightened them.

---

## Page 1750

And this constitutes their consensus on giving ‘Uthmān precedence over ‘Alī. Hence Ayyūb, Ahmad ibn Hanbal, and al-Dāraqutnī said:

“Whoever gives ‘Alī precedence over ‘Uthmān has disparaged the Emigrants and the Helpers.”

For if ‘Uthmān was not more entitled to precedence, yet they gave him precedence, they must have been either ignorant of his merit or unjust in preferring the less virtuous without any religious grounds for doing so. Whoever accuses them of ignorance and injustice has disparaged them.

If someone were to claim that they preferred ‘Uthmān because some of them harboured resentment towards ‘Alī, and that those harbouring this resentment wielded power—or make similar claims of the kind advanced by people who follow their own desires—he would be accusing them of being unable to uphold the truth, and claiming that the adherents of falsehood among them prevailed over the adherents of truth. All this when they were at the height of their honour and strength!

When ‘Umar died, Islam enjoyed unparalleled strength, honour, ascendancy, unity, and harmony. ‘Umar had strengthened the believers and humbled the unbelievers and hypocrites to such an extent that the strength and ascendancy he achieved are evident to anyone with even the slightest knowledge of these matters. Whoever portrays them, in such circumstances, as ignorant, unjust, or unable to uphold the truth has disparaged them and portrayed the best nation brought forth for mankind in a manner contrary to Allah’s testimony concerning them.

This is the foundation of “the doctrine of the Rāfidah (rejectionists).” The man who introduced rejectionism was a Jew who hypocritically professed Islam and insinuated subversive ideas among the ignorant, thereby undermining the very foundation of faith. Thus rejectionism has been one of the greatest gateways to hypocrisy and heretical unbelief. A man may initially withhold judgment, then become

---

## Page 1751

one who gives ‘Alī precedence, then a reviler, then an extremist, and finally a denier and negationist. This is why the “leaders of heretical unbelief”—the Ismā‘īlīs, Nusayrīs, and their various offshoots among the Qarmatians, Bātinīs, Druze, and similar groups of heretical unbelief and hypocrisy—joined the Rāfidah.

Disparaging the best generation, who accompanied the Messenger, amounts to disparaging the Messenger himself, peace be upon him. As Mālik and other leading scholars said:

“These people attacked the Companions of Allah’s Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. They attacked his Companions only so that someone would say: ‘He was an evil man with evil companions. Had he been righteous, his companions would have been righteous.’”

Moreover, these were the people who transmitted the Quran, Islam, and the laws taught by the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. They were also the ones who transmitted the merits of ‘Alī and others. Disparaging them necessarily undermines trust in what they transmitted of the religion. Consequently, no merit could be established for ‘Alī or anyone else.

The “Rāfidah” are ignorant people who possess neither reason nor transmitted evidence, neither religion nor worldly success. If a Nāsibī—someone who hates ‘Alī and regards him as a sinner or an unbeliever, such as the Khārijites and others—were to demand that they establish ‘Alī’s faith and merit, they would be unable to do so. Indeed, the Khārijites would prevail over them in argument. For ‘Alī’s merits were transmitted only by the Companions whom the Rāfidah disparage. Thus, on their own principles, no known merit of his can be established with certainty.

If they attack some of the caliphs by fabricating allegations that they sought leadership and fought for it, then similar accusations against ‘Alī by the Khārijites, and accusations many times more severe, would be more plausible than such allegations against those who were obeyed without fighting. But the Rāfidah are ignorant followers of heretical unbelievers.

---

## Page 1752

And the “Quran” has praised the “Companions” in numerous passages, as in the words of the Most High:

“And the foremost, the first of the Emigrants and the Helpers, and those who followed them in goodness—Allah is pleased with them, and they are pleased with Him.”

And His words:

“Those of you who spent and fought before the conquest are not equal to those who did so afterwards. They are greater in rank than those who spent and fought afterwards. Yet Allah has promised all of them the best reward.”

The Most High also says:

“Muhammad is the Messenger of Allah, and those with him are firm against the disbelievers and merciful among themselves. You see them bowing and prostrating, seeking bounty from Allah and His pleasure. Their mark is on their faces from the effects of prostration. That is their description in the Torah. Their description in the Gospel is like a crop that sends forth its shoots, strengthens them, grows thick, and stands firmly upon its stalks, delighting the sowers—so that through them He may enrage the disbelievers.”

And the Most High says:

“Allah was certainly pleased with the believers when they pledged allegiance to you beneath the tree. He knew what was in their hearts, so He sent down tranquillity upon them and rewarded them with a conquest soon to come.”

It is established in Sahīh Muslim that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“No one who pledged allegiance beneath the tree will enter the Fire.”

In the two Sahīh collections, Abū Sa‘īd reports that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Do not revile my Companions. By Him in whose hand is my soul, if any of you were to spend gold equal to Mount Uhud, it would not match a single mudd spent by any one of them, nor even half of it.”

It is also established in the authentic hadith collections, through several chains of transmission, that he said:

“The best of generations is the generation among whom I was sent, then those who follow them, then those who follow them.”

These hadiths concerning the Companions’ merits, praising them and affirming the superiority of their generation over subsequent generations, are widely transmitted—indeed, transmitted in a manner that establishes certainty. Thus, disparaging them amounts to disparaging the Quran and the Sunnah. For this reason, scholars have discussed declaring the Rāfidah unbelievers, as we have explained at length elsewhere. And Allah, glorified and exalted is He, knows best.

---

## Page 1753

He—may Allah be pleased with him—was asked about the disputes that arose among the Companions—‘Alī, Mu‘āwiyah, Talhah, and ‘Ā’ishah—and whether they would be held accountable for them or not.

He replied:

It is established by authentic texts that ‘Uthmān, ‘Alī, Talhah, al-Zubayr, and ‘Ā’ishah are among the people of Paradise. Indeed, it is established in the Sahīh collection that:

“No one who pledged allegiance beneath the tree will enter the Fire.”

Abū Mūsā al-Ash‘arī, ‘Amr ibn al-‘Ās, and Mu‘āwiyah ibn Abī Sufyān are among the Companions, and they possess virtues and praiseworthy qualities. Much of what is related about them is false. As for the reports that are true, if they were exercising independent judgment in those matters, then one who exercises such judgment receives two rewards when correct, and one reward when mistaken, and his error is forgiven.

---

## Page 1754

And even if it is assumed that they committed sins, sins do not automatically entail entry into the Fire unless the factors preventing it are absent. There are ten such factors, including repentance, seeking forgiveness, good deeds that erase sins, afflictions that expiate sins, the intercession of the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—the intercession of others, the supplications of believers, rewards gifted to the deceased together with charity and the freeing of slaves on their behalf, the trial of the grave, and the terrors of the Resurrection.

It is established in the two Sahīh collections that the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

“The best generation is the generation among whom I was sent, then those who follow them, then those who follow them.”

Thus, anyone who asserts that any one of these people committed a sin for which he will certainly enter the Fire is a liar and a fabricator. Merely speaking without knowledge would make him a purveyor of falsehood; how much worse, then, when he says something that abundant evidence contradicts? Whoever discusses the disputes that arose among them by disparaging them or unjustly taking sides with some of them—something Allah has forbidden—is an unjust transgressor.

It is established in the Sahīh collection that the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

“A renegade faction will break away at a time of division among the Muslims. They will be killed by whichever of the two groups is closer to the truth.”

It is also established in the Sahīh collection that he said of al-Hasan:

“This son of mine is a leader, and Allah will bring about reconciliation through him between two great groups of Muslims.”

---

## Page 1755

And in the two Sahīh collections, it is reported that the Prophet said concerning Ammār:

“He will be killed by the transgressing faction.”

Allah Most High says in the Quran:

“If two groups of believers fight each other, make peace between them. But if one of them transgresses against the other, fight the transgressing group until it returns to Allah’s command. If it returns, make peace between them with justice and act equitably. Indeed, Allah loves those who act equitably.”

Thus, the Quran, the Sunnah, and the consensus of the early generations establish that they were believers and Muslims, and that Alī ibn Abī Tālib and those with him were closer to the truth than the group fighting against him. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 1756

Shaykh al-Islam Ibn Taymiyyah said:

A Beneficial Point

It should be understood that, although the preferred course is to refrain from discussing the disputes that arose among the Companions, to seek forgiveness for both sides, and to remain loyal to them, it is not obligatory to believe that everyone in the forces was merely exercising independent judgment on the basis of an interpretation, as scholars do. Rather, some were sinful and acted wrongly, while others fell short in exercising independent judgment because of some personal inclination. But if a misdeed is set against many good deeds, it is outweighed and forgiven.

“The people of the Sunnah” speak well of them, pray for mercy upon them, and seek forgiveness for them. However, they believe that only the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, is protected from being left uncorrected in sins or errors of independent judgment. Anyone else may be left uncorrected in sin or error. Yet they are as Allah Most High says:

“Those are the ones from whom We will accept the best of what they did and overlook their misdeeds”—to the end of the verse.

The merit of deeds lies only in their results and ultimate outcomes, not in their outward forms.

---

## Page 1757

Section: The Enemies of the Rightly Guided Caliphs and Guided Imams

The four Rightly Guided Caliphs were tried by the hostility of some who professed Islam and belonged to the people of the qiblah: they cursed them, hated them, and declared them unbelievers. Abū Bakr and ‘Umar were hated and cursed by the Rāfidah (Rejectionists), unlike the other sects.

For this reason, Imam Ahmad was asked, “Who is a Rāfidī?” He replied, “Someone who reviles Abū Bakr and ‘Umar.” This is how the Rāfidah acquired their name: they rejected Zayd ibn ‘Alī when he affirmed his loyalty to the two caliphs, Abū Bakr and ‘Umar, because they hated them. Thus, someone who hates them is a Rāfidī. It has also been said that they were called Rāfidah because they rejected Abū Bakr and ‘Umar.

“The origin of rejectionism” lies with hypocritical heretics. It was invented by the heretic Ibn Saba’, who openly promoted excess concerning ‘Alī by claiming the imamate for him, asserting that he had been explicitly designated for it, and claiming that he was infallible. Since its beginnings lay in hypocrisy, some of the early generations said, “Love for Abū Bakr and ‘Umar is faith, and hatred of them is hypocrisy; love for the clan of Hāshim is faith, and hatred of them is hypocrisy.”

‘Abd Allāh ibn Mas‘ūd said, “Love for Abū Bakr and ‘Umar and recognition of their merit are part of the Sunnah.” That is, they are part of the religious law of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, which people are commanded to follow. For he said:

“Follow the example of the two who come after me: Abū Bakr and ‘Umar.”

Recognizing their superiority over those who came after them is therefore obligatory, and it is impermissible to withhold judgment on this. The case of ‘Uthmān and ‘Alī is different: there are two views on whether it is permissible to withhold judgment concerning their relative merit. Likewise, is it permissible to exercise independent judgment in preferring ‘Alī over ‘Uthmān? There are two reported positions:

(The first is that this is impermissible. Whoever prefers ‘Alī over ‘Uthmān has departed from the Sunnah into religious innovation by opposing the consensus of the Companions. For this reason, it has been said, “Whoever gives ‘Alī precedence over ‘Uthmān has indeed

---

## Page 1758

disparaged the Emigrants and the Helpers.” This is reported from several scholars, including Ayyūb al-Sakhtiyānī, Ahmad ibn Hanbal, and al-Dāraqutnī.

The second position is that someone who gives ‘Alī precedence is not deemed an innovator, because ‘Uthmān and ‘Alī were close in rank. The Sunnah is the religious law: what Allah and His Messenger have prescribed as religion, commanding it as either obligatory or recommended. It is therefore impermissible to believe otherwise. A recommended act may, however, be omitted, though it is impermissible to believe that it is not recommended. Knowledge that it is recommended is a communal obligation, lest any part of the religion be lost. Since the “scriptural evidence” established the obligation to follow Abū Bakr and ‘Umar and give them precedence, it was impermissible to abandon this.

As for (‘Uthmān, some of the Zaydī Shi‘ah and the Khārijites also hated him, reviled him, or declared him an unbeliever, alongside the Rāfidah. As for (‘Alī, the Khārijites and many of the Umayyads and their partisans who fought and reviled him hated and reviled him, or declared him an unbeliever. The Khārijites declare ‘Uthmān, ‘Alī, and all other members of the community unbelievers.

As for “‘Alī’s partisans,” who supported him after the arbitration, and “Mu‘āwiyah’s partisans,” who supported him after the arbitration, there was opposition between the two sides, with some cursing one another and some declaring one another unbelievers. The partisans who were with ‘Alī did not display any disparagement of Abū Bakr and ‘Umar, nor did they include anyone who gave ‘Alī precedence over Abū Bakr and ‘Umar. Reviling ‘Uthmān was not widespread among them; rather, some would do so, and others would rebut them. Likewise, preferring ‘Alī over him was not prevalent among them. By contrast, reviling ‘Alī was

---

## Page 1759

widespread among Mu‘āwiyah’s followers.

For this reason, ‘Alī and his companions had a greater claim to the truth and were closer to it than Mu‘āwiyah and his companions. As reported in the two Sahīh collections, Abū Sa‘īd narrated that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“A renegade faction will emerge at a time of division among the Muslims, and the one of the two factions with the greater claim to the truth will kill them.”

Another wording reported in the Sahīh reads: “The one of the two factions closer to the truth.”

Reviling and cursing ‘Alī were among the acts of rebellion by which that faction deserved to be called “the rebellious faction.” Thus, al-Bukhārī reports in his Sahīh, through Khālid al-Hadhdhā’, that ‘Ikrimah said:

“Ibn ‘Abbās said to me and to his son ‘Alī, ‘Go to Abū Sa‘īd and listen to his hadith.’ We went and found him tending a garden. He took his cloak and wrapped it around his back and drawn-up knees. He then began relating hadith to us, until, when ‘Alī arrived, he mentioned the building of the mosque, saying, ‘We used to carry one brick at a time, while ‘Ammār carried two at a time. The Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, saw him and began brushing the dust off him, saying, “Poor ‘Ammār! The rebellious faction will kill him. He calls them to Paradise, while they call him to the Fire.”’ He said, ‘‘Ammār would say, “I seek refuge in Allah from tribulations.”’”

Muslim also reports it from Abū Sa‘īd, who said:

“Someone better than I—Abū Qatādah—told me that, when ‘Ammār began digging the trench, the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, began stroking his head and saying to him, ‘Poor son of Sumayyah! A rebellious faction will kill you.’”

Muslim also reports from Umm Salamah that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“The rebellious faction will kill ‘Ammār.”

This also indicates the legitimacy of ‘Alī’s leadership and the obligation to obey him, and that whoever calls people to obey him calls them to Paradise, while whoever calls them to fight him calls them to the Fire—even if acting on an interpretation—and this is

---

## Page 1760

evidence that fighting ‘Alī was not permissible. Accordingly, anyone who fought him was in error, whether acting on an interpretation or rebelling without one. This is the sounder of (the two views held by our scholars: namely, that those who fought ‘Alī were in error. It is also the position of the leading jurists, who derived from this the rulings on fighting rebels acting on an interpretation.

Yahyā ibn Ma‘īn objected to al-Shāfi‘ī’s use of ‘Alī’s conduct as evidence concerning the fighting of rebels acting on an interpretation. He said:

“Are Talhah and al-Zubayr to be regarded as rebels?”

Imam Ahmad replied:

“Woe to you! What else could he put forward in this matter?”

He meant that, unless al-Shāfi‘ī followed ‘Alī’s conduct in this matter, he would have no precedent from the Rightly Guided Caliphs concerning the fighting of rebels.

The second view is that both sides were correct. This rests on the position of those who say, “Every scholar exercising independent judgment is correct.” It is held by groups of speculative theologians among the Mu‘tazilah and the Ash‘ariyyah.

There is also a third view: that only one side was correct, without specifying which. Ibn Hāmid, al-Qādī, and others mentioned these three views.

This view resembles the position of those among the people of Basra, hadith scholars, and speculative theologians who withheld judgment regarding ‘Alī’s caliphate, such as the Karrāmiyyah, who say that both men were imams and permit the appointment of two caliphs.

However, Ahmad’s explicitly transmitted position is to declare anyone who withholds judgment regarding ‘Alī’s caliphate an innovator. He said:

“He is more astray than his family’s donkey.”

He ordered that such a person be shunned and forbade intermarriage with him. Neither Ahmad nor any of the imams of the Sunnah hesitated or doubted that no one had a greater claim to the truth than ‘Alī. Thus, declaring one of the two sides correct without specifying which allows the possibility that someone other than ‘Alī had a greater claim to the truth. Only a misguided innovator with an element of hostility toward ‘Alī would say this, even if acting on an interpretation. Yet it may be that

---

## Page 1761

some of them refrain from declaring anyone mistaken, just as they refrain from censuring or disparaging him, withholding comment on the disputes that arose among them. This resembles the view of those who regard both sides as correct.

The imams of the Sunnah and the hadith scholars had no doubt that ‘Alī had a greater claim to the truth and was closer to it, as the transmitted text indicates, even if they had doubts about describing the other side as unjust or rebellious. Those who described it as unjust and rebellious—on the basis of the hadith concerning ‘Ammār—regarded anyone exercising independent judgment in that matter as acting on an interpretation.

There remains the following objection: Allah Most High commanded that the rebellious group be fought, so fighting it alongside ‘Alī would have been obligatory. Yet those who abstained from fighting included a number of leading Companions, such as Sa‘d, Zayd, Ibn ‘Umar, Usāmah, Muhammad ibn Maslamah, and Abū Bakrah. They narrated texts from the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, concerning abstaining from fighting during civil strife, including his saying:

“The one who sits during it is better than the one who stands; the one who stands is better than the one who moves toward it; and the one who moves toward it is better than the one who rushes headlong into it.”

And his saying:

“Soon the best property a Muslim possesses will be sheep that he takes to the mountain peaks and places where rain falls, fleeing civil strife to safeguard his religion.”

They also narrated his instruction to someone possessing a sword during civil strife “to take a wooden sword,” and the hadith that Abū Bakrah related to al-Ahnaf ibn Qays when he intended to go and fight alongside ‘Alī, namely the Prophet’s saying, peace and blessings be upon him:

“When two Muslims confront one another with their swords, both the killer and the one killed are in the Fire.”

And the remainder of the hadith.

His following saying is also cited as evidence for this:

“Do not revert after me to disbelief, striking one another’s necks.”

This is the position of the hadith scholars and the majority of the imams of the Sunnah, so much so that he said: “Our scholars do not differ over the fact that ‘Alī’s refraining from fighting would have been better

---

## Page 1762

for him had he refrained.” This is evident from his hesitation over fighting, his dissatisfaction with it, and the remonstrance of his son al-Hasan, who said to him:

“Did I not advise you against it, Father?”

And from his own saying:
