“What an admirable stance Sa‘d ibn Mālik and ‘Abd Allāh ibn ‘Umar took! If it was righteous, its reward is indeed great; and if it was sinful, the fault in it is indeed slight.”

This conflicts with the obligation to obey him. They used this argument to urge Imam Ahmad not to recognize him as the fourth caliph. When he publicly affirmed this recognition, some of them said to him:

“If you say that he was an imam whom it was obligatory to obey, this entails disparaging Talhah and al-Zubayr, since they did not obey him but fought him instead.”

Ahmad replied:

“I have nothing to do with their warfare.”

He meant: “I do not intervene in the dispute between ‘Alī and his brothers, for it involved independent judgment and interpretation, matters of which they had greater knowledge than I do. Nor is it among the scholarly questions that concern me, requiring me to ascertain the precise circumstances of each of them. I am commanded to seek forgiveness for them and to keep my heart free of ill will toward them. I am commanded to love them and maintain allegiance to them. Their early services to Islam and their virtues cannot be disregarded.

“Yet belief in his caliphate and leadership is established by the transmitted text, and whatever is established by the transmitted text must be followed, even if some eminent figures did not follow it. Likewise, the leadership and caliphate of ‘Uthmān remained established until the end of his life, whatever interpretive grounds some may have had for withholding obedience or support from him, or for opposing him, since this was the least grave of what occurred during ‘Alī’s caliphate.”

This is the point on which the independent judgments of earlier and later scholars differed. Some maintain that fighting alongside ‘Alī was obligatory, as did those who fought alongside him, and as many maintain

---

## Page 1763

among scholars of speculative theology and juristic reasoning who wrote on fighting rebels. They held that fighting alongside him was obligatory, because obedience to him and fighting rebels were obligatory. This formulation originated with the jurists of Kūfah, whom others followed.

Others maintain that, rather, the prescribed course is to refrain from fighting during civil strife, as indicated by numerous well-known texts and practised by those who abstained from fighting. For the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, taught that:

“Refraining from fighting during civil strife is better,”

and that:

“Escaping civil strife by keeping sheep on mountaintops is better than fighting in it.”

He also forbade a man to fight in it and instructed him to take a wooden sword. Moreover, ‘Alī did not condemn those who refrained from fighting alongside him; indeed, he may even have envied their position in the end. Because of these texts, our scholars do not disagree that it would have been better for ‘Alī to have refrained from fighting. The texts explicitly state that one who remains seated during such strife is better than one who stands, and that keeping away from it is better than becoming involved in it.

They argue: The superiority of an action is shown by the superiority of its outcome. It is well known that, if they did not initiate fighting against him, then, had he not fought them, nothing more would have occurred than their withdrawal from obedience to him, which had already taken place. Yet the fighting intensified the affliction, blood was shed, hearts became estranged, the Kharijites rebelled against him, and the two arbitrators were empowered to adjudicate, until his rival came to be called Commander of the Faithful. Thus, harms arose that had not existed before the fighting, while no outweighing benefit was achieved through it. This indicates that refraining from it was better than engaging in it, for the merits of actions are determined only

---

## Page 1764

by their results and consequences. The Quran only commands fighting the transgressing group after fighting has broken out. For Allah Most High says:

“If two groups of believers fight, make peace between them. But if one of them transgresses against the other, fight the one that transgresses”—to the end of the verse.

He did not command fighting alongside either group at the outset. Rather, He commanded reconciliation and fighting the transgressing group.

If it is said that “the transgressing group” includes both initial aggression and aggression after fighting has broken out, the reply is: The verse does not command either group to fight the other; rather, it commands the rest of the believers to fight the transgressing group. The point under discussion here is simply that ‘Alī was not commanded to undertake the fighting; rather, refraining from it would have been better.

If, however, he fought because fighting was permissible, even though refraining from it was better, or because he exercised independent judgment concerning it, even though it was not in fact permissible, then the question is whether it is obligatory to fight alongside him against the transgressing group or to refrain from fighting during civil strife. This is a point of conflicting evidence and independent judgment among the scholars and those believers who fought, once it is firmly established that he and his followers were the closer of the two groups to the truth.

Two lines of reasoning are possible. First: The command to fight the transgressing group is conditional upon ability and feasibility. Fighting them takes no greater priority than fighting polytheists and disbelievers, and it is well known that this too is conditional upon ability and feasibility. The benefit intended by Islamic law may sometimes lie in winning people over through gifts, maintaining peace, and making treaties, as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, did on more than one occasion. If the ruler believes that the requisite ability exists when it does not, then refraining is, in reality, the better course.

---

## Page 1765

And whoever judges that this fighting causes more harm than benefit knows that it is fighting amid civil strife, in which obedience to the ruler is not obligatory. Obedience to him is obligatory only when the person commanded does not know, on the basis of a scriptural text, that the command entails disobedience to Allah. Whoever knows that this is fighting amid civil strife—which is better avoided than undertaken—is not obliged to set aside a specific, directly applicable text in favour of a general, unrestricted text concerning obedience to those in authority. This is especially so since Allah Most High has commanded that disputes be referred to Allah and the Messenger.

This is supported by the fact that the Messenger foretold the injustice and transgression of rulers after him, yet forbade fighting them, because doing so would be beyond people’s capacity: its harm would outweigh its benefit. Likewise, Muslims were forbidden to fight in the early days of Islam, as Allah mentions in His words:

“Have you not seen those who were told, ‘Restrain your hands’?”

Similarly, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and his Companions were commanded to endure patiently the harm inflicted by the polytheists and hypocrites, and to pardon and overlook their offences until Allah brought His command.

The second line of reasoning is that the group became transgressing as events unfolded, through such overt acts as appointing a ruler and calling him Commander of the Believers, cursing the rightful ruler, and similar acts. These constitute transgression, unlike the fighting that preceded them, which was fighting amid civil strife.

Allah, glorified is He, mentioned the fighting between the two groups of believers, then said:

“But if one of them transgresses against the other”.

Since He commanded fighting when one of the two warring groups transgresses, this indicates that one of those groups may be transgressing at one stage but not at another.

Thus, the texts enjoining abstention from fighting amid civil strife would apply before the transgression, while those describing the group as transgressing would apply thereafter. Fighting alongside ‘Alī would then be obligatory once

---

## Page 1766

transgression had occurred. On this basis, what Ibn ‘Umar reported is interpreted in this light, “if taken to refer to fighting in that situation.”

After the arbitration, partisan alignment, and the emergence of transgression, ‘Alī did not fight them, nor did his partisans obey him in fighting. From that point onward, these partisans were censured for withholding support when it was obligatory. It was at that time that they were called Shī‘a (partisans). They then became blameworthy for disobeying the ruler whom they were obliged to obey: the Commander of the Believers, ‘Alī ibn Abī Tālib. By failing to give him the support they owed him, they became people of falsehood and injustice, for this can arise either from abandoning what is right or from exceeding its bounds.

At that point, the partisans of ‘Uthmān who were with Mu‘āwiyah became superior to them; hence their victory over them. Thus the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“A group of my community will continue to prevail over those who oppose them.”

Mu‘āwiyah cited this as evidence, and Mālik ibn Yukhāmir stood up; it was reported from Mu‘ādh ibn Jabal that they were in Syria.

‘Alī was one of the Rightly Guided Caliphs, whereas Mu‘āwiyah was the first of the kings. The question therefore belongs to this category: fighting powerful kings alongside people committed to justice and to following the way of the Rightly Guided Caliphs. Many people hasten to call for this, believing that it will establish justice, while overlooking the fact that this is not achievable and that its harm would instead outweigh its benefit.

For this reason, the position of the scholars of hadith was to refrain from armed rebellion against transgressing kings and to endure their injustice patiently until a righteous person finds rest or people are relieved of a wicked person. This may be one of the Quran’s subtle wisdoms: it did not command fighting at the outset, but commanded fighting the transgressing group only after the two groups had fought, and commanded reconciliation between them. For if two groups from among the people of

---

## Page 1767

partisan passions—such as Qays and Yemen—fight one another, they must be reconciled, for the verse was revealed concerning situations of this kind. If reconciliation fails, the ruler and the Muslims must fight the transgressing group, because they are capable of doing so and must therefore fulfil this duty. This shows why the position adopted at the outset is preferable. In the first situation, there was neither full capacity to fight nor manifest transgression; in the second, transgression had occurred, while the inability to act had become more severe. His was, without qualification, the party more entitled to the truth and closer to it, whereas the other was described as transgressing, as stated in the authentic hadith narrated by Abū Sa‘īd cited earlier.

Mu‘āwiyah, al-Mughīrah and others used the following saying of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, recorded in both Sahīh collections, as evidence for the superiority of the Syrian party:

“A group of my community will continue to uphold Allah’s command, unharmed by those who oppose them or abandon them, until the Hour comes.”

Mālik ibn Yukhāmir then stood up and said, “I heard Mu‘ādh ibn Jabal say, ‘They are in Syria.’” Mu‘āwiyah said, “Here is Mālik ibn Yukhāmir stating that he heard Mu‘ādh say, ‘They are in Syria.’”

This report appears in both Sahīh collections through Mu‘āwiyah. Both also contain a similar report through al-Mughīrah ibn Shu‘bah, in which the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“A group of my community will continue to prevail upon the truth until Allah’s command comes, while they remain in that state.”

They use this as evidence for the superiority of the people of Syria in two ways. The first is that they were the ones who prevailed, gained victory, and assumed authority after the fighting and turmoil. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Those who oppose them will not harm them.”

This implies

---

## Page 1768

that the group in this community which upholds the truth is the one that prevails and is granted victory. Since these people gained victory, they were therefore the people of truth.

The second is that the texts specify that they are in Syria, as in the statement of Mu‘ādh and the report recorded by Muslim in his Sahīh, from Abū Hurayrah, that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“The people of the west will continue to prevail.”

Imam Ahmad said, “The people of the west are the people of Syria.”

This is because the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, resided in Medina: whatever lies west of it was west relative to him, and whatever lies east of it was east relative to him. He used to call the inhabitants of Najd and the lands to the east “the people of the east,” as Ibn ‘Umar related:

“Two men from the people of the east came and delivered speeches. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said, ‘Indeed, some eloquence is like magic.’”

Reports from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, have been widely transmitted stating that evil originates in the east, such as his saying:

“Turmoil comes from here! Turmoil comes from here!”

He was pointing towards the east. He also said, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“The fountainhead of disbelief lies towards the east.”

There are other similar reports.

He thus stated that the victorious group of his community which upholds the truth is in the west—that is, Syria and the lands west of it—whereas turmoil and the fountainhead of disbelief are in the east. The people of Medina used to call the people of Syria “the people of the west.” They would say of al-Awzā‘ī that he was the imam of the people of the west, and of Sufyān al-Thawrī and others like him that he was an easterner, an imam of the people of the east.

This is because Syria’s furthest boundary, at the Euphrates, lies in alignment with the city of the Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, both having the same longitude. Beyond that, Harrān, al-Raqqah and similar places lie in alignment with Mecca. For this reason, their direction of prayer was the most accurately aligned of

---

## Page 1769

all directions of prayer, meaning that they face the Syrian corner of the Ka‘bah with the northern celestial pole directly behind them, without turning to the right, as the people of Iraq do, or to the left, as the people of Syria do.

They said: Since these texts indicate that the group within his community which upholds the truth, unharmed by those who oppose or abandon it, is in Syria, this conflicts with his sayings:

“‘Ammār will be killed by the rebellious group.”

“Of the two groups, the one closer to the truth will kill them.”

This forms part of the argument of those who consider both sides equal and both correct, or who refrain from giving preference to either—the latter being closer to the truth. Some of these have used this argument against those others. However, this view is to be rejected. It is among the views of the Nāsibīs, standing opposite the views of the Shī‘ah and Rāfidīs, these followers of their own desires. Here, however, we are addressing people of knowledge and justice.

There is no doubt that these texts must be reconciled and harmonized. Thus, we say: As for his saying, may Allah bless him and grant him peace,

“The people of the west will continue to prevail,”

and similar statements indicating that the people of Syria would prevail and gain victory, this is indeed what happened and how matters stood: they continued to prevail and gain victory.

As for his saying, peace be upon him,

“A group of my community will continue to uphold Allah’s command,”

and the mention of those who prevail, this does not imply that there cannot be among them people guilty of rebellion, or people less entitled to the truth than others. Rather, both are to be found among them.

As for his saying,

“Of the two groups, the one closer to the truth will kill them,”

this is evidence that ‘Alī

---

## Page 1770

and those with him were closer to the truth at that time than the other group. If a person or group is less meritorious in certain circumstances, this does not prevent them from upholding Allah’s command or from prevailing through upholding His command in obedience to Allah and His Messenger. An act may constitute obedience, while another act constitutes greater obedience.

As for some of them being guilty of rebellion at certain times, when their rebellion was a forgiven error or a forgiven sin, this likewise does not contradict what the texts attest. For the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, spoke of the people of Syria as a whole and of their greatness. There is no doubt that, taken as a whole, they were superior in most circumstances.

Likewise, during his caliphate, ‘Umar ibn al-Khattāb preferred them to the people of Iraq. He visited Syria more than once but refrained from going to Iraq; he sought advice and was advised not to go there. Similarly, when he was dying after being stabbed, he first admitted the people of Medina, who were then the best of the community, then the people of Syria, and then the people of Iraq, who were the last to enter his presence. This is how it is reported in al-Sahīh.

Likewise, Abū Bakr al-Siddīq devoted greater attention to conquering Syria than to conquering Iraq, to the point that he said:

“A single village among the villages of Syria is dearer to me than the conquest of a city in Iraq.”

The texts in Allah’s Book, the Sunnah of His Messenger, and the reports of his Companions concerning the superiority of Syria and the people of the west over Najd, Iraq, and the rest of the people of the east are too numerous to mention here. Indeed, from

---

## Page 1771

the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, come authentic texts censuring the east and reports “that strife and the fountainhead of disbelief come from it,” which this is not the place to discuss. The east’s superiority over them was due solely to the presence of the Commander of the Faithful, ‘Alī, and that was a temporary circumstance. Thus, after ‘Alī was gone, such strife, hypocrisy, apostasy, and religious innovations appeared among them as demonstrate that the others were superior.

Likewise, there is no doubt that among their individual scholars and righteous people are those superior to many of the people of Syria. Thus, ‘Alī, Ibn Mas‘ūd, ‘Ammār, Hudhayfah, and others like them were superior to most of the Companions in Syria. Yet comparing groups as a whole and preferring one does not preclude the other group from possessing a particular advantage.

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, distinguished the people of Syria by their continual upholding of Allah’s command until the end of time, and by the victorious group remaining among them until the end of time. This foretells a lasting, continuous state among them, together with their numbers and strength. No Islamic land other than Syria possesses this distinction. For in the Hijaz—the birthplace of faith—knowledge, faith, victory, and jihad have diminished in later times, as they have in Yemen, Iraq, and the east. As for Syria, knowledge and faith have always remained there, along with those who fight for that faith, granted victory and support at all times. Such is the case, and Allah knows best.

This demonstrates the superiority of the Syrian group in certain respects, even though ‘Alī had a greater claim

---

## Page 1772

to the truth than those who parted from him, and even though ‘Ammār was killed by the transgressing faction, as the texts state. We must therefore believe in everything that has come from Allah and acknowledge the truth in its entirety. We must neither follow our own inclinations nor speak without knowledge; rather, we must follow the paths of knowledge and justice. This is what it means to follow the Quran and the Sunnah. Adhering to some of the truth while disregarding the rest is the source of division and disagreement.

Thus, when groups of jurists held that fighting alongside ‘Alī was obligatory, they made this a “legal rule”: if a faction rises against the ruler on the basis of a defensible interpretation, while it is with him, the ruler is to write to them. If they mention an injustice, he must remove it; if they raise a doubt, he must clarify it. If they desist, well and good; otherwise, fighting them becomes obligatory upon him and the Muslims.

They then brought “al-Siddīq’s fighting those who withheld zakat” and “‘Alī’s fighting the Kharijites who broke away from the religion” under this rule. When considering kings, caliphs, and others who govern the Muslims, they came to designate whoever they believed entitled to that position as the just side, and those fighting him as rebels. They do not distinguish between fighting in civil strife—which is forbidden, and which it is better to refrain from than to engage in, as occurs among kings, caliphs, others, and their followers, such as the fighting between al-Amīn and al-Ma’mūn—and fighting the Harūriyya “Kharijites,” apostates, and hypocrites, “such as the Mazdakites,” and others like them.

You will find that this view originated with some of the jurists of Kufa and their followers, then passed to al-Shāfi‘ī and his companions, and then to many of Ahmad’s companions who wrote the chapter on fighting rebels. They followed the model of those earlier jurists; this is how you will find them proceeding. For al-Khiraqī followed the model of

---

## Page 1773

al-Muzanī, while al-Muzanī followed the model of Muhammad ibn al-Hasan’s abridgment, even if only in some aspects of its chapter divisions and arrangement.

Those who write on legal rulings discuss fighting rebels and Kharijites together. Yet there is no hadith from the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, concerning “fighting rebels” except the report transmitted by Kawthar ibn Hakīm from Nāfi‘, which is fabricated.

As for hadith collections such as Sahīh al-Bukhārī and the Sunan collections, they discuss only fighting apostates and the Kharijites, who are adherents of deviant doctrines. The same applies to books on the Sunnah transmitting the statements of Imam Ahmad and others like him. Likewise—as far as I believe—the books of Mālik and his companions contain no chapter on fighting rebels; they mention only apostates and adherents of deviant doctrines.

This is the principle established by the Book of Allah and the Sunnah of His Messenger: the distinction between fighting those who depart from the revealed law and the Sunnah—which is what the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, commanded—and fighting those whose only departure is from obedience to a particular ruler. The scriptural texts contain no command to engage in the latter. Thus, the jurists mentioned earlier fell into three objectionable errors:

The first: fighting someone who withdraws from obedience to a particular king, even if he is close to that king and comparable to him—with regard to the Sunnah and the revealed law—because division has occurred. Yet division itself is civil strife.

---

## Page 1774

The second: equating these people with those who have apostatized by abandoning some of the laws of Islam.

The third: equating fighting these people with fighting the Kharijites, who pass out of Islam as an arrow passes through its quarry. Hence you find that this group indulges many of the whims of kings and rulers, and commands people to fight alongside them against their enemies, on the grounds that they constitute the just party while their opponents are rebels. In this they resemble those who are partisan toward certain leading scholars, speculative theologians, or shaykhs against their peers, claiming that the truth lies with their preferred leaders or that their position carries greater weight. Such claims arise from personal inclination, which may involve an interpretation born of negligence rather than diligent scholarly judgment. This is common among the community’s scholars, worshippers, rulers, and soldiers. It is part of the internal strife that has not been lifted from this community. We therefore ask Allah to make us just, for there is neither power nor strength except through Him. This is why the most just of all groups are Ahl al-Sunnah, the adherents of hadith.

You find that when these people are commanded to fight those who have passed out of Islam or apostatized by abandoning some of its laws, they direct that the conduct of ‘Alī in fighting Talhah and al-Zubayr be followed: none of their children are to be taken captive, none of their property taken as spoils, none of their wounded finished off, and none of their prisoners killed. They abandon what the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, commanded and ‘Alī practised in fighting the Kharijites, and what Allah commanded His Messenger and al-Siddīq practised in fighting those who withheld zakat. Thus they do not group together those whom Allah has distinguished—apostates and those who pass out of Islam, on the one hand, and Muslims who act wrongfully, on the other—yet they distinguish between those whom Allah has grouped together: kings and leaders fighting one another for sovereignty, even when acting on an interpretation.

And Allah, glorified and exalted is He, knows best.

---

## Page 1775

The Shaykh—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

When did “Mu‘āwiyah ibn Abī Sufyān” embrace Islam? Was his faith like that of others or not? What else has been said about him?

He replied:

The faith of “Mu‘āwiyah ibn Abī Sufyān”—may Allah be pleased with him—is established by mass-transmitted reports and the consensus of the scholars, just as is the faith of others like him who embraced Islam in the year of the conquest of Mecca: his brother “Yazīd” ibn Abī Sufyān, Suhayl ibn ‘Amr, Safwān ibn Umayyah, ‘Ikrimah ibn Abī Jahl, al-Hārith ibn Hishām, Abū Asad ibn Abī al-‘Ās ibn Umayyah, and others like them.

These people are called “the released ones,” for they embraced Islam in the year when the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, conquered Mecca by force. He released them, bestowed favours and gifts upon them, and sought to win their hearts.

It has been reported that Mu‘āwiyah ibn Abī Sufyān embraced Islam before that and emigrated, just as Khālid ibn al-Walīd, ‘Amr ibn al-‘Ās, and ‘Uthmān ibn Talhah al-Hajabī embraced Islam before the conquest of Mecca and emigrated to Medina. If this report is authentic, then he is one of the Emigrants.

---

## Page 1776

As for his being a Muslim in the year of the conquest, together with those mentioned, the scholars agree on this, whether he had embraced Islam earlier or only in the year of the conquest of Mecca. Yet some liars have claimed that he reproached his father for embracing Islam. The scholars of hadith unanimously agree that this is a lie.

Those mentioned were among the finest people in their practice of Islam and the most praiseworthy in their conduct. They were not accused of wrongdoing, nor did any scholar accuse them of hypocrisy, as others were accused. Rather, their exemplary practice of Islam, obedience to Allah and His Messenger, love for Allah and His Messenger, striving in Allah’s cause, and observance of the limits set by Allah demonstrated the soundness of their inward faith and the excellence of their practice of Islam.

The Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, appointed some of them to positions of authority as his deputies. For example, he appointed ‘Attāb ibn Asīd as governor of Mecca on his behalf. He was among the finest Muslims and used to say: “People of Mecca! By Allah, if I learn that any of you has stayed away from prayer, I will have him beheaded.”

The Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, also appointed “Abū Sufyān” ibn Harb—Mu‘āwiyah’s father—as his deputy over Najrān. When the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, died, Abū Sufyān was still his governor over Najrān. The scholars agree that Mu‘āwiyah was better in his practice of Islam than his father, just as his brother “Yazīd ibn Abī Sufyān” was superior to both him and his father.

For this reason, Abū Bakr al-Siddīq, may Allah be pleased with him, appointed Yazīd to lead the fighting against the Christians during his conquest of Syria. He was one of the commanders appointed by Abū Bakr al-Siddīq, who gave him well-known instructions that the scholars transmitted and relied upon, and that were recorded by

---

## Page 1777

Mālik in al-Muwatta’ and elsewhere. Abū Bakr, may Allah be pleased with him, walked beside his mount to see him off. Yazīd said to him: “Successor to the Messenger of Allah, either you must ride or I must dismount.” He replied: “You will not dismount, and I will not ride. I seek reward for these steps in the cause of Allah, Mighty and Majestic.”

‘Amr ibn al-‘Ās was also one of the commanders, as was Abū ‘Ubaydah ibn al-Jarrāh. Abū Bakr placed Khālid ibn al-Walīd over them because of his courage and effectiveness in jihad. When Abū Bakr died, ‘Umar ibn al-Khattāb appointed Abū ‘Ubaydah commander over them all. ‘Umar ibn al-Khattāb, may Allah be pleased with him, was stern in Allah’s cause, so he appointed Abū ‘Ubaydah because he was gentle. Abū Bakr, may Allah be pleased with him, was gentle, whereas Khālid was stern towards the disbelievers. Thus, the gentle man appointed the stern man, and the stern man appointed the gentle man, so that a balance would be maintained. Each did what was most beloved to Allah Most High in his particular circumstances.

Our Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, is the most perfect of all creation. He was stern towards the disbelievers and hypocrites, and Allah Most High described him in terms of the most perfect religious laws, just as Allah Most High said in describing his community:

“Firm against the disbelievers, compassionate among themselves.”

He also said of them:

“Humble towards the believers, mighty against the disbelievers, striving in the cause of Allah and fearing no blame from anyone.”

It is established in the Sahīh:

“When the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, consulted his Companions about the captives of Badr, Abū Bakr advised him to accept ransom from them and release them, while ‘Umar advised him to have them beheaded. The Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said: ‘Allah softens the hearts of some men for His sake until they become softer than cloth, and He makes the hearts of other men firm for His sake until they become harder than rock. Your likeness, Abū Bakr, is that of Abraham, Allah’s intimate friend, when he said: “Whoever follows me is indeed of me, and whoever

---

## Page 1778

disobeys me—You are indeed Forgiving, Merciful.” And that of Jesus son of Mary, when he said:

“If You punish them, they are Your servants; but if You forgive them, You are indeed the Almighty, the All-Wise.”

Your likeness, ‘Umar, is that of Noah, peace be upon him, when he said:

“My Lord, do not leave a single disbeliever on the earth.”

And that of Moses son of ‘Imrān, when he said:

“Our Lord, obliterate their wealth and harden their hearts, so that they will not believe until they see the painful punishment.”’”

Throughout the Prophet’s lifetime, peace and blessings be upon him, the two were as the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, had described them. They were his two ministers from among the people of the earth. It is established in the Sahīh, on the authority of Ibn ‘Abbās, may Allah be pleased with them both:

“When the bier of ‘Umar ibn al-Khattāb, may Allah be pleased with him, was set down and people came to offer the funeral prayer over him, Ibn ‘Abbās said: ‘I turned around and there was ‘Alī ibn Abī Tālib, may Allah be pleased with him. He said: “By Allah, there is no one on the face of the earth whose deeds I would rather meet Allah Most High with than those of this deceased man. By Allah, I hope that Allah will gather you with your two companions, for I often heard the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, say: ‘I entered with Abū Bakr and ‘Umar; I went out with Abū Bakr and ‘Umar; and I went with Abū Bakr and ‘Umar.’”’”

It is also established in the Sahīh:

“On the day of Uhud, most of the Muslims fled. Then there was Abū Sufyān—and the people were the object sought (1)—when he said: ‘Is Muhammad among the people? Is Muhammad among the people? Is Muhammad among the people?’

The Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said: ‘Do not answer him.’ Then he said: ‘Is there among

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) Thus in the original.

---

## Page 1779

the people Ibn Abī Quhāfah? Is Ibn Abī Quhāfah among the people? Is Ibn Abī Quhāfah among the people?’

The Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said: ‘Do not answer him.’ He then asked: ‘Is Ibn al-Khattāb among the people? Is Ibn al-Khattāb among the people? Is Ibn al-Khattāb among the people?’

The Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said: ‘Do not answer him.’” The hadith continues at length.

Thus Abū Sufyān, the leader of the confederates, asked only about these three: the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, Abū Bakr and ‘Umar, may Allah be pleased with them both, because he knew that they were the leaders of the Muslim army.

Al-Rashīd said to Mālik ibn Anas: “Tell me about the standing of Abū Bakr and ‘Umar in relation to the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him.” He replied: “Their position in relation to him during his lifetime was like their position after his death.” Al-Rashīd said: “You have set my mind at rest, Mālik.”

When the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, passed away and Abū Bakr was appointed his successor, Allah Most High endowed him with a firmness he had not possessed before, to the point that he surpassed ‘Umar in this respect. He fought the apostates after equipping Usāmah’s army. This perfected his character in keeping with the perfection of the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, whose successor he had become.

When ‘Umar was appointed successor, Allah endowed him with a tenderness and mercy he had not possessed before, perfecting his character so that he became Commander of the Faithful. This is why the former appointed Khālid, while the latter appointed Abū ‘Ubaydah.

Yazīd ibn Abī Sufyān governed Syria until ‘Umar took office. Yazīd ibn Abī Sufyān then died, and ‘Umar appointed Mu‘āwiyah in place of his brother Yazīd ibn Abī Sufyān. And Mu‘āwiyah remained

---

## Page 1780

in his governorship throughout ‘Umar’s caliphate. ‘Umar and his subjects praised him and his conduct toward them, supported him and loved him for the forbearance and justice they had witnessed in him. Indeed, none of them complained about him or alleged that he had wronged them.

Yazīd ibn Mu‘āwiyah was not one of the Companions of the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him; he was born during ‘Uthmān’s caliphate. He was named Yazīd after his uncle, who was one of the Companions.

Mu‘āwiyah, his brother Yazīd, Suhayl ibn ‘Amr, al-Hārith ibn Hishām and others who embraced Islam at the conquest of Mecca participated with the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, in the expedition of Hunayn. They were included in the words of Allah Most High:

“Then Allah sent down His tranquillity upon His Messenger and upon the believers and sent down soldiers whom you did not see and punished those who disbelieved. And that is the recompense of the disbelievers.”

They were among the believers upon whom Allah sent down His tranquillity alongside the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him. They also participated in the expedition of al-Tā’if, when they besieged al-Tā’if and he bombarded it with a catapult. They also faced the Christians in Syria, and Allah revealed Sūrat Barā’ah during that expedition.

This was the Expedition of Hardship, in which ‘Uthmān ibn ‘Affān, may Allah be pleased with him, equipped the Army of Hardship with a thousand camels in the cause of Allah Most High. When these proved insufficient, he completed the provision with fifty more camels. The Prophet said:

“Nothing ‘Uthmān does after today will harm him.”

This was the last of the Prophet’s military expeditions, peace and blessings be upon him, and no fighting took place during it. The Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, personally took part in more than twenty military expeditions, and did not

---

## Page 1781

fight except in nine expeditions: Badr, Uhud, Banū al-Mustaliq, the Trench, Dhū Qarad, and the expedition of al-Tā’if. The largest army the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, assembled was at Hunayn and al-Tā’if, numbering twelve thousand. The largest army to accompany the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, on an expedition was the army of Tabūk: it was so numerous that it could not be counted, but no fighting took place during that expedition.

Those mentioned above are included in the words of Allah Most High:

“Not equal among you are those who spent and fought before the conquest; they are greater in rank than those who spent and fought afterward. Yet Allah has promised the best reward to all.”

For these men who were granted amnesty and embraced Islam at the conquest were among those who spent and fought after the conquest, and Allah promised them the best reward. They spent at Hunayn and al-Tā’if and fought in both expeditions—may Allah be pleased with them.

They are also included among those with whom Allah is pleased, as He says:

“The foremost pioneers among the Emigrants and the Helpers, and those who followed them in righteousness—Allah is pleased with them, and they are pleased with Him.”

The pioneers were those who embraced Islam before al-Hudaybiyah, such as those who pledged allegiance to him beneath the tree, concerning whom Allah revealed:

“Allah was certainly pleased with the believers when they pledged allegiance to you beneath the tree.”

They numbered more than fourteen hundred, and all of them are among the people of Paradise, as authentically reported from the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, who said:

“No one who pledged allegiance beneath the tree will enter the Fire.”

Among them was Hātib ibn Abī Balta‘ah, and he had committed sins

---

## Page 1782

that were well known, such as his correspondence with the polytheists conveying information about the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, and his mistreatment of his slaves. It is authentically reported:

“His slave came to the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, and said, ‘By Allah, O Messenger of Allah, Hātib will surely enter the Fire.’ The Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, replied, ‘You have lied. He was present at Badr and al-Hudaybiyah.’”

It is also authentically reported:

“When he wrote to the polytheists informing them that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, was marching against them, he sent ‘Alī ibn Abī Tālib and al-Zubayr to the woman carrying the letter, and they brought her back. He said, ‘What is this, Hātib?’ Hātib replied, ‘By Allah, O Messenger of Allah, I did not do this out of apostasy from my religion, nor did I embrace disbelief after Islam. Rather, I was merely affiliated with Quraysh, not one of them. Your Companions who were with you had relatives in Mecca through whom they could protect their families. Since I lacked that, I wanted to do them a favor so that they would protect my relatives in return.’ ‘Umar ibn al-Khattāb said, ‘Let me strike off this hypocrite’s head.’ The Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, replied, ‘He was present at Badr. For all you know, Allah may have said, “Do whatever you wish; I have forgiven you.”’”

This hadith shows that, by virtue of their early commitment, faith, and striving in His cause, Allah forgives these pioneers—such as those who were present at Badr and al-Hudaybiyah—grave sins for which no one is permitted to punish them, just as Hātib did not have to be punished for what he had done.

This is also cited as evidence that, regarding what occurred between ‘Alī, Talhah, al-Zubayr, and others like them:

---

## Page 1783

it was either an exercise of independent judgment involving no sin, in which case there is nothing further to say. For it is authentically reported that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“When a judge exercises independent judgment and reaches the correct decision, he receives two rewards; when he exercises independent judgment and errs, he receives one reward.”

If, however, there was a sin, it has been established that Allah is pleased with these men and has forgiven them for what they did. Thus, whatever sins they committed—if any sin was indeed committed—will not harm them. Rather, if any of them committed a sin, Allah had erased it through one of the means by which He expiates sins. For example, he may have repented and Allah accepted his repentance; he may have had good deeds that erase evil deeds; or Allah may have expiated his sin through an affliction He visited upon him. For it is authentically reported that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“No fatigue, illness, worry, distress, sorrow, or harm befalls a believer without Allah thereby expiating some of his sins.”

As for those who came after these earliest pioneers—namely, those who embraced Islam after al-Hudaybiyah—they are included in the words of Allah Most High:

“To each Allah has promised the best reward.”

And in His words:

“And those who followed them in righteousness—Allah is pleased with them, and they are pleased with Him.”

Khālid ibn al-Walīd, ‘Amr ibn al-‘Ās, ‘Uthmān ibn Talhah al-Hajabī, and others embraced Islam before the conquest of Mecca. The people of Tā’if embraced Islam after those who had been set free, and they were the last people to embrace Islam. Among them was ‘Uthmān ibn Abī al-‘Ās al-Thaqafī, whom the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, appointed to govern the people of Tā’if. He was among the finest Companions, despite having embraced Islam late.

---

## Page 1784

For a man may embrace Islam later yet be superior to some who embraced it before him. Such was the case with ‘Umar: it is said that his conversion brought the number to forty, yet Allah favoured him over many who had embraced Islam before him. ‘Uthmān, Talhah, al-Zubayr, Sa‘d, and ‘Abd al-Rahmān ibn ‘Awf had embraced Islam before ‘Umar through Abū Bakr, yet ‘Umar surpassed them in merit.

The first free adult man to embrace Islam was Abū Bakr; the first free boy was ‘Alī; the first among the clients was Zayd ibn Hārithah; and the first woman was Khadījah, the Mother of the Believers. The scholars agree on this.

Allah Most High said:

“Indeed, those who believed, emigrated, and strove with their wealth and their lives in the cause of Allah, and those who gave shelter and support—they are allies of one another.”

The passage continues to His words:

“And those who believed, emigrated, and strove in the cause of Allah, and those who gave shelter and support—they are the true believers. For them are forgiveness and a generous provision.”

“And those who believed afterwards, emigrated, and strove alongside you—they are of you.”

This is general in scope.

He Most High also said:

“For the poor emigrants who were driven from their homes and their possessions, seeking bounty from Allah and His pleasure, and supporting Allah and His Messenger—they are the truthful.”

“And those who had settled in the city and embraced the faith before them love those who emigrated to them and feel no resentment in their hearts over what they were given. They give them preference over themselves, even though they themselves are in need. And whoever is protected from the greed of his own soul—it is they who are successful.”

“And those who came after them say, ‘Our Lord, forgive us and our brothers

---

## Page 1785

who preceded us in faith, and put no resentment in our hearts toward those who have believed. Our Lord, indeed You are Kind and Merciful.’”

This verse and the preceding one encompass all who come within their scope after the earliest forerunners, until the Day of Resurrection. How, then, could they exclude the Companions of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, who believed in him and strove alongside him?

He, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said in an authentic hadith:

“The emigrant is one who abandons what Allah has forbidden.”

Thus, whoever among those freed at the conquest of Mecca had embraced Islam and abandoned what Allah had forbidden fulfilled the meaning of this migration and was included in His words, Most High:

“And those who believed afterwards, emigrated, and strove alongside you—they are of you.”

Likewise, he was included in His words, Most High:

“To each Allah has promised the finest reward.”

He Most High also said:

“Muhammad is the Messenger of Allah, and those with him are firm against the disbelievers and merciful among themselves. You see them bowing and prostrating, seeking bounty from Allah and His pleasure. Their mark is on their faces from the effect of prostration. That is their description in the Torah. And their description in the Gospel is like a crop that sends forth its shoots, which strengthen it until it grows thick and stands upright on its stalks, delighting the sowers—so that through them He may enrage the disbelievers. Allah has promised those among them who believe and do righteous deeds forgiveness and a great reward.”

This encompasses, without restriction, those who believed alongside the Messenger. It has been widely transmitted from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, in the collections of authentic hadith and elsewhere, through more than one

---

## Page 1786

chain of transmission, that he said:

“The best generation is the generation among whom I was sent, then those who follow them, then those who follow them.”

It is also authentically reported from him in the Sahīh:

“There was a dispute between ‘Abd al-Rahmān and Khālid, and he said: ‘Khālid, do not revile my Companions. By Him in whose hand is my soul, if any of you were to spend gold equal to Mount Uhud, it would not equal a mudd (a measure of food) given by any one of them, nor even half of it.’”

He said this to Khālid and others like him who embraced Islam after al-Hudaybiyya, comparing them with the earliest forerunners. He was saying that if any of them spent gold equal to Mount Uhud, it would not equal one mudd given by any of those forerunners, nor even half a mudd. Those who embraced Islam after al-Hudaybiyya were included, with this standing, in His words, Most High:

“Not equal among you are those who spent before the conquest and fought. Those are greater in rank than those who spent afterwards and fought. Yet to each Allah has promised the finest reward.”

How, then, could he come after his Companions?

Companionship is a general term encompassing anyone who kept company with the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, whether briefly or for a long time. Each person’s share of companionship, however, corresponds to its extent. Whoever kept his company for a year, a month, a day, or an hour, or saw him while being a believer, has a corresponding share of companionship. Thus, it is authentically reported in the Sahīh that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Groups of people will go forth to battle, and they will ask: ‘Is there anyone among you who kept company with the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace?’ In another wording: ‘Is there anyone among you who saw the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace?’ They will say, ‘Yes,’ and victory will be granted to them. Then groups of people will go forth to battle, and they will ask: ‘Is there anyone among you who kept company with someone who kept company with the Messenger of Allah

---

## Page 1787

, may Allah bless him and grant him peace?’ In another wording: ‘Is there anyone among you who saw someone who saw the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace?’ They will say, ‘Yes,’ and victory will be granted to them. Then groups of people will go forth to battle, and they will ask: ‘Is there anyone among you who saw someone who saw someone who saw the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace?’ In another wording: ‘Who kept company with someone who kept company with someone who kept company with the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace?’ They will say, ‘Yes,’ and victory will be granted to them.”

In some chains of transmission, a fourth generation is likewise mentioned. Thus, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, linked this outcome both to companionship with him and to having seen him. He stated that Allah grants victory to the Muslims on account of someone who saw him while believing in him. This distinction belongs to no one other than the Companions, even if others’ deeds exceed those of an individual Companion of his, may Allah bless him and grant him peace.

---

## Page 1788

Section:

Once this is clear, it is well known that the means by which the faith of an individual Companion is established are the same as those by which the faith of his peers is established. Likewise, the means by which his companionship with the Prophet is established are the same as those by which the companionship of others like him is established.

Those granted amnesty who embraced Islam in the year of the conquest include Mu‘āwiya and his brother Yazīd, ‘Ikrima ibn Abī Jahl, Safwān ibn Umayya, al-Hārith ibn Hishām, and Suhayl ibn ‘Amr. Their acceptance of Islam and their adherence to it until death are established among specialists through mass transmission.

Mu‘āwiya’s adherence to Islam was more evident than that of others, for he held authority for forty years: twenty years as a governor under ‘Umar and ‘Uthmān, including his time in office during the caliphate of ‘Alī, may Allah be pleased with him, and twenty years as an independent ruler. He assumed authority in the year sixty, fifty years after the death of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace.

Al-Hasan ibn ‘Alī, may Allah be pleased with them both, relinquished authority to him in the year forty, known as the Year of Unity because the Muslims became united and the strife among them came to an end. This action of al-Hasan, may Allah be pleased with him, was among the things for which the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, praised him, as is established in Sahīh al-Bukhārī and elsewhere, on the authority of Abū Bakr—may Allah be pleased with him—that the Prophet

---

## Page 1789

may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“This son of mine is a leader, and through him Allah will reconcile two great groups of Muslims.”

Thus, one of the reasons the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, praised and commended his son al-Hasan was that Allah Most High reconciled two great groups of Muslims through him. This occurred when he handed authority over to Mu‘āwiya, after each had marched against the other with great armies. Since the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, praised al-Hasan for bringing about reconciliation and abandoning fighting, this indicates that reconciling those two parties was more beloved to Allah Most High than their fighting. It therefore indicates that their fighting was not commanded. Had Mu‘āwiya been an unbeliever, appointing an unbeliever to authority and handing power over to him would not have been something Allah and His Messenger loved. Rather, the hadith indicates that Mu‘āwiya and his followers were believers, just as al-Hasan and his followers were believers, and that al-Hasan’s action was praiseworthy in the sight of Allah Most High, beloved and pleasing to Him and His Messenger.

This accords with what is established from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, in the two Sahīh collections, in the hadith of Abū Sa‘īd al-Khudrī, that he said:

“A breakaway faction will emerge at a time of division among the people, and the one of the two parties with the greater claim to the truth will kill them.” In another wording: “Those closest to the truth will kill them.”

This authentic hadith is evidence that both warring parties—‘Alī and his followers, and Mu‘āwiya and his followers—had truth on their side, and that ‘Alī and his followers were closer to the truth than Mu‘āwiya and his followers. For it was ‘Alī ibn Abī Tālib who fought the breakaway faction: the “Harūrī Kharijites,” who had been among ‘Alī’s supporters, then rebelled against him, declared him and those who supported him unbelievers, showed hostility towards him, and fought him and those with him. It is they about whom the Prophet spoke

---

## Page 1790

may Allah bless him and grant him peace, in authentic, widely transmitted hadiths—indeed, mass-transmitted ones—in which he said of them:

“Any one of you would consider his prayer insignificant compared with theirs, his fasting compared with theirs, and his recitation compared with theirs. They recite the Quran, but it does not pass beyond their throats. They pass out of Islam as an arrow passes through its quarry. Wherever you encounter them, kill them, for killing them brings a reward with Allah on the Day of Resurrection. Their distinguishing sign is that among them is a man with malformed hands and a quivering fleshy growth bearing a few hairs.”

These are the people who showed hostility towards ‘Alī and those who supported him. They considered it lawful to kill him and declared him an unbeliever. One of their leaders, “ ‘Abd al-Rahmān ibn Muljim al-Murādī,” killed him. When these enemies of ‘Alī—the renegade Kharijites—claimed that ‘Uthmān, ‘Alī ibn Abī Tālib, and those with them were unbelievers and apostates, the Muslims’ arguments against them included the mass-transmitted evidence of the Companions’ faith, together with what is established in the Book and the authentic Sunnah concerning Allah Most High’s praise and commendation of them, His pleasure with them, His declaration that they are among the people of Paradise, and other such texts. Whoever does not accept these proofs cannot establish the faith of ‘Alī ibn Abī Tālib and others like him.

For if this enemy of ‘Alī were to tell a Rāfidī that ‘Alī was an unbeliever, or a sinful wrongdoer; that he fought for dominion, seeking leadership rather than serving the religion; that at the Battle of the Camel, Siffīn, and Harūrā’, he killed thousands upon thousands of “members of the faith” from the community of Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace; and that after the Prophet’s death, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, he neither fought an unbeliever nor conquered a city, but instead fought those who face the qiblah—and were to make other such claims, fabricated by those enemies who hated ‘Alī, may Allah be pleased with him—

---

## Page 1791

then none could answer these enemies of ‘Alī except Ahl al-Sunnah wa-l-Jamā‘ah, who love all the earliest forerunners in Islam and remain loyal to them.

They would say to them:

The faith, migration, and jihad of Abū Bakr, ‘Umar, ‘Uthmān, Talhah, al-Zubayr, and others like them are established through mass-transmitted reports. Allah’s praise of them and His pleasure with them are established in the Quran, while the Prophet’s praise of them, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, both individually and collectively, is established through authentic hadiths. These include his widely transmitted saying:

“Were I to take an intimate friend from among the people of the earth, I would take Abū Bakr as an intimate friend.”

And his saying:

“Among the nations before you were men who received divine inspiration. If there is anyone such as this in my community, it is ‘Umar.”

And his saying about ‘Uthmān:

“Should I not feel shy before someone before whom the angels feel shy?”

And his words to ‘Alī:

“I shall certainly give the banner to a man who loves Allah and His Messenger, and whom Allah and His Messenger love. Allah will grant victory through him.”

And his saying:

“Every prophet has devoted supporters, and my devoted supporter is al-Zubayr.”

There are other statements of this kind. A Rāfidī, however, cannot establish the case against those enemies who hate ‘Alī as Ahl al-Sunnah can, for they love them all.

If he says, “ ‘Alī’s acceptance of Islam is known through mass-transmitted reports,” his opponent replies, “So too is the acceptance of Islam by Abū Bakr, ‘Umar, ‘Uthmān, Mu‘āwiyah, and others. Yet you impugn either their Islam or their uprightness.”

If he says, “ ‘Alī’s faith is established by the praise of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace,” we reply to him:

“These hadiths were transmitted only by the Companions whom you impugn. Those who transmitted accounts of their virtues include Sa‘d ibn Abī

---

## Page 1792

Waqqās, ‘Ā’ishah, Sahl ibn Sa‘d al-Sā‘idī, and others like them, whom the Rāfidah impugn. If the reports of these men and women and others like them are weak, every reported virtue of ‘Alī is invalidated, and the Rāfidah have no proof. If their reports are authentic, then the virtues of ‘Alī are established, as are those of others whose virtues they narrated, such as Abū Bakr, ‘Umar, ‘Uthmān, and others.”

If the Rāfidī says, “‘Alī’s virtues are established among the Shia through mass-transmitted reports”—just as they say that his designation as imam is established through mass-transmitted reports—he is told:

“As for those ‘Shia’ who were not Companions, they neither saw the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, nor heard his words. Their reports have incomplete, disconnected chains of transmission; unless they trace them back to the Companions, they are not authentic. The Companions to whom the Rāfidah profess allegiance are only a small group—a little over ten, or roughly that number. Their transmission does not establish mass transmission, since collusion is possible among so few people. Yet the Rāfidah impugn the overwhelming majority of the Companions who transmitted accounts of their virtues. Moreover, if they consider lying and concealment possible on the part of the majority whom the Quran praises, then all the more readily may they consider these possible on the part of a small group.”

Furthermore, if the Rāfidī says, “Abū Bakr, ‘Umar, and ‘Uthmān sought leadership and kingship, and thus wronged others by assuming authority,” the reply to them is:

“These men did not fight any Muslim over authority. Rather, they fought the apostates and the disbelievers. They were the ones who shattered the power of Chosroes and Caesar, conquered the lands of Persia, established Islam, strengthened faith and its adherents, and humbled disbelief and its adherents.”

---

## Page 1793

‘Uthmān ranked below Abū Bakr and ‘Umar. Nevertheless, when they sought to kill him while he was in office, he neither fought the Muslims nor killed any Muslim to retain his authority.

If you allow that these men might have been unjust rulers and enemies of the Messenger, then the argument of the Nāsibī (one hostile to the Prophet’s family) against you is stronger still. If you malign these men and accuse them of injustice and hostility towards the Messenger and his followers, you thereby furnish the renegade Khārijite Nāsibīs with an argument against you. For they say:

“Who is more deserving of being accused of seeking leadership: someone who fought Muslims—not disbelievers—to retain his authority, initiating hostilities to make them obey him when they would not, and killing many thousands of ‘those who face the qiblah’—people who establish prayer, pay zakah, perform pilgrimage to the Ancient House, fast the month of Ramadan, and recite the Quran—or those who fought no Muslim, but instead strengthened, supported, and sheltered those who observed prayer and paid zakah? Or someone who was killed while in office, who neither fought nor defended himself, until he was killed in his own home among his family—may Allah be pleased with him?”

If you allow that someone like this might have exercised kingship unjustly and wronged the Muslims through his rule, then such an accusation is all the more applicable to someone who fought to retain authority and killed Muslims for its sake.

This and similar considerations make it clear that the Rāfidah are a community with neither sound reason, authentic transmitted evidence, acceptable religion, nor worldly success. Rather, they are among the most dishonest and ignorant sects. Their religion opens the way into the Muslim community for every heretic and apostate, as the Nusayriyyah have entered their ranks,

---

## Page 1794

and the Ismā‘īliyyah and others. They deliberately turn against the best of the Muslim community, while allying themselves with Allah’s enemies among the Jews, Christians, and polytheists. They reject manifest truth established through mass transmission, while upholding fabricated lies whose falsehood is known. They are as al-Sha‘bī—who knew them exceptionally well—described them:

“Were they beasts, they would be donkeys; were they birds, they would be vultures.”

Thus, they are among the most shameless slanderers and fabricators, as illustrated by what they relate about Mu‘āwiyah. It is established through mass transmission that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, assigned Mu‘āwiyah duties as he did others, and that Mu‘āwiyah fought alongside him. The Prophet trusted him to write down revelation for him and never suspected him of dishonesty in doing so.

‘Umar ibn al-Khattāb appointed him to office. ‘Umar was exceptionally discerning in his judgment of men, and Allah had established truth upon his tongue and in his heart; he did not suspect Mu‘āwiyah of dishonesty in his administration. The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, had also appointed his father, Abū Sufyān, to office, and he remained in that position until the Prophet died. By the Muslims’ agreement, Mu‘āwiyah was better than his father and more upright in his Islam. If the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, appointed his father, then Mu‘āwiyah’s appointment was all the more permissible and appropriate.

He was never an apostate, nor did any scholar ever accuse him of apostasy. Those who accuse such men of apostasy are the very people who attribute unworthy things to Abū Bakr, ‘Umar, ‘Uthmān, the great majority of those who fought at Badr and those who gave the Pledge of Divine Pleasure, and the other earliest pioneers among the Emigrants and the Helpers, as well as those who followed them in righteousness.

---

## Page 1795

Some of those who accuse these men of apostasy say: “He died facing east, with a cross upon his face.” Any reasonable person knows that this is among the most outrageous lies and slanders against him. Were someone to say this about Umayyad and Abbasid rulers of lesser standing than Mu‘āwiyah—such as ‘Abd al-Malik ibn Marwān and his sons, Abū Ja‘far al-Mansūr and his two sons, known as al-Mahdī and al-Hādī, al-Rashīd, and others who assumed the caliphate and governed the believers—any reasonable person would recognize someone who accused one of them of apostasy and of dying as a Christian as among the greatest fabricators. How, then, can such a thing be said about Mu‘āwiyah and other Companions like him?

Indeed, even his son Yazīd, despite the wrongs he committed, has been slandered by anyone who calls him an unbeliever and an apostate. Rather, he was a Muslim king, like other Muslim kings. Most kings have both good deeds and bad deeds: their good deeds are great, and their bad deeds are grave. Anyone who singles out one of them for condemnation while overlooking his counterparts is either ignorant or unjust.

The same considerations apply to them as to other Muslims. Some have more good deeds than bad; some have repented of their bad deeds; some have had their sins expiated by Allah; some He may admit to Paradise; some He may punish for their bad deeds; and for some Allah may accept the intercession of a prophet or another intercessor. Thus, declaring that any particular one of these men is destined for Hell is among the claims of the people of innovation and misguidance.

---

## Page 1796

Likewise, deliberately singling out any one of them for a curse is not among the deeds of the righteous and virtuous.

It is authentically established that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Allah has cursed wine, the one who presses it, the one who has it pressed, the one who carries it, the one who serves it, the one who drinks it, the one who sells it, the one who buys it, and the one who consumes its proceeds.”

It is also authentically reported:

“During the lifetime of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, there was a man called Himār who frequently drank wine. Whenever he was brought before the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, he would flog him. On one occasion, he was brought before him to be flogged, and a man said, ‘May Allah curse him! How often he is brought before the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace!’ The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said, ‘Do not curse him, for he loves Allah and His Messenger.’”

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, thus cursed wine drinkers in general, but forbade cursing an individual believer.

Likewise, we affirm what Allah Most High has said:

“Indeed, those who unjustly consume the property of orphans are only filling their bellies with fire.”

Yet no one should declare that a particular individual is destined for Hell, for he may repent, or Allah may forgive him through good deeds that erase sins, afflictions that expiate them, accepted intercession, or Allah’s pardon, or otherwise.

The same applies to an individual king or anyone else: even if he has committed injustice, that does not warrant our cursing him and declaring him destined for Hell. Whoever engages in this is among the people of innovation and misguidance. How much more so, then, when a man has great good deeds on account of which forgiveness may be hoped for him despite his injustice, as established in Sahīh al-Bukhārī, where Ibn ‘Umar reports that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace,

---

## Page 1797

said:

“The first army to campaign against Constantinople will be forgiven.”

The first army to campaign against it was commanded by “Yazīd ibn Mu‘āwiya.” Abū Ayyūb al-Ansārī was among those who accompanied him on the campaign. He died there, and his grave remains there to this day.

For this reason, the moderate imams of the early generations would say concerning Yazīd and others like him: “We neither revile them nor love them”—meaning that we do not love the injustice they committed. A single person may combine good deeds and bad deeds, obedience and disobedience, righteousness, wickedness, and evil. Allah rewards him for his good deeds and punishes him for his bad deeds if He wills, or forgives him. He loves the good that person has done and hates the evil he has done. As for those whose sins are minor, even the Mu‘tazilah agree that Allah forgives them.

As for someone who commits a major sin, the early generations of the Muslim community, its imams, and all other Sunni Muslims do not declare him destined for Hell. Rather, they acknowledge that Allah may forgive him, as He Most High says:

“Indeed, Allah does not forgive associating partners with Him, but forgives anything less than that for whomever He wills.”

This applies to someone who has not associated partners with Allah, for He made such forgiveness conditional upon His will. As for His words, Most High:

“Say, ‘O My servants who have transgressed against themselves, do not despair of Allah’s mercy. Indeed, Allah forgives all sins.’”

This applies to someone who has repented; hence He stated it without qualification and included all sins.

The Khawārij and the Mu‘tazilah say that someone who commits a major sin will remain in Hell forever. Furthermore, they

---

## Page 1798

may mistakenly suppose that certain righteous people have committed major sins, just as the Khawārij mistakenly suppose that ‘Uthmān, ‘Alī, and their followers will remain in Hell forever, and just as some hold similar mistaken beliefs about Mu‘āwiya, ‘Amr ibn al-‘Ās, and others like them. They base their doctrines on two false premises:

The first is that a particular person has committed major sins.

The second is that everyone who commits a major sin will remain in Hell forever.

Both assertions are false. The second is categorically false. As for the first, its falsity may be known, or judgment on it may be withheld.

Anyone who claims that Mu‘āwiya and others like him—whose Islam, prayers, pilgrimage, and fasting were publicly evident—never embraced Islam and remained unbelievers is like someone who makes the same claim about others: al-‘Abbās, Ja‘far, ‘Aqīl, Abū Bakr, ‘Umar, or ‘Uthmān. It is likewise comparable to claiming that al-Hasan and al-Husayn were not the sons of ‘Alī ibn Abī Tālib, but rather the sons of Salmān al-Fārisī; or that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, did not marry the daughters of Abū Bakr and ‘Umar, and did not marry his two daughters to ‘Uthmān.

Indeed, denying Mu‘āwiya’s Islam is more outrageous than denying these matters, for some of them are known only to scholars. Mu‘āwiya’s Islam, however, and his authority over the Muslims as governor and caliph are matters known to the vast majority of people. If anyone were to deny ‘Alī’s Islam or claim that he remained an unbeliever, one could not argue

---

## Page 1799

against him except with the same evidence used against anyone who denies the Islam of Abū Bakr, ‘Umar, ‘Uthmān, Mu‘āwiya, and others. Although some were superior to others, their differences in merit do not change the fact that the Islam of all of them was publicly evident.

As for the claim that “Mu‘āwiya’s faith was hypocrisy,” this too is a fabricated lie. No Muslim scholar accused Mu‘āwiya of hypocrisy; rather, the scholars agree on the soundness of his Islam. Some of them withheld judgment concerning the soundness of his father Abū Sufyān’s Islam. As for Mu‘āwiya and his brother Yazīd, however, they did not dispute the soundness of their Islam, just as they did not dispute that of ‘Ikrima ibn Abī Jahl, Suhayl ibn ‘Amr, Safwān ibn Umayya, and others who embraced Islam at the conquest of Mecca.

How could a man govern the Muslims for forty years, first as a deputy and then as a ruler in his own right—leading them in the five daily prayers, delivering sermons and exhorting them, enjoining good and forbidding evil, enforcing the prescribed punishments among them, distributing among them their revenues acquired without fighting, their war booty, and their alms, and leading them on pilgrimage—yet conceal his hypocrisy from them all? Among them were many eminent Companions.

More telling still is that—praise be to Allah—not one of the Umayyad or Abbasid caliphs who exercised general authority was accused of heresy and hypocrisy. None of the Umayyads was accused of heresy and hypocrisy. Although one of them might be accused of some form of religious innovation or injustice, none of them was accused by the scholars of heresy and hypocrisy.

---

## Page 1800

Rather, those known for heresy and hypocrisy were the descendants of ‘Ubayd al-Qaddāh who ruled in Egypt and the Maghreb. They claimed to be descendants of ‘Alī, whereas they were actually descended from unbelievers. The scholars agreed in accusing them of heresy and hypocrisy. Likewise, some regional kings—the Būyid and other caliphs—were accused of heresy and hypocrisy. As for a caliph exercising general authority over the Muslims, Allah has spared the Muslims from having a heretic and hypocrite as their ruler. This is something that should be known and understood, for it is useful in this discussion.

The scholars agreed that Mu‘āwiya was the best king of this community. The four who preceded him were caliphs who followed the prophetic model, whereas he was the first king. His monarchy was marked by mercy, as stated in the hadith:

“Rule will take the form of prophethood and mercy, then a caliphate and mercy, then monarchy and mercy, then monarchy and coercion, then oppressive monarchy.”

His reign displayed such mercy, forbearance, and benefit to the Muslims that it is evident that his rule was better than that of other kings.

As for those who preceded him, they were caliphs who followed the prophetic model, for it is authentically reported that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“The caliphate following the prophetic model will last thirty years, then become a monarchy.”

Abū Bakr, ‘Umar, ‘Uthmān, and ‘Alī, may Allah be pleased with them, were the rightly guided caliphs and divinely guided leaders concerning whom the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said: “You must adhere to my Sunnah and the sunnah of the caliphs

---

## Page 1801

who are rightly guided after me. Hold fast to it and cling to it with your molar teeth. Beware of newly introduced matters, for every newly introduced matter is an innovation.”

Many people disputed ‘Alī’s caliphate, saying: “His time was a time of civil strife, and the community was not united during his rule.” One group said: “It is valid to appoint two caliphs: he was a caliph, and Mu‘āwiya was a caliph, because the community neither agreed upon him nor united under his caliphate.”

The correct position, upheld by the leading scholars, is that ‘Alī, may Allah be pleased with him, was one of the rightly guided caliphs, as established by this hadith. During his rule, ‘Alī called himself Commander of the Faithful, and the Companions addressed him by that title. Imam Ahmad ibn Hanbal said:

“Whoever does not count ‘Alī, may Allah be pleased with him, as the fourth caliph is more astray than his family’s donkey.”

Nevertheless, each caliph has his own rank. No one equals Abū Bakr and ‘Umar, as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Follow the two who come after me: Abū Bakr and ‘Umar.”

There was no disagreement among ‘Alī’s supporters who accompanied him concerning the precedence of Abū Bakr and ‘Umar. It is established through numerous reports that ‘Alī said: “If any man is brought before me who considers me superior to Abū Bakr and ‘Umar, I will flog him with the prescribed punishment for slander.”

Their disagreement concerned only ‘Uthmān and ‘Alī, may Allah be pleased with them both. However, ‘Uthmān’s precedence over ‘Alī was established by the agreement of the early Muslims to pledge allegiance to (‘Uthmān willingly, without coercion, after ‘Umar entrusted the choice of his successor to a council of six: ‘Uthmān, ‘Alī, Talha, al-Zubayr, Sa‘d

---

## Page 1802

and ‘Abd al-Rahmān ibn ‘Awf. “Three” relinquished their claim to it: Talha, al-Zubayr, and Sa‘d. The choice thus remained among “three”: ‘Uthmān, ‘Alī, and ‘Abd al-Rahmān. One of the two was then appointed, and ‘Abd al-Rahmān continued consulting the Emigrants, the Helpers, and those who followed them in righteousness for three days. He then reported that they regarded no one as equal to ‘Uthmān.

The account of his death and caliphate is lengthy; whoever wishes to learn it should consult the reports of trustworthy narrators. Allah knows best. May Allah bless our Prophet Muhammad and grant him peace.

---

## Page 1803

Shaykh al-Islām, may Allah have mercy on him, said:

Section

People have divided into three groups concerning “Yazīd” ibn Mu‘āwiya ibn Abī Sufyān: two extremes and a middle position.

Those at one extreme said that he was an unbeliever and a hypocrite, and that he sought to kill the grandson of the Messenger of Allah to vent his spite against the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, take revenge upon him, and avenge his grandfather ‘Utba, his grandfather’s brother Shayba, his maternal uncle al-Walīd ibn ‘Utba, and others whom the Prophet’s Companions—may Allah bless the Prophet and grant him peace—killed at the hands of ‘Alī ibn Abī Tālib and others at Badr and elsewhere.

They said: “These were grudges stemming from Badr and remnants of pre-Islamic ignorance.” They attributed these verses to him:

“When those laden camels appeared, and those heads … rose into view upon the hills of Jayrūn,
The raven croaked, and I said, ‘Lament or do not lament … for I have settled my scores with the Prophet.’”

They also said that he quoted the poetry which Ibn al-Ziba‘rā recited on the day of Uhud:

“Would that my elders at Badr had witnessed … the anguish of the Khazraj under the blows of spears

---

## Page 1804

We have slain many of their elders … and balanced it against Badr, so the score is even.”

They say other things of this kind as well. Such a view comes easily to the Rāfida, who declare Abū Bakr, ‘Umar, and ‘Uthmān unbelievers; declaring Yazīd an unbeliever is far easier still.

Those at the other extreme think that he was a righteous man and a just ruler, and that he was among the “Companions” born during the Prophet’s lifetime, whom the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, carried in his arms and blessed. Some of them even consider him superior to Abū Bakr and ‘Umar, while others go so far as to regard him as a prophet. They falsely attribute to “Shaykh ‘Adī” or to Hasan, who was killed, the claim that seventy friends of Allah had their faces turned away from the direction of prayer because they withheld judgment concerning Yazīd. This is the view of extremists among the ‘Adawiyya, the Kurds, and other misguided people like them.

Shaykh ‘Adī belonged to the Umayyads and was a righteous, devout, and virtuous man. Nothing is recorded from him indicating that he called them to anything other than the Sunnah upheld by others, such as Shaykh “Abū al-Faraj” al-Maqdisī, whose creed agreed with his. Yet they added lies and misguidance to the Sunnah: fabricated hadiths, false likening of Allah to His creation, excessive veneration of Shaykh ‘Adī and Yazīd, and excessive condemnation of the Rāfida, claiming that their repentance would not be accepted, along with other things.

Both views are plainly false to anyone possessing even the slightest reason and knowledge of these matters and the lives of earlier generations. Accordingly, neither view is attributed to any scholar known for adherence to the Sunnah, nor to any sensible person possessing sound judgment and experience.

---

## Page 1805

The third view is that he was one of the Muslim kings, with both good deeds and bad. He was not born until the caliphate of ‘Uthmān, and he was not an unbeliever. Nevertheless, it was because of him that al-Husayn was killed and the people of al-Harra suffered what they suffered. He was neither a Companion nor one of Allah’s righteous friends. This is the view generally held by people of sound judgment and knowledge who adhere to the Sunnah and the community.

They then divided into (three groups: one cursed him, another loved him, and a third neither reviled nor loved him. This last position is explicitly reported from Imam Ahmad and is followed by the moderate among his followers and other Muslims.

Sālih ibn Ahmad said:
