And Allah Most High says:

“In houses which Allah has permitted to be raised and in which His name is mentioned”—and the rest of the verse.

Allah Most High also says:

“Do not have sexual relations with them while you are staying in the mosques for worship.”

In the two Sahīh collections, he, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, is reported to have said:

“A man’s prayer in the mosque is twenty-five degrees superior to his prayer in his home or marketplace.” Another wording reads: “Congregational prayer is twenty-five degrees superior to the prayer any one of you offers individually.”

In the Sahīh collection, he, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, is reported to have said:

“The most burdensome prayers for the hypocrites are the night prayer and the dawn prayer. If they knew what reward they hold, they would come to them even if they had to crawl. I have indeed considered ordering the prayer to be commenced, then appointing a man to lead the people in prayer, and then setting out with men carrying bundles of firewood to people who do not attend the prayer, and burning their houses down upon them.”

In Sahīh Muslim, Abū Hurayra, may Allah be pleased with him, said:

“A blind man came to the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and said: ‘O Messenger of Allah, I have no guide to lead me to the mosque.’ He asked Allah’s Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, to allow him to pray at home, so he granted permission

---

## Page 1847

to him. When he turned to leave, he called him back and asked: ‘Do you hear the call to prayer?’ He replied: ‘Yes.’ He said: ‘Then answer it.’”

The same collection also reports that Abū Saʿīd, may Allah be pleased with him, said:

“Whoever wishes to meet Allah tomorrow as a Muslim should diligently observe these prayers wherever the call to them is made. Allah has prescribed for your Prophet the ways of guidance, and these prayers are among those ways. If you prayed in your homes as this man who stays away prays in his home, you would abandon your Prophet’s sunnah; and if you abandoned your Prophet’s sunnah, you would go astray. Whenever a man purifies himself thoroughly and then sets out for one of these mosques, Allah records a good deed for every step he takes, raises him a degree thereby, and removes a sin from him thereby. I remember a time among us when no one stayed away from these prayers except a hypocrite whose hypocrisy was known. Indeed, a man would be brought, supported between two men, until he was placed in the prayer row.”

This is a broad subject. What we have written points to the path of guidance in this matter, which distinguishes the upright adherents of monotheism—the followers of Abraham’s faith who adhere to the religion of Allah with which He sent His messengers and revealed His Books—from those who mix truth with falsehood and taint pure monotheism with the association of partners with Allah.

Allah Most High says:

“And ask those of Our messengers whom We sent before you: ‘Did We appoint any gods besides the Most Merciful to be worshipped?’”

Allah Most High also says:

“We never sent a messenger before you without revealing to him: ‘There is no deity except Me, so worship Me.’”

---

## Page 1848

Allah Most High also says:

“We certainly sent a messenger to every nation, saying: ‘Worship Allah and shun false objects of worship.’”

Allah Most High also says:

“They were commanded only to worship Allah, devoting their religion sincerely to Him and inclining to the truth,” to the end of the verse.

Allah Most High also says:

“So set your face toward the religion, inclining to the truth. Adhere to the natural disposition upon which Allah created mankind. There is no changing Allah’s creation. That is the upright religion, but most people do not know. Turn to Him in repentance, fear Him, establish prayer, and do not be among those who associate partners with Him—those who divided their religion and became sects, each faction rejoicing in what it has.”

And Allah, glorified and exalted, knows best.

---

## Page 1849

Shaykh al-Islām, may Allah have mercy on him, said:

Section

As for the Companions and the Successors, more than one of the imams stated that everyone who accompanied the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, is superior, without qualification, to anyone who did not accompany him. They specifically applied this to the comparison between Mu‘āwiya and ‘Umar ibn ‘Abd al-‘Azīz, despite acknowledging that ‘Umar ibn ‘Abd al-‘Azīz’s conduct was more just than Mu‘āwiya’s. They said, however, that the rank the Companions attained through companionship cannot be matched by what others attain through their knowledge.

They cited as evidence his saying, recorded in the two Sahīh collections:

“Do not revile my Companions. By Him in whose hand is my soul, were any one of you to spend gold equal to Mount Uhud, it would not equal a mudd given by one of them, nor even half of it.”

They said: If gold equal to Mount Uhud does not equal half a mudd given by one of them, this disparity in merit makes clear that no one has attained ranks like those they reached through accompanying the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. This issue calls for an extensive discussion and explanation for which there is no room here.

---

## Page 1850

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked about two men who disagreed concerning someone who reviled Abū Bakr. One said, “Allah will accept his repentance,” while the other said, “Allah will not accept his repentance.”

He answered:

The correct view, held by the imams of the Muslims, is that Allah accepts the repentance of everyone who repents, as Allah Most High says:

“Say, ‘O My servants who have transgressed against themselves, do not despair of the mercy of Allah. Indeed, Allah forgives all sins. Indeed, He is the All-Forgiving, the Most Merciful.’”

In this verse, He states that He forgives all sins for whoever repents; hence, He leaves forgiveness unqualified and all-inclusive. In the other verse, He says:

“Indeed, Allah does not forgive associating partners with Him, but He forgives anything less than that for whom He wills.”

This concerns someone who has not repented; hence, He qualifies and limits forgiveness here. Reviling some of the Companions is no graver than reviling the prophets or reviling Allah Most High. The Jews and Christians who revile our Prophet privately among themselves have their repentance and acceptance of Islam accepted when they repent and embrace Islam, by the consensus of the Muslims. The report narrated as a hadith:

“Reviling my Companions is a sin that will not be forgiven”

is a lie falsely attributed to the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Even the sin of associating partners with Allah, which Allah does not forgive, He forgives

---

## Page 1851

for those who repent, by the consensus of the Muslims. As for the claim that this involves a right owed to another human being, it may be answered in two ways:

First: Allah has commanded thieves, those who call others offensive nicknames, and others who commit sins involving people’s rights to repent, as in His saying:

“As for the thief, male or female, cut off their hands in recompense for what they committed, as a deterrent from Allah. And Allah is Exalted in Might and Wise.”

“But whoever repents after his wrongdoing and reforms, Allah will indeed turn to him in forgiveness. Indeed, Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful.”

He also says:

“And do not insult one another with offensive nicknames. Wretched is the name of disobedience after faith. And whoever does not repent—it is they who are the wrongdoers.”

Part of such a person’s repentance is to compensate the person wronged by doing good to him in proportion to the harm inflicted upon him.

Second: These people act on an interpretation. If the Rāfidī repents of that, acknowledges the virtue of the Companions, loves them, and prays for them, Allah has replaced his evil deed with a good deed, as He does for other sinners.

---

## Page 1852

He was also asked about a “group” who had come together to engage in various forms of wrongdoing. Among them were those who, whenever hadiths of the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, narrated by “ʿAbd Allāh ibn Masʿūd” were read to them, or they were told, “This is the position of ʿAbd Allāh ibn Masʿūd,” began disparaging him and attacking his reputation, describing him as a weak narrator. They claimed that he was held in low esteem among the Companions, to the extent that some of them did not include his reading in the copies of the Quran, and that he used to omit the two surahs of refuge from the Quran?

He replied, may Allah have mercy on him:

“Ibn Masʿūd,” may Allah be pleased with him, was among the most illustrious and eminent Companions. Indeed, ʿUmar ibn al-Khattāb used to describe him as:

“A small vessel filled with knowledge.”

Abū Mūsā said:

“We regarded ‘ʿAbd Allāh ibn Masʿūd’ as none other than a member of the household of Allah’s Messenger, peace and blessings be upon him, because we so often saw him coming and going.”

The Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said to him:

“You have my permission to lift the curtain and listen to my private conversation, unless I forbid you.”

The Sunan collections record:

“Follow the two who come after me, Abū Bakr and ʿUmar, and hold fast to the guidance of Ibn Umm ʿAbd.”

The Sahīh records:

“Whoever wishes to recite the Quran fresh as it was revealed should recite it according to the reading of Ibn Umm ʿAbd.”

When ʿUmar conquered Iraq, he sent Ibn Masʿūd to its people to teach them the Book and the Sunnah. He was thus the most knowledgeable of the Companions

---

## Page 1853

whom he sent to Iraq. Abū Mūsā said of him:

“Do not ask me about anything so long as this learned man is among you.”

Ibn Masʿūd used to say:

“If I knew of anyone more knowledgeable about the Book of Allah than myself whom camels could reach, I would go to him.”

He was one of the three whom Muʿādh ibn Jabal named on his deathbed. When Mālik ibn Yukhāmir al-Saksakī wept, Muʿādh ibn Jabal asked him:

“What makes you weep?”

He replied:

“By Allah, I am not weeping because of any kinship between you and me, nor for any worldly benefit I receive from you. Rather, I weep for the knowledge and faith that I used to learn from you.”

Muʿādh said:

“Knowledge and faith remain available; whoever seeks them will find them. Seek knowledge from four people. If these are unable to help you, the rest of the people on earth will be less able still.”

He named Ibn Masʿūd, Ubayy ibn Kaʿb and ʿAbd Allāh ibn Salām; I believe the fourth was Abū al-Dardāʾ.

ʿAlī was asked about the learned men among the people. He replied:

“There is one in Iraq: Ibn Masʿūd.”

In knowledge, Ibn Masʿūd was of the same rank as ʿUmar, ʿAlī, Ubayy and Muʿādh. He belonged to the foremost rank of scholars among the Companions. Whoever disparages him or says that he is a weak narrator is therefore of the same ilk as the Rāfidah, who disparage Abū Bakr, ʿUmar and ʿUthmān. This indicates either his extreme ignorance of the Companions or his heresy and hypocrisy.

---

## Page 1854

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked:

A man was debating with another about “the case of an animal whose milk has been allowed to accumulate in its udder” and whether the buyer may return it if he wishes. The man who maintained that returning it was permissible cited the hadith of Abū Hurayrah recorded by both al-Bukhārī and Muslim. His opponent objected, saying:

“Abū Hurayrah was not one of the jurists among the Companions. ʿUmar ibn al-Khattāb rebuked him for narrating so many reports, forbade him to narrate hadith, and said: ‘If you resume narrating, I shall do such-and-such to you.’ Ibn ʿAbbās and ʿĀ’ishah likewise objected to certain things he reported.”

Is what this opponent said correct or not? What is required of someone who speaks about Abū Hurayrah in this manner?

He answered:

Praise be to Allah. This objector is mistaken in several respects.

First, he claims that Abū Hurayrah was not one of the jurists among the Companions. Yet ʿUmar ibn al-Khattāb appointed Abū Hurayrah governor of Bahrain, whose people were among the finest Muslims. Their delegation had migrated to the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace: the delegation of “ʿAbd al-Qays.” Abū Hurayrah, their governor, was the one who gave them legal rulings on intricate questions of jurisprudence, such as “the question of

---

## Page 1855

a divorced woman” who has been divorced fewer than three times: if she marries another husband who consummates the marriage, does she return to her first husband with three divorces available to him anew? This is the position of Ibn ʿAbbās and Ibn ʿUmar, the school of Abū Hanīfah, and one view reported from ʿUmar. It rests on the understanding that consummation with another husband erases the effect of fewer than three divorces just as it erases the effect of three. Or does she return with only the remaining number of divorces available to her first husband? This is the position of ʿUmar and other senior Companions, and the view adopted by Mālik, al-Shāfiʿī, and Ahmad, according to the well-known report from him. It rests on the understanding that consummation with the second husband merely marks the end of the prohibition established by three divorces: that prohibition alone is removed by it. A woman divorced fewer than three times has not become forbidden to her first husband, so consummation removes nothing in her case. Abū Hurayrah gave his ruling according to this latter view.

He then asked ʿUmar, who endorsed his ruling and said: “Had you ruled otherwise, I would have given you a painful beating.” That Abū Hurayrah likewise gave rulings on intricate “questions of jurisprudence” alongside the jurists among the Companions, such as Ibn ʿAbbās and others, is very well known. His transmitted legal opinions demonstrate this.

Even if ʿUmar and ʿAlī were more learned in jurisprudence than ʿImrān ibn Husayn and Abū Mūsā al-Ashʿarī, that did not disqualify the latter two as jurists. Likewise, even if Muʿādh, Ibn Masʿūd, and others were more learned in jurisprudence than Abū Hurayrah, ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUmar, and others, that did not disqualify the latter as jurists.

(Second: This objector should be told that all the scholars of the Muslim community have acted upon hadith narrated by Abū Hurayrah even where they run counter to analogical reasoning and the apparent meaning of the texts. Thus, they all acted upon his report that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“A man must not be married to a woman and her paternal or maternal aunt at the same time.”

Abū Hanīfah also acted

---

## Page 1856

along with al-Shāfiʿī, Ahmad, and others, upon his report from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“Whoever eats or drinks forgetfully should complete his fast, for it is Allah who has fed him and given him drink.”

Yet analogical reasoning, according to Abū Hanīfah, would mean that his fast is broken. He therefore set aside analogical reasoning in favour of Abū Hurayrah’s hadith. Similar examples are too numerous to recount. Mālik, together with al-Shāfiʿī and Ahmad, acted upon Abū Hurayrah’s hadith requiring a vessel to be washed seven times after a dog laps from it, even though analogical reasoning, according to Mālik, would require no washing, since he considers it ritually pure. Indeed, the imams set aside analogical reasoning for reports less firmly established than Abū Hurayrah’s hadith. Thus, Abū Hanīfah set it aside in the question of “loud laughter” on the strength of a report lacking the Companion link, whose Companion narrator is unknown. By the consensus of the Muslim community, Abū Hurayrah’s hadith is more firmly established than that report.

(Third: This objector should be told that when a hadith narrator accurately retains the wording he has heard, it does not matter that he is not a jurist. The same applies to those who teach the words of the Quran, the tashahhud (testimony recited in prayer), the call to prayer, and the like. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“May Allah brighten the face of a person who hears a hadith and conveys it to someone who has not heard it. For many a bearer of legal knowledge is not a jurist, and many a bearer of legal knowledge conveys it to someone more learned in jurisprudence than himself.”

This clearly shows that his hadith containing legal knowledge is accepted from a transmitter who is not a jurist, and that one may receive it from someone less learned in jurisprudence than oneself. Legal understanding is needed in transmission only when a report is conveyed by meaning and there is concern that a non-jurist might unwittingly alter its meaning. “Abū Hurayrah” was among those in the Muslim community with the strongest memories, and the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prayed that he be granted “a retentive memory.” He said: “After that, I never forgot anything I heard.” Thus, he narrated the hadith concerning the animal whose milk had been allowed to accumulate in its udder, and other hadith, in the very words of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace.

---

## Page 1857

Fourth:
All the Companions accepted and acted upon Abū Hurayrah’s hadith, including ʿUmar, Ibn ʿUmar, Ibn ʿAbbās, and ʿĀʾishah. Anyone who examines the hadith collections will recognize this.

Fifth:
None of the Companions—not ʿUmar or anyone else—criticized anything narrated by Abū Hurayrah by saying, “He was mistaken in this hadith.” Rather, Abū Hurayrah would sit beside ʿĀʾishah’s chamber, narrating hadith and saying:

“O lady of this chamber, do you dispute anything I am saying?”

When ʿĀʾishah finished her prayer, she did not dispute what he had narrated, but said:

“The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, did not relate hadith in rapid succession as you do. Rather, he would speak in such a manner that someone counting his words could have memorized them.”

Thus, she objected to his manner of delivery, not to what he conveyed.

Likewise, Ibn ʿUmar was asked:

“Do you dispute anything that Abū Hurayrah narrates?”

He replied:

“No, but he narrated, while we held back out of apprehension.”

Abū Hurayrah said, “What fault is it of mine if I remembered and they forgot?”

They considered the sheer number of his narrations extraordinary, to the point that some would say, “Abū Hurayrah narrates too much.” In response,

{Abū Hurayrah said: “People say, ‘Abū Hurayrah narrates too much,’ and before Allah we shall meet. My brothers among the Emigrants were kept busy trading in the marketplaces, while my brothers among the Helpers were kept busy tending their properties. I was a poor man who stayed close to the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Thus, I was present when they were absent, and I remembered when they forgot. The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, once related something to us, then said, ‘Which of you will spread out his garment?’ I spread out my garment, and he prayed for me. Thereafter, I never forgot anything I heard from him, may Allah bless him and grant him peace.”}

---

## Page 1858

It is also reported that he divided the night into “three parts”: one third spent praying, one third reviewing hadith, and one third sleeping. He thus explained that his retention was due to his constant companionship with the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, his setting aside other attachments, and the Prophet’s supplication for him.

ʿUmar ibn al-Khattāb would ask Abū Hurayrah to relate hadith and question him about them. He neither forbade him to transmit the knowledge people needed that he had heard from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, nor threatened him for doing so. Rather, ʿUmar favoured careful verification in narration, lest people grow audacious and additions be made to hadith. For this reason, he asked Abū Mūsā al-Ashʿarī to bring someone who could corroborate his narration concerning permission to enter, even though the leading scholars unanimously regarded Abū Mūsā as one of the foremost and most trustworthy Companions.

(Sixth:
On questions of Islamic law, the Companions would consult people less versed in it than Abū Hurayrah. Thus, ʿUmar ibn al-Khattāb consulted Hamal ibn Mālik and others concerning “compensation for the loss of a fetus”; ʿUthmān ibn ʿAffān consulted al-Furayʿah bint Mālik concerning a widow’s obligation to remain in “the home she occupied when her husband died”; ʿUmar ibn al-Khattāb and others consulted al-Dahhāk ibn Sufyān al-Kilābī concerning “a wife’s inheritance from the blood money paid for her husband”; and Zayd ibn Thābit and others consulted a woman from the Helpers concerning the exemption of a menstruating woman from the farewell circumambulation.

---

## Page 1859

Likewise, when Ibn Masʿūd ruled that “a widow whose dower had not been specified” was entitled to the dower customary for women of her standing, men from Ashjaʿ stood up and testified: “The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, gave the same ruling concerning Birwaʿ bint Wāshiq as you have given.” ʿAbd Allāh was overjoyed at this. Abū Bakr al-Siddīq also granted a grandmother a share of inheritance on the basis of the hadith reported by al-Mughīrah ibn Shuʿbah and Muhammad ibn Salamah. There are many similar examples.

(Seventh:
It may be said that someone who rejects Abū Hurayrah’s hadith concerning “an animal whose milk has been allowed to accumulate in its udder” argues that it contradicts established legal principles or analogy based on those principles.

The answer is: Rather, this case should be treated like comparable cases in which the scriptural texts were followed. This hadith concerns a case that differs from others, not one that resembles them. Analogy means treating like cases alike and distinguishing between unlike cases.

The objection raised by someone who rejects it is this: It establishes the right to return defective goods and fixes the compensation for what has been consumed. Yet, if the item is replaceable in kind, compensation should be made with its equivalent; otherwise, its value should be paid. Here, however, compensation is neither an equivalent nor its value. Moreover, liability is imposed on the buyer, whereas the yield belongs to the one who bears liability.

The answer is: By agreement of the leading scholars, the right to return an item arises both from deceptive misrepresentation and from its failure to possess the stated characteristics. “The person who misrepresents the goods,” making them appear to possess a characteristic they do not possess, is like someone who describes them as possessing it in words. This kind of option to rescind is distinct from the option to return goods because of a defect.

---

## Page 1860

It is also said in reply: The buyer is not liable for the milk produced while the animal was his property. Rather, he is liable for what was already in its udder. When he purchased the animal whose milk had been allowed to accumulate, it contained milk that was subsequently consumed while in his possession, and he therefore became liable for it. The Lawgiver fixed the compensation only because the original milk became mixed with the newly produced milk, so that the quantity of the original milk could no longer be determined.

It was therefore impossible to compensate for it with either its equivalent or its value. Accordingly, the Lawgiver fixed compensation that would settle the dispute, just as He fixed compensation for loss of life, bodily organs and their functions, and other prescribed amounts that settle disputes between people. When the amount rightfully due can be determined, that is what must be paid. When this is impossible, the Lawgiver prescribes the most appropriate method and the one closest to securing what is rightfully due.

Thus, He sometimes directs that estimation be used when measuring by volume or weight is impossible, allowing a probable assessment to stand in place of certain knowledge when certainty is unattainable. He also directs that lots be drawn to identify the entitled person when this is entirely unclear. At other times, He fixes compensation for what is due when there is no other way to end the dispute. The buyer’s payment of a sāʿ (a measure of volume) in compensation for the milk taken falls into this category.

There is a second account concerning this issue, related by Abū Saʿīd ibn al-Samʿānī from the spiritually discerning shaykh Yūsuf al-Hamadhānī, from the jurist Shaykh Abū Ishāq al-Shīrāzī, from Judge Abū al-Tayyib al-Tabarī, who said:

“We were sitting in the congregational mosque in Baghdad when a man from Khurasan came and asked us about an animal whose milk had been allowed to accumulate in its udder. We answered his question, citing Abū Hurayrah’s hadith as evidence, whereupon he disparaged Abū Hurayrah

---

## Page 1861

and a snake fell from the ceiling, entered our circle, approached that non-Arab man, and struck him, killing him.”

A similar incident is related by al-Tabarānī in his “Book of the Sunnah,” from Zakariyyā ibn Yahyā al-Sājī, who said:

“We used to visit certain shaykhs to hear the hadith of Allah’s Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. We quickened our pace, accompanied by an irreverent young man. He said, ‘Lift your feet off the angels’ wings! Do not break them!’”

He continued: “He carried on until his legs failed him.”

There are other similar incidents. We ask Allah Most High to enable us to hold fast to His Book and the Sunnah of His Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and to follow the evidence He has established. Allah, glorified is He, knows best.

---

## Page 1862

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was also asked:

There is a group of Muslims who affirm the two testimonies of faith, fast, perform Hajj, pay zakat, and strive against their own souls to please Allah. Yet they declare those who insult the Companions of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, to be unbelievers. They hold out no hope that anyone who repents of this will have his repentance accepted, and maintain that whoever persists in it will remain in Hell forever. They call those who affirm that such people’s repentance can be accepted “al-Rajawiyya.” They pray only with people whose creed they have ascertained. None of them says anything or asks about anything without saying, “If Allah wills.” Are they correct in their actions, or mistaken in their statements?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. These people are Muslims, entitled to what other Muslims like them are entitled to. Allah rewards them for their faith in Allah and His Messenger and their obedience to Allah and His Messenger. Their mistakes in these matters do not nullify their faith and piety. They are like other groups of Muslims who are correct in most of what they believe and practise, but mistaken in a few matters. These people therefore have the same standing as other Muslims like them.

---

## Page 1863

And their claim that the repentance of someone who insults the Companions is not accepted, and that he will remain in Hell forever, is mistaken. Rather, the position of “the early generations and the imams,” including the four imams and others, is that a Rāfidī’s repentance is accepted just as the repentance of others like him is accepted. The purported hadith, “Insulting my Companions is an unforgivable sin,” is baseless; no scholar has transmitted it. Even if it were assumed to be authentic, it would refer to someone who does not repent, for Allah will exact from him what is due to the Companions.

As for someone who repents, Allah Most High has said:

“Say, ‘O My servants who have transgressed against themselves, do not despair of the mercy of Allah. Indeed, Allah forgives all sins.’”

This concerns the repentant: He declares that He forgives all sins. If someone insults the Companions believing that doing so is permissible, he is a misguided innovator, like others who have gone astray, and the right involved here belongs to Allah. This is like someone who insults the Messenger, believing him to be a sorcerer or a liar: if that person embraces Islam, Allah accepts his Islam. Likewise, when the truth becomes clear to a Rāfidī and he repents, Allah accepts his repentance. If, however, he acknowledges that such conduct is forbidden, he is a wrongdoer, like someone who slanders and backbites another. Repentance from wrongs committed against others is valid. He should pray for them and praise them in proportion to how much he cursed and insulted them, for good deeds erase evil deeds.

If someone says, “This is a stone,” yet also says, “I cannot say with certainty that this is a stone,” he is mistaken. But if he means, “If I assert with certainty that it is a stone, I have thereby declared Allah incapable of changing it,” he should be told: “Rather, it is certainly a stone now, and Allah is able

---

## Page 1864

to change it.” If, by saying “if Allah wills,” he means that Allah is able to change it, this meaning is correct. But if he doubts whether it is a stone, he is feigning ignorance and should receive disciplinary punishment for doing so.

It is permissible to pray behind any Muslim whose inward state is unknown, by agreement of the four imams and all the other imams of the Muslims. Whoever says, “I will not perform Friday prayer or congregational prayer except behind someone whose inward beliefs I know,” is an innovator who opposes the Companions, those who followed them in righteousness, and the imams of the Muslims—the four imams and others. And Allah knows best.
